                           Introduction

                    by T. A. B. CORLEY


Otto Wolff (1881-1940) was a rarity among biographers, a
businessman who found the time, while presiding over the
fortunes of a vast industrial and commercial empire which he had
built up, to write the life of an earlier man of affairs. The fact that
the author’s life-work—unlike that of his subject—endured after
his death, in spite of war, defeat, inflation and reconstruction,
shows that he was well qualified to assess Ouvrard’s gifts and
limitations. He was familiar with the world of financial deals and
with the lore of the commodity and stock markets; similarly he
fully understood the subtle and often intricate relationship be¬
tween commerce, politics and diplomacy and the problems of
survival and success in an epoch of violent changes in the social
and political scene both at home and abroad.
  As he tells us in his Preface, Wolff was primarily interested in
probing the real relationship between economics and politics, a
subject that today still presents a challenge to historians and
economists alike. In particular, we still do not know nearly
enough about the economics of the period 1793-1815, that lies at
the centre of Ouvrard’s life; indeed, except for the nature and
immediate effects of the Continental System, the economic
aspects of this turbulent period of European history have not yet
been described in a coherent form to the English reader. This bio¬
graphy of a Frenchman, who was the leading army contractor and
financier of many of the campaigns that took place at that time,
therefore helps to fill a gap and to illuminate some hitherto
VUl                         Introduction

unsuspected connections between economic trends, problems of
logistics, and political, diplomatic and strategic events.
   Ouvrard’s enterprises and operations were on such a grand
scale, so abundantly was he endowed with imagination, resource¬
fulness and sheer animal spirits, so impressively did he combine
the lack of scruples of a financial soldier of fortune with out¬
standing powers of improvisation and innovation, that the con¬
temporary comparisons between him and Napoleon no longer
appear as entirely without substance. “I was born with a passion
for big business, which I find essential to the full activity of my
mind”, wrote Ouvrard in his Memoirs. “It gives me a kind of
gambler’s excitement which neither experience nor reason can
overcome.” Together with his genuine passion for gambling he
had the looks, bearing, spontaneous generosity and charm of
manner of the man of the world. These qualities and his unfailing
resilience made him capable time and again of rapid moral and
intellectual adjustment to the sudden transition from palace to
prison. And, as Wolff shows, he had his moments of greatness, of
constructive foresight and economic vision, which carried him at
times to the forefront of the stage of European history.
   The star of this great drama was of course Napoleon, who at
once hated Ouvrard and found him indispensable in times of
necessity. Not unexpectedly, there were close similarities in the
circumstances of the two men’s rise to fame. They were contem¬
poraries: Napoleon was born in 1769 and Ouvrard in 1770. Both
men courted official suspicion by their activities after 1789 and,
having come to Paris at the age of twenty-two in order to plead
their respective cases with the authorities, they became rapidly
drawn into the centre of affairs, partly through their remarkable
gifts and partly through getting to know the right people: the
Director Barras, Josephine de Beauharnais and Madame Tallien.
   While Ouvrard did not endear himself to the Directory or to
Napoleon by the sixty million francs he amassed between 1795
and 1799 as army contractor and naval victualler-in-chief, he did
nevertheless tender very sound advice to the Directors about the
folly of feeding inflation by continuing to print unbacked paper
money—the notorious assignats—and about the need to fund the
                            Introduction                         ix


national debt in an attempt to mobilize the country’s resources.
After the coup d’etat of the 18th Brumaire, Napoleon had to call
on him to supply the Marengo campaign of 1800 and to relieve
the severe famine of 1802. Yet Ouvrard could not expect any
favour of the First Consul, whom he later criticized in his
memoirs as “knowing no other sources of revenue but taxation
and conquest”. Napoleon, indeed, was constantly trying to
squeeze Ouvrard’s war profits out of him. Ouvrard chose not to
resist openly, but to bide his time and meanwhile to apply himself
to building up financial empires behind “men of straw” and to
salting away his wealth beyond the reach of the authorities.
   Wolff has rightly described his agreement of December 1804
with the King of Spain as “unparalleled in economic or political
history”. Its main object was to bring Spanish treasure worth
more than 260 million francs—nearly twice the combined
reserves of the Bank of France and the Bank of England at that
time—through the British blockade. It is amazing how nearly he
succeeded. The bold conception and the incisive analysis of a
given situation was, once more, sustained by his personal powers
of persuasion and charm of manner, which later captivated even
the crusty Duke of Wellington. Thus he managed to win over all
the men whose services were essential to his scheme, from
Napoleon and his ministers to the two foremost bankers in
Europe, Hope of Amsterdam and Baring of London. What almost
surpasses belief is that Baring, at Ouvrard’s behest, should have
persuaded Pitt to despatch four frigates of the Royal Navy in
order to bring back the first sixty millions. The Bank of England,
however, badly needed the bullion in order to fortify its reserves,
as British subsidies to Allied governments and other war expen¬
diture overseas totalled for 1805 and 1806 alone the equivalent
of seventy-five million francs. Once more Ouvrard’s grasp of
circumstances and opportunities shows the touch of a master.
   Pitt’s less sagacious successors refused to allow the other 200
millions to pass through the blockade as specie. In accordance
with Ouvrard’s alternative plan, therefore, this vast wealth
streamed across the Atlantic in the form of colonial goods, bought
in the United States with the Mexican piastres and carried to
X                           Introduction

Continental ports in neutral ships. Nelson’s old captains, still
maintaining their endless vigil offshore, shook their heads over
this apparent wide-open breach in the blockade. Yet although the
French benefited directly or indirectly from the use of these goods,
the British, living up to Napoleon’s contemptuous characteriza¬
tion as a “nation of shopkeepers”, were rapidly expanding their
exports of manufactured goods to the New World in order both
to acquire the piastres and to replace the markets now closed to
them on the Continent through Napoleon’s decrees.
    Wolff suggests that the whole gigantic operation collapsed as
a result of the Emperor’s high-handed interference. However,
later research has revealed that it continued until its completion
in the summer of 1808, interrupted only temporarily by Jefferson’s
Embargo Act of 1807 which forbade American ships to sail to
foreign ports. Yet it was regarded as important enough for the
United States Secretary of the Treasury specifically to exempt it.
The total proceeds did not come up to Ouvrard’s original expec¬
tations, but world trade benefited by the millions of francs’ worth
of bullion that had been drawn into international circulation, and
a number of the main parties did well enough out of the trans¬
action. Barings, Hopes and their American agents pocketed
twenty-two million francs, while Ouvrard’s own share was
allegedly a further twenty-four millions.
    Throughout the years of Napoleon’s ascendancy, Ouvrard’s
foremost allies were characteristically Talleyrand and Fouche,
These three great pragmatists and opportunists knew better than
anyone how to anticipate subtle shifts in social and political
power. Thus they formed a harmonious trio, as each member
could only profit from co-operation with the others—the wily
diplomatist, the astute and ever-alert police chief and the in¬
dispensable financier. That the two former were in the latter’s pay
most of the time was of equal benefit to all three: but that was
not the whole story, for irrespective of such simple monetary
considerations, their affinity of outlook, method of operation and
interest would surely have brought them together. In consequence
the three of them, by deliberate choice, or impelled by circum¬
stances beyond their control, were bound to part company with
                            Introduction                          xt

the Emperor, once he appeared to have embarked irrevocably on
his collision course against the rest of Europe and specifically
against Britain and Russia. As soon as it became clear that no
manoeuvre and no persuasion could have stopped Napoleon and
healed the fundamental rift in European power, all three—though
each in his different way—were waiting for the day of reckoning,
biding their time in readiness for a new regime to take over after
the collapse of the Empire, which now seemed to them un¬
avoidable.
  Although Napoleon was enlightened enough to establish the
Bank of France and lay the foundations of an equitable taxation
system, to the end he adhered to his outdated weapons of econo¬
mic warfare, namely indemnities and blockades. As Ouvrard
said in his Memoirs, “Napoleon, when he came to power, knew
no other means of replenishing his Treasury than those he had
employed as general to fill his military chest: war indemnities, the
spoliation of sovereigns and peoples, subsidies imposed on his
allies—who were often as ill-treated as his enemies—blockades,
ports closed to free commerce and opened by licences.... Credit
was to him an abstract idea, in which he saw merely the dreams of
ideologists and the empty notions of economists.” The Emperor
even smugly pointed to the Rothschilds’ shipments of bullion
across the Channel from 1811 onwards as evidence that his
blockade was driving Britain to her knees. He never grasped that
the network of international finance was, by means of bills and
smuggled letters of credit, simply transferring the money to
Wellington’s Peninsular Army, which would otherwise have
starved for lack of supplies. Ouvrard could have explained all
this, and on the eve of the Russian campaign of 1812 he did try
to impress on the Emperor, by letter, that in the end it is cheaper
to pay for supplies than to requisition them. As it was, he was
kept in more or less close confinement until 1814 when, as
Talleyrand’s protege, he accompanied the Bourbons on their
triumphal re-entry into Paris.
  This change of loyalties did not deter Napoleon from summon¬
ing him urgently to the Tuileries at the outset of the Hundred
Days, appointing him army contractor-in-chief and extracting
XU                          Introduction

from him a loan of fifty million francs, cash down. Ouvrard was
present on the field of Waterloo, but the Bourbons were not too
proud thereafter to make use of his talents or to follow the
Napoleonic practice of squeezing large sums out of him. Even if
the grand project of paying off the Allied reparations by borrow¬
ing the funds from the Allies themselves was not solely Ouvrard’s
intellectual property, he certainly played an important and
characteristically audacious part in its conception and execution.
This operation successfully mitigated the financial plight of the
Bourbons and in 1818 prematurely terminated the occupation of
France. Thus Ouvrard and his friends anticipated by a century
the similar solution to the German reparations problem after the
First World War. Incidentally, how well does the ingenuity—
and one may almost say, painless elegance—with which the
great bankers and statesmen of old Europe arranged matters
compare with the tortuous and dilatory methods of their succes¬
sors in the twenties of this century!
     The memory of this momentous operation had hardly begun
to fade before Ouvrard figured prominently and with un¬
diminished powers in the highly bizarre episode of the French
invasion of Spain in 1823. France had a quarrel with her neigh¬
bour; the army was massed on the Pyrenees. The Duke of
Angouleme arrived at Bayonne to take command, and found an
army virtually without provisions for man or beast. He therefore
did what all sensible men in those straits had done for the past
thirty years: he sent for Ouvrard. Ouvrard, having foreseen this
situation, had already cornered the market in those commodities
that the army most needed. He then put into practice his maxim
of paying handsomely for the necessary supplies in Spain, and
thus did precisely what Napoleon had so disastrously failed to do
during the Peninsular War a decade earlier. Now, fortified by
ample provisions, the royal Commander-in-Chief emulated the
grand old Duke of York by marching all the way to Cadiz via
Madrid—with Ouvrard energetically buying ahead even as far
afield as Morocco—and then marching back again in triumph to
Paris. French honour was amply satisfied; that is, until Ouvrard
presented the bill. The subsequent affaire Ouvrard raged for
                            Introduction                         xui

years in and out of the Chambers, the courts, commissions of
enquiry and the Press. The cause of it all, once more behind bars,
added to the sensation by writing his memoirs.
  After that episode, a slow decline in his fortunes set in. Ouvrard
was eventually released from prison and no doubt he was still
able to back his assessment of trends, which rarely failed him. He
was still able to play the market with a nimble but firm grip on
operations. That neither zest nor cunning had deserted him was
amply borne out by his large-scale bear operations against the
French rentes. He had convinced himself that the Bourbons were
on the way out and that the Rothschilds, by then the established
government bankers, were still backing the regime and thus the
wrong horse. But these were considerable though temporary
successes, brilliant coups which had no lasting impact on affairs,
on an historical scale. The Rothschilds and their competitors,
imitators and successors were by then “in” for keeps, and Ouvrard
was “out”. At the end Ouvrard, the one-time grand seigneur of
Rainey, died in 1846 in a small apartment in London, off Oxford
Street, of no account now, and entirely oblivious of the fever of
railway speculation that then raged in the country.
  In a sense Ouvrard was born before his time, as have been most
bold and imaginative innovators, not only in the field of finance
and commercial enterprise. Unlike the Comte de Saint-Simon—■
who had also grown rich and had lived in splendour under the
Directory—he gave little conscious thought to the future of
man and of society. Nevertheless, the promoters who impelled
France into her most rapid rate of growth in the nineteenth
century owed their economic ideas partly to Saint-Simon and
partly to Ouvrard. The watchword of the Pereire brothers,
founders of the Credit Mobilier, was almost identical with his
own phrase that “the present can be safeguarded only by mort¬
gaging the future”; like him, they eventually overreached them¬
selves and vanished into obscurity.
  Nor did his vision of exploiting the New World’s untold
wealth die with him. Whilst in prison, in 1826, he sketched out a
project for linking the Atlantic and Pacific oceans by means of a
canal through Nicaragua; exactly twenty years later a political
XIV                           Introduction

prisoner, who happened also to be Bonaparte’s nephew and heir,
wrote a pamphlet on the same topic. Louis Napoleon grasped the
boundless economic possibilities of Mexico and, when he had
become Emperor, launched his disastrous expedition against that
country.
    Ouvrard had another link with the Second Empire. As Wolff
tells us, he had hoped to found a financial dynasty similar to that
of the Barings, but his legitimate son rose no higher than county
councillor and deputy, serving without distinction on the
Chamber’s finance committee. It was left to the offspring of his
union with Madame Tallien to re-create in the society of the
Second Empire some of the elegance and dash that Ouvrard had
displayed in the First. Dr. Cabarrus was a good-looking man
of the world, whose wit, bedside manner and skill in treating
ailments of the larynx made him highly sought after, both pro¬
fessionally and socially, by the theatrical monde and their friends.
Oddly enough, Dr. Cabarrus married the sister of Ferdinand de
Lesseps, the man who realized the Saint-Simonian dream of
linking East and West by means of the Suez Canal. Mademoiselle
de Lesseps was also a cousin of Empress Eugenie. How the
subject of this fascinating biography would have relished the
irony of such a bond, however remote, between his family and
that of Napoleon.


                               NO TE

During most of Ouvrard’s lifetime the rate ofexchange was 2$ francs
to fl. In order to convert sums mentioned in this book to present-day
values, the New Franc being worth about is. 6d., it is necessary
to multiply those sums by four or five.
  In 1801, for instance, a franc (or 9\d.) would buy about 5 lb. of
white bread in the provinces, but prices were of course higher in Paris
and other big towns. The daily wage of an unskilled worker was
about l\ to 2 francs (say, is. to is. 6d.), but a highly skilled man, par¬
ticularly in the building industry in Paris, might earn up to 4 francs,
or 3s., a day. In England at this time the 4W. loaf cost about j\d., and
the wages of a skilled worker in the building trade were about 3s. a day.
                       Author s Preface


In the year 1818 the banker and military contractor, Ouvrard,
devised and executed an ingenious financial operation, which
resulted in the premature withdrawal of the Allied forces from
his native soil. In my search for historical parallels, which might
give me a better understanding of the problems of my own time,
I came upon Ouvrard and felt an urge to enquire more closely
into the fortunes of this extraordinary man. His contemporaries
were so passionately divergent in their judgements of him that
it is extremely difficult to paint an objective picture of his life
and his business career. So strong was the imprint of his per¬
sonality upon his own period that even today, a hundred years
later, writers continue to attack him as if he were still alive.
  Ouvrard lived at the turn of a century and of an epoch. He was
a child of the French Revolution. He developed as the age in
which he lived developed, with all its ups and downs. Although
at the outset he was concerned only with his own advancement,
the great economic problems of his day became an overriding
interest. The real relationship between economics and politics is
something we know little of, either from history or from tradition;
this book is an attempt to ascertain it.
  My thanks are due to Dr. Alfred Ludwig Schmitz, Berlin, for
his collaboration in this book.
                                                  OTTO WOLFF
                                 One



                    A Controversial Figure




 J- HE chief source for Ouvrard’s life is his own autobiography.
However, as a source autobiographies suffer from obvious faults,
since they often lack objectivity and do not always tell the reader
what he would like to know. Other sources are the comments of
contemporaries, but here the biographer of Ouvrard is over¬
whelmed by the diversity of their opinions.
  For example, Fouche wrote, “It is impossible to find a man
who is more skilful and, in his character, more obliging and
more captivating.” Marshal Marmont, Due de Ragusa, described
Ouvrard as “a clever man who is equal to any critical situation
and whose foresight and shrewdness never fail him”. Savary, Due
de Rovigo, Napoleon’s Minister of Police, said of him that he was
“the man who, by his powerful and highly skilful intervention in
the financial affairs of Spain, had at one time more weight and in¬
fluence in Spain than her leading politician, Godoy”. His agent
Nolte said Ouvrard was “indisputably the man who first revealed
to his own country the basic elements of the credit system and
used it himself in his audacious ventures at a time when Napoleon
barely understood, much less seriously grasped, the art of national
economy”. Villele publicly acknowledged the importance of
Ouvrard’s work in providing supplies for the Spanish campaign.
Lamartine, whose political experience lends weight to his views,
wrote   with   enthusiastic   admiration   of Ouvrard:   “Just   as
Archimedes discovered the lever, Newton the law of gravity,
Mirabeau public opinion, Frederick II and Napoleon the science
    b                             i
2                             Ouvrard.

of modern warfare, and Law credit, so Ouvrard discovered con¬
fidence and speculation, immeasurable and mysterious forces con¬
cealed in the depths of commercial life. They can, in one single
moment, increase a hundredfold the prodigious power of private
and public wealth for the wellbeing of the individual, of society
and of the State.”
  On the other hand, Thiers condemned him as “one of the most
successful and shrewdest speculators in any kind of business
transaction”. Sarran called him a sinister financier, unscrupulous
in commerce, shamelessly and incurably fraudulent, a notorious
purveyor with a dozen rogues at his beck and call. Beaurepaire
said that from the beginning he was an intriguer. Tourton, a
fellow financier, described him as a genius at duping those with
whom he did business. Contemporary pamphleteers called him a
trickster; a nobody, important only because of his money; almost
illiterate.
    Partisan strife has always been kindled by outstanding per¬
sonalities. But this remarkable conflict of opinions may also be
due to the nature of his career. For more than half a century, from
his arrival in Paris in 1793 to his death in 1846, there was not a
single government in France which did not benefit by his advice,
his help or his loans. Yet he spent several years in prison for debt.
At the age of twenty-one he had already made 300,000 francs and
at twenty-nine he possessed twenty-nine million francs. Five
years later, Napoleon’s refusal to honour debts incurred in his
service brought Ouvrard to the brink of ruin. Yet he immediately
embarked upon the vast Mexican Silver project which Nolte
described as “the most splendid edifice which commercial enter¬
prise had ever undertaken for the benefit of both monarchies”. In
the second decade of the nineteenth century he seemed a kind of
financial Vicar of Bray, for though he had served Napoleon, he
was asked to help the restored monarchy. He then financed
Napoleon during the Hundred Days and after Waterloo was
asked by Richelieu for his assistance and gave it. His greatest
achievement was the plan which became the basis of the Treaty of
Aix-la-Chapelle and helped to end both French insolvency and
the occupation.
                      A Controversial Figure                     3

   But after his rapid initial rise to fame complete success nearly
always eluded him. He founded no dynasty of financiers, and
died—as far as is known—relatively poor. What characteristic or
capacity was it that he lacked? It is to consider why he failed as
well as to tell the story of his remarkable career that this biog¬
raphy has been undertaken.
                                Two




                       The First Deal




(3   UVRARD was seventeen when he left the high school at
Clisson and went to the nearby town of Nantes. His character
was already completely formed and remained basically unaltered
until his death. In his memoirs, he says that he was born on nth
October 1770 at Clisson in the district of Poitou et Bretagne,
that he was one of eight children, and that his father owned a
paper-mill. That is all he has to say of his youth. He seems to have
decided from quite an early age that he was interested in com¬
merce, rather than in industry. His father’s factory provided—
and apparently provided amply—for a family of ten, but Ouvrard
had made his own observations on industry. Although the paper-
mills in the Department of Charente were important and there
were other major industries, such as the flourishing lace-manu¬
facture in the north, the silk-production of Lyons and the linen-
mills of Brittany, industrial production seemed to him to be too
risky. He may already have heard of industrial failures like that
which some years later befell his friend Marmont. When the
Marshal retired from the army to his chateau at Chatillon, he
built a large iron-foundry on the English pattern complete with
rolling-mill, puddling- and blast-furnaces, in which he could
produce thirty tons of iron in twenty-four hours. Before long this
expensive installation was at a complete standstill. Instead of using
French workers, who could produce only bad iron, skilled labour
had to be imported from England at great expense. Wood-firing,
which should have been cheaper and on which the whole enter-
                                  4
                           The First Deal                        5

prise was costed, proved impracticable, because the smaller
iron-foundries in the provinces forced up the timber prices to
prevent competition. After fresh estimates had been drawn up,
based on coal, the price of iron, which was high, came down.
Finally, when a cartel of small ironmasters brought the price
down still further, Marmont was forced out of business.
   Incidents of this kind were certainly not unfamiliar to Ouvrard.
But his decision to concentrate on commerce was also vindicated
when, a few years later, the industries of Brittany were destroyed
by the Revolutionary and Counter-Revolutionary armies and
 Ouvrard had to petition the Committee of Public Safety in Paris
for compensation for the loss of his father’s factory.
   The city of Nantes played a crucial part in Ouvrard’s youthful
dreams and commercial ambitions. When he arrived there in 1788,
towards the end of his eighteenth year, it had a population of
80,000 people. By the standards of that period it was a large city.
His first impression was one of real wealth. He had never realized
until then that so much money could be concentrated in so few
hands. He met merchants who thought nothing of placing an
order for fifty tons of tobacco. He learned that men like the
Thoinet brothers were in a position to advance the city a round
million francs to purchase grain supplies. In these circles, into
which Ouvrard as the son of a Clisson manufacturer gained
immediate entry, he soon acquired a self-confidence which re¬
mained one of his most striking characteristics and which stemmed
from the conviction that his profession was of vital importance.
The merchants and financiers of Nantes were democratic citizens
long before it even occurred to anyone in France to abolish the
King, the nobles and the clergy. The leading men of Nantes had
felt closer than ever to America since the Peace Treaty of 1783.
They were ship owners, merchants and bankers. Trade which
satisfies the most elementary human needs seemed to them to be
the cornerstone of the State. A new era was beginning, a new
“Kingdom of Commerce” was being established. Those citizens
who were engaged in commerce regarded themselves as better
informed than any other section of society about public affairs;
they alone could offer a guarantee of permanent prosperity. They
6                              Ouvrard

looked upon themselves as the true backbone of the nation. It
was hardly to be wondered at that Ouvrard adopted this self-
assured view of life with enthusiasm. It grew and blossomed in
his lively imagination. The final result was an almost messianic
awareness of the importance of his commercial mission.
    If such an attitude was particularly appropriate in Nantes, this
was because commerce there had quite a different character from
that of other French ports. It was not internal trade within
national boundaries; it was not even confined to the Continent;
the citizens of Nantes were world traders and it was thanks to
their commercial activities that France was able to buy the pro¬
ducts of many a remote foreign country. They imported goods
unobtainable in Europe: coffee-beans and tea, tobacco and cane-
sugar, a great variety of spices and a particularly fine brand of
wheat. They also, of course, had interests in “black ivory”, the ex¬
port of African negroes to America, and far from being troubled
by any moral scruples, they simply regarded such interests as
confirmation of their versatility. When their ships sailed up the
Loire they were laden with the riches of Cayenne and San
Domingo, of India and Africa.
    For young Ouvrard all this was an unforgettable spectacle. At
first he was filled with wonder, but he very soon realized what it
meant, that there were undreamt-of possibilities for anyone not
merely engaged in European trade but also conducting inter¬
national operations from bases overseas. At this early stage
Ouvrard acquired the limitless vision which was to shape his
subsequent career. His early experiences in Nantes ultimately
bore fruit in the most grandiose venture he ever embarked upon,
the scheme he devised some fifteen years later to recover the silver
treasure of Mexico.
    In Nantes, however, one could learn not merely how to make
money but also how to spend it. The orators of the French
Revolution condemned the “Asiatic luxury” of life in Nantes, but
this was an exaggeration. On the whole, the merchants lived
well but not luxuriously. In the household of the Hummels, for
instance, five thousand gold francs were spent on food in the
year 1793 and the family dressed expensively but with restrained
                           The First Deal                          7

elegance. Merchants took an interest in architecture. All the
splendid houses along the Loire from the Bourse to Salorges were
built during this period. The city acquired a new Chamber of
Commerce and a large theatre. Ouvrard, who was, as it were, a
country boy, acquired a completely new outlook on how the
individual should spend money. Everything the citizens of Nantes
undertook was the result of careful and sometimes even timid
planning; in Ouvrard’s bold imagination their schemes assumed
gigantic proportions and soon the pupil was to leave his teachers
far behind. Once he was installed in Paris his guests marvelled at
such princely luxury.
  Ouvrard came to know and understand the power of credit; he
realized “how it increases one’s financial resources and how, by
adding to one’s private fortune, one also increases the prosperity
of the nation”. A building contractor named Graslin became,
quite unknowingly, his teacher. When the Peace Treaty was
signed with America in 1783 a wave of prosperity broke over
Nantes, bringing with it a taste for a better, more comfortable
and more modern way of life. Graslin, the building contractor,
was the man to satisfy this taste. He had the courage to plan on a
large scale and to weigh up the public’s needs. He was not content,
as was customary at the time, to build single apartments or houses.
He planned in the more ambitious style of our own time, erecting
whole blocks of houses, whole streets, in fact a whole new
quarter of the city. The people of Nantes, for whom this was
something new and spectacular, expressed their approval by
naming the new quarter after Graslin. But in his boldness he
overreached himself. He had put a large proportion of his money
into the building sites, most of his building materials had to be
paid for on delivery, and a considerable time had to elapse before
the dwellings and shops were ready for occupation and yielded
any cash return. As Graslin could not see why a shortage of ready
cash should rob him of the certain success that awaited him, he
extended his financial backing artificially. Trusting in his reputa¬
tion, he issued a kind of token money in the form of pieces of
paper giving an assurance that they were covered by the building
sites, which had already been paid for, and by the houses under
8                           Ouvrard

construction. These were known as “Boris Graslin” or “building
papers”. Inevitably they went into circulation, because the
workers received no other form of wages. And, lo and behold,
the new currency was accepted everywhere without question,
although it was without cover.
   For Ouvrard, who had seen through this trick and followed it
with amused interest, the affair took a dramatic turn when the
basis of the whole credit operation, confidence in Graslin, began
to falter. His competitors spread the rumour that he was in
difficulties, and at once people came running with their “Bons
Graslin”. Anxious shopkeepers, into whose tills this private
currency had found its way, quoted the printed assurance on the
Ions that they could be converted into cash at any time. But
Graslin thought of a way out. The people, who rushed into his
office, gesticulating angrily, were assured that they would get
their money. But in copper coins! They must surely realize that
there was just not enough silver, which was already in short
supply, to cover sackfuls of paper! They would have to make do
with small change! But repayment in pennies and halfpennies was
naturally a slow business, and when it became known that
Graslin was, in fact, paying, day in, day out, slowly but surely,
confidence in him was gradually restored, the bons were no longer
presented and building was resumed.
   Ouvrard saw how successful Graslin had been and how he had
achieved it. He found it hard to believe that public confidence
could have such magical powers. He had learned a lesson which
he never forgot. “This incident showed me”, he wrote in his
memoirs, “how credit adds to one’s resources.” In these few
sober words the budding capitalist drew his own moral. Yet there
can be no doubt that this was the most important single ex¬
perience in Ouvrard’s life. Many years later he embodied the
same moral in a much more pretentious axiom: “Treasures of
gold and silver do not last for ever; the only treasure which is
never exhausted, which grows and multiplies, is credit.”
   Before long it was clear that everything Ouvrard had seen and
learned at Nantes assumed quite different dimensions and opened
up quite different commercial possibilities in his mind. When he
                            The First Deal                         9

arrived in Nantes in 1788 his first aim was to serve some kind of
apprenticeship. He joined the firm of Guertin, Loret & Co.,
wholesale grocers, but he did not stay there long. Not sur¬
prisingly, for what others took years to learn he picked up in a
flash. He could tell almost by the smell of a place if he was
wasting his time there. So Ouvrard left. It all happened very
quickly, because he derived no pleasure from watching others
making money. For the time being, however, he remained in the
trade. “Guertin et Ouvrard”, also dealing in wholesale groceries,
was his first business. But it was not his first venture in big
business. Ouvrard himself sensed that this was not the right
line for him. He twisted and turned on the high stool behind the
counter. It gave him no real pleasure to be chained to a desk and
to write reports on currants, sugar and liquorice. Sooner or later,
and probably later, it would bring in money and in Nantes it was
a highly respected occupation, but to him it seemed dull and
unappetizing. He was itching to embark on something more
ambitious, to take up commerce in a big way. So long as he
remained a wholesale grocer he would be one citizen among
many, a bourgeois among bourgeois. He had to get out. And the
very moment when he was ready to take the plunge coincided
with one of the great moments of history: on 14th July 1789 the
Revolution broke out in Paris.
   This was all Ouvrard needed to bring off his first major coup.
It was as simple as the story of Columbus and the egg. The
storming of the Bastille gave rise to a nation-wide debate on the
form of the new social order. Characteristically, Ouvrard kept
his own counsel. His head was quite unaffected by the new ideas,
his vision undimmed by the fog of emotional verbiage. He left
others to do the talking. Determined to move onwards and up¬
wards, his sure instinct told him which class of society would be
in the ascendant. He sensed that the bourgeoisie, not the pro¬
letariat, were preparing to take over from the old aristocrats. They
would do so anyway, and certainly without his help. His sole           \
preoccupation was to engineer for himself as favourable a position
as possible in this new society. There was no room for sentimen¬
tality.

          JT
10                              Ouvrard

     While Ouvrard was looking round for some way to exploit the
new situation, which would free him from an office desk and
enable him to enter the great world of unlimited wealth, he
suddenly found himself, to his surprise, back in his father’s firm.
Not physically but in a business context. When he had decided
a year before—and a mere twelve months had, in fact, elapsed—
to trade in groceries rather than produce paper, it had not
crossed his mind that he might ever again take an interest in
paper. When this did happen in 1789, however, it did not imply
any radical change in Ouvrard’s plans. He was not harking back
from trade to production but merely trading in paper. For the
knowledge he had acquired automatically in the family firm
made him realize at once that there was one market in particular
which must be the first to be affected by the Revolution.
     At this early stage the Revolution had produced few tangible
results. But one thing was clearly apparent: the freedom to speak,
the freedom to write, the freedom to print. The freedom of the
Press had been won. There was a positive deluge of placards:
speeches, manifestos and official declarations. Periodicals would
follow. Newspapers would soon appear: the Paris Revolution, the
Picardy Correspondent, the Free Press, the People’s Tribune and
many others. Ouvrard saw from the beginning the way things
were moving and he had enough expert knowledge of the trade
to realize that the French paper industry was too small to meet
such an increase in demand. Supply must inevitably lag behind
demand, which meant that the price of paper would rise steadily.
     The price would, of course, only rise if the freedom of the Press
remained. But would it remain? No one could answer that
question, because no one could possibly foresee what course
political events would take over the next two years. The Revo¬
lution, after all, did not lead to the immediate establishment of a
republic. The monarchy, which was to survive another three
years, might well have regained its former hold, in which case
public opinion would have been even more fettered than before.
Another possibility was that one man might come to power who
would declare, as Napoleon did later, that he could not hold out
three months with a free Press. Napoleon allowed only one news-
                           The First Deal                         ll

paper in each Department and reduced the number of papers in
France from seventy-three to thirteen. He introduced postal
and stage censorship. Louis XVIII after his restoration even
censored every book of less than twenty pages. Ouvrard, in short,
was taking a considerable risk when he set out to gain control
of the paper market. But throughout his career one of his out¬
standing qualities was courage, the intellectual courage which is
much subtler and rarer than its physical counterpart. He himself
had very little capital to invest, but he had learned from Graslin
that, for a time at least, one can pay with trust. The trust he
needed could be borrowed from his father, who was known and
respected in the trade.
  At the beginning of the Revolution Ouvrard travelled round
the paper-mills of Poitou and Angoumois. From the unsuspecting
manufacturers he bought up their entire production for the next
two years; at normal prices, of course; and on his own account.
The demand for paper began to increase, the prices rose and
printers were compelled to seek out Ouvrard.
  This, his first major business deal, was also his first masterpiece
and bore all the hall-marks of subsequent ventures. It was charac¬
teristic of him that he refused to become involved in questions of
detail. They no longer interested him. What excited and satisfied
him was the overall conception; it was enough for him to realize
an overall profit. The entire paper supply for two years, produced
and unproduced, was sold en bloc to a few firms in Tours and
Nantes. Ouvrard had made 300,000 francs. He was nineteen years
old.




            S>
           /
                                 Three




             A Fugitive from the Revolution




    O be nineteen years old and in possession of 300,000 francs
is unusual. The appetite of youth for enjoyment, for a life of
ease and elegance, can complicate matters and may even undo
much of what has been done. But where the money has been
earned, not inherited, this danger is greatly reduced. Ouvrard was
never mean and even at this early stage he did not count his
pennies. He lived up to his greatly improved circumstances but
nevertheless exercised the sort of moderation that comes from
respect for one’s own capital. Apparently calm and relaxed, he
was in fact waiting with feverish impatience for an opportunity
to repeat his first, brilliantly successful, coup. His fellow citizens
might be content with the modest and pious ambition to emulate
those gentlemen whose dignified gait, as they paced through the
streets of Nantes, was indicative of their millions, but Ouvrard
had other ideas.
  He could not go around indefinitely with the 300,000 francs in
his pocket. But it was no easy matter to know where else to put
them. A revolution had broken out and it was too early for any¬
one to foresee or even guess what effect it would have on trade.
In Nantes in 1789, on the other hand, there had so far been
relatively little change. Life went on much as before. The King
was still on the throne, the same city council was in office, and the
face of the city was unaltered. Had Ouvrard not possessed that
sixth sense, which no amount of rational thought can replace,
then he would undoubtedly have followed the example of all sea-
                                  12
                    A Fugitive from the Revolution                  13

loving people in Nantes and invested his capital in property over¬
seas. There was much to be said for it. Anyone who acquired a
sugar plantation in the French colonies could be sure of in¬
creasing his capital by io per cent each year. Cotton plantations
brought in between 12 and 15 per cent. Coffee plantations
yielded at least 20 per cent. These were sound investments, for
people have always remained loyal to their pleasures, especially
those of a more noxious variety. Ouvrard could see only one
drawback—and no one else seems to have realized this—that
there was no guarantee how long the slaves in the colonies would
remain slaves working for starvation wages. There was always
the danger that the growing unrest inside France would lead to
a breakdown of authority in overseas territories, that the natives
would rebel and that property and investments would disappear.
  Ouvrard was not prepared to expose his money and his future
prospects to such a risk. It was not the element of speculation
that he minded. Nothing was further from his mind than to look
for gilt-edged securities. Speculation was the breath of life to
him. But the future of overseas property was too unpredictable.
So, for the time being, he returned to the business he knew; he
bought, not colonial property, but colonial produce. Again he did
not act on his own. He had his reasons. Ouvrard’s associates on
this occasion were wholesale grocers in Nantes and the Brothers
Baour & Co. in Bordeaux. This deal was based on the same kind
of assumption as the paper transaction, namely that groceries, in
particular, must become scarcer and the prices must rise. The
precise outcome of this venture is not known. Ouvrard’s terse
comment was: “I did very well out of it.”
  At this point the historian loses track of Ouvrard until 1793.
He probably spent these years in similar, highly profitable, deals,
buying produce from the colonies wholesale and selling it by the
bag to shopkeepers. He seems to have returned voluntarily to
the routine of wholesale trading.
  Then    something    happened     that   changed   the   course   of
Ouvrard’s life yet again. As luck would have it, the Convention
in Paris chose to despatch to Nantes a particularly fanatical
revolutionary,   Jean-Baptiste   Carrier, as    Commissioner, and
          J’
14                            Ouvrard

Ouvrard was compelled, this time almost against his will, to
return to mid-stream. It was in the year 1793. A few months
earlier Nantes had sent Joseph Fouche, who was later to become
Napoleon’s Minister of Police, to Paris to represent it on the
Convention. Before his election Fouche had sworn by all that
was sacred that he would do everything possible to protect
trade and property. Apart from the fact that he had never been
foolish enough to regard any promise he made as binding, it
would have been virtually impossible for Fouche to fulfil his
promise. By the end of 1792 there was such a general shortage
that prices, especially of grain, sugar, coffee and wine, doubled
and trebled. At the same time there was also a shortage of money.
Monsieur Graslin would no longer have dared to build his
terraces of houses. The English were just outside the city gates.
It was impossible to sail out of the Loire into the open sea. Ships
rotted at the quaysides, transport stood idle, ships’ carpenters and
ropemakers, wheelwrights and caulkers were all out of work, and
the city council was finding it more difficult every week to provide
adequate supplies, even of flour, for a city which had always
received most of its provisions by sea. A general malaise spread
over the city like a dark cloud. Threatened from outside by the
Counter-Revolutionary Army, the city was also under increasing
strain internally as conditions deteriorated. It was the sort of
atmosphere which breeds bitterness. Fouche’s Amis de la Con¬
stitution, which was a fairly harmless club, was replaced by the
Club des Cordeliers, with a shopkeeper as president. Wigmakers,
weavers, coopers and umbrella-manufacturers and members of
other small trades dabbled in revolutionary jargon. For those
citizens who were still influential this was no laughing matter;
within a few months this faction was to launch a bloody reign of
terror.
     To begin with, however, this new activity took rather comic
forms. The shortage of supplies became increasingly acute. This
encouraged trade in small quantities and, while this was the kind
of trade in which the big businessman had little or no interest,
for that very reason it opened up new vistas for the little man.
Ouvrard and his colleagues found the situation more and more
                    A Fugitive from the Revolution                  15

difficult as the masses, not the money, assumed more and more
importance. The Societe Populaire Vincent-la-Montagne re¬
placed the Club des Cordeliers and took over control of the city.
A colder wind was now blowing. Food supplies were rationed.
Trade was strictly controlled. It was difficult for the big whole¬
saler to dispose quietly of a hundredweight, but a black market in
ounces and pounds was a simple matter for the small retailer.
Shoemakers took to selling oil, soap could be bought at a black¬
smith’s, and wigmakers traded in sugar and coffee. This was not,
of course, a situation that pleased Ouvrard. When little people
were making money and the big people could not do business,
the world was standing on its head. When a city was supplied
not by wholesalers but by bureaucrats, the divine order of things
was completely upset. Ouvrard was incapable of tolerating a
system in which the first became the last and the last the first.
  Ouvrard might have awaited the outcome of these develop¬
ments with the same philosophical calm as he was to show later
during five years in a debtors’ prison in Paris. But gradually he
found himself being too closely involved for his own comfort.
He and his colleagues were being pushed against the wall. Anyone
who did not loudly proclaim himself a Jacobin was suspect. The
Compagnie Marat, the Inquisition of the Societe Populaire
Vincent-la-Montagne, issued a manifesto demanding death for
the monarchists. (Ouvrard did not feel threatened.) Death to the
elegant fops. (This touched him rather more, for Ouvrard had
always been elegant in his dress.) Death to all moderates, “regard¬
less of the mask or colour behind which they may conceal them¬
selves”. (This formula was as meaningful and meaningless as a
blank cheque.) At all events, the situation in Nantes was growing
uncomfortable.
  It became even more uncomfortable when Jean-Baptiste
Carrier arrived from Paris with full powers as Commissioner.
He had only one aim and one passion: to “sansculotte” the
refractory citizens of Nantes. It was left entirely to him, a renegade
from Holy Orders, to decide exactly how he did it. Ouvrard
sized the man up, as soon as he set eyes on him: a fanatic, an
unfrocked priest, and not a man to be trifled with. This character

            I
i6                            Ouvrard

would keep the promise he had given to the Convention in Paris:
to teach the citizens of Nantes to dance the carmagnole and like it.
     Carrier was not so bloodthirsty and perverse as history makes
him out to be. His sullen expression, his harsh manner and, above
all, his dour silence had alienated even the Jacobin friends of this
forty-seven-year-old native of Auvergne. He was consumed by a
burning passion for the ideas and blessings of the new age. When
he made one of his tempestuous speeches in the People’s Assembly,
tossing his black mane of hair, no one could possibly doubt his
belief in humanity. Nor was it possible to believe that he would
ever compromise even in his own interest. While, on the one hand,
his devotion to his social mission was utterly disinterested—when
he died a year later, he was a poor man, although more than half
a million francs had passed uncontrolled through his hands—he
showed no mercy to those who tried to impede him in his life’s
work.
     Rather naturally, Ouvrard, like the other leading citizens of
Nantes, was not interested in understanding Carrier’s character.
It was a matter of complete indifference to him that this man
helped the poor, loved flowers and was kind to animals, that the
anti-revolutionary propaganda might be quite wrong in depicting
Carrier as an alcoholic who raped women prisoners, that Carrier,
who was both Commissioner of Nantes and the mainstay of the
French army in the west, worked twenty hours a day and con¬
trived somehow to keep the beleaguered city supplied with food.
Ouvrard was bound to treat him as an enemy, who threatened his
livelihood and even his life. In the fear-ridden minds of the people
of Nantes the number of arrests was multiplied a hundredfold.
(Yet this occurred before the unfortunate prisoners from the
Counter-Revolutionary army were drowned in their thousands
in the Loire by the leaders of the Revolutionary army in Nantes.)
But Ouvrard had no intention of joining the hundred or two
hundred or five hundred people who were to be sacrificed to the
Revolutionary cause. He had no intention of exposing his person,
which he held very dear, to Carrier’s dogma that the individual
is nothing, the Republic everything.
     Ouvrard had all the more reason to feel disturbed as he
                    A Fugitive from the Revolution              ly

belonged to a social group which in troubled times invariably
attracts unfriendly attention from the masses. His money was
not stowed away safely in a mattress or even invested in houses,
plantations, timber or State securities, but in large quantities of
edible goods, which would be one of the first targets for the mob.
It is sometimes a short step from a raid on a baker’s shop to a
campaign against a wholesaler’s.
  In Nantes any attempt to conceal stocks was severely punished
and profiteering was punishable by death. The Widow Mallet
was thrown into prison because she had been hoarding 70,000 lb.
of tobacco. The Brothers Thoinet fled the city when a night
search of their house revealed 3,000 cwt. of flour. There was
general consternation when some people who had laid in a stock
of turnips, and others who had tried to bribe sentries with a few
ducks, were shot out of hand. Carrier knew everything that was
going on, because he had turned the secret police, which he
originally set up in the Army of the West, on to the city fathers.
There were spies everywhere. People no longer dared to write,
they barely spoke and they almost stopped thinking. Carrier’s
secret police was omnipresent.
  Soon it was Ouvrard’s turn. Maybe his wealth was in itself
enough to make him suspect or maybe he really did something
that was not permissible in Nantes in 1793. At all events he was
denounced as a profiteer.
  It was difficult to defend oneself. So far, few had succeeded in
escaping from the city. There seemed to be even less future in
waiting calmly for a trial, which would be loaded with prejudice.
For a man with Ouvrard’s unerring instinct, however, there was
a third way of escape: he must place himself under the protection
of some influential person.
  Ouvrard did not hesitate. He knew he had no chance with the
Jacobins. Carrier could not be swayed by money, personal charm,
or the knowledge of a man’s position in society. That left only
the commanding General. It was not pure chance that Ouvrard
chose Boivin. Unless he was very much mistaken, the General
must know something about him. And indeed, when Ouvrard
sent in his card, General Boivin thought he remembered reading

            /'
18                             Ouvrard

the name somewhere before. In the course of conversation with
his elegant visitor, the General learned that Ouvrard was a
partner in the firm of Guertin et Ouvrard, whose invoices he had
frequently seen in the house of his father, a grocer in the Saint-
Sulpice quarter of Paris. This reminder was the best possible
reference; under the influence of nostalgic memories the General
proved accommodating. He appreciated that the situation in
Nantes condemned Ouvrard to idleness, that he hated nothing
more, and that it seemed only right to give him some occupation
in the framework of the New Order. He appointed Ouvrard his
adjutant.
  In his new function Ouvrard was not only rendered immune
from the civil authorities, he also felt more confidence than ever
in his own metier. For once again it was commerce that had
saved him; its solidarity had once more been demonstrated.
     For the time being, however, his sole concern was not to trade
but to serve. For someone as adaptable as Ouvrard, who in later
years had to make the best of quite different situations, it was not
too difficult to fit himself unobtrusively even into a military
machine with which he was quite unfamiliar. His position was
that of a private secretary rather than an officer. Moreover,
thanks to his exceptional talents, he succeeded before long in
making a niche for himself quite outside his official post. In
consequence he was soon able, if his own account is to be believed,
to act more or less independently. For example, the Revolutionary
Committee issued a decree, condemning a number of outstand¬
ing and wealthy citizens of Nantes to be shot:


       “We hereby order the Commandant of Nantes to muster
     three hundred soldiers. Half of them will march to the
     Boufflay prison, where they will remove the prisoners
     named in the attached list. They will be bound together by
     their hands in pairs. These people will be taken to
     Eperonniere. The second group of soldiers will conduct
     prisoners from Saintes-Claires, whose names are on the
     list, to the same place. The inmates of the prison at
     Eperonniere are also to be brought out, and all, without
                    A Fugitive from the Revolution                    19

  exception, are to be shot. The precise method of execution
  will be left to the Commandant.

     Nantes, 15 Frimaire of the year II of the Indivisible
   Republic [5th December 1793]
                                Sgnd. Gaulin, M. Grandmaison,
                                                     J-R. Minguet.”


  When the list of condemned men reached the adjutant’s office,
Ouvrard saw to his horror that it included a number of business
friends, whom he knew to be innocent. Within a few seconds the
fateful document had disappeared. When enquiries were made, it
was pointed out that the list had unfortunately gone astray. There
was acute embarrassment in the Revolutionary Committee, for no
copy had been made. The executions had to be postponed. But
clearly the real guardian angel of Nantes was the General himself,
for only his humanity and sense of justice made it possible for his
adjutant to take such liberties without risking his head. Never¬
theless, Nantes gradually became too hot for Ouvrard. Sus¬
picions were mounting, rumours gaining ground, and it was not
exactly reassuring to see one former class-mate after another
disappear behind prison bars. Ouvrard finally decided to make
his escape. He realized, however, that it would have to be done
quite openly. Any other way would be too dangerous.
  He escaped to the army. In modern times it has become cus¬
tomary for men who wish to avoid danger to claim exemption
from military duty; in those days one was more likely to succeed
by showing willingness to shed one’s blood for la patrie. A
soldier of the National Guard, who shouted on parade shortly
before the storming of the Tuileries: “We will fight for the
King to the death,” only escaped with his life at the hands of the
republicans by taking refuge in an Alpine Corps.
  Ouvrard did the same; he joined the Western Army. He went
with recommendations to General Kleber, who was stationed at
Remouille, and, as luck would have it, at headquarters he met
Merlin de Thionville, Commissioner of the Convention. Merlin
once saved the life of one of Ouvrard’s brothers, and, as a good
20                              Ouvrard

deed often proves more binding on the doer than on the recipient,
Ouvrard felt he could count on Merlin’s friendship.
     It is not easy to visualize Ouvrard, who, as Boivin’s adjutant,
had always been a civilian soldier, in the camp at Remouille. He
was now in the uncomfortable position of hearing the rattle of
musket-fire. Although he was spared an actual battle, he was
obliged to witness the skirmish at Torfou. He suddenly found
himself “commander of a detachment”, but this detachment is
not likely to have been large enough to require any decisive
action on his part. As it turned out, Kleber was wounded and his
troops defeated. Ouvrard, on the other hand, was unharmed and
far from beaten.
   It is a measure of his complete self-confidence that he agreed to
take part in an unintentional piece of comedy: the good soldier
Ouvrard was entrusted with the task of taking the captured enemy
colours to Paris! Standards captured from the foe have always
been greatly prized, more especially in France. In December 1797
when the Directory met in special session to greet the young
general who had conquered Italy, Napoleon found the courtyard
of the Palais du Luxembourg festooned with flags captured in the
revolutionary campaigns. Berthier wanted to set up on the banks
of the Seine the trophies which had been won on the banks of the
Nile. And when on one occasion Napoleon, as Consul, bowed
his head before the tattered, powder-blackened colours of French
regiments in the courtyard of the Tuileries, the crowd burst into
a mighty roar.
     Ouvrard’s colours were of a different, rather special kind. They
too were trophies, which had been wrested from the foe in battle
and which were doubtless stained with the blood of both the
assailant and the assailed, but they were, nevertheless, trophies of
defeat. The engagement was lost, the General wounded.
     There is, unfortunately, no record of how Ouvrard and his
unusual flags were received in the capital. The Convention in
Paris can hardly have felt any great urge to play out this comedy
with the civilian Gabriel Julien Ouvrard, wholesale grocer, and
his trophies of military prowess. The colours were probably
deposited together with other relics in an appropriate room.
                   A Fugitive from the Revolution            21

  No one could have been less put out than Ouvrard. If he was
entrusted with this rather delicate assignment, then it was un¬
doubtedly because he himself had asked for it. Ouvrard knew
what he was doing. He saw the army as merely a stepping-stone
to the conquest of the world. For him the world was Paris.
                              Four




               Paris: the Great Opportunity




w HEN Ouvrard arrived in Paris the atmosphere was one of
personal insecurity and complete political instability. The situa¬
tion was, in fact, every bit as critical as in Nantes. The cobbled
streets of the capital were slippery. Only a few weeks before, in
October 1793, Marie-Antoinette had laid her head under the
guillotine. Anyone who was someone or had something, or even
who had been someone or had had something, was in particular
danger. The banker Duruet was sent to the guillotine by the
Revolutionary Tribunal because he honoured a bill for 6,000
francs made out to him in London and thereby, it was held, had
conspired with a foreign power. A former banker, van den Niver,
suffered the same fate despite his advanced years. The ninety-
one-year-old citoyenne Prevost, who had a fortune of 100,000
francs, was accused of counter-revolutionary sympathies and was
punished for her affluence by imprisonment in the building which
still bears the name “Port libre”. So it went on, day in, day
out. It paid, if one wanted to remain both rich and free, to be as
shrewd as M. Recamier, who took his seat under the guillotine
every morning to show in public how interested he was in
the progress of the Revolution. But even this was not always
sufficient.
  Ouvrard was undoubtedly aware of all this before he decided
to go to Paris. He must have heard the grossly exaggerated reports
that circulated from house to house and from town to town.
                                22
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                23

According to counter-revolutionary sources, 32,000 people, in¬
cluding 15,000 children, were drowned in Nantes, whereas the
real figure was 4,074, of whom 3,793 were members of the
Counter-Revolutionary Army. Ouvrard must also have heard
figures from Paris which were just as astronomical. If he decided,
none the less, to walk into the lions’ den, then he clearly had his
own good reasons. It is not difficult to guess what they were.
Ouvrard had a feeling that the great opportunity of his life lay in
Paris. It was a dangerous city, in which the mere business of
staying alive was fraught with risks. But Ouvrard, as a merchant
of standing, knew that, where the risks were greatest, there also
were the greatest opportunities.
  He had studied the French character. Earlier than most others
he realized that in the not too distant future stirring words and
exalted ideas would lose their potency. The French were an
industrious people, as he well knew, and he could not believe that
they would let themselves be fooled indefinitely. He wanted to be
there when the reaction set in, so that he could have his share of
the power which must take over when the rule of ideas came to
an end: the power of money.
  Ouvrard was prudent enough, however, to plan only a short
visit to Paris to begin with. He made arrangements that, if life
in the capital was too uncomfortable, he could hand over his
trophies and, after a few days of “leave”, return to the safety of
the army. But, after studying the situation at first hand, he
decided that he could stay. The risks involved in doing business
so close to the epicentre of the Revolution seemed, after all, not
unreasonable. His completed mission was now followed by an
extended leave.
  The time had now come for Ouvrard to discard the petty
bourgeois guise he had worn in Nantes. Before long that city
meant little more to him than a name on the map. At most it
served to remind him of the distance he had travelled since he first
set out. In the first few months, however, there was still much
talk of Nantes. He could not easily forget the fate suffered by so
many of his fellow citizens at the hands of the incorruptible
Carrier, and, before he could launch out on fresh business
24                             Ouvrard

ventures, he became quite unexpectedly involved in an errand of
mercy, which was not without its dangers. In a Paris prison lay
132 citizens of Nantes, whom Carrier had despatched to the
Revolutionary Tribunal at five o’clock in the morning of 27th
November 1793. Chained together in pairs, they had been driven
out of the town without seeing their families to walk the long
road from Nantes to Paris in all kinds of weather. Dragged from
gaol to gaol, they finally ended up on the rotting palliasses of the
Conciergerie. “Prison sickness” took its toll, the bucket which
had to serve their most primitive needs polluted such air as there
was and their diseased bodies were weakened still further by the
inadequate and often uneatable food. Before the tribunal had
even seen them their number had been reduced from 132 to 84.
These citizens from the provinces, who had no contacts in Paris,
would have stood utterly defenceless before the Revolutionary
Tribunal had not Ouvrard intervened on their behalf as soon as
he had found his feet in Paris. Just as he never lacked moral
courage, so too he was always ready to help others, un¬
conditionally.
     However, the rescue of his fellow citizens was no easy matter.
In dealing with fanatics like Robespierre or Fouquier-Tinville,
the notorious Public Prosecutor, money was of no avail. There¬
fore he decided to appeal to Robespierre himself through the
family of Duplay, a cabinet-maker, with whom Robespierre
lived in the Rue St. Honore. The Duplay family exercised con¬
siderable influence over Robespierre, because they had been
amongst the first to give him unqualified support. “Father,
mother, sons, daughters, and cousins, both male and female—all
swore by Robespierre and by him alone”, wrote a contempo¬
rary observer contemptuously. “Mere Duplay was the Mother
Superior of Robespierre’s pious acolytes, and the daughters
became the overseers of this devout Community.” Ouvrard,
having sized up the situation with his usual shrewdness, addressed
himself to the daughters, confident that he would be able to make
a sufficiently strong impression. With all a young man’s elo¬
quence he described the appalling sufferings of the unfortunate
citizens of Nantes. The gamble—this time a purely psychological
                       Paris: the Great Opportunity                      25

one—came off: the girls promised to try to arrange an audience
with Robespierre.
   When Ouvrard was actually received two days later, in the
most informal atmosphere, he thought the game was already
won. There was something almost idyllic about that friendly yet
cautious conversation between a revolutionary dictator and a
wholesale merchant, who was at heart a counter-revolutionary,
with two carefree young girls dispensing morning coffee. The
outcome, however, was completely negative. Robespierre could
not trespass on the preserve of Fouquier-Tinville, the Prosecutor
of the Revolutionary Tribunal and executioner of Paris, and he
could only advise Ouvrard to approach Fouquier-Tinville or his
private secretary. Ouvrard, who was a past-master at making
contacts, knew how much influence was wielded by subordinates.
But he only succeeded in seeing the private secretary’s wife. On
the other hand, she gave him an excellent piece of advice: the only
way to achieve anything with Fouquier-Tinville was through a
beautiful woman.
   Ouvrard at once thought of the young and very beautiful
Mademoiselle de M., whose father was very ill in prison. She
actually gained an audience with Fouquier and, in a moving
display of indignation and despair, timidity and innocence,
begged him to have the citizens of Nantes moved to a hospital.
Fouquier seemed quite amenable but he made one condition:
the beautiful young woman must meet him alone the following
day at two in the afternoon, on the terrace of the Tuileries. With
such a preamble, one would be justified in assuming the worst.
But nothing untoward happened. The only hazard to which
Mile de M. was exposed was a strange dejeuner a deux, during
which scarcely a word was uttered. And it ended in complete
success: her plea was granted. The unfortunate prisoners were at
least transferred from the Conciergerie, the prison which was
usually the final stage before the guillotine.*
   The man to whom the Committee of Public Safety looked for
concrete evidence against the prisoners was Robert Lindet, the

   * Six months later it was Fouquier-Tinville’s turn to lay his head under
the guillotine.
26                              Ouvrard

financial expert of the Revolution and a man with a highly
developed sense of justice. He deliberately took so long to
provide any evidence at all that by the time the prisoners came
up for trial Robespierre had fallen and they were acquitted. It
is virtually certain that Ouvrard was partly responsible for the
fact that the evidence produced was favourable. Lindet also
supported Ouvrard in another plea which at that time was
fraught with danger.
   Ouvrard had not lost sight of his original purpose in coming to
Paris: to do business. He set about it in an unusual, original and
fearless way: he asked the Committee of Public Safety for sub¬
stantial compensation for the loss of his father’s paper-mill,
which had been burnt down during the fighting in the Vendee
between the Revolutionary and Counter-Revolutionary armies.
The prisons were full of citizens who were suspected, quite
wrongly, of having robbed the State or even a neighbour.
A certain citizen Fougeret had just been guillotined, because
he thought his private fortune had been much too highly
assessed, at 30,000 francs, and was rash enough to voice his objec¬
tions. Ouvrard clearly took the view that everything depended on
the individual and his approach. Success proved him right. On
the basis of a report by Robert Lindet, the Committee of Public
Safety, which was the official organ of the Terror, approved an
indemnity of 200,000 francs.
     Within a few months of his arrival in Paris, Ouvrard had, there¬
fore, scored a second major success. He managed to add a third
almost immediately. At the age of twenty-four he married the
daughter of one of the citizens of Nantes, for whose release he
had been largely responsible. As one can well imagine, she came
from no ordinary family. On such an important question Ouvrard
acted completely in character. This was not a pure love match.
Her father was Monsieur Tebaud, a man of excellent antecedents,
who belonged to the same class from which Ouvrard had acquired
a pride in his calling. And he was very rich. Jean Baptiste Tebaud
was one of the most competent and wealthiest merchants of
Nantes, a fact which undoubtedly influenced Ouvrard’s choice of
a wife. He lived in an age which was anything but emotional and
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                 27

still less sentimental. Whenever one of his generals discussed a
prospective wife with Napoleon their main concern was usually
with the dowry.
  Mademoiselle Tebaud was the first woman to play any part in
Ouvrard’s life. She was not the ideal partner for him. On the
contrary, her simplicity was in striking contrast to his brilliance,
and she is not likely to have shown much understanding of her
husband’s boundless energy and intellectual drive. Ouvrard
devoted to her only a few sentences of the thousand pages of his
memoirs. But he spoke well of her. The simple wedding cere¬
mony—in 1794 it could hardly be otherwise—would, even in
more congenial times, still have appealed to the bride. “The
quality of her mind and the nobility of her soul enabled her to
find in a life of retirement the sort of tranquil pleasures which a
worldly life could not have given her.” {Memoirs.) She found
natural fulfilment in bringing up her three children. Ouvrard
took the view that the main reason for his wife’s early death in
1818 was the unjust persecution to which he was subjected for
much of his life. There is no doubt that Mme Ouvrard was
deeply affected by her husband’s misfortunes, but a more im¬
mediate reason for her early death was probably the distress
caused her by his infidelity.
  But that still lay a few years ahead. Meanwhile, Ouvrard used
his own fortune and the large dowry his wife had brought to
build up the enormous financial power which made him banker
to three governments. All this was, of course, only possible
once he enjoyed complete freedom to negotiate, and this he
achieved with the fall of Robespierre and the end of the Terror—
on 9th Thermidor, or 27th July 1794. The face of Paris was
changed overnight. When the head of the dictator from the Rue
St. Honore dropped into the basket all opposition to unrestricted
private trading disappeared. But with it, for the time being at
least, went an incorruptible government. Money ascended the
empty throne of the Kings of France. Ouvrard, as one of its
most loyal and experienced servants, stood to gain more than
most by this new reign.
  Not that his sole concern was to amass riches for himself. He
          J’
28                             Ouvrard

was always ready, without receiving any immediate reward, to
help strengthen the financial foundations of the State, the supreme
importance of which he recognized more clearly than anyone else.
Even during the Terror he tried to help by impressing upon the
Committee of Public Safety the relationship between paper
money, the value of gold and credit. The government cut the
ground from under its own feet by flooding the country with
assignats, so that by the beginning of 1794 the real value of this
paper currency was only a third of its nominal value; a galloping
inflation had set in. In November 1795, when the Directory
needed twenty-four millions, this represented 3,000 millions in
paper. By the end of 1796 the louis d’or was worth between 23,000
and 25,000 paper francs. And yet the government persisted in up¬
holding what to any merchant was an absurd fiction, that a franc
was still a franc. In vain Ouvrard proposed that assignats should
be accepted in payment only at the current rate, thus ensuring that
taxes, not worthless scraps of paper, were collected. Those in
authority were too busy with personal quarrels and discussions,
political demonstrations and manifestos to give any thought to
vital problems of State. They failed to realize that, just as financial
collapse alone had made the Revolution possible, so their own
untenable financial position was paving the way for the counter¬
revolution. Ouvrard, whose approach to such problems was
quite elementary and in consequence correct, warned them in
vain. “Is it not remarkable,” he said in retrospect, “that among the
many talented people who influenced the Legislative Assembly,
and among the senior State officials, so few really knew anything
about finance!”
   As Ouvrard’s services were not required in public life, he was
able to concentrate on furthering his own plans. The complexion
of the firm very quickly changed. Wholesale groceries were no
longer the main line of business, for Ouvrard was now a banker
and the firm was a “Banking and Trading House”. The transition
to purely monetary transactions opened up undreamt-of pros¬
pects at a moment when the value of money was constantly
changing and when the timid and uninitiated had no inkling of
the direction in which things were moving. Ouvrard’s head-
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                 29

quarters was in the Rue d’Amboise, one of the narrow streets
which ran parallel to the Boulevard des Italiens, and in which
two carriages could barely pass. But in the overcrowded centre
of Paris this was a distinguished address, for the house had been
the head offices of Rougemont et Hottinguer, a firm of high
repute.
  Little is known of Ouvrard’s monetary transactions. He was
apparently reluctant to talk about the profits he made then. He
had, at all events, become the centre of attraction in the renascent
Bourse, where his big decisions and his bold gestures aroused
more and more admiration. Ouvrard only becomes talkative
again when his original line of business crops up: the grocery
trade. On this subject he is unashamedly vain. In one single
sentence he records—it must have been early in 1796—how,
together with a few other capitalists from Bordeaux, he bought
a consignment of groceries on which he alone made a profit of
half a million francs in three months. But he was careful not to
give precise details which would enable the reader to compare
the returns with the size of the order, to work out the ratio
between risk and profit!
  This is the only concrete transaction that has been recorded
between November 1793, when Ouvrard arrived in Paris with
the captured trophies of war, and the spring of 1796. It may be
that he simply felt no desire to give an account of each of the many
small transactions, which seemed to him insignificant trifles.
And, following his report of the 500,000 francs which he earned
in Bordeaux, no more is heard of profits running into thousands.
From then on Ouvrard dealt in millions.
  Profits of this magnitude were only feasible if he could extend
the scope and increase the tempo of his commercial transactions,
and there was only one way to achieve this, namely, by obtaining
government contracts. Since the fall of Robespierre and the end
of the Terror, it had become increasingly easy to establish good
relations with leading men. The period of the Directory, 1795-9,
was one of strong reaction against the ascetic Puritanism of the
Terror. Even in the first month or two after the 9th Thermidor,
though trade and industry were at a standstill, public amusement-
           J’
30                            Ouvrard

places shot up in the bloodstained streets of Paris in astonishing
numbers.
  In the first year of the Directory there were 644 dance-halls in
Paris. A ball, called the “Bal des Victimes”, was held and only
those who could show the death-certificate of a father, mother,
brother, sister or other relation liquidated by the Revolution, were
admitted. As in the period after the First World War, moral
ties were relaxed or broken. Relations between the sexes became
more uninhibited. Since divorce had been introduced in 1794,
marriages had been breaking up. The number of prostitutes was
rapidly increasing. The theatres were crowded. There were
