famous hairdressers about whom the women raved. The gulf
between rich and poor grew wider every day. The communist,
Babeuf, displayed his nth Article on placards: “The Revolution
is not over. The rich still have the money. It is they who still
govern. And the poor, as before, are no more than slaves of
labour.” But it made no impression. Everyone, even the man in
the street, lived beyond his means. More and more food and
drink were consumed and gambling spread. There was a universal
craving for attractive or expensive clothes. Trading began again
in the street and on the Bourse, and never since the South Sea
Bubble had so many people been interested in speculating. En¬
joyment and easy earnings were the popular cry.
     The men who came to power in 1795 were not strong enough
to stem the current. Tallien had already been open to corruption
when, as Commissioner of the Convention in Bordeaux, he
controlled the guillotine.    Barras, the key figure, handsome,
pleasure-loving and a former officer from a noble family, had
also forgotten, when he ordered executions in Toulon and
Marseilles, that he was present at the storming of the Bastille.
When he became, at forty, a member of the Directory, he seemed
content merely to satisfy his taste for elegance. In his lace cravat,
knee-breeches and silk stockings he was a picturesque figure,
but he also created an impression of strength and determination.
In fact he was too susceptible to feminine charm and sensual
pleasures. Life was much more enjoyable in a beautiful old
chateau outside the gates of Paris, where splendid fetes could be
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                 31

held in an enormous park, than in the exhausting round of State
affairs. “Barras combined all the vices of the ancien regime and
the new era.” It took money to satisfy these vices, a great deal
more money than a member of the government could earn. “At
that time everything was for sale,” Napoleon said later to
Bourrienne. “Government posts, supplies, foodstuffs, uniforms,
military equipment; they sold everything and were even paid
seventy-five million in advance.”
  Ouvrard knew exactly how to handle a man like Barras. He
knew from experience that really big business with the govern¬
ment was not possible without personal outlay. He waited for a
convenient occasion. Since the autumn of 1795 he had met Barras
on several occasions in Madame Tallien’s salon, where the cream
of Parisian society forgathered. It may well be that Ouvrard had
been of service to the beautiful Madame Tallien and had thereby
gained the confidence of her intimate friend, Barras. It was about
the same period, in September 1795, that Napoleon, still a “little”
man, a mere brigade-commander in the artillery, obtained
material for a new uniform by virtue of a letter from Madame
Tallien to the Paymaster of the 17th Division.
  Ouvrard had certainly had plenty of opportunities to explain
to Barras his proposals for the restoration of a solvent paper
currency before February 1796, when the assignats were with¬
drawn from circulation and a new currency, the “mandat”, was
introduced. It is not known whether his advice was taken, although
he maintained that Barras was very impressed by his proposals.
Ouvrard would have had good reason not to advertise any con¬
tribution he made, for in quite a short time the mandats proved
just as valueless as the assignats had been. Moreover, Ouvrard’s
main concern at this point was not to help the government achieve
real financial stability, for he could see that, for the moment, the
right personalities were lacking.
   His primary objective was to make an impression on Barras
and win his support. No one could rival Ouvrard at this game. It
was very noticeable, on occasions when people were still eager to
play the market but were short of ready cash, that Ouvrard was
always in funds and had ample resources to draw on for further
32                             Ouvrard

investments. Credit facilities were, on the whole, hard to come
by. But, even when he was asked for credit, Ouvrard displayed
the same nonchalance as in any major business deal. His inex¬
haustible optimism carried conviction simply because his re¬
markable business acumen was so obvious. There was no dearth
of bankers and businessmen in Madame Tallien’s salon: Recamier,
the Brothers Michel, Perregaux, Hamelin, Tourton, Bazin and
many others, but, as soon as Ouvrard appeared, he stood out
unmistakably as a financial genius.
     Ouvrard, who was also an accomplished and elegant con¬
versationalist, can have found no difficulty in convincing Barras,
even without talking to him, that he stood head and shoulders
above the rest of the business community. It was not in his nature
to sit brooding over a desk, like a chess-player, laboriously
working out new ideas and fresh schemes. Always friendly and
cheerful, he seemed quite happy to decide on the impulse of the
moment what he should do or not do. It was as if he carried an
invisible divining-rod about with him. His magic formula re¬
mained a mystery, and mystery lent an even brighter halo to a
head which only a few months before had been completely un¬
known.
     Barras, the grand seigneur, also paid his tribute. He realized
that Ouvrard was a man with whom it would pay to co-operate
and who, however big the deal, would always remain solvent.
Soon it was the most natural thing in the world for Ouvrard to
suggest an informal meeting. Indeed, the day came when the
leading statesman in France called on Ouvrard at his country
residence at Vitry just outside Paris. What the two men discussed
between four walls was never disclosed. Barras makes no reference
to it in his memoirs; Ouvrard maintains a discreet silence in his.
But that the discussion was fruitful for both parties can be
deduced from Ouvrard s terse observation: “That day marked
the beginning of my business relations with the government. It
changed the whole course of my career.” {Memoirs.)
     This change involved Ouvrard in a return to his first field of
activity. His banking activities receded once more into the back¬
ground, and trading in foodstuffs was resumed. The scope,
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                 33

however, was enormously increased and the market was guaran¬
teed. To begin with, at least, Ouvrard did not have to worry
about selling his goods. For a year and a half, from May 1793
until the end of 1794, the people of Paris had been largely
dependent on the government for their food supplies, but that
ideal state of affairs was now over. On the other hand, the State’s
fighting troops were lamentably short of supplies, and Barras was
ultimately responsible for the army’s Commissariat.
  Naturally, Ouvrard found no reason to criticize such a situa¬
tion. What did irk him was that private capital was not allowed to
operate freely in the navy. Whereas the army had always received
its supplies direct from private concerns, the navy’s supplies were
controlled by the State. Ouvrard was undoubtedly right when
he said that in consequence “the administration of the navy was
just as inefficient as that of almost all other government depart¬
ments”. It was, therefore, with the public interest as well as his
own in mind that Ouvrard set about the formidable task in 1797
of completely reforming the navy’s victualling system. Ironically
enough, although it was no mere accident, when a government
commission was set up, he was called upon to give a report on
the merits and demerits of private enterprise. Naturally he put
forward the view, which, in the prevailing circumstances and
taking into account the people involved, was undoubtedly the
correct view, that the most efficient business is the one run by a
man with a financial stake in its success. He stressed the obvious
fact that since 1793 dishonest officials had been conducting a
regular black market in naval stores and that the quality of wood
and iron, of cordage and sails, had left a great deal to be desired.
It was well known that the Minister of Marine, Pleville-Peley,
had proved himself to be a rare bird in the administration by
returning to the Treasury 33,000 francs of the 40,000 set aside
for a particular mission.
   Ouvrard, on this occasion, certainly did not reveal all that was
in his mind, otherwise he would have been obliged to add that
the victualling of the navy would guarantee immense wealth to
the private individual entrusted with it, that the overall cost to
the State might be somewhat increased but that the result would
34                           Ouvrard

be an efficient organization. In his report Ouvrard showed a
marked concern that the government, which must be both the
controlling and executive    authority, should    be   relieved of
many small responsibilities and thereby spared much unnecessary
work.
  Ouvrard’s intervention was completely successful. Naturally
he was entrusted with the new administration. He was even given
a splendid title, General Victualling Master of the Navy, and a
budget of nearly sixty-four million francs. His annual profit from
that point onwards was not ascertainable. Even individual prices
could not be controlled. When the original listed price for wine
was raised from 494 francs per ton to 650 francs, thus yielding a
surplus of 775,000 francs on 5,000 tons of wine alone, this could
not fairly be regarded as pure profit without taking into account
the fluctuation in the wholesale purchase price and in the value of
money. But one thing can be said with certainty: in a transaction
of this magnitude the annual revenue ran into millions. And the
most important clause in the contract, which in a period of in¬
flation was also most unusual, required payments to be made in
silver dollars.
     It must have come as a great relief to Ouvrard when the
meticulous Pleville-Peley, who on resigning even left his entire
savings behind, had to give way to Rear-Admiral Bruix. The
new Minister of Marine was a sailor of considerable repute, with
whom the General Victualling Master was soon on the best of
terms. In the years to come Ouvrard had much to thank him for.
Nor was he forgetful of this when the Admiral died an early death
in 1805. With a generosity which was one of his many distinctive
traits, he immediately paid the widow of a man who could no
longer be of use to him the sum of 500 louis d’or—a substantial
amount in those days. He makes no mention of it in his memoirs.
     It may have been Bruix, and most probably it was, who
negotiated an extension of Ouvrard’s naval contract to cover the
ships of a friendly nation. The Minister also had under his com¬
mand twenty-five ships, which lay in Brest, the chief port of
Brittany. The Admiral succeeded in breaking through the
English blockade with his fleet and bringing back the ships of the
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                 35



Allied Spanish fleet. He sailed right round the Iberian Peninsula
to a point near Gibraltar, escorted the Spaniards out of the port
of Cadiz and brought them back safely to France. These ships,
cut off from their home base, had to be provisioned. The cost
was, of course, borne by the Spanish Government. Nothing
seemed more natural than that the Spanish Admiral Massaredo
should entrust the victualling of his ships to the same man whose
relations with the Minister and Admiral were so cordial and who,
moreover, had proved his ability to carry this heavy responsibility.
A formal contract was drawn up, which was signed by the Spanish
Premier Urtigo.
  From Ouvrard’s point of view this was a particularly profitable
arrangement. As the Spaniards were on a war footing and ready
to sail at a moment’s notice, supplies were commissioned on a
day-to-day basis, so that Ouvrard was able to demand the higher
prices which had been agreed for actual operations. At the same
time the English saw to it that the Spaniards were unable to
return to their home ports for some considerable time. As a result,
the victualling of the Spanish fleet went on for several years and
earned Ouvrard, on his own admission, a clear profit of four
million Mexican piastres, between fifteen and twenty million
francs, varying with the expenses Ouvrard incurred in realizing
the drafts on the Mexican treasury, which he received in lieu of
cash. This is the third sum mentioned proudly by Ouvrard. The
first was the 300,000 francs he cleared from the paper deal at the
outbreak of the Revolution in 1789, the second was the 500,000
francs he earned from the sale of groceries in Bordeaux, and
now, between 1798 and 1799, there was the 15—20 million from
the Spanish contract. This was the sort of rising scale of profits
which would make any merchant’s heart beat faster. Yet Ouvrard
would not have felt so elated had he been able to foresee that
this enormous access of wealth would almost ruin him ten years
later.
   For the moment, however, Ouvrard moved on from one
success to another. It is inherent in the sheer logic of economic
development and trading practice that increasing profits required
new ideas, further investments and fresh partnerships. He put
36                            Ouvrard

several millions into the firms Giradot & Co., Charles Rougemont
& Co., Charlemagne & Co. in Paris and three firms in Brest,
Bordeaux and Orleans. Before long these names were mere
covers for his own. They were part of the “concern”. His Paris
offices were steadily expanding and already looked like a Ministry.
They were run by Turpin, the former Director of the Treasury,
and Cambaceres, both remarkable men.
  In choosing Cambaceres as a collaborator, Ouvrard had
again shown that shrewd psychological insight to which he
owed so much of his success. Cambaceres had a rare, almost
paradoxical, capacity for forgetting insults quickly and yet never
forgetting services rendered to him. His friendship was of in¬
calculable value to Ouvrard for Cambaceres rose steadily from
one influential government post to another. President of the
Council of the Five Hundred, Minister of Justice under the
Directory, a Consul together with Napoleon, Deputy Head of the
Government, Arch-Chancellor under the Empire, editor of the
Code Napoleon, Duke of Parma: these were the various stages in
a brilliant career. In a magnificent house on the Place du Carrousel
he would receive the leading men of France, wearing, we are
told, a velvet suit embroidered with gold and an enormous
powdered wig.
     Yet he continued to help Ouvrard. It was almost certainly he
who sent a secret message from a session of the State Council to
warn Ouvrard that Napoleon had ordered his arrest. Bonaparte
himself certainly suspected as much, although Cambaceres stood
high in the Emperor’s favour. Some years later, on 25 th Decem¬
ber 1805, when Napoleon wrote from Austria to his brother
Joseph in Paris that he intended to imprison Ouvrard and his
associates, the Michel Brothers, because they were endangering the
State Treasury, he expressly forbade him to inform Cambaceres.
But even so Cambaceres managed to save the merchants, and
particularly Ouvrard, from imprisonment at Vincennes and to
arrange for the affair to be settled on a purely commercial basis.
     Cambaceres’ previous participation in Ouvrard’s firm was all
the more fruitful as the interruption of his political career cannot
have been a long one. It probably came at the same time as
                      Paris: the Great Opportunity                 37


Ouvrard, the private financier, was first approached by a French
government for a loan to tide it over a barren period. That was
in 1798. Defeat after defeat was being suffered in Italy while
Napoleon was in Egypt. The large sums of money which
Napoleon had been sending regularly from the conquered
territories to the Directory, had ceased. The government was not
even in a position to pay the fighting forces. The whole army was
poorly equipped and badly in need of uniforms, arms and
ammunition. The State coffers were empty; no further tax
revenue could be expected, although some of it had already been
spent in advance. So Ouvrard was asked for an advance of ten
million. This was not a large amount for him even in a time of
general stagnation. Again one of his basic principles had stood
him in good stead: always have a substantial amount of ready
money to hand.
    The momentary dependence of the government gave Ouvrard
an opportunity to indulge one of his pet ambitions: cleaning up
the State’s finances. For the past five years he had watched three
successive governments bungling through. His ten million francs
would only stem the flood for a few weeks. Ouvrard was not so
foolish as to believe that the individual would go unscathed, or
still less that trade could prosper for long, if the State went bank¬
rupt. But the persistence with which he returned to his financial
theories throughout the years (including much more stable
periods than that preceding the fall of the Directory) is a clear
indication that his interest in solving the problem was partly a
genuine and unselfish desire to master it for its own sake. And
added to this was the awareness, which he had acquired from the
patricians of Nantes, that he and the men of his profession could
make or mar the future, if not of the world, at least of those living
in it.
  Ouvrard had discussed the financial situation with leading
statesmen on several occasions. He had condemned the system
of assignats as unworkable. He had advised on the introduction
of the mandats. Now, in 1798, he felt convinced that his financial
theories would at last be taken seriously. He sat down to work
them out systematically, something he had never done before. It
 fj & ^aft'/uoP 0^"'‘

3$                            Ouvrard

was, in fact, the first time—though by no means the last—that
he had put his thoughts in writing. His main recommendation
was the establishment of a National Debt on the British pattern.
His thesis as a whole was a repetition of the familiar arguments
about the productive power of credit, which he had learned in
Nantes from Graslin, the building contractor.
     An essential feature of Ouvrard’s memorandum was a criticism
of the national budget procedure. Each year saw a new and
completely fictitious budget based on hypothetical economies,
which no government department had the slightest intention of
making. “The only result achieved by this economy race is that,
before the end of the financial year, the coffers are empty and the
Ministers are not in a position to meet real expenditure.” Par¬
ticularly unfortunate (for suppliers and therefore for Ouvrard)
was the fact that “in the last resort the State’s creditors have to
carry the deficit”. Ouvrard warned against groundless expecta¬
tions of unforeseen revenue, which could lead to a quite irre¬
sponsible acceptance of obligations. “The national economy must
be run on the same principles as private economy.”
     Once the State has acquired debts—unfortunately not a
National Debt—it must try to pay them. “Otherwise it falls into
the unhappy situation of a bad debtor, with whom one may
sympathize but with whom one can no longer do business.”
(Memoirs.) These debts could not be paid out of normal revenue;
a special sinking fund must be set up, which would draw its
income from a comprehensive National Debt Administration.
This debt would have to be consolidated on a long-term basis
and secured by adequate refunding operations. “Interest and
amortization must be limited to a third or a quarter of the
national revenue.” {Memoirs.)
     Even with her existing debts, France would benefit by a large
National Debt. It was the only way to stimulate the country’s
development; money would come pouring in from all over
Europe, if a shrewd government, which enjoyed international
confidence, could offer stocks with attractive prospects and a
good measure of security. France would then be able to spend this
money on generous, long-term projects. “Loans in the form of
                        Paris: the Great Opportunity                      jg

fixed-interest bonds (or rentes), which are secured by a sinking
fund, represent a public debt, which happily decreases steadily
and is finally liquidated, without imposing too heavy a burden
on the taxpayer and without in any way damaging the lenders.
The floating of such a debt mobilizes capital and must, in fact,
lead directly to an increase in the money and credit supply. It
serves, in short, as a stimulus to every form of business activity.
As a consequence, the taxpayers are prosperous, the tax revenue
itself must automatically increase, and a sense of utter confidence
prevails between the government and the people.”*
   Had there been a government in France at that time which
was filled with the desire to govern well, Ouvrard’s eloquence
would certainly have succeeded in pressing home such clear,
simple ideas. But in 1799, the last year of the Directory, as in
early 1794, the period of the Terror, no one had time to study
his financial system. It was easier to accept his credit than his
advice.
   Ouvrard understood the financial problems of his day because
he was a born financier. He had never had any doubt about his
vocation. From the moment when he first stood on his own feet
he had known only one ambition: the accumulation of money.
That is why he had remained unperturbed in 1796 and 1797,
when the Revolution in Paris was in its final throes and Babeuf,
the communist, tried to emancipate the people still further by
abolishing private capital. These two men, so unlike in every
respect, do not ever appear to have met. Babeuf, who had himself
adopted the name “Gracchus” amongst his disciples, the “Equals”,
had a passionate contempt for money; Ouvrard was its most
persistent and fortunate devotee. Babeuf went out of his way to
quarrel with anyone in a position of power who might have been
of use to him; Ouvrard knew how to get on with people, even
with the hard men of the Terror. The navvy’s son from Roye
paid with his life for his proclamation of the new gospel—the
gospel which promised universal happiness on the day that “mine”

  * At this time more than half the French national debt was a floating debt
and therefore in theory repayable at short notice. Interest on the public
debt accounted for about half of the annual budget.—T.A.B.C.
40                           Ouvrard

and “thine” disappeared from the language. The man from
Nantes was accused of having acted against the welfare of the
people in his financial transactions. In view of Babeuf’s poverty
and lack of influence, Ouvrard considered his arguments about
dress reform and town planning to be futile. One can well
imagine Ouvrard pausing before a hoarding at a street corner to
read with a contemptuous smile the communistic manifesto of
Gracchus Babeuf.
  For the ideas proclaimed by Babeuf fell on deaf ears in Paris,
his threats had no effect. Babeuf’s movement remained half¬
hearted, uncertain and ineffectual. Ouvrard, the realist, never
had occasion to give it serious thought. In his memoirs he makes
no mention of the last of the revolutionaries. Just how completely
unmoved he was by the gospel of equality can be seen from the
fact that he lived a life of luxury which at that time was almost
undreamt of. The faubourgs of Paris, particularly the working-
class quarter Faubourg St. Antoine, were still on the borders of
starvation between 1796 and 1799, when Ouvrard was openly
displaying his wealth. The number of dancing establishments in
1799 had, admittedly, almost doubled since 1795 to about 1,500,
but people were still dying of exhaustion in the streets. The
luxurious life of the upper classes in pre-Revolution France was
now so far beyond the reach of even a few that in 1800, when
Napoleon made his ceremonial change of residence from the
Luxembourg to the Tuileries, carriages had to be hired for the
State Councillors.
     Ouvrard was one of the few who, during this period of in¬
flation before 1800, had a very large income, and, while houses,
lands, estates and chateaux were still going cheap, he invested
extensively in property. He bought Preuilly with 18,000 acres of
forest. By acquiring Chateau Neuf, Saint Brice, Marly, Luciennes
and Vitry he secured his vast earnings against devaluation. Round
Cologne in Gers he purchased twenty-four farms, which brought
in 600,000 francs a year in rent. In Paris itself he bought the
Palais Montesson in the Rue du Mont Blanc, houses in the
Rue de Provence, on the Place Vendome and in many other
areas. More than one seller, who could not afford to keep his
                     Parts: the Great Opportunity

property, complained bitterly because Ouvrard, being a merchant,
would pay only in accordance with the law of supply and
demand.
  General Thiebault, in particular, protested furiously in his
memoirs because the Chateau of Saint Gratien de Villandry,
which his father-in-law himself offered to the General Victualling
Master, was bought by Ouvrard for 350,000 francs, although
Monsieur Chenais had originally paid 850,000 for it. The angry
heir swore that the owner of Villandry was bemused by Ouvrard’s
personal magnetism, by his luxurious house, by the Grand
seigneur’s fine manners and finally by a well-timed bottle of
champagne (“the most expensive that was ever drunk”), but this
story is hard to believe. Chenais had lost his entire fortune
through the revolution in San Domingo, thereby confirming
Ouvrard’s foresight in 1789, when he decided not to invest the
300,000 francs he had earned from the paper deal in colonial
plantations. There were few people who could afford 350,000
francs for a chateau, and if Chenais wanted to sell he had to be
content with less than half the original price. Ouvrard was not
exploiting an individual but the prevailing circumstances. Chenais,
in fact, was in precisely the same position as all other property
owners, the value of whose real estate had dropped following the
confiscation of emigre properties by the government. In the year
1799 alone the Directory tried to sell 125 million francs’ worth
of nationalized property, but, owing to a shortage of buyers, was
able to dispose of only a small percentage even at very reduced
prices.
   One of these nationalized estates was the Chateau of Rainey,
three to four miles outside Paris, which Ouvrard also bought. It
must have been about 1797. But Rainey was no ordinary chateau,
like the “residence with two weather-vanes” which was so much
sought after, or like Vitry, Villandry and Chateau Neuf. Rainey
was Ouvrard’s great extravagance, the symbol of his wealth, his
power, his social accomplishments and his scintillating personality.
It was an old Bourbon property which had been inhabited by the
Duke of Orleans until the Revolution. Rainey was so magnificent
that in 1808 Napoleon took it from General Junot merely on the
42                             Ouvrard

pretext that he must have a chateau near Paris. Recamier, who
had a country seat at Clichy-la-Charenne which formerly belonged
to a Due de Levis; even Barras, who had acquired the former
seat of Louis XVI’s brother at Grosbois; and later Talleyrand at
Neuilly—they all lived in a style befitting their station, but none
of them possessed Ouvrard’s consummate skill or had his truly
aristocratic feeling for things cultural.
     The chateau stands in the middle of a splendid park, which was
designed by Le Notre. The woods are full of deer, which today
gaze in anxious wonderment at the traffic streaming past from
Paris. Ouvrard always regarded hospitality as “the finest attri¬
bute of wealth”. After alterations had been made by the new
proprietor, no house could have been more suitable than Rainey.
In the splendid vestibule thirty-two Doric pillars, each one a
monolith, were surmounted by an enormous entablature. The
adjacent salon, octagonal in shape, was a delightful and unusually
exciting room. The features, mood and colour of the figures in
Lebrun’s large pictures kept changing with the reflection of a
thousand candles in the water of a huge basin, twenty feet in
diameter, in the centre of the room. At the top of the wide stair¬
case one entered the large Italian salon, along the entire length
of which—some eighty feet—were Ionic pillars right up to the
ceiling. Doors of mirror glass led to the master of the house’s
bedroom. But the real piece de resistance of this house was the half¬
moon-shaped bathroom with its floor of rare yellow marble. The
two enormous bathtubs, hewn out of blocks of grey-and-black
Vosges granite, were the talk of Paris. A stove of verde antico
gave out a comfortable warmth and when, after his bath, the
master of the house reclined on a velvet-covered sofa in an alcove,
he could feast his eyes on a rich fresco on the ceiling, which
represented Diana and her nymphs bathing.
     Twenty years later, in 1816, when the Bourbons were restored
and recovered their property, the Duke of Orleans declared on
seeing the bathroom at Rainey: “To create that needed a greater
man than I have ever been.” Here indeed Ouvrard had strained his
ambition to the uttermost. Only one thing was lacking: the note
of contrast struck by Fabre d’Eglantine, who also acquired his
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                 43

wealth fairly quickly, and who set aside one room in his palace
which contained souvenirs of his early poverty—a pallet bed, a
walnut table, two straw-bottomed chairs and a dilapidated
chamber-pot.
  Fetes at Rainey, which were organized by one of the leading
architects of Paris, Bertheaux, always provided an impressive
programme, and the guests who did not want to miss anything
had to leave Paris in the early afternoon. In front of the chateau
huntsmen blew their horns, the horses were led forward, hunting
carriages drove up and the pack strained impatiently at the leash.
The company moved off with Ouvrard at the head. When the
happy throng returned at dusk they were greeted with hundreds
of torches and coloured lanterns. The rigours of the hunt gave
added zest to the luxury that followed. The dining-room was
fringed by a long row of beautiful orange trees, their blossom
and fruit exuding the most delicious scent. In the marble basin
multi-coloured fish turned and twisted over a foundation of gold
dust. The choicest of dishes were served on silver plates and
golden salvers. Rare fruits from the Orient and the Occident
were heaped on priceless porcelain. The walls of the adjoining
coffee-room were covered with vines laden with bunches of
sweet grapes. In the four corners stood shell-shaped marble
basins with small gushing fountains of punch, orange-juice and
almond juice. Concealed behind banks of flowers an orchestra
played softly, until the time came for it to break into dance
music.
  But Ouvrard’s hospitality was not nearly so impersonal as this
glittering scene suggests. With uncommon generosity he used to
offer his house and his hospitality to his friends even when he
himself was not there or did not join the guests. It was also not un¬
usual for one of his acquaintances, such as Monsieur de Norvins-
Monbreton, to receive permission to spend a day with a score of
his friends in the park at Rainey and to live in the charming
Telegraph Pavilion. When they arrived with their picnic baskets,
they found to their surprise that an excellent lunch was waiting
for them, followed in due course by a no less excellent dinner.
When, towards nine o’clock, tea, ice-cream, sherbet and other
44                            Ouvrard

delicacies were served, there was still no sign of the master of the
house. He excused himself from each meal in a short note. “The
reception we were given was as rich, as distinguished as one could
possibly imagine, but the most distinguished touch of all was the
modesty and tact of our host, who had not considered his presence
fitting.”
    Ouvrard was certainly not exaggerating when he said later
that, if visitors’ books had been in vogue at that time, amongst the
recorded guests at Rainey would have been not only kings, princes
and lords but also the cream of the nobility, of literature and of
the arts. Charles James Fox spent a whole week there hunting.
Napoleon’s furious enquiry as to how long his Ministers had
held council at Madame Recamier’s might well have been
applied to Rainey after 18th Brumaire. All his leading generals,
Moreau, Junot and Marmont in particular, were constant visitors.
Napoleon himself went only once, to visit his War Minister when
he was staying there. A number of famous women could be seen
there. On one occasion Mesdames Recamier, Tallien and de
Stael, the beautiful Duchess of Courland, and the seductive
Visconti, the War Minister’s mistress, all took the floor at a
splendid ball. Another welcome guest at Rainey was Lucien
Bonaparte, who on one occasion at dinner took a sensational
revenge on Madame Recamier for rejecting his advances; when
he asked his fellow guests for their attention and proposed a
toast to the most beautiful woman present, all eyes turned to the
blushing Madame Recamier, but after a brief, dramatic pause,
Lucien toasted “La Paix“It is Peace we all long for.”
     Especially favoured guests had a pavilion in the park all to
themselves, so serving Ministers could enjoy free lodging on
Ouvrard’s estate for more or less indefinite periods. Chief among
these were Talleyrand and Berthier. Ouvrard knew what he was
doing, for here again his natural pleasure in social life had a firm
core of commercial calculation. Berthier, Napoleon’s right-hand
man, lived for a lengthy period in a hunting-lodge in the park
with the famous and beautiful Visconti. Rainey provided the
ideal seclusion for this passionate love-affair, which was almost
savage in its intensity.
                      Paris: the Great Opportunity                 45

   Ouvrard was seldom mistaken in his assessment of the politi¬
cians and soldiers with whom he collaborated. As early as 1794,
when General Bernadotte, later to become King of Sweden but
at that time entirely without private means, was anxious to get
married, Ouvrard advanced 50,000 francs, which was later repaid
by the firm of Bernadotte’s father-in-law. It may be, however,
that this friendly gesture had some influence on an army contract
which Ouvrard obtained under the name Boissonneau, when
Bernadotte was Minister of War (1798—99). In any case he was
flattered by the knowledge that he had been able to help the man,
who, having begun in 1780 as a private soldier, later became
Crown Prince and finally King of Sweden. Berthier became not
only Minister of War but eventually one of the few men whom the
suspicious Napoleon really trusted. It was Berthier who had to
recover Napoleon’s letters from Fouche after the dismissal of the
Chief of Police. It was Berthier who was delegated to ask for the
hand of the Austrian Emperor’s daughter on Napoleon’s behalf.
And it was Berthier who in January 1806, having returned from
Austerlitz to Paris a day before Napoleon, sent Ouvrard an
urgent message—before he even indulged his longing for a hot
bath—advising him to take “precautionary measures” against any
inroads Napoleon might make on his private fortune. This advice
was certainly not wasted. It is, indeed, quite likely that Ouvrard’s
friendly relations with Berthier, the Prince of Neufchatel and
Wagram, had already borne fruit earlier, for around 1800 Berthier
was removed from the War Ministry and made Army Com¬
mander, because he had not been severe enough in his business
negotiations. “He was too complaisant regarding payments due
and the conclusion of new contracts.” Bourrienne, who knew
everything that went on in the inner Cabinet, makes no reference
to Ouvrard’s benefiting more than anyone else from this com¬
plaisance. Nevertheless, he probably did.
  On the whole, however, Rainey was for Ouvrard anything but
a means to an end. This was the one place where his inner self
found its fullest satisfaction and expression, for at heart he was a
Grand seigneur with a natural talent for the grand and esoteric
gesture and for a wide range of social activities. Ouvrard regarded
46                            Ouvrard

patronage of the arts as “one of the greatest joys of a large for¬
tune”, but to it he brought his own individual touch. He not
only had his own collection of old masters but, in contrast to his
contemporaries, he also commissioned a number of pictures from
living artists. The whole ensemble, seen in the context of his
personality, was consistent. Only one thing was lacking to make
the picture complete: an intelligent and elegant wife of the same
stature as himself, who would appreciate and cultivate his tastes.
     Having come to Paris, a mere provincial merchant, Ouvrard
had become, within the incredibly short time of five years, one of
the most influential men in France. His wife could not keep pace
with him. She never dared do the honours in the brilliant society
at Rainey. But Ouvrard wanted to conduct the whole of his
life, including his relations with the other sex, in the grand style.
     So he turned to the woman who for the past five years had
been the toast of Paris, Madame Tallien. On many occasions
throughout the years he had seen her together with other elegant
women in her salon at the Chaumiere, the thatched house on the
Allee des Veuves. He watched her conjure out of the social chaos
of the Terror many of the former social graces of Versailles and,
by sheer personal charm, attract all the influential men in the
government, the army, the arts and high finance. In her salon she
combined seductive grace and an air of indulgence with a strong
sense of purpose; no one would ever say of her, as of Madame de
Stael, that she had an extraordinarily powerful mind and an
unusual command of words. But she was wholly feminine and
had a woman’s shrewdness. She had a full-bodied, voluptuous
beauty with perfectly proportioned limbs. She liked to display
her splendid white shoulders. Her pitch-black hair hung in
ringlets round her face, “like a frame of polished ebony”: her
skin was white, smooth and fresh, her eyes were large under
finely arched brows and her mouth was small with pearl-white
teeth. This was a description given not by some man she might
have bewitched but by a critical member of her own sex, the
Duchesse d Abrantes, who was fascinated by the beauty of those
features and the “transfiguration of this apparition” by the “re¬
flection of a gentle soul ’. The Duchesse felt unable to express in
                     Paris: the Great Opportunity                47

words the almost supernatural perfection” and even speaks of
“an aura of divine beauty”.
  Madame Vigee-Lebrun confesses with the charming simplicity
of a rival that she “sought in vain for a defect in this beautiful
woman”. Most disarming, however, is the tribute paid by the
highly critical Madame de Stael, who in conversation described
Madame Tallien as “a new manifestation of all that is noble and
divine in the human intellect”. Lamartine was particularly im¬
pressed by her temperament. “She combined in her person the
fire of the South, the equanimity of the North and the grace of
France. Thus she was a living embodiment of the beauty of all
countries.” One can begin to understand contemporary reports
to the effect that people who saw Madame Tallien for the first time
involuntarily uttered a cry of delight.
  Ouvrard must have realized at a very early stage that he and
this woman, to whom everyone paid homage as to a queen, were
made for each other. He too was .tall, nearly six foot, and well
built. He had a well-shaped head, a thin mouth, a nose that was
rather too sharp, and eyes that were calm yet very alive. The over¬
all impression he gave was one of striking self-assurance, and
even his enemies could not help admitting that he had great
charm. He was an agreeable and highly accomplished conver¬
sationalist with a polished wit and most attractive manner. He had
no need to indulge, as did the banker Recamier, in original mis¬
quotations from Horace and Virgil, which were all too often
repeated.
  Ouvrard and Madame Tallien had, however, much more in
common than a marked similarity of personality and bearing.
Ouvrard, who with characteristic reticence devoted only a few
lines in his memoirs to the woman who was his close companion
for several years, gave no hint of their intimate relationship when
he said that “she combined courage and character with beauty
and intelligence”. Intellectual courage was something Ouvrard
himself had always possessed. It was almost second nature to
him to produce unorthodox ideas, even if others considered them
disturbing or alarming, and there is no doubt that he owed much
of his success to his unconventional methods. Madame Tallien’s
48                            Ouvrard

career had shown that she, for her part, took a much bolder and
more independent line than most other women in accepting the
new gospel of freedom.
  Born in 1773, Johanna Maria Ignatia Theresia de Cabarrus was
three years younger than Ouvrard. From the beginning, however,
she found it difficult to follow the examples set by her pious name¬
sakes. A more potent force was the influence of her mother, who
had allowed herself to be abducted by Monsieur de Cabarrus.
When Theresia was twelve a completely infatuated uncle made
a formal request for her hand in marriage. In 1788, at the age of
fifteen, she married a small, red-haired Senator, de Fontenay,
who lost no time in getting through her considerable fortune
while having numerous affairs with shopgirls. In this hard school
Theresia learned to stand on her own feet. In 1793 came her first
divorce. In Bordeaux a Commissioner of the Convention, Tallien,
whom she had met briefly in Paris, arranged her release from
prison. He was a forceful, powerful man and she became his
mistress. Once more in prison—this time in Paris—she sent a
note to Tallien, saying that she had been told she was to be
executed the next day. “Last night I dreamt that Robespierre
was dead and the prisons were open.... But thanks to your
cowardice there will soon be no one left in France capable of ful¬
filling such a dream.” Three days later Robespierre fell, and she
married Tallien in 1795. But the bourgeois way of life did not
suit her and Tallien did not live up to his earlier promise. A new
suitor appeared in Barras, a handsome, elegant ex-officer, who
was the son, not of a lackey like Tallien, but of an old and noble
family. Before long she was presiding openly at the table of the
new ruler of France in the Luxembourg. In 1799 she was divorced
but her relations with Barras remained as before.
     After Napoleon became Emperor, he is said to have remarked
contemptuously to Madame Tallien at a masked ball that she had
had two or three husbands, and children by God knows whom!
This left Ouvrard quite unmoved. As soon as he saw the possi¬
bility of winning this remarkable woman for himself, all that
interested him was the singleness of purpose which motivated all
her actions, the originality and consistency which were the hall-
(-Above) The merchant J. J. Vanlerberghe. Lithograph
     by Auguste Bry. {Paris, Biblioth'eque Nationale)

                   Madame Tallien. Miniature by Isabey

marks of her character. At a time when both legality and legitimacy
had disappeared from all spheres of life, the only criterion that
mattered was vitality. And this woman possessed it to a much
higher degree than any others he knew.
  Like Ouvrard, who lent a helping hand to many, she was also
kind-hearted. In Bordeaux she succeeded in taming Lacombe, the
savage President of the Revolutionary Military Committee, and
on more than one occasion in Paris she left her bed in the middle
of the night to plead for prisoners. And if at times—though by
no means always—the help she gave was not entirely disinterested,
Ouvrard was both interested and amused to find a woman with
such a highly developed business instinct. His early visits to her
salon paved the wray to an agreement with Barras. The con¬
versation behind closed doors at Vitry bore considerable fruit,
particularly his appointment as General Victualling Master of the
Navy and subsequently the contract with the Spaniards. He did
business with Madame Tallien’s brothers in Bordeaux, and later
she presented him with a son, who himself developed a marked
talent for business and who was to lend him much-needed support
around 1830. But most significant perhaps was the connection
Ouvrard established with her father, Count Francois de Cabarrus,
who had founded the Bank of St. Charles in Madrid. So it was
through Madame Tallien that Ouvrard first became interested in
the country which was later to inspire the most ambitious financial
project not only of his whole life but of his century.
   Perhaps the strongest of all the bonds between Ouvrard and
Madame Tallien was their mutual interest in good living. They
had the same passion for luxury and society. He was delighted
when she added to her already considerable supply of blond,
black, rust-coloured and red wigs, each of which cost 150 to 200
gold francs, for each one seemed to present her beauty in a new
and charming light, or when she employed various expensive
Indian shawls to lend fresh colour to her beautiful head. Madame
Ouvrard had never learned the secret of encouraging and receiv¬
ing gifts, but Ouvrard took a special pleasure in giving with taste
and with discrimination. Madame Tallien responded to this; she
took pleasure in receiving and showed it. At one point during
50
                                  Ouvrard

their liaison Ouvrard chose a particularly charming way of
presenting her with a house in the Rue Babylone. He invited her
one day to go for a drive in his carriage. Suddenly they stopped
before a magnificent palais in the style of Louis XV. Ouvrard
took a small gold key, set with jewels, from his pocket and, when
they had inspected the rooms and the splendid gardens, handed it
to her with the words: “Adieu, Madame, this is your home.” And,
indeed, when she rang, the bell was immediately answered by
servants, whom Ouvrard had let into the secret.
     In the circumstances Madame Tallien did not find it difficult
to break with Barras and attach herself to Ouvrard. So in 1799
she ceased to be, as she was popularly known, “/a propriete du
gouvernement” and became, so to speak, Ouvrard’s left hand. One
of Barras’s enemies, the hunchback La Revelliere-Lepaux, who
later tried to found a special party of his own, started the in¬
credible rumour that Madame Tallien had been the object of a
disgusting business deal. Ouvrard, it was said, had acted the
lover very much against his will. He was a Grand seigneur who
was prepared to sell his manhood, a gallant who, rather than
forfeit profitable military supplies, agreed to relieve the head of
the government of a mistress he had grown tired of. There was,
of course, no truth in the story. Madame Tallien was the woman
Ouvrard had long been seeking, a born mistress for Rainey,
where she accepted the homage of Ouvrard’s guests like a queen.*
     It was at Grosbois, Barras’s estate, that Ouvrard finally made
his conquest. “Ouvrard and Madame Tallien”, writes a con¬
temporary, “were in adjoining rooms. The following morning,

  * Wolff is here perhaps taking an over-charitable view of a not very

when the hunting-horns sounded, Theresia rode one of Ouvrard’s
horses with Ouvrard at her side. Behind them rode two boys,
one in Tallien’s colours, the other in Ouvrard’s livery. The
couple became parted from the rest of the company. When they
came down late to dinner, it was generally accepted that Madame
Tallien had become the purveyor’s ‘friend’.”
  That day marked the culmination of Ouvrard’s life, for, after
only five years, he had virtually conquered Paris. At the age of
twenty-nine he was not merely a multi-millionaire but had also
won the heart of one of the most beautiful and most coveted
women in France.
                               Five




       The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte




TTHROUGHOUT those five years between 1794 and 1799,
when Ouvrard made his meteoric rise in Paris, there was not
a single hitch, not the slightest setback. Success followed on
success, both in his personal and in his business life. It was a
steep and rapid climb upwards.
   The picture changed when the current of events changed. So
far Ouvrard had only had to deal with people who were follow¬
ing a similar road to his own. Barras, the bon-vivant head of the
government, who constantly needed money, was the prototype
of this period.
   On 9th November 1799, the 18th of Brumaire, the change
came. At ten o’clock in the morning a new and quite different
type of man, Napoleon Bonaparte, who had returned from Egypt
on 16th October, marched along the boulevards of Paris followed
by a glittering escort of generals across the Place de la Concorde
to the Tuileries, where the Council of the Ancients, without
knowing what it was doing, issued a special decree giving the
leader of the coup (That supreme military power for the pro¬
tection of the apparently threatened Republic.
   One of the first to realize what had happened was Ouvrard. Yet
even he seems to have underrated the effect this fairly open
transfer of power would have on the country’s economic life, for
apparently he had no stake in the State rente, which rose in
value within a few days from seven to thirty francs. But on the
morning of the 18th Brumaire he was out early, very soon after
                                 52
                 The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte              55

  the unsuspecting Barras had removed his night-cap in die Palais
 du Luxembourg. As soon as Ouvrard drove up to the palais he
 realized that the situation of the man who had so often and so
 ably befriended him had radically changed. The Directory’s body¬
 guard had disappeared; the sentries had been replaced by
 Napoleon’s troops. The building itself was almost deserted.
 Breakfast was laid for thirty people* but the chairs were empty.
 Ouvrard alone had the strength of character and the courage to
 stay with Barras in his moment of downfall. He was there at
 nine o’clock in the morning when General Moreau rode into the
 courtyard of the Luxembourg to a roll of drums and occupied the
 palace with half a brigade. Soon Talleyrand and Admiral Bruix
 appeared and forced Barras, on Napoleon’s behalf, to hand in his
 resignation. Ouvrard’s all-powerful friend became a private
 citizen literally overnight and from then on the only authority
he wielded was on his estate at Grosbois, to which he retired.
    The man with whom Ouvrard now had to deal was no stranger
 to him. But Ouvrard, the merchant, must have realized that the
 General who had just taken over power was not likely to take the
same interest in business affairs as the former head of government
had shown. Ouvrard remembered with disquiet how he had first
met Napoleon at Madame Tallien’s in 1795, when the squat,
black-haired officer, then almost unknown, had come to beg for
a new uniform, while Ouvrard, who was the same age, strolled
round the salon a great man and a millionaire. Ouvrard had good
reason to assume that Napoleon had not forgotten his first feeling
of antipathy. And, in fact, from the moment he seized power,
Napoleon took every opportunity of cold-shouldering and
harming Ouvrard. But he had other reasons. Ouvrard’s success
had branded him as a symbol of the revolutionary period and this
was anathema to the leader of the counter-revolution, who was
destined to establish an imperial throne. Ouvrard was a prominent
associate of Barras, Napoleon’s enemy, whose downfall was one
of his most cherished ambitions.

  * Barras was accustomed to entertaining on a grand scale. On the 18th
Brumaire, however, only Ouvrard sat down to breakfast with him.—
T.A.B.C.
54                              Ouvrard.

     Ouvrard, the sharp-eyed habitue of the Tallien salon, knew
too much at first hand about the dubious past of Josephine de
Beauharnais, and Napoleon had no wish to be reminded of it.
Finally, Ouvrard was fabulously wealthy. Bonaparte had always
appreciated the real power of money, and for that very reason he
regarded people with a great deal of money with the utmost
suspicion. Of Ouvrard himself he said that a man who had thirty
millions and seemed comparatively carefree was dangerous. More¬
over, if he was to carry out his ambitious plans and satisfy his two
overriding passions, war and fame, he himself would need vast
sums of money in the public coffers. He believed that every franc
a merchant earned from the State acted as a brake on his own
progress.
   Ouvrard, as a merchant and purveyor, was faced on 18th
Brumaire with the task of maintaining his relations with a govern¬
ment whose new head was known to loathe the class of merchants
and purveyors. To quote his own words to Bourrienne, they
were “the scourge and the scum of nations”, and “no aristocracy
could be more intolerable”. They were “sponges” which must
be squeezed. “I have no love for merchants,” he said later in an
address to a trade delegation from Antwerp, “for a merchant
would gladly sell his country for a dollar.” And even in 1811,
when he assured a deputation of merchants and manufacturers,
who had exposed the ruinous effect of the Continental Blockade
on the economy, that trading was “an honourable profession”, he
added a stern warning that they must be satisfied with minimum
profits, a warning which showed that, at heart, he still regarded
trade as legalized robbery. Not surprisingly, therefore, he came
to be known abroad as the “arch-enemy of all trade”, to quote a
Dutch phrase, and even his closest advisers at home, such as the
Minister of the Treasury, Mollien, admitted that Napoleon “was
often unjustly prejudiced against the merchant class; and at the
same time he liked to find excuses for delaying payments”.
     It is only fair to say, however, that, to begin with, Napoleon’s
antipathy was not entirely unjustified. In his first Italian campaign
he had some shocking experiences with army purveyors. Although
money for the supplies had been paid to the purveyors, the troops
               The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte            55

were still badly equipped. English goods to the value of forty
million francs, which had been confiscated for the army in the
port ol Leghorn, soon found their way into the hands of army
purveyors, who had bought them cheaply from soldiers and
officers. In Milan in the late summer of 1796 Napoleon had a
number of supply scandals to deal with. Even his own generals
were making money. Two million francs disappeared from the
loan office in occupied Verona. His own wife even earned good
commission by acting as an intermediary in dubious business
deals, and Madame Talleyrand is also alleged to have received
400,000 francs from Genoese businessmen. Napoleon simply
generalized particular experiences of this kind to cover the whole
of the merchant community.
  The uneasiness Ouvrard felt about the future on the 18th
Brumaire was well founded. Napoleon was so completely biassed
against all merchants as “rogues” and “a pack of scoundrels”
that he was quite incapable of distinguishing the good from the
bad. And it was soon clear that he was also incapable of recogniz¬
ing that the selling price of supplies was frequently high because
of the government’s unrealistic methods of payment, the amount
of risk involved and the constant threat of heavy losses in interest.
Bourrienne, Napoleon’s Military Secretary, Marmont, his Marshal,
Count Mollien, his Minister of the Treasury, and Savary, Due de
Rovigo, his Minister of Police, state in their various memoirs how
difficult it was, by virtue of the number of departments involved,
to settle even a long-outstanding and perfectly legitimate account.
  The situation in France was not quite so bad as in Austria,
where the Archduke Karl, when he became War Minister in 1792,
found in the cellars sacks stuffed with 154,000 unpaid bills for
deliveries which had long since been carried out. But Napoleon
also clung to the general principle that, wherever possible, pay¬
ments should be deferred, either permanently or long enough to
force the supplier to bring down his price. His far-sighted and
devoted Minister of the Treasury, Mollien, tried in vain to achieve
the lowest price with the best quality by applying the principle
of prompt payment. In 1803 an impartial German observer, the
musician Reichardt, wrote in his Intimate Letters from Paris that
                                  Ouvrard
56

“if only one tenth is true of what one hears of the way the govern¬
ment is again .. . exploiting private suppliers, how harshly ... it
often deals with their most legitimate demands and how the
ruling family is once more using them for its own ends,—then
that protection must cost them dear”. Napoleon did not see the
connection. He noticed that though Josephine’s debts were not
paid for years and that though only half of the 1,200,000 francs
that she owed was ultimately paid, the              merchants of Paris
suffered no apparent ill-effects. This merely confirmed his low
opinion of commerce. And when, only a few years before Bona¬
parte’s downfall, the banker Laffitte, by a skilful verbal manoeuvre,
succeeded in telling the Emperor that “trade is by its very nature
a reasonable and far from aggressive occupation, which is of
necessity bound to maintain stability, on which it depends for
its very existence, and it only takes to opposition when it feels
threatened and insecure”—even then the whole idea was still
strange to Napoleon.
     Such then was the man and such were the preconceptions with
which Ouvrard was suddenly confronted. There were, however,
certain important traits in his character which were invaluable in
an emergency of this kind: his indefatigable optimism, the basic
principle that he must always be solvent, and, above all, his pro¬
found sense of mission as a member of the merchant class. In
1523 Charles V was called “Emperor by the Grace of Fugger”.
In 1525 Bartholomaus Welser kept him afloat with two and a
half millions. About the same period Sir Richard Gresham made
the English money market independent of the Dutch bankers.
His son, Thomas Gresham, restored the devalued coinage to its
full value and rehabilitated the country’s credit. The Papal
Nuncio kissed the hand of John Law’s daughter before an
assembled company and opened the ball with her.* Louis XV

   * John Law (1671—1729), a Scotsman by birth, was an adventurer and
financier who succeeded in convincing the French authorities that credit
creation was the only effective means of expanding their economy. He was
allowed to set up the first bank in France, under royal patronage and with
the power to issue its own notes; he was also granted a charter to establish
a trading company in order to put into effect the famous Mississippi scheme.
In 1720 he was made Controller General of the French Finances, but in the
                  The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte                   57

walked in shirt-sleeves in the park at Marly with a Jew, Samuel
Bernard, in order to arrange a loan. And Ouvrard may well
have thought that he was destined to finance the ambitious genius,
Bonaparte.
   If so, Ouvrard was mistaken. Even if he succeeded in suppress¬
ing his antipathy for the conqueror in the hope of achieving his
own aims, Napoleon made no such effort. True, he needed money.
The 500,000 francs, which his friend Collot had placed at his
disposal before the coup d’etat, lasted only a few days. The master
of France needed millions, about twelve to begin with. As early
as 18th Brumaire Ouvrard offered them in a letter to Admiral
Bruix, who was one of Napoleon’s leading advisers. “It occurred
to me that someone, who is already responsible for the major
victualling services in your command, could make such an offer
without indiscretion, and that you will regard this merely as
proof of my devotion to the national cause, which I shall always
feel in duty bound to serve.” But Ouvrard waited in vain for a
summons. Napoleon, it seems, was not impressed by such out¬
spoken patriotic sentiments an^ preferred to go on wrestling with
the finances of his movement |tbit longer, before he stretched out
his little finger to the merchant from Madame Tallien’s salon.
Ouvrard had to sit idly and angrily by, while the first Consul
summoned the leading financiers of Paris on 24th November and
deliberately ignored him. But at least he had the satisfaction of
knowing that Napoleon, who liked to dictate, had to resort to
persuasion and pleading to get an advance of twelve millions
from Messrs. Recamier, Perregaux, Fulchiron, Daviliers and
others, who, in the event, paid only three millions. So deep-rooted
same year both his enterprises were ruined when the speculative “bubble”,
similar to the contemporary South Sea Bubble in England, suddenly burst
and he was compelled to flee from France. During his brief period of in¬
fluence he had done some good to the French economy; however, his ill-
starred schemes served only to engrain even more deeply in the minds of
Frenchmen, their traditional mistrust of credit and their inclination to hoard
cash and buy real estate rather than employ their savings productively.
There was no State bank until Napoleon founded the Bank of France in 1800,
and these prejudices severely hampered both Ouvrard’s and (during the
Second Empire) the Pereires’ ambitious plans to develop the country.—
T.A.B.C.
^8                           Ouvrard

was Napoleon’s antipathy for Ouvrard that he even sent one of
his officers, Marmont, who later became Due de Ragusa, to
Holland with extraordinary terms for a loan. The “Regent”
diamond was to be deposited as security against supplies of
French timber to the value of the loan. The inhabitants of a
country “in which there was nothing but shrubs and bushes”
(Marmont) shook their heads over this strange offer, and besides
Marmont gained the impression, probably quite rightly, that the
citizens of Amsterdam would have had more confidence in him
if he had been acting on behalf of his father-in-law, Perregaux,
than of General Bonaparte.
   If Napoleon was prepared to go to such absurd lengths to
borrow money, then there is no sound reason why he should not
have accepted it from Ouvrard’s outstretched hand. A loan is not
a supply contract, and in the prevailing circumstances the risk
would have fallen on the creditor, not on the debtor. Napoleon
must have been swayed by purely personal considerations, and of
these probably none was so decisive as the woman whose whole
mental outlook and whose past had so attracted Napoleon in
Madame Tallien’s salon, that he felt confident he could win her
away from that milieu.
   Josephine, however, was the third woman who played some
part in Ouvrard’s life, “the good Josephine, against whom one
can make no reproaches, except perhaps that she was too femi¬
nine” (Bourrienne). Ouvrard, who has recorded that “Madame
de Beauharnais, by her gentle nature, her kind heart and her grace¬
ful bearing, was an ornament to Madame Tallien’s circle”, may
have been on even more intimate terms with her. While there is
no firm evidence to support this, she was “somewhat frivolous
and accommodating”, and, in a period when permanent bonds
were frowned upon, intimate relationships were easily entered
into and easily broken. In any case, she remained sufficiently
friendly with Ouvrard, even after her marriage, to turn to
him whenever she was short of money. When Napoleon was
in Egypt, Josephine was anxious to buy the small chateau at
 Malmaison. But she could not even scrape together a deposit
 of 37,000 francs, much less pay the purchase price of 160,000,
                The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte             59

  although she borrowed 15,000 from a servant. There seems little
  doubt that Ouvrard advanced the missing 22,000, thereby com¬
  mitting an unforgivable crime in the eyes of the jealous Napoleon,
 who doubtless got to hear of this and other financial transactions
 between Ouvrard and his wife, even before Ouvrard’s house was
  searched. Suspicious by nature, Napoleon must have concluded
 that Ouvrard s readiness to help betokened something deeper.
 And yet the bond between the two survived and it was Madame
 Bonaparte who one day warned her friend that the Consul was
 preparing to take certain measures against him.
     Ouvrard, who was no sentimentalist, could not see why the
 past should prevent him from doing business with the leaders of
 the counter-revolution. Nor was he prepared to be ignored by a
 government which was indebted to him. Part of the legacy
 Napoleon had taken over from the Directory was the loan of ten
 million francs which Ouvrard had made to the State in 1798.*
 In December 1799, shortly after the Paris bankers had been
 summoned to the Luxembourg, Ouvrard put in a claim for re¬
 payment of the loan. But Bonaparte was the last person to yield
 to such intimidation. His reaction showed that Ouvrard had at
 last met an adversary worthy of him. He curtly rejected Ouvrard’s
 claim for repayment in eight fixed instalments and decreed that he
 should receive instead letters of credit and national debt certi¬
 ficates (i.e. bills and government bonds). Ouvrard maintained
 that, as a result of this peculiar method of repayment, he even¬
 tually lost the money. He did not hesitate to describe it as robbery.
    At first he found himself wondering whether he should regard
this as one isolated act of vengeance or as the expression of a
“long-term plan of deceit”. He opted for the first, because he
simply could not bring himself to believe that the happy days of
the Directory were really over. As before, he came to the con¬
clusion that the government was incapable of working out a
proper financial policy and therefore needed his advice. So, after
having already submitted his credit plan to the Committee of
Public Safety and to the Directory, he went through it once again
and a few days later submitted it to the new, the third, government.
  * See above page 37.—T.A.B.C.
6o                             Ouvrard

  Of course, no more was heard of it, for Napoleon was so hostile
 to Ouvrard that it was not given serious consideration. Strangely
 enough, Ouvrard could not understand this. So obsessed was he
 with the thought of stabilizing the national finances by launching a
 permanent loan and setting up a sinking fund, that his normal
 vision was completely obscured. But Napoleon’s was no less dis¬
 torted. His personal feeling of antipathy deprived him of the co¬
 operation of a man whose mental calibre was equal to his own.
 Later, after the colossus had crashed, Ouvrard was able to point
 out with understandable satisfaction that Napoleon rejected a
 financial project in 1799, which the Due de Richelieu—again on
 Ouvrard’s advice—adopted in 1817 to France’s great benefit.
    Although it was a project which depended for its success on
public confidence and was, therefore, rather Utopian under the
Directory, it would have been perfectly feasible in the more stable
conditions of the Consulate. But Napoleon probably did not
even look up when Ouvrard’s memorandum was laid on his
desk. “He told me nothing out of the ordinary; he is an ideologist,
a banker. That type of person must of necessity have limited
views.” This was Napoleon’s opinion of Necker and it was a
standard cliche with which he condemned and brushed aside men
like Ouvrard.
    Napoleon was, however, not content merely to ignore Ouvrard.
He had not forgotten that he had had the temerity to stand up to
the new ruler and demand his money back at a highly incon¬
venient moment. So Ouvrard’s great adversary played a second
trump card. He ordered an enquiry into the victualling of the
navy. Forfait, the new Minister of Marine, was one of Napoleon’s
most trusted men. There could be no doubt what the General
wished the enquiry to reveal; faulty deliveries and illicit claims
were, in due course, reported. It is impossible today to judge
an investigation of this kind, for it was subject to no normal legal
control and did not even allow of an appeal. It was a method
that suited Napoleon admirably, for he had never shown any
inclination to subject his own judgement to that of a court of law.
On 27th January 1800—less than three months after Napoleon
came to power—he ordered Ouvrard’s arrest.
                The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte          6l

    The skill with which Ouvrard chose his friends was once more
  apparent. Josephine sent a message from Malmaison to warn
 him what her husband intended to do. For “the charming
  Madame Bonaparte had not forgotten a few small services which
 I had rendered her in the earlier stages of our social life”. Help
 even came from the fateful session of the State Council. One of
  Napoleon s Councillors—probably Cambaceres—who had tried
 in vain to save his friend from disaster, managed to send a
 pencil-written message with his coachman: “You are to be
 arrested; make for a safe place and issue any statements you
 have to give from there.” It was one o’clock in the morning when
 the note was handed to Ouvrard’s door-keeper with express
 instructions to give it to his master before he entered the house.
    It seems that Ouvrard had time to put his papers in order
 before the house was searched, but this respite was hardly
 necessary. Anything he had to conceal was not likely to be in
 black-and-white. In his memoirs he himself referred ironically to
 the informality of “business usages” under the Directory. So
 there was little for him to do. But he could not resist the tempta¬
 tion to take a slight revenge. Amongst his correspondence were
 several perfumed letters from a lady, with whose handwriting
 Napoleon was only too familiar. Ouvrard deliberately left them
 on his desk, with the result that Napoleon’s worst suspicions were
 confirmed—that Josephine, even after her marriage, had repeat¬
 edly received money from Ouvrard for Malmaison and for her
 personal expenses. Napoleon was furious. The blow to his vanity
 was all the greater because these documents had been seen by a
third party.
    Ouvrard gave himself a breathing-space of three days, then
surrendered to the police. It was characteristic of him that he
always faced up to his opponents, even when his position was
morally, legally or politically unfavourable. He never once tried
to run away, although on most occasions he could have escaped
arrest. In the last resort this policy always paid dividends. On
this occasion, in January 1800, when he paid his respects to the
Prefect of Police, he learned that he was merely to be subjected
to a kind of protective house-arrest. On his return home he found
62                              Ouvrard

that every article of furniture containing documents had been
sealed. To judge by Bourrienne’s account, Napoleon was far from
pleased by Ouvrard’s self-confidence. “The fool does not know
what is in store for him. He would have the public believe that his
hands are clean, but he is playing a dangerous game. He won’t
get the better of me. Let him talk as much as he likes! You can be
sure, Bourrienne, that, when such a man has money, he cannot
have acquired it honestly—besides, all such people with money
are dangerous. In a revolutionary age no man should have more
than three millions, and even that is too much.”
   Ouvrard’s arrest created a tremendous sensation in Paris. The
merchants of the capital were extremely alarmed by the high¬
handed way the new man had dealt with their most prominent
colleague. A deputation of twelve businessmen was sent to
Napoleon to impress upon him the disastrous effect such action
could have. Meantime for several days there was a steady stream of
visitors to Ouvrard’s palais, where more than five hundred people
called to sign their names in his visitors’ book as an expression
of sympathy. Ouvrard himself, encouraged by the widespread
indignation, wrote a courageous letter on 31st January:

     General,
        In defiance of the laws, which protect a citizen’s property
     and person, you are threatening my freedom. In time of war
     and in a general’s tent such an act might perhaps be tolerated,
     but in France and committed by the Head of State it must
     arouse the indignation of all decent people who are con¬
     scious of their rights.
        If you are of the opinion that I have behaved contrary to
     the law, then allow a court of justice to decide. Till this
     happens, I shall not cease to protest against your action.
     While I may not be able to prevent your despotism, I can
     at least warn my fellow citizens of the danger that threatens
     them.


  The remarkable thing is that Napoleon beat a partial retreat.
Ouvrard was actually set free, although he remained under
               The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte             63

police supervision. The financial dispute continued. Six State
Councillors spent several months studying his papers and finally
estimated the value of his property, money and securities at
twenty-nine million francs. The outcome of the investigation
was never recorded. But, after all that had been done, said and
written, Ouvrard made a surprising offer to the government: he
proposed to send the State Treasury the sum of 14,167,680 francs
(livres), until such time as a balance-sheet had been drawn up
of the income and expenditure of the former Chief Purveyor to
the Army. By a formal decision of the Consuls the offer was
accepted. Ouvrard carefully omits any mention of this in his
memoirs and nothing more was heard of the fourteen million
francs. There is no way of knowing whether this was a repayment
to an overdrawn account, a refund to cover overcharging, or
perhaps something quite different, a simple acceptance of power
politics. At all events, Ouvrard had to submit. He also had to
write off the ten millions lent to the Directory. Still worse, he was
relieved of his post as Victualler-in-Chief to the Navy. His
assurance that “he had always had the country’s welfare at heart”
did not help.
   One would be justified in assuming that Ouvrard, in disgrace,
was finished. Yet only a few weeks later, in March or April of the
same year, 1800, Ouvrard could be seen re-entering the War
Ministry. He had suddenly been summoned to play a decisive
part in preparing Napoleon’s biggest venture so far: a new Italian
war, the Marengo campaign. Ouvrard, whose self-confidence
played him many a trick, was convinced that Napoleon himself
had summoned him and had finally crossed the Alps on his
advice. After all that had gone before, this is unlikely. A much
more probable explanation is that Ouvrard’s personal contacts
had once again proved their worth. Berthier, the Minister of War,
was still living at Rainey, where he occupied one of the delightful
pavilions in the park with his mistress, Madame Visconti.
Berthier had full powers to plan and organize the whole campaign,
so he restored his friend to the position from which Napoleon,
who now had other things on his mind, had only recently re¬
moved him. On this occasion, however, Ouvrard had to share the
64                            Ouvrard

business with a grain merchant, Ignace-Josephe Vanlerberghe,
who was reputed to be a shrewd businessman and had acquired
the post of Head of General Supplies. But Ouvrard had only to
concede a third part of the contract and had no difficulty in
coming to an agreement on prices. From then on he had a
partner who understood him, who shared his optimism, who
admired his energy, and who did not quarrel with him when
things went badly. Nor did the government make any difficulties.
There was an atmosphere of reconciliation and rejoicing when
the Battle of Marengo brought Italy back into the French fold
on 14th June 1800.
   Although the Italian campaign came to an end without any
personal summons to Ouvrard from Napoleon, the great moment
arrived earlier than either of them had really expected. In the year
1802 a situation arose which was very similar to that of the 18th
Brumaire. Bonaparte was in a cleft stick. This time it was not so
much ready money as practical advice that he needed. Prices had
risen so steeply that in places there was a state of near-famine
and of anarchy. Grain stores had to be guarded by soldiers. In
Amiens and various other towns the granaries had already been
plundered. Napoleon was faced with the choice between making
himself unpopular by using force and losing authority by turning
a blind eye. He turned once more to the bankers. Eight of them
appeared, led by Perregaux, Recamier and Fulchiron. But they
merely shook their heads. This was not their line of country.
Supplies could be dealt with only by an expert. Napoleon now
had no option but to turn to Ouvrard. The great moment had
come. Bonaparte was forced to admit that the merchant whom
he had so contemptuously maltreated was now indispensable to
him.
  It was eleven o’clock at night when Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe
were summoned. That same night they arrived at Malmaison.
Napoleon explained that he did not want money but help in
organizing food supplies. In the special circumstances Ouvrard
and Vanlerberghe were willing to accept only 2 per cent com¬
mission. They set to work immediately. By means of express
couriers they bought up all the grain shipments which were
                 The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte                   65

either lying in Dutch and English ports, or which were due there.
The grain was transferred to Havre and Rouen and distributed
from there.* In quite a short time the operation was completed,
popular unrest died down, and order was restored.
   The whole transaction had cost the tidy sum of twenty-six
million francs. When the time came for the government to pay,
the trouble began. Napoleon “had literally rushed at our offer”,
Ouvrard reported. “He made no difficulties and raised no objec¬
tions, promised everything we asked for, accepted all our con¬
ditions, and all this with a joy and gratitude, which in that hour
of need were certainly sincere.” But when the hour of need had
passed, Napoleon had forgotten everything. He had suddenly lost
interest. In vain Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe presented the bills
for the twenty-six million francs when they fell due, bills which
had been made out to them personally. The government had no
money. It was prepared to pay only the commission, but, having
failed to get the partners to agree to halve their commission, it
refused to pay even this. Months went by and nothing happened.
Napoleon has recorded that he never forgot anything and never
changed his mind. “The State always calls upon me in an emer¬
gency,” said Ouvrard, “but once the emergency is over, my
services are rewarded with bankruptcy.”
   Nevertheless, in May 1803, when Napoleon proposed that he
should take over naval supplies again, Ouvrard did not refuse.
Bonaparte had certainly not forgotten that he had previously
cancelled Ouvrard’s contract in the most high-handed way, but
he had apparently learned not to allow his personal feelings to
interfere with the work in hand. In 1803 he was preparing to
make a landing in England. Ouvrard, for his part, simply could
not say “No”. In spite of all his previous experiences, he could
not resist the prospect of a major business transaction which
would continue for six years, but, quite apart from that, the
government owed him so much money that he could not afford
to reject the offer.
