   * It was possible to buy grain in England as the two countries had been at
peace since the Treaty of Amiens, concluded two months before, in March
1802.—T.A.B.C.
66                            Ouvrard

    Once again Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe joined forces. Building
up a large landing-force, which was quite a new type of operation,
was incredibly expensive, and a year later, in April 1804, when
they worked out their first statement of costs, the govern¬
ment, according to Ouvrard, owed them 67,845,000 francs.
Napoleon acknowledged receipt of the statement with a satisfied
smile: “Soon Ouvrard will not even have any breath left.” And,
indeed, even Ouvrard, who appeared to have unlimited resources
to draw on, gradually found himself in serious difficulties. Ouvrard
and Vanlerberghe had to advance the State so much money, not
all of which could come from their own pockets, that, according
to Ouvrard, the overdraft finally reached the staggering figure of
46,296,000 francs. The government paid only a little and slowly.
Ouvrard alone was twenty millions out of pocket in April. He
and his partner had no alternative but to mobilize their private
property. Ouvrard lost, amongst other things, half of his twenty-
four estates round Cologne in Gers and with them an annual
income of some 300,000 francs. Vanlerberghe had a complete
mental breakdown.
   These frequent incidents are so striking that one finds it hard
to believe they could really have happened. But there is not the
slightest doubt they did. And other merchants fared no better
at Napoleon’s hands. Even his friend Collot, who contributed
500,000 francs free of interest and at considerable personal risk
to enable Napoleon to carry out his coup d'etat on 18th Brumaire,
was just as shabbily treated. Bonaparte behaved towards mer¬
chants—Bourrienne’s comparison was very apt—more or less
as he behaved towards the Beys in Egypt, whom he squeezed for
money. Even the faithful General Rapp, when he tried to find
excuses for Napoleon, merely succeeded in making matters worse:
“Whenever a creditor, whose rights were indisputable, had to be
paid, doubts were sown in his [Napoleon’s] mind about the just¬
ness of the demand (precisely because he was so anxious to have
doubts); he was encouraged to reduce the amount by half, to a
third, frequently even to nothing at all.” Ouvrard in his forth¬
right language called this a long-term system of swindling.
   In fact, Napoleon’s very pronounced concept of ownership
                  The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte                   67

  rested on two things. Napoleon displayed no contempt for money
  where he himself was concerned.* As Consul he began with a
  fixed annual salary of half a million francs. As Head of the Italian
  Republic he earned a further half-million a year. Half a million
  came from the Treasury to put the Tuileries in order for him.
 Alterations to Malmaison cost three million, to St. Cloud six.
  The imperial coronation (“/e pape sacre un fin demon,s) swallowed
 another 500,000 francs in direct expenditure alone. In 1802, the
 year in which Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe found it impossible to
 get the bills for the famine relief honoured, Napoleon spent
 thirteen million francs on household expenses. A year later they
 had increased to more than sixteen million. His wife’s personal
 allowance alone amounted to 600,000 francs. In the cellars of
 the Louvre he had treasures collected during his campaigns worth
  500 or 600 million francs, which none could touch except himself.
 Not uncharacteristic was the repayment of Josephine’s debts
 (600,000 francs) in 1800 from a fine of four and a half million paid
 by the State of Hamburg to Napoleon, naturally as the represen¬
 tative of France.
     There might be a hint of meanness in calling someone to account
 for his millions, when his unlimited power opened up unlimited
 financial possibilities, but Napoleon could hardly complain of
being judged by standards which he applied so arbitrarily to
 condemn others. Compared with Robespierre’s puritanical life or
even the modest style in which Pitt lived, he amassed enormous
wealth. And with a remarkable absence of logic he encouraged
luxury and riches in his officers and statesmen but branded it as
a vice in merchants, to whom it was, after all, a part of their
environment. He heaped expensive gifts on Talleyrand, although
they were paid for by the State. The principality of Benevento,
which he bestowed on him, brought an annual revenue of
1,200,000 francs. And the same Talleyrand, who managed to
combine the two professions of politics and business, was allowed
   * English readers are accustomed to regard Napoleon’s tastes in clothes,
food and personal needs as having been simple in the extreme; it therefore
comes as a surprise to learn of his profligacy. However, the recollections of
his valet Constant, quoted in Bourrienne’s Memoirs of Napoleon, make it
clear that this was so.—T.A.B.C.
68                           Ouvrard

to increase his private fortune unmolested from eighteen to
fifty million francs. As Foreign Minister, Talleyrand made an
estimated profit of ten million merely by his advance knowledge
of the terms of the Treaty of Luneville and the repartition of
Germany. Though on a lesser scale, Berthier had an annual
income of one million francs.
    Claims of that kind were promptly met, but merchants not
only went empty-handed, they even made advances that ran into
millions. When Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe finally realized this,
in March 1804, it seemed to be too late. Ouvrard had to dispose
not only of his estates round Cologne in Gers but also of the
Chateau Neuf, Saint Gratien de Villandry, Saint Brice, Marly,
Luciennes, a house on the Place Vendome and even his own town
house, the Palais Montesson in the Rue du Mont Blanc. Rainey,
over which Madame Tallien still presided, remained his for the
time being.
   During the past few years, however, the house in the Rue du
Mont Blanc had become almost more important than the chateau
outside Paris. If, in spite of Napoleon’s more or less official
hostility, in spite of the general change of climate, Ouvrard had
succeeded in remaining in the forefront, this was due as much to
the social talents he had developed, even in the new era, as to his
genius as a merchant and his indispensable professional knowledge.
Around 1800 the Rue du Mont Blanc was the “millionaires’ row”
of Paris. Necker had lived there. In the same house, which his
daughter Madame de Stael could not afford to keep, the beautiful
Madame de Recamier entertained everyone who was anyone in
Paris. The banker Perregaux also lived there. Nearby, at the
corner of the Rue de Provence and the Rue Cerrutti, the banker
Laffitte took over the Hotel de l’Empire. So, when Ouvrard
moved into the Palais Montesson around 1800 and enlarged it by
buying the house next door in the Rue de Provence, he chose a
brilliant setting. Ten years later the palais became the official
residence of the Austrian Ambassador, Prince von Schwarzenberg.
    Ouvrard had the good sense to allow the former owner,
Madame de Montesson, a charming old lady, to continue to occupy
her apartments, and carry on her social life as before. She was
               The Great Adversary: Napoleon Bonaparte           6g

the morganatic widow of the Due d’ Orleans and had the best
possible connections with any who had survived from the ancien
regime. (Moreover, Napoleon held her in very high esteem. By
one of those accidents of history, it was from her hands that he
had received a madiematics prize at the military academy of
Brienne in 1783.) He saw with pleasure how a new social group
was formed from past and present, drawn together in his house
by her magical charm and gaiety. So for the fourth time a woman
appeared at a critical moment to act as Ouvrard’s sponsor.
Madame de Montesson’s function was quite different from that of
Madame Ouvrard, Madame Tallien or of Josephine deBeauharnais,
 but it was none die less important. In the evenings when hundreds
 of candles blazed throughout the palais, when carriage after
 carriage drove up, when Napoleon’s friends entered in their
 brilliant uniforms, then not merely the material but also the moral
 and social foundations on which Ouvrard’s life stood were
 broadened and strengthened.
    His social status was still of enormous importance to him in
 this period from 1799 to 1804. This princely way of life had
 become a form of natural self-expression. He had an immense,
 spontaneous capacity for enjoying life, and even the frequent
 clashes with his great adversary merely served to underline his
 remarkable resilience. Moreover, in good or in bad times, he
 was always generous, particularly where the neglected arts were
 concerned. A characteristic example of this, and one which was
 known to only a few people, is shown by an incident which
 occurred at the beginning of the Consulate.
    He visited a musical “Studio” in the Hotel de Salm, where
 excerpts from an opera by an unknown composer, Nicolo Isouard,
 who later became fashionable, were performed before one of the
 most distinguished audiences in Paris. As Ouvrard left the house,
 he found bailiffs waiting in the courtyard with a writ for 3,000
 francs, the alternative being imprisonment in the debtors’ prison.
 To his astonishment Ouvrard learned that the unfortunate
 victim was none other than the composer whose music had so
 delighted him. Without a moment’s hesitation he paid the debt
 and the following day he wrote a charming letter to the composer,
            A
jO                            Ouvrard

 informing him that the debt had been settled and adding, almost
 apologetically, that he was enclosing a further sum of money.
 “As the finder of the writ takes a lively interest in your future
 and knows all too well how material obstacles may weigh like
 lead upon the most splendid ambitions and impede their progress,
 he begs you to accept the ten bank notes of 1,000 francs each,
 which you will find enclosed. Do not thank him, sir, for what is
 to him a trifle compared with the success the future will bring
you. All that he expects of you, however, is that you will continue
on the course you have chosen, that you may make ever-in¬
creasing efforts to deserve the fame which awaits you, and the
pleasure this must give him will surely far outweigh the small
service which he now asks you to accept.”
    One can easily imagine what it meant for a man, who was
accustomed to make such gestures, to find himself pushed to the
very brink of financial ruin in 1804 by the Napoleonic govern¬
ment’s methods of payment. When he was suddenly compelled
not merely to liquidate the greater part of his estates but also to
dispense with his town house, the lights went out in the palais
in the Rue du Mont Blanc. For the remaining two years of her
life, Madame de Montesson had to seek shelter elsewhere, and
Ouvrard had to make a supreme effort to maintain a reasonable
standard of living and ensure that his chances for the future
were not completely destroyed.
    This, then, was the situation at the end of Ouvrard’s second
five years in Paris. For the time being at least, his great adversary
was in full possession of the battlefield.
                                 Six



 The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure




O U VRARD’S fall, demonstrated by the sale of his palais in
the Rue du Mont Blanc in 1804, was so dramatic that one would
have expected to find him hobbling painfully on financial
crutches. All his commercial interests had been so severely
damaged that one would have at least assumed that he would
be forced on to the defensive. And his experiences with the
Napoleonic regime had been so disastrous that one would have
thought any further interest in doing business with the State
must have disappeared.
  On the contrary, Ouvrard remained an incurable optimist,
whose faith in his destiny was unshaken. He needed no crutches
as long as he could draw on the vast, almost inexhaustible, reserves
in his brain. He watched impatiently for an opportunity to launch
a fresh offensive. For him the contest with his great adversary
was by no means decided, and whenever an opportunity arose to
take his revenge on the government he seized it without a
moment’s hesitation.
  Even although the time had come when his whole future was
uncertain, Ouvrard felt no need to call on special reserves of
energy. He did not even have to look far for a new and ambitious
plan of action. For several years he had been brooding on a
commercial project of prodigious dimensions. Now more than
ever, when everything was at stake, he had one great expectation:
the silver treasure of Mexico. If he could succeed in releasing those
hundreds of millions that lay captive, his own personal gain
would be enormous.
                                  71
72                              Ouvrard

     The prospects were good, for         Ouvrard had established
excellent relations with Spain, the country to which Mexico and
the transatlantic treasure belonged. He had connections there
with such men as Monsieur de Cabarrus, Madame Tallien’s
father, who controlled the Bank of St. Charles in Madrid. Also
since 1797 Ouvrard had been in official contact with the Spanish
Government, which under a long-term agreement with the Spanish
Prime Minister had entrusted him with the victualling of the
Spanish fleet in French harbours. And Ouvrard arranged in the
most cunning way for this close link to be formally sealed: when
Napoleon gave Prince Louis of Bourbon-Parma the Kingdom of
Etruria in August 1801, the royal couple, though official guests
of the French Government, lived in Ouvrard’s house, the Palais
Montesson. For two months, while his guests moved to and fro
between his house and the Tuileries and were the talk of Paris.
Ouvrard had ample opportunity to make himself indispensable.
As usual, he spared no effort and no expense. He even had the
charming idea of commissioning the famous furniture maker,
Jacob, to produce a special ebony cradle for the fifteen-month-
old Crown Prince* Naturally the Spanish Ambassador, Chevalier
d’Azara, had sent detailed reports to Madrid, so the desired effect
was duly produced and Ouvrard received a flattering letter of
thanks from Charles IV.
     This cordial relationship proved of very real value. A year
later, in 1802, when Ouvrard sent his brother to America on a
reconnaissance tour, he was given the highest recommendations
from the Spanish Government to the Viceroy of Mexico. In
Mexico City itself Ouvrard had considerable interests, for Spain,
cut off from her overseas gold reserves, had to pay for her naval
supplies from 1797 to 1800 with letters of credit on her Mexican
treasure. The Treasurer of Mexico himself conducted Ouvrard’s
brother to the Treasure Chamber, in which, over the years,
seventy-one million piastres or about 350 million francs had been
amassed. The French merchant’s representative was shown four
million piastres—about twenty million francs—which had been

  * Napoleon later gave it to the Empress Marie-Louise in exchange for a
hundred-year-old state coach.
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure     73

set aside to cover Ouvrard’s letters of credit. The bags of coins
were actually labelled as Ouvrard’s personal property. Ouvrard,
the apostle of credit, felt almost intoxicated when he heard of this
vast supply of cash.
  Moreover, Ouvrard was naturally impressed by the scrupulously
correct business methods of the Spanish Government, for he had
become accustomed to receiving nothing but promises from
Napoleon and uncovered promissory notes from his Ministers.
He decided to do everything possible to extend his business
dealings with such a loyal government. The precise nature of
them was a foregone conclusion, for it was clear that Spain’s
only real source of capital was her colonies. Ouvrard’s plans
were also confirmed by the reports he received from his brother,
who spent a whole year studying the economic situation in each
of the various American countries, their products and their
sources of revenue.
  It is one of many remarkable coincidences in Ouvrard’s life
that the first opportunity to put his Spanish-Mexican plans into
operation, after years of careful preparation, finally came in 1804,
the same year in which Napoleon drove him to the brink of ruin.
Faced with the most desperate crisis of his career thus far, he
turned all his attention to what was the most ambitious project
of his whole life. The acute financial difficulties in which the
Napoleonic government again found itself gave him both his
incentive and his opportunity. In spite of his enormous military
expenditure, Napoleon still refused to accept credit of any kind
or to tolerate any other form of revenue except taxes and State
levies. But when, as in 1804, no war contributions could be im¬
posed, because the actual battles had not yet been fought, then
the government’s coffers were emptied in a matter of months. At
such times Napoleon was like a captain of industry who had more
imagination and intellectual capacity than financial skill. He acted
like a man who builds new factories, buys the most modern
machinery, installs new furnaces and opens up new branches
without bothering to find out if the finance department of his
firm has the necessary money.
   In the circumstances, it was not surprising that Napoleon’s
74                            Ouvrard

Minister of the Treasury, Monsieur Barbe-Marbois, treated
 Ouvrard as an unwelcome visitor when he called on him on 4th
April and reminded him of the twenty million francs still owing
to him for the preparations for the landing in England. Ouvrard,
who was usually better informed about what went on in the
various Ministries than the Ministers themselves, naturally fore¬
saw that he would not be given a very warm reception. The
reminder was only intended as a pretext for renewing business
dealings with the government. The plan he brought with him
was admirably designed to give him the revenge he was seeking.
Just as the government had not hesitated, when he had completed
his deliveries, to take full advantage of his defenceless position,
so he was determined to exploit the government’s acute shortage
of money. Barbe-Marbois confessed that he was in desperate and
immediate need of fifty millions and that the bankers had refused
to let him have it. Without batting an eyelid, Ouvrard, who had
just been compelled to sell many of his estates and even his town
house, offered to obtain that sum. The Minister was astounded,
moved and delighted. But it was now Ouvrard’s turn to lay down
conditions: the fifty million must include the twenty million
which the government owed him. In this way all the debts would
be paid at one and the same time, and, furthermore, the new loan,
which was a purely financial transaction, was something very
different from the commercial credit, which the government had
shrugged off with all kinds of moral excuses. Ouvrard was no
longer prepared to accept mere friendly assurances. The govern¬
ment must provide full security and the best at its disposal,
namely, general tax-collectors’ debentures.
   Dealings in these debentures, covered by taxes and customs
duties, which usually took fifteen to twenty months to reach the
Treasury, were subject to special legal restrictions. They were
equivalent to hard cash, could not be discounted and were designed
exclusively to cover Ministerial bonds. Ouvrard’s proposal was,
of course, simply that these debentures should be discounted
through him, and at no less than 9 per cent per annum in his
favour as against the 6 per cent discount of the Bank of France,
which, however, apparently had not thirty million to spare at
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure       75

 that moment. The government’s financial position must have been
 really critical at the beginning of April, for Napoleon, surprisingly
 enough, signed this contract without hesitation.
    This was a deal after Ouvrard’s heart. It was a great success,
 despite the fact that he must certainly have borrowed the thirty
 millions, which he had to pay on top of his own outstanding
 credit. But the success of this operation was by no means his
 sole interest. He regarded the whole affair as a prelude to his
 great Spanish-Mexican venture. Previously he had only supplied
 Napoleon with goods. Now he was his government’s banker, the
 financier who had saved it from bankruptcy. As such he found
 himself on quite a different footing which made it much easier
 for him to develop his foreign contacts.
    The decisive moment came two months later, when the Minis¬
 ter of the Treasury, faced with a further crisis in the nation’s
 finances, was forced to ask Ouvrard again for help, this time for
 150 million francs. It was hard to reconcile this kind of situation
 with Napoleon’s brave words in 1805, which were intended for
 foreign consumption, that “finances based on a healthy agricul¬
 ture cannot be undermined”. Yet on 8th June 1804 another,
highly unfavourable, agreement was signed by the government. It
had to surrender further tax-collectors’ debentures to the value of
 150 million francs, while receiving only 102 million: the remain¬
ing forty-eight million was to cover still more debts outstanding
from previous supply contracts between the War Ministry and
 Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe, who had joined forces with him.
A third person was involved in this latest gigantic deal, an
entrepreneur and engineer called Seguin. Twenty years later
Ouvrard was to spend several years in prison as a debtor to
Seguin. But for the moment the position seemed extremely
satisfactory. Ouvrard had become even more actively involved
in France’s financial administration, and it looked as if the country
could not survive without his helping hand. Now, at last—how
the situation had changed since 18th Brumaire!—he could be
sure of a fair hearing, if he came forward with suggestions for
sources of revenue.
    The sources which Ouvrard proposed to tap were, in fact, not
76                            Ouvrard

 new; all he had in mind was ways and means of collecting revenue
owing but not paid. He had a personal interest in this, for France’s
main debtor was none other than Spain. The Spanish Government
had agreed—somewhat reluctantly—to give Napoleon an annual
subsidy of seventy-two million francs, but, in spite of all the
Emperor’s pleas and threats, not even the first instalment of
thirty-two million had been sent. Napoleon’s mistake lay in
reckoning upon fixed sums, which Spain, in the circumstances,
simply could not pay. Nothing could have been more unrealistic
than his remark to Charles IV in January 1805, that there was no
reason why he should be short of money, for all he had to do was
to sell the treasures of the Knights of Malta and impose a patriotic
levy on clergy and religious orders. And a few years later, when
the tide had started to turn against Napoleon in the peninsula
itself, he still boasted that he would get 2,000 million francs
out of Spain. Ouvrard was much more realistic. He knew the
situation in the country from many economic reports and
realized that there was only one way to be sure of receiving the
Spanish subsidies of seventy-two million a year: the Continental
Blockade must somehow be broken, so that Spain could once
more draw on her transatlantic resources. The silver treasure of
350 million, on which Ouvrard’s brother had feasted his eyes in
the Mexico Treasury in 1802, must be mobilized.
   The blockade of Continental ports by the British fleet had been
so effective that such a plan seemed preposterous and no one
took it seriously. So Ouvrard contented himself with offering to
raise the first thirty-two million francs in Madrid itself, without
referring to the vast project he had in mind. In order to be sure
of clearing at least the first hurdle, he even acceded to Barbe-
Marbois s request—though not without difficulty—to advance
the money before he left. Where Ouvrard found a further thirty-
two million is a complete mystery, but he had become so ob¬
sessed by his dream, which now seemed about to come true, that
he threw caution to the winds. His scheme involved reconciling
the political and economic interests of Napoleon and France with
those of Charles IV and Spain, taking British interests into
account, and—last but not least—also expanding his own
            The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure       77

business interests. The aim in itself was a bold and ambitious
one, but its really decisive and original feature was the method
Ouvrard had chosen to realize it.
   At the end of September 1804 he left for Madrid. Barbe-
Marbois was reluctantly obliged to relax his grip on the coat¬
tails to which he had been clinging in his hour of need. Ouvrard
had not yet crossed the Spanish frontier, when a pathetic letter
from the Minister caught up with him: “... I can no longer doubt
that you are really on your way to Madrid. I... implore you
once again not to depart before you have settled the outstanding
business. ... I count upon your zeal and your shrewdness....”
   Letters such as this, official compliments of the most flattering
kind, the realization that he was indispensable, and the magnetic
attraction of a goal that was now in sight, all combined to give
Ouvrard an exhilarating sense of assurance, which made success
seem almost inevitable.
   But if Ouvrard, who had been given the rank of State Com¬
missioner, thought that he could start negotiations as soon as he
stepped out of his carriage, he was gravely mistaken. It was his
first visit to Spain, and he suddenly found himself in an atmo¬
sphere that was completely new and even alien to him. He had
come from an enlightened country, in which a revolution of
unprecedented violence had broken with a past which had long
since ceased to have any real meaning; he now found himself
suddenly in an ancient monarchy whose State ceremonies were
conducted with a centuries-old ritual. Accustomed to a modern
style of dress, he now met courtiers who might have stepped
straight out of pictures of Louis XIV’s battles. Every step he took
in Madrid brought fresh surprises:* the large number of monks
in the streets, the heavy carriages which looked exactly like the
coach in which Philip V had entered the city, the rigid etiquette,
the gothic traditions, the Spanish love of formality. Ouvrard, the
enlightened Frenchman, did not make the mistake of laughing at
all this. The spontaneous respect he felt for these traditions proved,

   * He committed his first faux pas when he politely declined a cigar
offered to him by the lady sitting—and smoking—next to him at his first
dinner party.
78                             Ouvrard

almost without his knowing it, to be an excellent psychological
introduction to the work he had to do.
   He was in urgent need of some such introduction, for, in the
last resort, he was a mere merchant. He was neither a Minister
nor a general, neither a diplomat nor a prince; he had no aris¬
tocratic connections, no centuries-old family tradition behind
him. He was, it is true, on a mission for the French Minister of the
Treasury, but his mission was not particularly welcome in Spain.
The Spanish Government had observed with understandable dis¬
quiet that France, the creditor, had sent her cleverest merchant
to collect money, countless millions which could be much better
spent in Spain itself.
     So when Ouvrard made his first overtures to the appropriate
government authorities he was told the same hard-luck story as
he had heard so often from Napoleon, that Spain wanted to pay
and would pay, as soon as she had the money, but that she had
none and was not likely to have any “until such time as it pleases
Providence to ease our present situation”. Ouvrard, in fact, had
to admit before long that the accounts of Spain’s critical financial
position were not exaggerated. The country’s basic needs were
not being satisfied, and even the Court—which really meant
something in Spain—had not been paid the 500,000 francs for the
annual visits to the various royal residences. But the most serious
problem was an acute food shortage, which only money could
overcome.
     Ouvrard was unique in that he was not prepared to leave it to
Providence to solve the Spanish problem, which also happened
to be France’s and his own. More to the point, perhaps, he had to
convince the Spanish Government that he, and he alone, had
been sent by Providence to solve it. And he must bring this con¬
viction home to the man who really counted in the Spanish
Government. This man was not hard to find. The government was
dependent on the King, the King was dominated by the Queen,
but the Queen was completely swayed by her lover, Godoy,
Prince of La Paz.
     Here Ouvrard’s business interests led him into an unusual
family idyll. The central figure was Godoy, who had arrived in
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure     79

Madrid in 1788 as a humble officer in the Royal Bodyguard.
Charles IV took a liking to the tall, volatile young officer, but he
appealed even more to Queen Marie-Louise. She was a little
voluptuous woman, who had no pretensions to beauty but was
starved of love and, according to Napoleon, her secret leanings
and her past were clearly written in her face. Godoy had developed
into a splendid specimen of manhood. (Though according to
Napoleon he looked like a bull.) He became the last word in
elegance and was an accomplished performer on the lute. He also
acquired consummate skill in political intrigue. Charles IV and
his wife showered favours on him, and soon Godoy, who knew
how to make the most of his opportunities, was—to quote a
tactful expression of the period—seeing the Queen at very close
quarters. Charles, however, remained unmoved. Secure in the
knowledge that he now had in the family, so to speak, a man who
could be trusted to carry the entire burden of State affairs, he was
able to devote all his time to the only two passions in his life,
hunting and the supervision thrice a day of several dozen clocks.
He was perfectly happy to be a mere figurehead and was not in the
least put out when Napoleon wrote to him in 1803 that the whole
of Europe was horrified by the scandal and expected him to dis¬
miss   Godoy and remount his throne. Far from following
Napoleon’s advice, he made his wife’s lover Prince of La Paz, and
gave formal recognition to a situation that had existed for some
time by officially appointing him Prime Minister of Spain. Godoy
was also made Commander-in-Chief of the Army and Grand
Admiral of the Fleet. The King heaped official and private presents
on this avaricious man, and he even sided with him in numerous
quarrels with his own son Ferdinand.
  Such was the man with whom Ouvrard had to come to terms,
if his political mission, the payment of the subsidies, was to be
successfully completed. The prospects of an agreement were not
inconsiderable, for Godoy, like Barras, was not uninterested in
feathering his own nest. Ouvrard mustered all his social talents to
conceal the carrot and the stick with which he was plying the
ruler of Spain. He assured Godoy that he was hewn from the
same wood from which Napoleon made his kings, but, at the
8o                             Ouvrard

same time, threatened him with the Emperor’s fearful wrath.
Undoubtedly he also offered him a financial incentive.
     Soon all that Godoy lacked was not the will to pay but the
opportunity. This came as no surprise to Ouvrard, who may even
have preferred such a deadlock, for it enabled him to unfold his
great plan. The scheme was doubly unusual in that it entailed
restoration of the finances of a whole nation by a foreign merchant.
     Ouvrard gradually explained to the Prince of La Paz, with
whom he usually took morning chocolate, a programme, which
can be summarized as follows:
     1. Internal stability is a prerequisite of any national economy
with international ramifications; for that reason the government
must solve the famine problem by finding supplies of grain.
     2. The agricultural productivity of the peninsula and with it
the national revenue must be increased; this could easily be
achieved by a land reform whereby property belonging largely
to the clergy or the nobility, which for the most part was lying
fallow, would be divided up and handed over to peasant farmers
for cultivation; the approval of Rome would be necessary,
and compensation for the mortmains would be paid in the
form of annuities, which would become part of the national
expenditure.
     3. The Spanish money lying in the Mexican State Treasury,
which had been rendered immobile by the British blockade, must
now be transferred, in spite of the renewal of hostilities. This was
not impracticable; all that was necessary was to entrust Ouvrard
with the task.
     4. Spain must pay France the promised subsidies without
waiting for this project to bear fruit, in order to avoid complica¬
tions and to fulfil her contract; this would require temporary
financial measures in the form of loans (for example in Holland),
which should be fully covered.
  5. The government must also set about paying off its internal
debts without delay.
     6. In order to carry out large-scale public development plans
to increase national productivity (the building of roads and
bridges and irrigation), a long term National Debt should be
King Charles IV of Spain. Anonymous contemporary en-       Queen Marie-Louise of Spain. Engraved by Esteve.
         graving. {Paris, Bibliothcque Nationale)      After the drawing by Bauzil. {Paris, Bibliothcque Nationals)
          The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure    8l

established, which would be backed by an independent and well-
endowed sinking fund.
   7. An essential prerequisite for the efficient implementation of
all these proposals was a sound banking system; the Bank of St.
Charles, founded by M. de Cabarrus, should be turned into a
kind of national “Bank of Spain”, and the establishment of
provincial banks was also essential.

   During those weeks, while Ouvrard was devoting all his
energies to working out his plans in Madrid, he received another
urgent letter from Barbe-Marbois: “See that all gold and silver
coins are despatched to Paris as soon as you receive them, so that
we do not have to wait too long—I count upon your zeal... .”
 One can imagine Ouvrard’s scornful smile as he consigned the
unsuspecting Minister’s letter to the archives.
   But even in Madrid the men he was dealing with were not much
wiser. His whole plan was so bold and many of its ingredients,
such as the land reform, were so far in advance of the times that
Ouvrard was forced time and again to employ all his powers of
persuasion. On one occasion the Prince of La Paz invited the
Finance Minister to morning chocolate to hear Ouvrard expound
his ideas. On another occasion the Queen came. Marie-Louise,
however, seemed more interested in displaying her affection for
the Prince and in showing how informally she could behave with
him than in listening attentively. Yet Ouvrard felt that her visit
had helped to further his cause.
   It was, on the other hand, not so much his ideas as his actions
that carried conviction. Not unnaturally his audience was only
prepared to take his miraculous powers seriously if he could
provide a few samples. Ouvrard did not hesitate to supply them.
To begin with, he provided the 500,000 francs which the Court
needed for its official journeys. Then he pointed out that, before
leaving Paris, he had himself arranged an advance payment of
thirty-two million on the Spanish subsidies, and he declared his
readiness to be a very forbearing creditor—in other words to
grant a sort of moratorium. The Spaniards were also very pleased
when Ouvrard, at Barbe-Marbois’s request, signed an agreement
82                           Ouvrard

on 24th October 1804 to pay a further instalment of the subsidies
direct to Napoleon and thereby replace France as creditor. His
major triumph, however, was the efficiency with which he dealt
with the famine in Spain. The situation was such that most of the
bakers in the country were unable to continue work as the price
of a fanega of flour (roughly a bushel) had risen fivefold to over
a hundred francs; infectious diseases were spreading and the
government appeared to be helpless.
   In November 1804 Ouvrard contracted to supply the Spanish
markets with 100,000 tons of grain. Two couriers were despatched,
one to London to ask for—and obtain—free passage for the grain-
ships through the blockade, the other to Paris to get Napoleon’s
permission to export the required quota of wheat, which France
could do without. The Emperor, businesslike as usual, imposed
only one condition: four francs per hundredweight or a net gain
of eight million would go to the French Government. Ouvrard
could afford to accept this, for he himself would still reap a
profit—though a gross profit—of eight million. Most of this,
however, was to disappear, for no sooner did the announcement
appear in the Spanish newspapers that Ouvrard would be
selling 100,000 tons of grain on the Madrid market at twenty-six
francs a hundredweight, than vast hoarded stocks of flour appeared
as if by magic. The announcement alone had been sufficient to
end the famine, and, before Ouvrard’s first grain ship had reached
Spain, the price of a fanega had dropped from 100 to 18 francs.
The government was suddenly placed in the very embarrassing
position of not knowing what to do with the 100,000 tons it had
guaranteed. Ouvrard had no alternative but to co-operate by
cancelling some of the shipments.
   This was, however, in his own interest, for he had not come to
Madrid to make a profit of a few millions but to gain access to the
wealth of the New World. And, indeed, his transatlantic plans
were now taken much more seriously in Madrid. On 26th Novem¬
ber and 4th December 1804 an agreement was signed, which is
unparalleled in economic or political history. The terms of the
main agreement and the collateral agreement, the texts of which
are given in the appendix, can be summarized as follows:
          The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure       83

  I. The contracting parties were Charles IV of Spain and
Gabriel Julien Ouvrard.
  II. They set up a company with monopoly powers.
  III. This company was given the official title of “Frangois
Ouvrard & Co.” (not, as it should have been, “Julien Ouvrard,
Charles IV & Co.”).
  IV. The contract was to remain valid as long as the war between
Spain and Britain lasted, which was likely to be many years.
  V. Sole managing director of the company was Ouvrard.
  VI. The firm was given exclusive rights in the following:
    (a) . The transfer of all gold and silver coins in Mexico
  belonging to Spain.
     (b) . The transportation of all goods imported by Spain from
  America into Europe or exported by Spain from Europe to
  America.
     (c) . The sale of all goods imported by the Spanish Govern¬
   ment from America to non-Spanish ports in Europe.
  VII. Ouvrard’s profit would accrue from the following
sources:
     (a) . Commission, i.e.
  \ per cent on the transfer of gold and silver coins,
  1 per cent of the Continental value of all goods exported to
     America or imported into Spain,
  2 per cent of the proceeds from the goods imported from
     America to non-Spanish ports in Europe;
     (b) . A personal trade concession, which gave Ouvrard the
  right to at least one third of the freight space on each ship;
     (c) . The direct costs, as well as the commission, were to be
  charged separately. Spain, for her part, undertook to cover a
  percentage of the basic transport costs, the local incidental
  expenses, the insurance, and the cost of obtaining the letters
  of safe conduct from England.

  One can imagine Ouvrard’s delight when Charles IV set his
name and seal under this long and detailed agreement: “Approved,
I the King.” Ouvrard had all the more reason to be pleased, as
the government immediately took steps to carry out its side of
84                            Ouvrard

the bargain. Ouvrard received 500 open trading concessions, in
which die destinations of the ships were left blank. By 18th
December 1804, only a fortnight after the conclusion of the
agreement, he had already been given 752 government bills of
exchange on the Treasury in Mexico and on the firm of Garochi
of Madrid. They totalled 52^ million piastres or 262^ million
French francs! In addition a second contract was signed which
gave Ouvrard two further important privileges: he was to raise a
loan for the royal consolidated fund and was empowered to
import silver dollars, which were rare in Paris, from Mexico into
France. Ouvrard was not exaggerating when he reported to
Barbe-Marbois that “no private firm had ever been entrusted
with more important and more useful business to transact”, and
he had every reason to feel confident that “this undertaking
should have the most positive effect upon both the French and
the Spanish national credit, trade, industry and all other factors,
which contribute to the prosperity of the State”.
     This was certainly the view held in Spanish Government
circles, and honours fell thick and fast on the merchant Ouvrard
from Paris, who, from the moment he had ended the famine, had
been looked upon with awe in Madrid as a worker of miracles.
He was the talk of the capital. The Prince of La Paz, who was not
in the habit of giving presents, sent him eight splendid horses.
The Finance Minister Soler invited him to stay at his house and
placed an apartment at his disposal. Soon, in fact, the only
honour which had not been accorded him was formal presentation
at Court. There was one obstacle: etiquette. Ouvrard had a some¬
what easier passage than Madame d’Abrantes, the wife of General
Junot, who in 1805 had to don a crinoline for the occasion and
made a laborious entry like an overloaded pack-mule, only to
fall flat on her face at the crucial moment. But Ouvrard wore his
hair cut short in the new French fashion, a Jacobin coiffure as the
Spaniards called it, which was quite unsuitable for a royal recep¬
tion. So he crammed an enormous powdered wig on his head and
the brilliant success of his visit to Madrid was crowned by formal
presentation at Court.
     Ouvrard had no difficulty in resisting the temptation to
          The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure    85

abandon himself to a round of social engagements. His one
absorbing interest in life was still his work. Moreover, no one
knew better than he how difficult it would be to carry through
the immense venture on which he had embarked. The first
problem was to acquire the English letters of safe conduct. To
begin with, free passage must be obtained for the ships carrying
the Mexican piastres. An agent was despatched to London by way
of Lisbon, but he found the English even less co-operative than
he had expected. Ouvrard had reckoned quite rightly with the
English merchants’ shortage of cash and had assumed that, if
he offered to get American silver to England, the British Govern¬
ment would put aside all political considerations. But war had
once again been declared on 12th December 1804. Pitt argued
that the English trading houses were bound to transfer the silver,
or at least its equivalent, to Spain, thereby lending aid to the
ally of his arch-enemy. On the other hand, there were several
individuals and companies in London which were actively
interested in the deal and gave Ouvrard their support. In par¬
ticular, the banking firm of the Baring Brothers and the East
India Company exerted all their influence to convince Pitt that
the benefit to England’s trade would be considerably greater than
to Spain and France. Pitt gave a certain amount of ground but
only to the extent of allowing four English frigates to pass
through with silver from the Mexican State Treasury. This would,
naturally, cover only a part of the money—although it trans¬
pired later that the ships transported some fourteen million
piastres, or about sixty million francs—and the affair suddenly
became extremely complicated. So it was more important than
ever that Ouvrard should find a large trading concern of world¬
wide connections to collaborate with him.
  Just such a concern existed in Amsterdam, at that time the
world’s leading trading centre, in which, as early as 1650, there
were no fewer than 500 certified brokers. The most important
firm in Amsterdam was the House of Hope, acknowledged
throughout the world to be the leading merchant-bankers of their
time. They had immense stocks of colonial produce in every port
on the Continent and considered themselves the equals of any
86                           Ouvrard

reigning sovereign in matters of finance. At the head of the firm
were two outstanding men, Henry Hope and P. C. Labouch^re.
Ouvrard called on them in Amsterdam in April 1805.
     His departure from Madrid was not merely triumphal, it was
a public occasion. Ministers and nobles paid him farewell visits;
cheering crowds lined the streets as his carriage passed. But with
the Hopes in Amsterdam Ouvrard found a much more sober
atmosphere. Ouvrard, proud of the spectacular agreement he
had reached with Charles IV, began by explaining his far-reaching
plans: gold shipments, Mexican silver dollars, exploitation of
American mines, transatlantic loans, import and export of goods
and foodstuffs, the virtual removal of the Continental Blockade,
all this echoed round the quiet room in disorderly abandon.
The first reaction of Ouvrard’s audience to this enthusiastic
outburst was one of mute astonishment: Hope and Labouchere
looked at one another, obviously wondering if this man was out
of his senses. They were accustomed to thinking and acting
daily on a continental and even transatlantic plane, but they
owed their phenomenal success to a profound sense of realism,
which forbade them to risk a single gulden on ingenious com¬
mercial propositions, which had nothing but their ingenuity to
recommend them. But gradually they were forced to admit that
Ouvrard was not just a visionary. He produced the contract with
its twenty-four clauses, bearing the joint signature of the mer¬
chant Ouvrard and King Charles IV and the royal seal of Spain.
Then he laid bills of exchange for 250 million francs on the
table, and they began to take him seriously.
     For Ouvrard a great deal, if not everything, depended upon
Hopes assuming the responsibility for carrying out his Spanish
contract. No other concern in the world had the means at its
disposal to carry through an essentially long-term transaction
of this magnitude. But most important of all was the fact that
Ouvrard could not have succeeded by himself. The painstaking,
systematic and essentially prosaic task of execution did not
appeal to Ouvrard. Out of his fertile mind and his restless blood
sprang a constant flow of ingenious plans, but once the plans
were completed he soon lost interest in them. He spent his
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure      87

millions lavishly, and it never occurred to him to save. He lacked
a sense of order and method; he had none of the ordinary
business man’s vigilance; being essentially far-sighted, his long-
range vision was astonishingly clear, but he neglected to take the
necessary precautions for more immediate situations. On the
other hand, Ouvrard was apparently aware of his own qualities
and shortcomings, for, as his first big operation, the paper deal
of 1789, showed, he confined himself to the overall planning of
business transactions, leaving any profit he might have made
from detailed execution of his plans to other merchants.
  In Hope’s office in Amsterdam he submitted to a minute
cross-examination. He was asked to give a detailed account of
all his contracts with the various Ministries in France and Spain,
a precise statement of his capital reserves, of his financial position
as a whole, and of the ready cash at his disposal at that time.
This cross-examination was conducted by hard-headed business¬
men who, unlike Ouvrard, were not in the habit of juggling
with millions, and he did not come out of it too well. Hope and
Labouchere were not able to gain the sort of clear picture that
they, as cautious financiers, would have liked. The solvency of
their new client, who appeared in some doubt as to how many of
his millions were owed to him and how many owed by him, was
not conclusively proven.
  For the first time in his life Ouvrard was up against men of his
own profession who were his equals, and even, in the last resort,
his superiors. It was characteristic of him that he immediately
bowed to their superiority and accepted unconditionally the
proposals which they, after a few days’ reflection, put before him.
One of the two parties had to show confidence; as Hopes knew
too little of Ouvrard and were generally recognized to be the
most stable and most reliable firm on the Continent, it was
clearly up to Ouvrard to show confidence. Ouvrard agreed
when he was told quite bluntly that, if he set any store by this
transaction, he must surrender all initiative, rely entirely upon
Hopes’ discretion, relinquish any right to interfere in the execu¬
tion of his plans, give his approval in advance to whatever the
outcome might be, accept in advance the balance-sheet drawn
88                           Ouvrard

up by Hopes and for this purpose sign twelve blank letters of
credit. Moreover, Hopes agreed to cover only a part of the
transaction, namely, to take up the trading concessions and to
negotiate the Spanish bills, the sale of which involved the enor¬
mous sum of 250 million francs and, in view of Pitt’s refusal
to let further silver shipments through the blockade, would
present problems enough. But Hopes were also prepared to issue
a loan in favour of the Spanish Government of thirty million
Dutch gulden (sixty million francs). The 500 trading concessions
were to be taken up in Ouvrard’s name against a commission of
5 per cent of the total turnover, but Hopes had to meet the cost
of the agencies. When the bills covering the Mexican piastres
were redeemed, Ouvrard was to be paid not at the theoretical
rate of five francs but at three francs seventy-five. Ouvrard
understood, agreed and signed. On 6th May 1805 this contract
was also completed.
     Whatever might happen, the spectacular agreements he had
concluded in Spain seemed now absolutely certain to reach
fulfilment. So Ouvrard had every reason to feel extremely pleased
with himself. And yet, although all he had to do was to sit and
wait till Hopes brought the Mexican pigeons home to roost, his
voracious appetite was far from satisfied. Before long he was back
in Madrid enjoying an extra titbit. Supplementary contracts were
signed giving him the concession for the lead and quicksilver
mines at the average price for the previous ten years and finally
making him sole supplier of the government’s tobacco monopoly.
     Ouvrard’s dream, to become owner or guardian of the silver
treasure of Mexico, was about to come true. Not surprisingly,
Ouvrard was a very happy man, and the Spanish Government, for
its part, was thankful for his help. On the other hand, Barbe-
Marbois in Paris was feeling anything but happy. His letters to
Ouvrard became more and more frequent, urging him to hurry
back home, where his presence was urgently needed, for the
Minister could no longer guarantee the continuance of the
national exchequer. The unfortunate man was quite unfitted for
such a difficult post.
     If Napoleon appointed Barbe-Marbois head of the newly
            The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure   89

created Ministry of the State Treasury in 1801, it was mainly be¬
cause he attached more importance to complete honesty than to
any other quality, including competence. Barbe’s uprightness was
certainly beyond dispute, and no one has ever spoken of him
without paying tribute to his sense of justice. Although he usually
gave a rather comic impression with his long, lean body dressed
in an inordinately long red velvet coat and white vest and with a
face that was invariably sombre and even sinister, yet he com¬
manded universal respect. Born in 1745, he had been a school¬
master, Minister of Marine, Consul General in North America,
Administrator of San Domingo, Counsellor in the Foreign
Office, Lord Mayor of Metz and Secretary of the Council of
Senators, before Napoleon gave him office. But, despite his varied
career, he had lost none of his mental sluggishness. This did not
particularly disturb Napoleon, as he preferred to do everything
himself and to have obedient servants around him. It must have
amused him to know that Barbe-Marbois had written a book
entitled    The best method of giving people a taste for virtue
and that his pen was active in the interest of the cultivation of
clover and esparto grass (1803). Napoleon’s sole reason for
calling him a blockhead was that “he imagines that one should
not lie”.
  Entrusting, of all things, the nation’s finances to such a man
was bound to place the country in peril when Napoleon was
absent for any length of time, as was the case in the summer and
winter of 1805, which he spent preparing and conducting the
Austro-German campaign. A Finance Minister, who was anxious
to create a popular taste for virtue, was not likely to be a match
for someone like Ouvrard, whose vast, long-term plans could
only be launched with substantial government support. Barbe-
Marbois realized too late that Ouvrard had not only transferred
no money from Spain to tide over the crisis in France but had
invested, though only temporarily of course, the reserves of the
Spanish National Treasury. Worse still, Ouvrard’s friends had
adopted special measures to lay their hands on the last of the
nation’s funds and had almost forced the Bank of France to close
down.
9o                           Ouvrard

     How this whole affair, which was eventually to ruin all
Ouvrard’s brilliant plans, came about remains something of a
mystery. On 4th April 1805 Ouvrard had offered the State fifty
million francs, less the twenty million owing to him. On 8th
June Barbe made a fresh appeal and received a further private
loan of 150 million, this time, however, against the equivalent in
bonds or debentures issued by national tax-collectors. Ouvrard,
who now enjoyed the complete confidence of the government,
came forward at this point with his plan to collect the outstanding
Spanish subsidies—seventy-two million a year—and, in order to
make his position absolutely inviolable, paid, at Barbe’s request,
the first thirty-two million of the Spanish money out of his own
pocket. Ouvrard did not hesitate to cover this advance, which
had been squeezed out of him, in a way that Barbe could not en¬
tirely control. As a businessman Ouvrard realized that an insol¬
vent creditor, like Spain, can only be expected to pay when he
has become solvent. But to make someone solvent costs money.
To make a country solvent costs millions, and, if the Treasury
is empty, these millions can only come from tax revenues, the
national reserve. This was the source from which Ouvrard met
the personal expenses of the Spanish Court and set up the Con¬
solidated Fund in Madrid, which issued its own notes. The
amount in question must have been at least thirty-six million but
was very probably as much as 36 -f- 24 =60 million.
     Ouvrard was able without great difficulty to use the bonds
as cover for the notes, because Barbe was completely at the mercy
of the bankers. When Ouvrard travelled to Spain as a State Com¬
missioner in September 1804, the Minister of the Treasury needed
someone who would be responsible for arranging payment of the
loan of 150 million francs pledged by Ouvrard and his friends,
Vanlerberghe, Seguin and Michel, against the delivery of bonds
on discount. Napoleon himself sought out this key figure: it was
Desprez. The banker Desprez was already a member of the
Board of Directors of the Bank of France, to which, so to speak,
not even a messenger could be appointed without Napoleon’s
personal approval. He had been for some time persona gratissima
with the Emperor’s entourage and had formerly spent ten years
          The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure      gi

as a Treasury official, where he was a colleague of Dufresne, the
Director to whom Napoleon paid eloquent tribute for his love of
order and matchless probity. So it is hardly to be wondered at
that everyone, and particularly Barbe-Marbois, placed almost un¬
limited confidence in Desprez. But Desprez was no longer a
government official, he was      a banker, who suddenly        found
himself leading an informal group of the most gifted business
men in France and who felt it incumbent on him to show what
he could do.
  He had no difficulty in arranging that Roger, the Secretary of
the Treasury and the Minister’s right-hand man, should be given
the authority to hand over to Desprez any bonds the latter
might demand in return for the equivalent in notes. And notes
were by no means confined to drafts issued by various govern¬
ment departments (which would have been in accordance with
Treasury regulations) but also included bills of exchange and
private promissory notes of all kinds. This meant, in fact, that the
Treasury was completely in the power of Ouvrard’s representa¬
tive and that Roger, the Secretary of the Treasury, had virtually
become an employee of the bankers. And very soon he confirmed
this by accepting the substantial sum of 1,200,000 francs.
  In these circumstances Ouvrard had naturally no difficulty
in obtaining from Desprez as many bonds from the Treasury as
he needed for the Madrid Consolidated Fund. In exchange the
equivalent in bills from the Spanish “mint” found their way to
the Treasury. Although for the moment they were virtually
worthless, they would acquire their full value when the Mexican
transaction was completed. Had matters been left like that,
Ouvrard would never have acquired the reputation of indulging
in dubious speculation. But Ouvrard and his friends could not
resist the temptation to exploit the fantastic situation, whereby
it was they who decided how much in the way of reserve bonds
should remain in the Treasury. Not only was Desprez responsible
for collecting and paying in the 150 million, not only was Ouvrard
responsible for making Spain solvent, but Ouvrard, Vanlerberghe
and their friends continued to hold the monopoly for supplies
to the army and the navy. This enormous expenditure, which
92                            Ouvrard

ran into many millions, had always been reimbursed in the past
by promissory notes payable by the Treasury, but, as Ouvrard
and Vanlerberghe knew from painful personal experience, most
of them had only been met, on Napoleon’s instructions, years
later and even then only partially. Now that Napoleon was away
at his headquarters, an opportunity presented itself of turning
the tables. They arranged immediate payment for all their de¬
liveries and—an unforgivable sin in Napoleon’s eyes—they even
took generous advances instead of advancing the money them¬
selves. All this was done with the aid of bonds, some of which
were privately discounted, while others—contrary to all agree¬
ments—were sold.
     Desprez did not have strong enough moral fibre to resist so
many temptations. He saw wonderful prospects opening up
before him, if only he had further means at his disposal. He took
advantage of his seat on the board of the Bank of France to talk
his fellow directors into honouring or discounting drafts bearing
the most dubious signatures. On one occasion Desprez lent the
firm of Grammont in Bordeaux fourteen million francs, although
this same firm, according to Ouvrard, was unable to obtain even
200,000 elsewhere. Napoleon, from his field headquarters, began
to criticize Barbe-Marbois, accusing him of being duped by
Desprez because, for example, he had drawn a bill for one million
on the paymaster of Arras, who could not pay. Bills of this mag¬
nitude were presented by Desprez, perhaps even bought up cheap
and fully honoured by the bank. At one point the Bank of France
had only one and a half million left in the till—and ninety-two
million in claims.
     The most remarkable feature of this situation was that for
every five million Desprez received he needed twenty-five. He
had managed to arrange for official instructions to be issued to
tax-collectors that the amounts due on their debentures should
be paid not to the Treasury but direct to the man who then
owned them, Desprez, and, for the time being, a simple receipt
was to be accepted. Desprez made the most of this by himself
issuing a circular letter in which he promised higher rates of
interest to tax-collectors who made abnormally prompt cash
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure    93

payments. But instead of returning these honoured bonds to
their drawers, he deposited them with the Bank of France and
—used them as security for further loans! This, of course, was
plain, unvarnished swindling.
  Meanwhile, Desprez’s financial juggling, in the course of
which he had drawn millions upon millions from the bank, had
been so successful that the Bank itself was no longer solvent.
It took the obvious course of presenting to the tax-collectors
what appeared to be its most valuable securities, namely, the
bonds which had long since been paid, only to get back in return
the receipts issued by Desprez. This was in November 1805.
The result was not only a major scandal but also a bank crash,
for Desprez had precious little of the money left, particularly as,
in an access of megalomania, he had set himself up as financial
backer of some highly dubious firms and had lent no less than
fifty million francs. By August the bank’s position was seriously
undermined, and in the second half of September—immediately
after Napoleon moved out into the field—the Bank of France
had to limit its payments. Anyone presenting paper money could
cash only a thousand-franc note. Night after night people queued
up in their hundreds in the Place des Victoires, hoping to be the
lucky ones the following morning. Then, in November, the
crisis reached its peak. Bank notes dropped as much as 12 per
cent in value. The police had their hands full, coping with a crowd
of 2,000 people in front of the bank. Barbe-Marbois proposed in
the Council of Ministers that the salaries of officials should be
reduced to a quarter. When the bank finally came to the end of
its resources, it chose a roundabout way of suspending payments:
a thousand-franc note could only be cashed if it was presented
with a certificate from the bearer’s local Town Council confirming
that he was in financial straits. Anyone fortunate enough to
advance thus far received a written summons and, as the bank
was playing for time, a bagful of hard cash was counted out,
coin by coin. All major transactions, however, came to a stand¬
still. Amongst these was the transfer of money to Madrid. As
Ouvrard’s Consolidated Fund was not yet self-supporting, it
collapsed with a bang that resounded throughout Spain.
94                           Ouvrard

     So Ouvrard, the State Commissioner, was brought down by
his own representative, Desprez, in his capacity as head of the
financial group. Robbed of his financial resources, Ouvrard could
no longer hope to make Spain solvent. And yet no sooner had he
returned to Paris than he made it clear that he still believed—
and with some justification—that the Mexican project was as
sound as ever. Once the Central American silver had reached
the Continent, then all the other problems, including the bill-
protests against the Consolidated Fund in Madrid, would solve
themselves.
     Ouvrard was not even shaken in his conviction by the be¬
haviour of Desprez and Vanlerberghe. Like many other concerns,
Vanlerberghe had been very hard hit by the bank crisis, and when
he too found it impossible to obtain any more money from the
government he suddenly announced that he would have to
declare himself bankrupt, if he did not receive an immediate
payment of twenty-six million francs for past deliveries. In the
Council of Ministers the War Minister voiced his dismay, pro¬
tested that, without the co-operation of the merchants, he could
not keep his department going nor could he find adequate
substitutes, and urged that the twenty-six million be paid. Then
came a sudden thunderbolt from Napoleon. Joseph Bonaparte,
Cambaceres and Barbe-Marbois received letters reprimanding
them for even considering such proposals. In the meantime,
following the Battle of Austerlitz on 2nd December 1805, the
economic situation in Paris had taken a turn for the better.
Ouvrard’s optimism was once again borne out by events, for,
without any change in the status of the Bank of France since the
suspension of payments, it suddenly became solvent again. The
thrill of victory revived so many hopes, that the run on the bank
stopped and no one thought any more of converting his paper
money.
     This could not, of course, bring back the millions which
Desprez by his sleight-of-hand had conjured out of the Treasury’s
coffers into his own pocket. Nor could the bills of the Con¬
solidated Fund in Madrid, which were lying in the Treasury
vaults and ran into tens of millions, be turned into hard cash
               The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure        95

overnight. But this was precisely what the victorious Napoleon
was concerned about, when he found time to look at the financial
reports from Paris. With mounting dismay, with indignation,
with the fury which always assailed him when he lost money but
which the sacrifice of thousands of human lives never aroused
in him, he saw through Desprez’              swindle, Barbe-Marbois’s
stupidity, and the illicit use of bonds to borrow money for Spain
instead of producing cash from it. He gave due warning in writing
of the frightful retribution to come.
   When it did come, following Napoleon’s return to Paris on
26th January 1806, it exceeded even the worst fears of all con¬
cerned. Although the Emperor did not carry out his threat to
return with all speed in order to clean up the financial mess—on
his way back from Vienna he remained almost three weeks in
Munich to marry off his stepson Eugene Beauharnais to a Bavarian
princess and, contrary to his normal practice, spent one to two
days in Augsburg, Stuttgart, Karlsruhe and Strasbourg—he had
no sooner reached Paris, at ten o’clock at night, than he ordered
the chief culprits to report at the Tuileries the following day.
When Barbe-Marbois entered the Imperial presence, he said,
with bowed head: “Your Majesty, I bring you my head.” To
which Napoleon gave the characteristically brusque reply: “Much
good that will do me, you stupid lout.” In a letter to Joseph
Bonaparte that same day, 27th January, the Emperor wrote: “I
arrived in Paris last night. I was indignant at the incompetent
manner in which M. Barbe-Marbois has conducted my finances.
I relieved him of his portfolio. I have appointed State Councillor
Mollien to succeed him.”
   The meeting, to which this brief account referred, was in fact
long and dramatic. It lasted from eight in the morning until five
in the afternoon, a nine hours’ ordeal for Barbe, Ouvrard,
Desprez, Vanlerberghe, Roger, Mollien and the Finance Minister,
Gaudin.* The first hour was the worst. Napoleon stormed and
raged, his eyes flashing fire. He threatened to lock up all the

  * The Minister of Finance was the Minister responsible for the collection
of revenue, while the Minister of the Treasury controlled expenditure.—
T.A.B.C.
 g6                               Ouvrard

merchants in the Tower of Vincennes and have them shot. Even
Ouvrard admitted that this first outburst of rage was terrifying.
Desprez broke down and wept, while the Secretary of the Trea¬
sury, Roger, tried to stammer out excuses. The only one who
remained apparently unmoved was Ouvrard. It was in crises of
this kind that he was at his most impressive. He sat there com¬
pletely motionless, silent, impassive, waiting patiently for the
storm to pass. When it finally subsided and one could hear one¬
self speak, it was Ouvrard who acted as spokesman.* Ouvrard
took advantage of the next eight hours to save what could still
be saved.
      The text of a decree was read out, making the financiers jointly
liable for the sum of eighty-seven million francs. “What security
can you give?” Ouvrard replied without hesitation: “Sire, the
Treasury will be paid, if you let us go free.” Napoleon was not
prepared for such an enormous commitment to be accepted so
promptly. He took Ouvrard aside to one of the windows, where
a more earnest exchange followed between the two great adver¬
saries.

napoleon:    Then I will march to Madrid myself; with five
             hundred thousand men one can do anything.
ouvrard:     Your Majesty can achieve anything with one clever
             man; with five hundred thousand you will not
