   When Ouvrard recorded this conversation in his memoirs
twenty years later he probably put down much of what he wished
he had said together with what actually passed between him and
the Emperor; not a few of the phrases he claims to have used
were much too bold for so critical a situation. But the fact remains
that in that conversation he paved the way for a businesslike and
strictly commercial solution of the crisis. At the same time,
Napoleon’s financial conditions were extremely harsh. The first
decree, which had been read aloud at the meeting and which
accused the financiers of having “embezzled” millions and
“employed them for private business deals”, was soon followed,
on 6th February 1806, by a second in which even harsher con¬
ditions were imposed on Ouvrard, Vanlerberghe, Michel and
Seguin for the honouring of all securities. And shortly afterwards,
following a report by Mollien, the total sum to be refunded was
increased from 87 to 141 million francs, again, of course, without
any process of law or independent administrative enquiry. Any
bills belonging to the financiers which could be found were im¬
mediately confiscated; their private fortunes disappeared, and
their warehouse stocks were requisitioned. Those like Desprez,
who could not pay, were flung into the debtors’ prison of Sainte-
Pelagie.
   Worst of all, Napoleon tried to lay his hands on the securities
Ouvrard had deposited with Hopes. His first attempt was so
arrogant and clumsy that it failed. To a letter, in which he made
the quite unnecessary accusation that the gentlemen in Amsterdam
had earned quite enough out of the Louisiana deal, he received
a cold and negative reply. When Napoleon followed this up by
sending Baron Louis to make a final settlement, Hopes answered:
“We are in no way obliged to inform you whether Monsieur
Ouvrard has money deposited with us or not. You yourself must
    h
98                           Ouvrard

surely realize that your demand is most unseemly.” But, as Spain
was in no position to stand up to the Emperor and as Ouvrard
was out of action, the Spanish-Mexican project, which Hopes
were on the point of putting into operation, went up in smoke.
“That marked the end,” said Ouvrard dejectedly, “of the biggest
commercial and political undertaking which was ever planned
and launched, an undertaking which, by virtue of the contract
between a crowned head and an ordinary private individual,
would have forged a closer and firmer link between the Old and
New Worlds and would have solved, at one stroke, the financial
problems of two States.”
  Napoleon behaved in a characteristic fashion. It did not occur to
him to draw any distinction between, on the one hand, Ouvrard’s
plans for making Spain solvent and for indirect collection of
outstanding subsidies and, on the other hand, the millions which,
according to Napoleon, were appropriated by Ouvrard for his
own personal ends—an accusation which Ouvrard hotly con¬
tested and which is no longer verifiable. Nor did the Emperor
pause for one moment to think that it might be better to leave
the Treasury bills which Ouvrard had invested in the Mexican
affair to work their passage home rather than to call them back
suddenly. He stared, as if hypnotized, at the deficit, which in¬
cidentally was no longer there, for he had brought back many
millions in forced contributions from Austria. As Ouvrard said,
credit for him was really “an abstract idea, the crazy conception
of political economists”. He thought of only two things: taxes
and conquests. The “compendium of the world”, as Goethe called
Napoleon, lacked any commercial sense; at         the same time,
Napoleon was completely blind to this defect and refused to
accept advice. “I do not want advice but news,” he told his
Minister of the Treasury, Mollien.
     He meddled in everything, however important, however in¬
significant. In 1800 he set up “the exchange office in Paris, which
bears the proud name of Bank of France”, as Ouvrard remarked
scornfully; side by side with the Finance Minister he appointed
 a Minister of the Treasury to enable him to divide and rule; the
First Consul changed the national holiday in celebration of the
            The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure   99

storming of the Bastille to a Sunday, in order not to lose a work¬
ing day, which did not prevent him, however, from turning an
increasing number of essential workers into soldiers. One of the
steps Napoleon took to combat British trade was to compel
Josephine very much against her will to wear Lyons silk instead
of the modern Indian muslin. In 1810 he supervised the transport
of 10,000 Spanish sheep to France, although the French army at
Saragossa alone consumed 200,000 sheep; one of the first things
he did on arriving in Elba was to go down into the iron quarries
like an inspector of mines and then conclude a trade agreement
with Leghorn.
  In tidying up the country’s finances, Napoleon certainly was
a great improvement on the Directory, but, as soon as he went
beyond the purely administrative into the commercial sphere, he
was out of his depth. He tried in vain, while Bourrienne was
Ambassador in Hamburg, to persuade the Hansa towns to accept
French protection against a payment of six million francs each;
he also failed in 1803 to buy from Louis XVIII his right to the
throne and, as the latter put it, his honour; he allowed himself to
be hopelessly entangled by the Rothschilds in 1811, when he
thought he had deprived the British of the hard cash they so
badly needed, whereas the Rothschilds used the bills drawn on
Madrid against this money to finance Wellington’s campaign in
Portugal.* Another of his plans which failed was the secret coun¬
terfeiting of large quantities of Russian and Austrian bank notes,
which the Councillor of State and Prefect of Police, Pasquier,
supplied and which were to be used in the 1812 campaign; Russian
merchants would only take them in very small quantities and they
finally had to be burnt. The Emperor was also over-optimistic
in his message from Russia to Paris that the salt-mines he had
seized would produce an extra revenue of eighty million francs.
Moreover, the relaxation of the Continental Blockade, which he
had imposed in 1806, with the introduction of expensive permits,
brought a marked but short-lived increase in customs revenue;
its long-term effects on the national finances were disastrous,
for the French economy was ruined by this impracticable and
  * See p. xi of Introduction.
100                          Ouvrard

ineffectual fiction. To take only one example, the price of sugar
rose to six francs a pound.
   Particularly disastrous in its consequences was Napoleon’s
refusal to take advice on pegging the market. At moments when
his devoted and experienced Minister of the Treasury, Mollien,
declared it was of no consequence whatsoever whether govern¬
ment stocks stood a few per cent higher or lower, he insisted on
face-saving measures, without having the necessary financial
backing; in 1803, the renewal of war cost him twelve million,
although the price of rentes still went down by 10 per cent. At
the outbreak of the Spanish War, Napoleon decreed, against
Mollien’s advice, that the 5 per cent stock must stand at 80 at
least; this cost him thirty million and was once again quite
ineffectual. Had the Emperor judged himself by the same
criterion as he judged Ouvrard and considered only the fact of
the deficit, not the circumstances which gave rise to it, he would
have been obliged to refund the millions, which had been
squandered through his stupidity and obstinacy, out       of his
own pocket or, alternatively, to condemn himself to the debtors’
prison of Sainte-Pelagie.
    The plan to make Spain solvent and the Mexican project were
on such a large and grandiose scale that even someone with as
little natural talent for commerce as Napoleon should have rea¬
lized their immense possibilities. His inability to see this was
perhaps partly due to the fact that his policy was essentially
European in character and that his whole attitude to commerce
was European. None of his own overseas ventures had turned
out well. The Egyptian campaign ended in complete military
and political defeat; Louisiana, France’s invaluable colonial
territory in America, had to be ceded for a mere eighty million
francs in 1803 under the growing threat of war with England;
San Domingo in Haiti could not be held, even with 50,000
men and an enormous amount of supplies and equipment, and
it was abandoned in 1803 without compensation; Napoleon’s
plan, to subdue his arch-enemy England by a cross-Channel
invasion, finally had to be dropped. In such circumstances
it was understandable that even someone like Napoleon, who
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure   101

had been so successful elsewhere, should suffer from hydro¬
phobia.
   Ouvrard’s efforts to make Spain and himself solvent foundered
on this aversion and finally on Napoleon’s outright refusal to
consider an enterprise which, in fact, had already reached a most
promising stage. “All the Emperor had to do was to open the
ports of France to immense treasures, which far exceeded all the
loot he had squeezed out of the conquered peoples with suffering,
brute-force and blood. Apparently gold held no attraction for
him, if it was acquired by the peaceful method of trade.” In
writing these words, Ouvrard overlooked the fact, of course, that,
had the agreement between him and Charles IV been carried out,
it would not have earned France more than the seventy-two
million francs a year which Spain owed her in subsidies. But,
with that qualification, what he said was true. For at the very
moment when Napoleon was clumsily tearing the whole finely
spun web to pieces, it became abundantly clear that Ouvrard’s
commercial transaction was perfectly practicable and utterly
different from Law’s dreams around 1716 of finding capital in the
mists of the Mississippi and the savannahs of Texas. Such of
Ouvrard’s Spanish bills of exchange as escaped Napoleon’s
clutches bore the promised fruit. Ouvrard’s first triumph came
when Hopes, in spite of the blockade, succeeded in getting four
frigates to England with Mexican silver piastres for the Spaniards,
which meant that fourteen million piastres were sold at 3-75 per
piastre on the Continent: from England to Hopes, from Hopes
to Ouvrard, from Ouvrard to Spain, from Spain to the French
Treasury. And even now, when Pitt was prepared to make
no further concessions, Ouvrard still had not played his last
card. His partnership with Hopes had been chosen with just such
an eventuality in mind. The second method, by which this firm
with its world-wide affiliations arranged for the remaining
Mexican silver to be released, was both costly and complex, but
it justified Ouvrard’s self-confidence, when he said later that,
as far as he was concerned, the blockade had never existed.
  This second method was to buy goods in America with the
Mexican piastres, transport them to Europe, sell them and hand
102                           Ouvrard

over the proceeds, after commission had been deducted, to
whoever was holding the bills of exchange, namely Ouvrard or
the Spanish Consolidated Fund. For a firm as experienced as
Hopes this presented no great difficulty, in spite of the enormous
sums involved, so long as England was prepared to wink an eye
at this camouflaged transfer of piastres. The deal was much too
big for Hopes to be able to pass off their shipments as neutral
freight. There remained only the much more complicated method
of making the shipments not apparently but really neutral. This
was only possible if American merchants could be won over, who
supplied direct to European firms in countries which were not
at war with England. But, as America was at an early stage of
development, it would obviously be very difficult to find reliable
North American firms big enough to take the risk.
  The plan of campaign, which was eventually worked out, was
as follows:
  Hopes, having received from Ouvrard the Spanish drafts on
Mexico, would send them with an agent from the Amsterdam
office, M. Lestapis, to Vera Cruz, where, to make matters easier,
he would carry Spanish papers in the name of Jose Gabriel de
Villaneuva.* Lestapis would then present the drafts in Mexico
and receive in return the silver dollars from the National Treasury.
These would be released gradually, as the opportunity arose to
transmit cash.
  The silver dollars would be loaded on ships specially built by
Hopes’ second agent, David Parish, stationed in Philadelphia,
the destination of the ships. These ships and their freight must be,
at least pro forma, American property, otherwise the British would
seize them. As no reputable firm would land itself in such
difficulties or incur such risks, everything depended on Parish’s
ingenuity.
  On arrival in Philadelphia, the silver dollars would be handed
over to carefully selected American commercial firms—as an
advance. In return they would be committed to buying goods—
cotton, tobacco, coffee, sugar and so on—and shipping them

  * A Spaniard, now dead, for whom A. P. Lestapis had worked some
years earlier.—T.A.B.C.
           The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure     103

to neutral countries in Europe. These European clients were to be
chosen by Hopes. The Americans would also be committed to
insuring these shipments in England, thus creating a more con¬
genial atmosphere for Hopes.
   The dollar advances would be secured by bills of exchange
from the American firms, to be handed to Hopes’ agent, Parish.
These bills would be paid by the European purchasers of the
goods, who, as soon as the shipments arrived, would make their
settlement with Hopes. Hopes, in turn, would settle up the
Spanish bills with Ouvrard, and the money, which the British
were so anxious to block, would finally begin flowing into the
coffers of France’s ally, Spain.
   In practice this American deal proved even more complicated
and risky, because in his contract with Charles IV Ouvrard had
succeeded in obtaining not merely permission to transport the
money but also a monopoly of trade with Mexico. The 500 trade
concessions, which Ouvrard was able to hand over in Amsterdam,
were extremely valuable, for it meant that the same number of
ships could be unloaded in the harbour of Vera Cruz. Hopes sent
these shipments (of German and English goods) not only from
European ports but also from Philadelphia, so that the freight-
space could be used on both the outward and the return journeys.
It was the responsibility of Hopes’ first agent, Lestapis, to see that
the goods which arrived in Mexico reached the buyers. But many
of the trading concessions could be sold at very high prices like
ordinary stocks: American firms paid up to a third of their net
profit as premium. The profits accruing from these sales had to
be shared out in accordance with the contract between Hopes and
Ouvrard and the contract between Ouvrard and Charles IV.
Both transactions, the transport of the money and the handling of
the trading concessions, were entrusted to a third agent, whom
Hopes sent to a point roughly midway between Vera Cruz and
Philadelphia—New Orleans. The name of this agent was Vincent
Nolte.
   Work on this vast edifice began in the summer of 1805, when
Hopes’ three agents moved to their respective stations in America.
In the spring of the following year, before they had even finished
104                           Ouvrard

laying the foundations, the entire project was ruined by Napo¬
leon’s intervention. Just how much was lost emerged from a
statement drawn up by Vincent Nolte for Parish at the end of
1808: the total turnover of this transaction, which had to be
prematurely abandoned, would have amounted to thirty-three
million Spanish dollars or 165 million French francs.
  The actual settlements with Ouvrard himself, the prime mover
of this vast undertaking, covered a mere fraction of the whole.
Even Hopes could not afford, in the long run,- not to work with
Napoleon’s government, which had taken over all Ouvrard’s
legal rights with his financial liquidation. Although the Hopes’
ledgers for the years 1804-13 do not show the enormous sum
involved, it is clear that the various enterprises he had launched
had greatly expanded. There is no mention of Ouvrard’s name
in the entries for 1804, as he first travelled to Spain in September
of that year. His 1805 account (“van Lerberghe & Ouvrard”)
showed 887,000 Dutch gulden. In 1806 there was a balance of
6,611,761 gulden, from which periodic deductions were made to
cover the advance on the loan, as Hopes disposed of their loan
issue. In the same year one million gulden were allowed to pass
again through a special account (“August Ouvrard”). At the end
of 1807 the account stood at 1,116,338 gulden, in 1808 at 1,709,049
gulden. In 1809 it shrank to 216,813, but rose *n 1810 to 223,692
gulden. In 1811 Ouvrard was 152,000 in debt, but in 1812 he
again had a balance of 160,721. In 1813 he disappeared from the
books of the firm of Hopes. As the account was balanced at the
end of each financial year, a total of twelve million gulden, some
twenty-four million    francs, had passed     through    Ouvrard’s
hands. So the prospects can hardly have been as bleak as Napoleon
made them out to be.
  The final irony, which must have given Ouvrard considerable
though belated satisfaction, came when Napoleon himself tried
to cash some of the Spanish-Mexican bills, which he had seized
from Ouvrard, directly, by-passing Hopes in Amsterdam with the
intention of evading the substantially lower European rate of
exchange (3-75 instead of 5 francs a piastre). The French Consul
General in Philadelphia, to whom the papers had been sent, was
            The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure            105

completely nonplussed when no one would touch the suspect
bills he was offering. The Consul had no alternative but to appeal
for help to Ouvrard’s business friend, David Parish of Hopes,
who was kind enough to obtain part of the money from Mexico.
But it was not the entire sum, which amounted to over two
million piastres.
   Whatever satisfaction Ouvrard may have felt, however, was
of little practical value to him. Together with Spain, with Vanler-
berghe, Michel and Seguin, he had been condemned to pay 141
million francs and, if he valued his freedom, he had to summon all
his resources to pay his share. It is a striking tribute to the financial
acumen of Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe that they were able to
reduce the total debt to eighty-five million by the end of 1806.
In March 1808, only fifteen months later, when the Minister of
the Treasury drew up a balance-sheet, only thirteen of the 141
million were still owing. But Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe had
already had enough of merely paying, particularly as the private
creditors were also growing restive. So they decided to have
recourse to the only legal expedient left to them, suspended
payments on 31st December 1807 and declared themselves
bankrupt.*
   It was, however, a rather unusual type of bankruptcy. The
two partners stressed the fact, with obvious relish, that their
liabilities did not exceed their assets but that they were simply
insolvent, because the government had quite unjustly confiscated
several millions which belonged to them and which it now owed
them. At the same time, Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe had appar¬
ently agreed to divide up their joint fortunes in such a way as
to make Napoleon’s decrees largely ineffective. They had no
intention, if they could help it, of paying out more than they had
invested in the Spanish-Mexican operation. This and that pro¬
perty must have been registered in another name, that of a friend
or relative, and in good time, probably before Napoleon returned
from the wars. Eighteen months later Ouvrard was in a position

  * They chose the 31st December because a new Commercial Code was
due to come into effect on the 1st January 1808, imposing stricter control on
commercial transactions and bankruptcies.—T.A.B.C.
io6                           Ouvrard

to pay a substantial sum in bail; in 1814 he was able to retire to an
estate near Orleans; as late as 1824 he was still considering the
possible industrial development of his estate at Preuilly (which
included the largest privately owned forest, some 18,000 acres,
in France), an estate of which Ouvrard himself said that he had
bought it “a long time ago” but, owing to pressure of business,
had never visited. This can only have been acquired during the
inflation period of the Directory.
   His ingenious method of handling his bankruptcy is illustrated
by the following incident:
   He still owed Monsieur Chenais 50,000 francs on the purchase
of Villandryand there was a mortgage outstanding. When frequent
demands for payment brought no response from Ouvrard, Chenais
sent his son-in-law, General Thiebault, to see him. Three days in
succession the General called early in the morning in the Fau¬
bourg du Roule but on each occasion Ouvrard was not at home.
When Thiebault finally forced his way in, he made his way
through a long series of empty rooms and eventually found the
master of the house just getting out of bed. Ouvrard pointed out
that, as he was bankrupt, he could pay off neither the cash debt
nor the mortgage. The General literally besieged him, threatening
to starve him out and only release him from his bedroom when
he had met his commitments. After two hours Ouvrard gave
way, wrote a cheque, and gave instructions for a mortgage to
be issued to Monsieur Chenais on a property which allegedly
belonged to his brother, August Ouvrard.
   This somewhat unorthodox form of bankruptcy was eventually
terminated as strangely as it had begun. Although Ouvrard
remained officially a bankrupt, he came to an agreement with his
private creditors on 26th October 1808 which gave him back
complete freedom of action. On the other hand, while Ouvrard
succeeded in resuming his full business activities and even built
up certain reserves, which would have seemed like considerable
wealth to the average businessman, for him, with his irrepressible
financial ambitions, the liquidation dictated by Napoleon was
nothing short of disastrous. A particularly striking symptom of it
 was the sale, which he could no longer postpone, of Rainey in
                The Great Expectation: the Mexican Silver Treasure           ioj

1806, and the loss of Madame Tallien, who, with her remarkable
ability to sense crises in the affairs of her friends, had rushed into
her third marriage with the Comte de Caraman in 1805.
   The announcement of Ouvrard’s bankruptcy brought his
reputation as a financier tumbling about his ears. People had come
to regard him as a sorcerer complete with magic wand and
divining-rod; now he stood exposed before the world, appar¬
ently as helpless as any ordinary mortal. Everyone knew that
Ouvrard had compelled the Emperor to turn to him in times of
need, but now that accounts had been settled it was clear that
Napoleon had emerged victorious, having won the contest
decisively and at a fearful price to his adversary.
   And yet, just how high the price had been was not to emerge
immediately, at the end of 1807, but some eighteen months
later. He spent this year and a half, when he must have missed the
company of Madame Tallien, “quietly with his family”, certainly
hoping in his heart that Napoleon would consider his bankruptcy
a thing of the past.* But on 21st June 1809, more than three years
after that dreadful day of reckoning in the Tuileries, Ouvrard’s
day-dreams were rudely shattered. He was made responsible for
the sum of a million and a half piastres (which, depending on the
rate of exchange, was between five-and-a-half and eight million

    * During 1809 he was mixed up in a number of schemes, political as well as
commercial. He was already in league with Talleyrand and Fouche, who
had agreed among themselves that if Napoleon should die in battle or
through assassination in Spain, his vain and pliable brother-in-law Murat
should succeed him. After Napoleon had learnt about this understanding
and had dismissed Talleyrand in a fury, Ouvrard coolly proceeded to make
arrangements with Murat, Fouche and Labouchere to ship a hundred cargoes
of goods from Malta to Murat’s kingdom of Naples against an equivalent
value of exports. This overt attempt to breach the blockade was frustrated
either by Ouvrard’s incarceration in June or by Napoleon’s authority being
strengthened as a result of his victory at Wagram a few weeks later. Never¬
theless, Murat did thereafter step up his exports to Britain of olive oil, grain
and silk. As in August 1805, so now Ouvrard’s schemes were helping to
shape future events. For the Tsar, who had reluctantly agreed at Tilsit in
1807 to participate in Napoleon’s Continental System, grew so discontented
at the news of these illegal grain exports that at the end of 1810 he resolved
to reopen his ports to neutral ships. Here then was one of the causes of
Napoleon’s invasion of Russia in 1812.—T.A.B.C.
           J'
108                             Ouvrard

francs) which Spain owed but had not yet paid. Ouvrard’s pro¬
tests were in vain. He sent a printed memorandum to the Emperor
which had no effect. He was treated like a defaulting debtor and,
as he did not pay, was locked up in the debtors’ prison. Deprived
of his freedom, Ouvrard lost what little remained to him of
pleasure in life and of his reputation. He would undoubtedly have
had to resign himself to an indefinite period of imprisonment in
Sainte-Pelagie if he had not managed to produce adequate bail
from his invisible reserves and thus saved himself from the worst
consequences of the Mexican affair.
   Only then, in this particular moment of crisis, was it clear
how far Ouvrard had fallen. And so once again, in 1809, another
five years of his life had ended with a hiatus, as if the ups and
downs of his career followed a regular time-pattern. Five years
earlier, in 1804, the government’s inability or refusal to pay had
forced him to dispose of much of his property, including his
town residence, the Palais Montesson. Ten years earlier, in
1799, his misfortunes had begun with the fall of his friend Barras
and Napoleon’s advent to power. Fifteen years earlier, in 1794,
he had started, as a soldier, on his meteoric career in Paris.
Twenty years earlier, in 1789, at the outbreak of the Revolution,
he had completed his first major deal with the purchase of paper
supplies. It had taken him altogether ten years, from 1789 to
1799, to reach his zenith and another ten years, from 1799 to
1809, to reach his nadir.
  If Ouvrard employed the days he spent in Sainte-Pelagie
wondering what the next decade would bring, it is unlikely,
despite all his setbacks, that he felt over-pessimistic. He still had
faith in his star. He realized that he still possessed capital resources
which were inalienable and irreplaceable: his great financial
talent. He could afford to feel reasonably confident about starting
afresh. After all, he was only thirty-nine.
                               Seven




                   The Great Disaster:
           the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations




In the final analysis, it was the combination of business and
politics that compelled Ouvrard to start once again from the
very beginning. He would not have fallen nearly so far and his
financial losses would not have been nearly so heavy, if he had
not felt obliged to dabble in politics in order to achieve greater
success in business. During the five years in Paris from 1794 to
1799, the period of the Directory and Barras, and in the first five
years of the Napoleonic era, he was content to interest politicians
in his plans and ventures. For a time this worked extremely well
and, even when it was less successful, at least his outstanding
achievements were never in danger. But when the Mexican
treasure became such an obsession that he agreed to go to Spain
as a State Commissioner and to become actively involved in
relations between France and Charles IV, between Spain and
England, between Spain and her colonies, then business and
politics combined proved too much for him.
  Strangely enough, Ouvrard, who in other respects was so far¬
sighted, did not even realize this, when Napoleon made him
responsible for the Spanish debt and deprived him of his freedom.
It still had not dawned on him that the Mexican dream was over.
Barely was he his own master again, than he was turning over
in his mind how he could change government policy to help him
in his business plans. He was convinced—and rightly so—that
everything would change for the better, if only the war with
                               109
110                              Ouvrard

England could be ended. Then the minted and the untapped
treasures of Central and South America would become freely and
openly accessible; even Napoleon’s opposition to Ouvrard’s
transatlantic plans would collapse; indeed, it would probably be
unnecessary even to ask for the Emperor’s approval.
   Having acquired this idee fixe, Ouvrard, with all his untiring
faith in any ideas he conceived, actually set to work to bring
about peace between France and England. It was a breath-taking
scheme, which called for at least the same degree of resourceful¬
ness as Ouvrard had employed in Spain. The two countries
had been at one another’s throats for more than fifteen years.
The time was long since past when, as during the Peace of
Amiens, the English came crowding over to Paris and could be
seen frequently in Madame Recamier’s salon. The main obstacle
to another peace treaty was, of course, Napoleon’s refusal to
compromise; he would not consider any treaty which did not
wring major concessions from the British. This was again made
abundantly clear when the peace overtures which Napoleon
himself authorized in February 1810 came to nothing because
the Emperor refused to budge from his position.* Ouvrard, who
knew that international negotiations were in progress and who
knew the English better than Napoleon, foresaw a fiasco and
tried his utmost to keep the negotiations going. The obvious
course was to attempt to win Napoleon over to a more reasonable
and more realistic view of the situation. But all his efforts to
obtain the “favour” of an audience were in vain. Again and again
his powerful friends Murat, Berthier, General Rapp and the
Councillor of State, Regnault, intervened with the Emperor on
his behalf, but without success.
  So Ouvrard’s direct approach had failed. But he had never
been one to abandon his plans lightly, and he immediately sought
another method of approach. He turned now for help to Joseph
Fouche, originally a Prefect in the Society of Oratorians, and

  * Already in January 1810 Fouche had anticipated his master’s intentions
by initiating his own unofficial peace talks in London through a certain
Francois Fagan. But although the talks were not unhopeful they produced
no results.—T.A.B.C.
         The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   Ill


subsequently one of the strange collection of renegade monks
and priests who played an important part in the Revolution;
men such as Carrier, who had started as a novice in a monastery
and then, as Commissioner of the Revolution, had taught the
citizens of Nantes to dance the carmagnole; the Abbe d’Espagnac,
who had amassed millions during the Revolution; Chabot, for¬
merly a Capuchin monk and later a Commissioner of the Con¬
vention; Sieyes, former Abbe and then a member of the Directory;
the priest, Robert-Thomas Lindet,* who voted for Louis XVI’s
execution and was the first member of the clergy to marry; Abbe
Louis, Louis XVI’s servant and the Emperor Napoleon’s financial
adviser; above all Talleyrand, who laid aside his bishop’s mitre
to devote himself to more worldly pleasures and pursuits. All
that distinguished Fouche from most of the others was that
he lasted longer. He had managed to shed the blood of counter¬
revolutionary citizens, to bring down the incorruptible Robe¬
spierre, to serve the Directory and Barras, to help the counter¬
revolutionary General Napoleon Bonaparte into the saddle and
at every deliberate change of course to steer clear of the rocks.
In fact, with each volte-face he had even become more powerful,
so that, when Ouvrard finally approached him, he was Napoleon’s
Minister of Police and the second most powerful man in France.
  If Ouvrard took his plan for an agreement with England to
Fouche, it was not merely because his own official standing had
been so undermined by the liquidation of the Spanish venture
that there was little or nothing he could do by himself. Fouche
was the ideal man because he had become a past master at intrigue
and because he had as little love for Napoleon as Ouvrard. So
the merchant, who had doubtless had frequent dealings with the
avaricious Minister, called on Fouche ostensibly, as he was still
under police supervision, to obtain a permit to travel to Amster¬
dam and settle his affairs with Hopes. The encounter was not un¬
like his first meeting with Barras at Ouvrard’s chateau at Vitry
in 1796. And, as on that occasion, little is known of what the two
men discussed in the Ministry of Police early in 1810. Later, after

  1 Brother of Robert Lindet, “the financial expert of the Revolution”
mentioned on pp. 25-6.—T.A.B.C.
112                          Ouvrard

the whole enterprise had failed miserably and Fouche was dead,
Ouvrard maintained that the whole idea of a rapprochement with
England had come from Fouche, who had brushed aside all
Ouvrard’s objections and used the request for a passport as a
means of forcing Ouvrard to sound out opinion in England
privately, without Napoleon’s knowledge. And historians have
so far assumed that the plan to open secret negotiations with the
British, unknown to Napoleon, did originate with Fouche, who
simply used Ouvrard as a means of adding to his own prestige
and perhaps even damaging Napoleon’s reputation. In fact the
state of Ouvrard’s affairs makes it likely that he had a share in
the initiation of this third attempt to reach a peace settlement.
Almost the only hope left to him of surmounting his bankruptcy
and resuming his former exalted position was a radical change
in the political situation, which would enable him, with or with¬
out Napoleon’s blessing, to pursue his American plans. But,
whoever may have started the ball rolling, when Ouvrard left
Fouche’s office it was as the Minister’s confidential ambassador.
His task was, while in Holland, to give the British the impression
that negotiations could be resumed and that, with the Emperor’s
if not official at least tacit approval, fresh proposals had been
worked out.
  Ouvrard arrived in Amsterdam in March 1810. Fouche sent
him a pass to England by courier. Ouvrard would certainly
have been happier if he could have travelled again, as he did to
Spain, in an official capacity, and if he did not move on to Eng¬
land at once it was because he was not at all sure what kind of
reception he would get. Labouchere, the man whom Napoleon
had entrusted in February with the task of putting out feelers in
England—although the initiative had to be made to appear to
come from Louis Bonaparte, King of Holland—had just returned
from London to Amsterdam. He was a partner and the effective
head of the firm of Hopes, and Ouvrard had collaborated with
him on the Mexican project; he was therefore the emissary Fouche
and Ouvrard were hoping to employ for their peace talks.
  A better mediator could hardly have been chosen. He was
French by birth, had married an Englishwoman and was resident
         The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   113

in a neutral country; he combined all the best qualities of a highly
respected businessman with the courtly manners of an aristocrat;
last, but by no means least, his wife was a daughter of Sir Francis
Baring, whose firm controlled the entire international money
market and who had excellent personal relations with members
of the British Government. This match alone, which became
known as the “Hope marriage”, had revealed Labouchere’s
extraordinary skill in achieving his ends. After a three years’
apprenticeship with Hopes he had asked, at the age of twenty-
two, for a partnership in the firm. Although his sterling qualities
were greatly appreciated, his request was, not surprisingly,
turned down. He then enquired whether, if he were Sir Francis
Baring’s son-in-law, he would be acceptable. The answer was in
the affirmative. When Labouchere asked Baring for his daughter’s
hand, he again received a negative reply, but, when he enquired
whether he would be acceptable if he were a partner in Hopes,
 the answer was again in the affirmative. Both marriage and
 partnership were now assured. This was a measure of the diplo¬
 matic skill which Fouche and Ouvrard were hoping to employ.
    However, the news Labouchere brought from London was
rather depressing. Although the British Cabinet had expressed
no interest in the fate of Holland, they did lay down one sub¬
 stantial condition, namely that the Bourbons—Charles IV and
his son Ferdinand—whom Napoleon had driven out of Spain by
 cunning and deceit, should be restored to the throne. This showed,
therefore, that the Emperor’s peace overtures had met with no
success whatever. Although Napoleon appeared to think seriously
for some weeks about resuming the talks, he finally realized that
these would be hopeless and quietly dropped the idea. In these
circumstances it was clearly impossible for Ouvrard to send
Labouchere back to London with fresh instructions. The memory
of the courteous but firm reception the envoy had received from
British politicians was still too fresh. Ouvrard therefore made
use of the delay to obtain some kind of legal backing for his own
plan. Having failed to gain an audience with Napoleon, he
decided to try an approach in writing. On 21st March he wrote a
letter to the Emperor from Amsterdam, in which he explained
114                          Ouvrard

how much was to be gained by making peace with England and
how, at the same time, the British could be outmanoeuvred: once
peace had been concluded, the Bourbons, exiled from Spain,
could be put on the throne in Central America, which, in any
case, belonged to them; this would give France a new political
base from which to operate, particularly as there would be the
possibility of arranging a marriage between the Spanish Crown
Prince, the Prince of Asturias, and a princess from the Bonaparte
family; Central America was an ideal centre from which to reach
other territories, particularly South America, where the British
were already expanding their interests; finally, “the Emperor of
America would give his daughter to the King of Mexico”,
England would thus be hemmed in and British trade would be
denied not merely Continental but also all American ports. “This
means the ruin of England”; to bring it about, all that was
needed was not a genuine but merely a patched-up peace, which
restored the freedom of the seas for about a year, so that any
concessions France might have to make need not be taken too
seriously.
  Needless to say, he waited in vain for an answer from Paris.
Napoleon was now preoccupied with preparations for his mar¬
riage to Marie-Louise and probably paid little attention to the
memorandum. Had he done so he would undoubtedly have
brought Ouvrard’s schemes to an abrupt end. However, Napoleon
did not even react a few weeks later when Ouvrard tried to renew
contact by a method he had already employed before, by making
a formal offer—although he was still an official bankrupt—of
ioo million francs in cash.
  So Ouvrard still had time to go on spinning his English web.
Through Francis Baring, Labouchere made another approach to
the Foreign Secretary, the Marquis Wellesley, and all three were
under the impression that the proposals they were discussing
stemmed from Napoleon. In fact the proposals, making several
concessions, came from Fouche. What Ouvrard was now sug¬
gesting as a basis for a peace settlement, in sharp contrast
to the ideas he had submitted to the Emperor, was nothing
more nor less than a partition of America between Britain
         The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   115

and France: Britain was to annex North America, and France
South America; Mexico in the middle (with her silver dollars
and mines!) was reserved for Ouvrard’s good friends the
Spaniards.
    This scheme was perhaps not so fantastic as it sounds. At any
 rate Wellesley and Canning (the previous Foreign Secretary, who
 advised Wellesley on foreign affairs) actually discussed at great
 length the proposals that Baring forwarded to them in April. On
 the 17th the proposals were accepted by the Cabinet as the basis
 for negotiations. When Baring reported this news from England,
 Ouvrard, his plans on the verge of success, set off for Paris to
 get further instructions from Fouche. The Minister of Police
 might well have been able to get official support for these plans.
 But unfortunately Napoleon decided to set out for Holland at the
 beginning of May to prepare it for formal annexation. His un¬
 suspecting brother, the King of Holland, told him all he knew
 of the second visit which Labouchere had paid to London at
 Ouvrard’s instigation. It was the first Napoleon had heard of a
 resumption of negotiations, without his approval and on an
entirely new basis, which was absolutely unacceptable to him.
Napoleon was beside himself with indignation. The earlier
talks had been authorized by himself while purporting to emanate
from the Dutch Government alone; the present series were en¬
tirely without his approval and yet were assumed to come from
himself. His grievance was not merely that the terms were
entirely unacceptable to himself, but also that Britain was a
country he hated more than any other because it was, in the last
resort, the British alone who stood between him and world
domination. They had not even recognized his imperial title;
in 1805, for example, he was informed that “His Majesty the King
[of England]” had received “the message of the Head of the
French Government”. Whereas the Press throughout the Con¬
tinent had to dance to his tune, in England it was free to caricature
and laugh at him!
   It was, therefore, not surprising that, when Napoleon returned
to Paris, a terrifying storm broke over Ouvrard’s head. When
it then emerged that Fouche was also implicated, Napoleon’s
ll6                          Ouvrard

rage knew no bounds. The session of the Council of State on
2nd June 1810, at which Napoleon sat in judgement on his
Minister of Police, was every bit as nerve-racking as the nine-
hour audience on 26th January 1806. The critical point came
when Fouche—with good reason—refused to arrest Ouvrard;
after all, Ouvrard had sent him regular reports from Amsterdam
in invisible ink by way of a Paris merchant. Fouche was immedi¬
ately dismissed and his successor, Savary, Due de Rovigo,
was told to arrest Ouvrard. So that there should be no warning
given, the order was to be carried out while the Council was still
in session. The police enticed Ouvrard to the house of Madame
Hamelin. He was taking tea there with Madame Hamelin and
Talleyrand, when a senior police officer arrived and asked to
speak to him alone; he was then taken to a waiting carriage, which
was escorted by eight officers of the gendarmerie.
  Ouvrard’s new, compulsory place of residence was, to begin
with, the Abbaye prison, then, a few days later, the Tower of
Vincennes. This was the prison for State criminals. Ouvrard
must have realized that the same prison had housed many dis¬
tinguished but also many ill-fated prisoners before him: Diderot
and Mirabeau, victims of the French Revolution, and a number of
priests and bishops, who had opted for the Pope and against
Napoleon. In one of the rooms in the Tower the Due d’Enghien
had spent the last hours of his life, before Napoleon committed a
travesty of justice and had him shot in the moat. And, as if to
make Ouvrard doubly conscious of his perilous position, the
prison governor objected, in his presence, to accepting the
prisoner under a false name and without a written order of com¬
mittal, on the grounds that, when the prisoner died, it would be
impossible to make out a proper death certificate.
  These gloomy prospects proved, however, to be without
foundation. On the other hand, Ouvrard’s position was unpleasant
enough, particularly as a warder was constantly with him in his
room, day and night. For someone with so many diverse interests
it was extraordinarily difficult to live without books and writing
material, and he kept his mind occupied by scattering a packet of
pins, the contents of which he had already counted, all over the
        The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   117

room and picking them up again. This sometimes took hours.
Fortunately, as the weeks passed, he saw signs that his position
was improving. The official investigation had revealed that he
did not go to London, contrary to what Napoleon had been led
to believe, that the peace proposals drawn up in Amsterdam on
21 st March had actually been reported to the Imperial Chan¬
cellery, and that Ouvrard had asked for the document to be
returned in the event of rejection, which had not been done.
Finally, after Madame Ouvrard had made a moving appeal for
clemency in a private audience with the Emperor, Ouvrard ceased
to be a prisoner of state.
  But Napoleon’s clemency took a peculiar form. No sooner
had Ouvrard been released than he was rearrested as a debtor
to the State. Napoleon was obviously reluctant to set such an
active and uncongenial character at liberty again. It was not for
nothing that he had written to Fouche’s successor at the Ministry
of Police to say that he had never encountered a worse example
of high treason and that this scoundrel had already cost him
two Ministers. So Ouvrard was confronted once more with the
decree of 21st July 1809, as a result of which he had already
been committed to prison, though only for a short time. He was
now held definitely responsible, as guarantor, for the millions of
francs owed by the Spaniards. On this occasion he was not able
to gain his freedom by merely giving bail; he was to spend more
than three years in the debtors’ prison of Sainte-Pelagie. As he
himself remarked, “in France it is always dangerous to be either
creditor or debtor to the government, quite regardless whether
it is a Republic, a Directory, a Consulate or an Empire—the
old traditions outlast every regime, and anyone who incurs the
enmity of a government is outside the law. For such wretches
there is no judiciary, no court of law and no justice—only
officials equipped with arbitrary powers.”
  The prison of Sainte-Pelagie to which Ouvrard was now trans¬
ferred from Vincennes, was at least convivial. Even in the criminal
section, the nephew of the Shah of Persia, Nadir Mirza Shah,
who was imprisoned there in 1802 for beating his coachman in a
public thoroughfare, had created complete chaos by organizing
118                          Ouvrard.

large parties at which the guests had to sit cross-legged and eat
with their fingers, and by laying on a constant supply of flowers,
women and wine. Such conditions were only permitted to foreign
guests in the criminal section but in the debtors’ prison these
facilities were available to any prisoner who could pay for them.
 Ouvrard, who always seemed to be able to produce money and
gold out of empty pockets like a magician pulling rabbits out of
a hat, soon made it clear that even in Sainte-Pelagie he could
afford to live up to his station. He furnished himself a room with
charming, expensive furniture, received many well-known people,
including Prince Laval who frequently lunched with him; there
were dinner-parties, small soirees with charades and whist; when
 Ouvrard received permission to hasten to the bedside of his
dying mother, a fast horse was waiting for him outside the prison
and he galloped off dressed in the most elegant riding habit.
   When Ouvrard ran out of visitors, he still had no reason to
be bored, for his old friends Vanlerberghe, Desprez and Seguin
were fellow inmates on the same charge; there must have been a
touching reunion when Ouvrard arrived from Vincennes. But
he did not enjoy their company for very long; either they did
not have his nerves of steel or perhaps they valued their freedom
more than their money; in short, they were not prepared to take
the risk of serving the maximum sentence of five years in Sainte-
Pelagie. They made their peace with the government. Ouvrard,
on the other hand, was not prepared to surrender his remaining
millions and refused to compromise. Not only did he turn the
situation to good account, he even made it a question of principle,
assuring all his guests that his sole reason for enduring imprison¬
ment was that at least one man must refuse to bend the knee to
force and despotism; if all victims of the government were to do
likewise, then it would find it much more difficult to abuse its
power; such a stand, he argued, was directed against enslavement
in general, in support of the community and against tyrants.
   If he was so determined to cling to his remaining millions,
even at the cost of five years in prison, it was perhaps because
the prospects of fresh conquests were slender indeed. His con¬
tracts with the Spaniards had been cancelled, the war between
         The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   119

Britain and France continued to have a ruinous effect on trade,
and he had even been relieved of the contract for supplying the
army. For all Napoleon’s fury in January 1806, he had still left
 Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe in charge of military and naval pro¬
visions, contrary to general expectations. With remarkable
shrewdness, which, in fact, was the first indication of a business¬
like approach by Napoleon, the Emperor made use of their
commitments to the armed forces to lay his hands on the 141
million francs he was demanding: they had to deliver the goods
 in full, as before, but received only a half or even a third of the
money due to them, the remainder being withheld to pay off
the debt. The new contract was drawn up for a period of six
years and should, therefore, have lasted until 1812, but in 1809
Napoleon cancelled the agreement unilaterally, brushing aside
 Ouvrard’s protests. He had suddenly decided that he would
have no more to do with these merchants, that he could now
 dispense with them.
    Whereas in 1797 Barras, at Ouvrard’s suggestion, had turned
over the provisioning of the navy from a government depart¬
ment to private hands, Napoleon, in order to free his own hands,
reversed the situation and placed the entire army administration
under State control. He set up a State organization, in which
employees of the commercial firms continued to work but which
was headed by State Counsellors. As long as the stocks taken over
by the new company lasted, all was well, but when they ran out
the trouble began. As soon as the young officials appeared in the
main corn-markets to buy up supplies, everyone, knowing their
lack of experience, did their best to cheat them. By the end of
the year the running costs of the new organization had already
gone up by a million francs compared with the cost under
Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe. So Ouvrard, sitting in prison, could
afford to gloat. In 1812, after he had spent exactly two years in
prison, he had even better cause to gloat, when, as a result of a
bad harvest in 1811, there was a serious shortage of grain. The
situation was much more critical than in 1802, when Napoleon,
who had hitherto refused to have any dealings with Ouvrard
and Vanlerberghe, had suddenly become very well disposed and
120                           Ouvrard

summoned them to Malmaison in the middle of the night. In the
spring of 1812 the price of grain was extremely high, and stocks
were   clearly being   hoarded. The shortage      was particularly
severe in the north; in Normandy crowds of hungry beggars
roamed through the fields; in Caen granaries were pillaged. In
such an emergency the Emperor had no choice but to turn once
more to the experts. On this occasion, however, he was not
prepared to leave the merchants to solve the problem by them¬
selves, although they had served him extremely well in 1802; he
thought it would be cheaper simply to take their advice, which
he did, free of charge, in early February, and then to set up a
“comite des subsistances”. But this experiment failed, because
action could only be taken by a majority decision and no one
individual was prepared to carry responsibility. Besides, the real
expert in famine, Ouvrard, was sitting behind bars.
  It was one of the most satisfying moments in Ouvrard’s life
when the government, after three months of fruitless delibera¬
tions, was finally compelled at the end of April to turn to him
for help, even although he was in prison. There was, however,
no suggestion of his release; he was merely asked for his advice,
free of charge. It was an unusual situation, yet Ouvrard behaved
with remarkable dignity. When the Prefect of the Paris Police,
Pasquier, appeared in person to explain the desperate straits in
which the government found itself, Ouvrard did not give him a
cool reception or even make conditions but, perhaps from
vanity, expressed his views on the situation unreservedly. The
following day, 30th April, in a memorandum, he once more gave
a detailed exposition of the whole supply system which he had
followed since the crisis of 1802. In 1802 the blockade had made
normal trade in grain impossible. Food supplies had therefore to
be treated as if they were public funds, which meant that the
demand could only be met in full if there was a reserve. There¬
fore, in this new emergency depots should be set up around Paris,
from which any unforeseen demand could be met by releasing
on to the Paris market specific quantities prescribed by the Prefect
of Police. The size of the reserve stocks would vary according
to the harvest: after a full harvest supplies for four months would
        The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   121

be stored, after a two-thirds harvest supplies for eight months,
after a half-harvest for twelve months. This was the pattern
Ouvrard had followed during his eight years’ contract; the
army Commissariat, on the other hand, had based its distribution
on a day-to-day basis. As a result, the crisis which had arisen
could only be overcome by importing supplies from abroad.
Ouvrard expressed his readiness to explain in greater detail how
the ban on imports could be surmounted. In giving this verbal
assurance, Ouvrard doubtless remembered how, in 1804, the
mere announcement that supplies were being imported had
magically changed the whole situation by bringing out hoarded
stocks on to the market at rapidly falling prices.
  Napoleon apparently decided not to take this last-minute
advice. A week later he cut the Gordian knot. Instead of tackling
the problem as one of supply and demand, he simply issued a
decree on 8th May fixing the price of grain at thirty-three francs
a hectolitre. If this was not enough, then police truncheons would
do the rest. In fact, at this particular time—the end of April and
beginning of May—Napoleon was much too busy with other
things. For it was 1812, the Emperor’s year of destiny. And
Napoleon himself, having decided to risk a Russian campaign,
was more than usually restless; he had a feeling that now was the
moment to stake everything on a single throw. The day after the
grain decree was issued, on 9th May 1812, Napoleon left Paris for
the Russian frontier.
  It is a striking testimony to Ouvrard’s foresight that, even
behind his prison bars, he should have sensed how dangerous
this Russian campaign was, and that he was one of the very
few, if not the only one, to lay his finger on the weakest point.
His first reaction was undoubtedly that of an impotent onlooker,
which for a man of his vitality and pride must have been tre¬
mendously frustrating. He simply could not understand why the
provisioning of an army of such unparalleled size should not
have been entrusted to him, for he alone was equal to it. And it
was beyond his comprehension that others should earn so many
millions while he had to stand idly by, his hands almost literally
tied behind his back. In spite of his strong personal interest,
122                          Ouvrard

however, for the fatally weak point in the Russian campaign
touched him professionally, the words of warning Ouvrard sent
to Napoleon had an almost prophetic ring about them. On 6th
August 1812, several weeks after Napoleon had begun his long
march, Ouvrard decided to point out not only the dangers to
which the Emperor was exposing himself but also ways and
means of avoiding them.
   “The English,” he said, “believe that the forces of nature and
local difficulties are the only weapons which can be used against
the Emperor. Therefore they want to lure him away from his
own lands, thus waging war without fighting battles. This new
system can be rendered ineffectual if all contingencies are pro¬
vided against. What are these hazards after all? The climate? Not
as long as the men are kept well clothed and well fed. Or dis¬
tances? Not as long as supply lines are maintained.”
   Ouvrard then worked out in detail the safeguards which he
thought were necessary and without which, as it turned out, the
whole gigantic enterprise was to collapse. The first was the pur¬
chase (not the requisitioning) of an enormous stock of grain;
for the divisions stationed west of Vilna silos would be set up,
especially in the Baltic area, for supplies would reach them
quickly and cheaply by the sea route; the troops on the march
would be supplied from special depots established at regular
intervals along the advance-route and adequately protected.
   Ouvrard sat in the Sainte-Pelagie prison waiting for an
answer, but none came. He never discovered if Napoleon, who
by this time had advanced beyond Posen, had even seen the
plans. But, if he did so, the Emperor probably decided that the
supply-depots in Warsaw, Modlin, Thorn, Bromberg, Marien-
burg, Marienwerder and Danzig were perfectly adequate, and
that the 300,000 cwt. of flour and two million packets of dry
biscuits in Danzig alone represented an enormous reserve. In
any case, even if he had recognized the value of Ouvrard’s
plans, it would have been too late. He could never have resigned
himself to further months of preparation; his sole thought was to
march and fight. But events showed that Ouvrard’s predictions
were completely accurate. The reserves of grain which Napoleon
        The Great Disaster: the Anglo-French Peace Negotiations   123

had built up were hopelessly inadequate for an army of more
than half a million men, and even the supplies available near the
bases could not be properly distributed because of lack of horses.
The Emperor, in fact, had once again adopted his old system of
supplying the army largely from the territories he conquered.
But on this occasion all such supplies were either removed or
destroyed. Very soon, surprisingly soon, a factor which Napoleon
had hitherto taken for granted became uppermost in his mind:
food. The hollow cry, “Food, food”, echoed across the vast,
frozen Russian steppes. Then came disaster. On 5 th December
1812 Napoleon began his flight home, abandoning his loyal but
defeated army. Behind him he left chaos and a quarter of a
million men dying the most horrible of deaths. Napoleon was
oppressed by the knowledge that ten out of sixteen million francs
in the Imperial Treasury had been lost in the retreat.
   Even if Ouvrard’s ideas had been adopted and carried out to
the last detail, defeat would probably not have been averted.
But the accuracy with which Ouvrard had predicted in his
memorandum that the dangers were the climate and the problem
of supply, showed yet again how foolish Napoleon had been to
refuse the advice of someone so far-sighted and so rich in ideas,
instead of making use of him.
   On 18th December 1812 the Emperor arrived back in Paris.
The circumstances of his return gave Ouvrard little satisfaction.
Napoleon was still so widely feared and, moreover, at that
moment his fate seemed so closely linked with that of France, that
his power did not seem to be in any serious danger. So Ouvrard
remained almost another year in Sainte-Pelagie; not until October
1813, after the Emperor’s authority had suffered a mortal blow
on the battlefield of Leipzig, did the gates of his prison open.
He was escorted to the Chief of Police, the Due de Rovigo, who
later referred to him in the most friendly terms in his memoirs.
On the pretext of a serious illness, which was described in the
official document as a digestive disorder, Ouvrard was released
prematurely and conducted by a police officer to his home.
  Ouvrard was, in a way, the victor. He had served only three
and a half years of his five years’ sentence in the debtors’ prison;
124                           Ouvrard

he had spent his period of imprisonment in the most agreeable
conditions, with comfortable quarters and plenty of diversion;
and he had not paid the government a single franc. Vanlerberghe,
Desprez and Seguin had paid for their weak-minded compromise
by losing all their money, while Ouvrard, the most remarkable
of all the bankrupts, had kept his hidden millions intact.
  For the time being he had to report regularly to the Chief of
Police and he was not allowed to leave the capital. But within
six months the hour of his great adversary had struck. At the
end of March 1814, when the Allies reached the outskirts of Paris,
Ouvrard was nowhere to be found. He had taken advantage of
the general confusion to pack his bags, and an hour before the
Cossacks rode down the Champs Elysees he had left for an
“unknown destination”.
   In fact he retired to an estate near Orleans, which was a symbol
of his victory over the Emperor, for it was one of several pro¬
perties which he had managed to keep out of Napoleon’s clutches.
                                 Eight




              The Banker of the Hundred Days




ON his estate near Orleans, Ouvrard had time enough to
reflect on the incredible turn events had taken. The colossus
had fallen. The man against whom he had fought so bitterly for
fifteen years, the unconquerable, who had forced him down step
by step from his former eminence, was now himself brought
low, deprived of all authority, and could count himself fortunate
merely to have been banished to Elba. Ouvrard, on the other
hand, had preserved his life, his freedom and the remains of his
fortune intact, and, although he had suffered a tremendous set¬
back in his profession, he was nevertheless free to try to regain
his old position.
   Ouvrard’s deep sense of satisfaction was increased when he
received a message from Talleyrand calling on him to return to
Paris. Early in April 1814 he was back in the capital. Here he
found an unexpected and unaccustomed task awaiting him: to
prepare for the return of the exiled Bourbons. Talleyrand in
person appealed to Ouvrard for his assistance.* By Ouvrard’s
own account he agreed with an alacrity which was matched only
by the shortage of time available.
   Ouvrard’s readiness to co-operate was undoubtedly sincere.
He had everything to hope for from the new regime: peace
abroad and stability at home. Peace would help trade and internal
stability would foster credit. The latter was especially important:

   * Talleyrand also made Ouvrard go to meet the Comte d’Artois—the
future Charles X—at Livry shortly before the ceremonial entry into Paris
on the 12th April.—T.A.B.C.
                                  125
126                            Ouvrard

“Treasures of gold and silver do not last for ever; the only-
treasure which is never exhausted is credit.” So Ouvrard had
good reason to welcome the restoration of the Bourbons, who,
in his view, would favour a peaceful, enduring and stable system
of government.
  Uppermost in Ouvrard’s mind was, of course, the determination
to play an active role in the new regime. The prospects were
good: Louis XVIII’s main problem, the state of the country’s
finances, was also close to Ouvrard’s heart. True, the Bourbons
found several hundred million francs in the National Treasury
alone, and tax revenue for 1814 was still to come, but the King
was heavily in debt and there were still many unpaid bills left by
Napoleon. Ouvrard adopted the same method of exploiting this
situation as he had adopted so often before. Without waiting to
be asked, and completely “a fonds perdu \ he had submitted a
finance plan to the Revolutionary Committee of Public Safety,
to the Directory, to the Consulate and to the Emperor Napoleon;
once more, in May 1814, he produced a memorandum which he
had printed and bound at his own expense.
  He began with a profound obeisance to the Royal Throne.
He addressed himself to the “good King”, who “has been recalled
by France to the throne of his fathers”, to the “enlightened
Prince” and “wise ruler”. The King, Ouvrard hoped, would
also lend an ear to what he had to say, for “all Frenchmen who,
by their experience, are in a position to inform the government”,
had a duty and a right to help fulfil the three main objects: to
secure peace externally, to set the internal administration in order,
and to restore financial stability and credit. The necessary supply
of money—and here Ouvrard began to display his wares—must
not be provided, as in the time of the Revolution and the Direc¬
tory, by an inflationary issue of assignats or mandats, by con¬
fiscation and public bankruptcy. He also condemned Napoleon’s
methods, which consisted of monopolies and excessive taxes at
home, with requisitioning and forced levies abroad. Now that a
new government had taken over, the monetary system could be
put in order in the only sensible way, namely, with the help of
credit. This meant, in plain terms, a loan which would be repaid
                   The Banker of the Hundred Days             127

over a specific period—eight to ten years—from the annual
State revenue. With this loan the State would gradually be able
to pay off its debts of between 800 and 1,000 million francs,
on which a moratorium would be declared. In consequence, the
entire loan would merely consist of entries in the State ledgers,
these entries to be offered, for the time being, to the nation’s
creditors in lieu of cash. In this way both the nation and its
creditors would be safeguarded. An essential condition was,
of course, that the budget, which Ouvrard went on to discuss in
detail, should also be put in order.
  Understandably enough, the King’s Finance Minister was not
greatly impressed by this memorandum. He had no intention of
allowing “unauthorized persons” to interfere in his department,
was annoyed by Ouvrard’s reference to a “miserable budget”,
and finally managed to shelve any official discussion of the new
proposals, although a parliamentary commission had been set
up to consider them. As things turned out, this represented a
considerable material loss to the State, for a few years later
Ouvrard’s proposals had to be revived. The loss to Ouvrard
himself was strictly immaterial, for a few months later, on 1st
September 1814, he found another, highly profitable, opening:
he went into partnership with Doumerc. In other words, the
entire State supplies of foodstuffs and fodder were once more
in his hands. It was a remarkable and difficult achievement.
Officially he was still a bankrupt and he did not have sufficient
liquid capital at his disposal for such a major operation. But he
was nothing if not resourceful. Once again he demonstrated the
truth of his basic principle that credit makes money. Hopes in
Amsterdam financed him and provided cover for his business
transactions abroad. In this case Ouvrard was more fortunate
than he was with the peculiar Russian deal of 1814. Talleyrand,
without informing the King, discussed with Ouvrard a plan to
keep—and supply—30,000 of the Russian occupation troops
in Paris for several years. The plan fell through as soon as the
King got wind of it for he realized that this was the surest way
for enemies to undermine his power.
  Before long, however, there was no need of a Russian occupa-
128                          Ouvrard

tion to undermine the position of the Bourbons. They themselves
saw to that. Since 1789 they had either learned nothing or for¬
gotten everything they had learned. The nation’s finances were
treated just as irresponsibly as before, although it should have
been clear by then that England, the real conqueror of Napoleon,
could not have carried on the struggle to the end, had she not
possessed an extremely sound monetary system. The unpopular
indirect taxes were retained, something that might have been
excusable if the Bourbons before the restoration had not promised
to remove them. The government could not make up its mind
to confirm ownership of properties which had been taken from
Royalists by the Revolution and sold. Orphanages were abolished
but colleges for the children of nobility were established. There
was a mass demobilization, as a result of which more than 22,000
officers were placed on the reserve list or sent home. In the
growing discontent, one man only was satisfied: Napoleon.
  On 26th February 1815, barely a year after he had been
deposed, Napoleon escaped from Elba. Everywhere he was
given an enthusiastic welcome. Four weeks later, on 20th March,
he re-entered the Tuileries without a shot having been fired.
Louis XVIII had fled the night before. Ouvrard, in common with
all the others who had assumed that the new House of Bourbon
would last and had made their peace with it, now had to decide
whether to remain or to flee with the “good King”. Characteristic¬
ally, he stayed behind. Once again he resolved to take the bull
by the horns. Not that he stood in any great danger. As he said,
he was no politician but a merchant, who must be permitted to do
business with whoever could make it worth his while. Indeed,
the only serious risk lay in flight, for if Napoleon were to
remain in power it would be extremely difficult for him to return
and resume business.
  So Ouvrard remained. Suppressing any personal sense of
animosity, which he never allowed to interfere with his work,
he immediately set about making himself indispensable to the
new ruler. He reckoned that, if he stood by the Emperor in this
hour of need, then he could surely count upon his lasting friend¬
ship thereafter. After all that had already passed between these
Broker on the Paris Bourse. Coloured woodcut by Guilbaut. (Otto IVolff
