                      The Banker of the Hundred Days                      129

two adversaries, after the way in which Napoleon, partly out of
vindictiveness, partly out of stupidity, had brought the whole of
Ouvrard’s lifework down about his ears, any such hope or
speculation on Ouvrard’s part may seem naive. But for the
moment Napoleon was really a different man. During his period
in Elba he had learned a number of lessons. Whereas before he
had always put the blame on others, at Grenoble, on his way to
Paris, he solemnly declared that he had been too addicted to war
and fame, that in future he would serve the cause of peace and
indeed of freedom, and that his ambition was to be the first
citizen rather than the sovereign of France. He would give the
people a liberal constitution. He now had need of people who
would give him advice and not just reports.
   Ouvrard lost no time in appearing at the Tuileries. In his
memoirs he said that Napoleon summoned him. At this first
encounter Ouvrard adopted an approach, which had not pro¬
duced the desired impression on Napoleon in the past but which
could not fail at such a critical time. He surprised Napoleon with
a question which had welcome implications: “Do you need
money?” There was nothing Napoleon needed more and an
agreement was immediately concluded for a loan of fifty million
francs in cash to be paid within twenty days. Ouvrard knew he
would have no difficulty in finding the money. He was in a
position to dictate terms, and the terms provided the money.
Firstly, the government was to pay immediately the fifteen
million francs which the State owed the firm of Doumerc, of
which Ouvrard was the real proprietor. Secondly, as security
in exchange for the 50 million, Ouvrard received five million of
consolidated rentes. Thirdly, the issue-price of the stocks was to
be fifty, regardless of the market rate.*
   * The French investor buys rentes as an annuity and therefore expresses
their value according to the interest they yield rather than by their face
value. His British counterpart, on the other hand, always refers to A100
nominal of two and a half per cent Consolidated Stock (for example) as
£100, never as the value of the interest (in this case £2 ioj.) to be obtained
from it. In the present deal, therefore, Ouvrard was getting an equivalent
in 5 per cent rentes to the fifty million francs of cash he was providing. At
the market price of fifty on which he insisted, he obtained 100 millions
nominal, which are here described as five millions.
    K
130                           Ouvrard

   The deal was satisfactory for both parties. The Emperor
received the money he so badly needed, while Ouvrard made a
substantial profit on the exchange. He maintained that the stocks
were quoted at no more than 53 on the market, but, according to
others, the rate varied between 63£ and 67I; it certainly seems
safe to assume that it was no lower than at the end of 1815 when
it stood at 57 or 58 francs. This was the first time Ouvrard had
dealt in government securities. Previously one had only general
reports of his stock exchange activities, which frequently in¬
volved him in appalling losses. But his first major deal in govern¬
ment securities was so promising that one understands why, from
then on, he always fell back on this form of investment, which pro¬
vided enough room for speculation but also an element of stability.
   From what one knows of Ouvrard—particularly the prepara¬
tions he made for the Spanish venture with Barbe-Marbois—
this loan, for all the handsome profit it yielded, was merely a
first step towards something quite different. Ouvrard wras too
well acquainted with Napoleon and the international situation
not to know that war was inevitable. The Allies, who had begun
to fall out with one another at the Congress of Vienna, had been
reunited by Napoleon’s return to the scene and were marching
in unison against him. On the French side the Purveyor General,
in other words the man responsible for all supplies on land and
water, was—Ouvrard. He had waited fifteen years for this
appointment, fifteen years to regain a position of which Napoleon
had deprived him in January 1800. The Emperor, on the other
hand, had certainly not decided to entrust him with supplies in
order that Ouvrard could carry out his latest plan of meeting
the cost of the war in enemy territory by general taxation instead
of the arbitrary and ruthless system of requisitioning. However
admirable Ouvrard’s plan may have been, those critical months
were no time for such experiments. Ouvrard was made respon¬
sible for supplies because there was no one else who could be
trusted to do the job efficiently.
  So, on 18th June 1815, Ouvrard was present on the fateful
battlefield of Waterloo. Even for him it was a painful experience,
for it brought the official status, for which he had fought so
                   The Banker of the Hundred Days                 131

skilfully, to an untimely end. Ouvrard’s dream had also lasted
a mere hundred days. But at least he tried to give it decent
burial. If Ouvrard is to be believed, Napoleon asked him to
give him several millions against guaranteed rentes and to take
custody of his personal valuables. Ouvrard refused, because he
might thereby have placed himself in a dangerous position. It is
not unlikely that Napoleon still had die one-and-a-half millions
in gold, which he had deposited before the Russian campaign on
the estate of his Minister of Postal Services, Count Lavalette.
But Ouvrard had no hesitation in hiding Napoleon’s brother
Jerome, the King of Westphalia, on his brother’s estate at
Domy (although he had not been particularly well treated by
any member of the family). He also allowed Colonel Labedoyere,
one of the first to join Napoleon after his escape from Elba, to hide
in his own house in Paris and gave him money to make what turned
out to be a tragically unsuccessful attempt to escape to America.
  Gestures of this kind, some of which became public knowledge,
were naturally not likely to recommend Ouvrard to the new
government. If he believed that he would at least be left in
peace, he was mistaken. The Reactionary movement spread
throughout the country. At times the royalists were as barbarous
as the Jacobins had been. Some supporters of Napoleon or of
the Republic were lynched. Marshal Ney and Colonel Labedoyere
were executed; the wife of Lavalette, the Minister, who had organ¬
ized her husband’s escape from the Conciergerie, was held so
long in prison that she lost her reason. And the Finance Minister
lost no time in demanding a refund of certain sums which Napo¬
leon had paid.
   This method was clearly designed to take revenge on such
people as Ouvrard. The Finance Minister had his own private
reasons for taking this step, for he was the Abbe Louis, Baron
by the grace of Napoleon. Abbe Louis had not forgotten the
reception he was given by Hopes in 1806, when Napoleon sent
him to Amsterdam to seize Ouvrard’s assets. Nor had he pre¬
sumably forgotten that Ouvrard, a year before, had described
his budget as “miserable”. Abbe Louis had coined the phrase
that there was no one in France powerful enough to receive
132                           Ouvrard

Ouvrard with impunity. He was, therefore, delighted to find
himself at last in a position to hit back at Ouvrard. He gave in¬
structions for the re-transfer of the five million francs in rentes,
which had been registered in Ouvrard’s name, the pretext being
that all the necessary formalities had not been observed in the
original transfer and that the profits from it had been too high.
It transpired, however, that the securities, in so far as they had
been paid out, had long since changed hands. The Finance
Minister, whose methods were somewhat unorthodox, tracked
down the new owners and, in order to force them to press
Ouvrard for compensation, imposed a general ban on the official
transfer of debt claims. This immediately created chaos on the
Bourse. The brokers rebelled against this preposterous ban and
the whole money market closed down. Ouvrard went in fury
to Talleyrand, the Prime Minister, who could not believe that his
Finance Minister had been guilty of such extraordinary behaviour.
Abbe Louis, with a sour face, withdrew his decree but he did
succeed in preventing Ouvrard from laying his hands on those
securities, originally worth 1,500,000 francs, which had not yet
been transferred. But, as Ouvrard had already sold them, although
they had not been formally handed over, he was now obliged
to compensate the purchaser at a higher exchange rate, and for
this purpose he had to find 2,500,000 francs.
  Thus the unfortunate Ouvrard had hardly succeeded in
settling all his differences with Napoleon when this financial
bureaucrat blocked his path. Abbe Louis’s talents were not much
above average and he had neither the breadth nor the keenness
of vision of Ouvrard, but he was the Minister and he was in
power. For the moment at least, Ouvrard could only bide his
time till the opportunity arose for him to take his revenge. And
the opportunity did arise.
  Throughout his period of office Abbe Louis was saddled with
a deficit, which he could not reduce, of 800 million francs. The
ultimate humiliation for the Finance Minister came when it was
eventually discovered that Ouvrard alone could devise a plan
whereby the government’s debts would be paid and the occupa¬
tion of the country brought to a premature end.
                               Nine




 The Great Achievement: a Merchant Frees the Nation




 The second Treaty of Paris was signed in November 1815.
The main problems now facing France were financial prob¬
lems, and of these one was uppermost in the minds of the public:
how to remove the burden of reparations and pay the occupation
costs. In the first Treaty of Paris the Allies, with what then may
seem incredible magnanimity, had waived their claims to war
 damages. But Napoleon’s return had necessitated the elaborate
preparation of a second campaign against France, and the Allies
were no longer in the mood to write off the debt which amounted
to 700 million francs. In view of what had happened, it was
decided to leave an army of 150,000 men on French soil, till
“peace and order” had been restored. The maximum period of
occupation was fixed at five years, the minimum at three. France
was to be responsible for the maintenance of the occupying
army, which amounted—at three francs per head per day—to
some 150 million a year. At the same time, the various Allied
governments demanded reparations totalling about 500 million.
The Bourbons had to meet outstanding internal debts of 650
million left over from the Napoleonic period. This made a
grand total of some 2,000 million francs, which had to be
found, quite apart from the money needed to run the country.
Moreover, the value of the franc inside France was still very high
in 1815.
  Even with someone more ingenious than Abbe Louis in charge
of its finances, the French Government would have been faced
                               133
134                           Ouvrard

with an almost insoluble problem. Where the money was to
come from was a complete mystery, as the public coffers were
empty and the screw of taxation had already been turned too
far. The possibility of floating an internal loan by issuing govern¬
ment securities seemed, to say the least, unlikely, as no one would
subscribe to it and the insolvency of France and her government
would merely be confirmed. The sole result would be that the
previous issue, which in any case had lost 50 per cent of its value
on the Bourse, would become mere waste-paper. So what was
obviously needed was some expedient whereby repayment of
debts could be deferred, but no one could think of a formula.
Abbe Louis created havoc in the world of commerce by trying
to impose a forced loan on large private fortunes, but it was soon
clear that this economically unsound principle would produce a
mere 100 million francs, which was only enough to cover the
support costs of the occupation troops for eight months.
  In France the year 1815 ended in an atmosphere of acute
depression. Only one man in Paris was still as optimistic as
ever—Ouvrard. He had reason to be. At a time when all the
leading men in the country were in a state of despair, because
they could see no way out of what seemed a hopeless situation,
Ouvrard was quite confident that he knew the solution. He was
convinced that, if France followed his advice, she would not
only be able to pay off these debts but to do so within a very
short time.
  Ouvrard’s ideas, which subsequently turned out to be per¬
fectly practicable, would probably never have been realized if
Abbe Louis, his personal enemy, had remained Finance Minister.
For he would rather have declared the State bankrupt than accept
help from Ouvrard. But Baron Louis disappeared in September
1815 with the Talleyrand administration. During the Vienna
Congress, in other words before Napoleon’s return from Elba,
Talleyrand had been working for an alliance between Austria,
England and France against Russia. The indignation this had
aroused in Alexander hardly made Talleyrand the ideal man to
co-operate with the Allies in liquidating what was left of Napo¬
leon’s empire. His place was taken by the Due de Richelieu.
            The Great Achievement: a Merchant Frees the Nation   135

Talleyrand remarked mockingly, and not without reason, that
it was an excellent choice, as there was no one in France who
knew more about the Crimea. For Richelieu was a complete
newcomer to politics and, indeed, a politician against his will.
Political activity of any kind had always been anathema to him.
Yet now, because his country called him, he was compelled “to
fling himself into this frightful abyss of passion, vice and cor¬
ruption”.
  It is, in fact, true to say that he could not ignore the call, for
he was the type of great leader who so often appears almost from
nowhere in a nation’s hour of need. Contemporary cartoonists
did him an injustice when they showed him walking on stilts in
an effort to correct his natural leanings. If the King chose to call
upon Richelieu, it was because he was extremely highly respected
abroad. He had spent twenty-five of his forty-eight years outside
France, fought with the Russian army against the Turks, and,
while serving as Governor of the Crimea, founded the modern
city of Odessa, where, on Alexander’s instructions, a bust of him
was erected with the words: “In honour of Richelieu from a
grateful Odessa.” There seemed reason to hope that this man
with the bluest of blood in his veins and a wide horizon acquired
from his travels would give outstanding service both at home
and abroad and that he was particularly well equipped to do
justice both to the Crown and to a liberal parliament. The more
so, as his excellent qualities were widely recognized: his tolerance
and   simplicity,   his   charitable   disposition and incorruptible
character, his nobility and his moderation were universally
acknowledged.
   Ouvrard naturally found no difficulty in gaining access to
someone with such an open mind and to his Finance Minister,
Count Corvetto. Richelieu’s profound sense of duty was in
itself a guarantee that even the ideas of a merchant without any
official status would be given a careful hearing. Ouvrard’s plans
certainly called for the utmost forbearance: in the context of 1815
they must have seemed fantastic. Ouvrard was riding his old
hobby-horse: “Taxes kill, debts stimulate.” His plan was essen¬
tially the same as the proposals he had submitted time and again
136                           Ouvrard

 to the Revolutionary Committee of Public Safety, the Directory
 and the government of Napoleon: the establishment of a very
 large National Debt, a very long-term loan, which would be
secured by an independent Sinking Fund. The present, said
 Ouvrard, could only be safeguarded by mortgaging the future.
These principles seemed, at first sight, particularly unrealistic,
because the government had already tried, without much success,
 to float a loan on the Paris market. But Ouvrard was given an
 opportunity to elaborate his plan at a Cabinet meeting.
 He argued that:
    A State which cannot raise credit cannot develop.
    A nation can raise credit only if it pays its debts.
    The war debts must, therefore, be paid and as soon as possible.
    As there was no ready money available and taxation to provide
       the required amount was not feasible, to borrow was the
      only alternative.
    Domestic loans were, for the time being, unobtainable; the
       foreign debts must, therefore, be paid with foreign money.
    Foreign money could be obtained by issuing French govern¬
      ment bonds, in other words, against loan certificates, but
      only if this National Debt was secured by a Sinking Fund.
    The Sinking Fund could draw on revenue from State forests
       and eventually on slightly increased taxation.
    This rapid liquidation of war debts, according to Ouvrard,
would immediately improve the State’s credit at home and so
automatically increase the exchange rate of the old government
bonds. At the same time, a run would begin in France on the new
issue as soon as foreign buying started. In this way France would
build up a “truly national public debt”, in which individual
citizens would actively participate.

   Ouvrard’s plan was received with the utmost bewilderment.
It seemed at that particular time highly paradoxical that money
should be found abroad to pay foreign debts. Furthermore, it was
hard to imagine what foreign buyers would be attracted by the
new issue, which would run into thousands of millions. Ouvrard’s
answer was that the Allies themselves were to buy the entire
           The Great Achievement: a Merchant Frees the Nation        137

issue, and, in fact, without any reservations concerning changes
in the market price; moreover, the Allied governments would
recognize the issue of the bonds in itself as tantamount to pay¬
ment. This last condition made Ouvrard’s plan seem even more
astonishing. It was inconceivable that die Allies, who were short
of money themselves, would accept bonds issued by a State
which was nearly bankrupt and which could give no sort of
guarantee that the market price of the loan issue would remain
stable. So the Allies would have to carry the entire risk. Further¬
more, it was almost certain that, as soon as they offered a large
consignment of these bonds for sale, their value would drop well
below die market price.
   Ouvrard was not in the least put out by such objections,
which he had already foreseen. He had worked out four safe¬
guards, which would protect the Allies against all risks. Firstly,
the issue must be large enough to cover all the reparations, thus
freeing France of all outstanding obligations and gradually
reviving international confidence in her; her stock would then
rise, not fall. Secondly, the stability of the market quotation
could, to some extent, be guaranteed by the size of the inde¬
pendent sinking fund, because every buyer could then be certain
of receiving payment in full, when it was due. Thirdly, the Allies,
for their part, could stabilize the price by not immediately selling
any of the bonds. Fourthly, when they were eventually sold,
it would be not by the governments themselves but by a bank
experienced in international finance and able to place major
stocks skilfully on the market.
   So the success of Ouvrard’s plan depended, in the final
analysis, on finding a firm able and willing to carry a tremendous
responsibility of this kind, and which enjoyed the confidence of
the Allied governments. There was no doubt in Ouvrard’s mind
that the only possible candidate was the firm of Baring Brothers
of London, at that time the biggest financial concern in the
world. In 1816 its head was Alexander Baring,* son of Francis
Baring, who, before his death in 1810, had turned the family

   * Alexander Baring’s grandfather had emigrated from Bremen, where his
father was a Lutheran preacher.
138                          Ouvrard.

firm of cloth manufacturers into a banking-house. The pheno¬
menal expansion of Barings under Alexander was due largely to
the fact that, while studying in America, he ignored the only
two pieces of advice given him by his father: not to buy unculti¬
vated land and not to marry. He bought 100,000 dollars’ worth
of land in Pennsylvania and married a woman who not only
bore him nine children but eventually inherited almost a million
dollars from her father.
   Ouvrard had connections with Alexander Baring, for the
leading figure in Hopes, P. C. Labouchere, had married Alex¬
ander’s sister. The English firm had also been involved, together
with Hopes, in the Mexican deal, planned by Ouvrard. More¬
over, Alexander himself, who served part of his apprenticeship
with Hopes before going to America, must have had business
dealings with Ouvrard.
   So there was nothing fantastic about Ouvrard’s proposal to
make his whole scheme dependent on the participation of such a
powerful, far-sighted and respected figure. The English firm was
interested. But the time was not yet ripe; the French Finance
Minister, Count Corvetto, had not Richelieu’s courage, and the
Allies were not yet in the right frame of mind. They still believed
that they would get their money without lifting a finger them¬
selves and, when it did not materialize, blamed the French for
the wastefulness of their national budget.
   Only with the passage of time and under pressure of events
did they begin to see reason. For another year and more, how¬
ever, as it became more and more difficult to get the French to
pay, the Allies threatened to increase their occupation forces and
even to occupy whole provinces of France in lieu of reparations.
“The French,” it was said, “will never change.” The French,
for their part, continued to complain of the “excessive demands”
made by the other side, and there was growing “dissatisfaction
throughout the entire country over the enormous reparations
and forced levies, which imposed a much too heavy burden on
the nation”. (Memoirs.) Finally the point was reached where
the Treasury in Paris was completely empty and where there
seemed no possibility of even paying the current occupation
          The Great Achievement: a Merchant Frees the Nation     139

costs. The leading French Ministers realized that this was bound
to produce tremendous political repercussions. To begin with,
they looked round once more for a stopgap. The bankers were
summoned but again gave a negative reply. Then Ouvrard was
asked for a loan of at least sixty million francs, but, while he was
quite prepared to make another deal with the government, he
had even fewer illusions about the facts of the situation than
the Ministers. His reply was couched in typically bold terms: “No
one can raise sixty million for the government’s present needs;
but it would be child’s play to raise a thousand million to enable
 the government to meet all the Allied demands.” Ouvrard was
 back on his old course.
    Meanwhile, the position of France had become so critical that
the government had no alternative but to adopt Ouvrard’s plan.
He was sent to London to discuss the situation with Barings.
Together with Labouchere he spent a weekend at Alexander
Baring’s estate outside London. The plan which Ouvrard put
forward was still essentially the same: the Allies would take over
the new issue of French bonds at a fixed rate, Barings and Hopes
would be jointly responsible for underwriting it and would
later sell against a fixed commission. Developments in 1816
had made it essential that the issue of bonds should also cover the
occupation costs, and Ouvrard suggested to his colleagues that
they should advance him 160 million a year for this purpose and
should agree to accept repayment in the new French bonds.
   Alexander Baring and Labouchere agreed in principle but
insisted that any final settlement must depend upon the attitude
of the Allies and on the details of the transaction being acceptable
to them. This was all that Ouvrard wanted. The agreement in
principle of two such powerful financial concerns provided
Ouvrard with a lever which he could apply to the Allies. More¬
over, when he returned to Paris, he was given the contract—
again in the name of Doumerc—to supply all the occupation
forces. But, while this was by no means unimportant to Ouvrard,
the overriding fact was that he was the man on whose skill and
acumen the success of a great national mission now depended.
The Prime Minister, Richelieu, gave full powers to Ouvrard to
 140                        Ouvrard

  conduct alone the preliminary negotiations with the Allies oil
  the payment of war debts. Once again, as in the splendid Madrid
 period, he had become a State Commissioner, and it seemed
 once more that the ideas he had formed during his years of
 apprenticeship in Nantes of the businessman playing a major
 part in world affairs were to be borne out in practice.
    At the beginning of December Ouvrard set out to meet the
 man who had more say in a settlement with France than all the
 Allied ambassadors in Paris put together—Wellington. The Duke
 of Wellington, who had reached the rank of major-general at
 the age of thirty-four, and who by his Spanish victories paved
 the way for the eventual overthrow of Napoleon, was universally
regarded not only as a man of rare distinction but also as possess¬
ing a cool, calculating brain, in fact as a very shrewd negotiator.
If he could be won over to Ouvrard’s plan, then it would not be
difficult to get the Allies to accept it. And there seemed, on the
face of it, no reason why Wellington should not agree, for he
knew better than most how powerful, prudent and responsible
the firm of Barings was.
    When Ouvrard met the Duke at the Hotel Bellevue in Brussels,
on 5 th December 1816, he found him highly mistrustful and full
of resentment over the difficulties of supplying the occupation
army under his command. He threatened to increase the number
of troops. Ouvrard, who always favoured the direct approach in
any such discussion, suggested that, on the contrary, the occupa¬
tion forces should be reduced by a fifth; this would make it all
the easier to supply the remainder, would cut down France’s
public expenditure, improve her credit, so raising the market
price of rentes and improving the prospects for a fresh loan—
which the Allies en bloc would take over in repayment of war
debts. Ouvrard then explained in detail the plan he had worked
out with Baring and Hope, underlined the fact that they had
agreed in principle, and within two days, thanks to his extra¬
ordinary powers of persuasion, had overcome Wellington’s
suspicions and convinced him that there was no other way by
which the Allies could receive their money.
    The plan was now set in motion. Wellington despatched a
            The Great Achievement: a Merchant Frees the Nation           141

 courier to Castlereagh in London, suggesting that he confer
with Baring, Ouvrard had no sooner reported back to Paris
 than he was off to England again on a fresh mission. In London
he won the enthusiastic support of the French Ambassador, the
Marquis d’Osmond, who for a whole year had been fruitlessly
searching for a solution. Wellington himself returned home and
talked the Cabinet into giving the scheme its blessing. So one of
 Baring’s conditions was already in part fulfilled: that all the
Allied governments should give their unanimous approval.
Wellington travelled in person to Paris and stayed in Ouvrard’s
house in the Rue des Champs Elysees. On 8th January 1817 a
reparations conference opened in Ouvrard’s house, which was
attended by Wellington, Richelieu, the Finance Minister,
Corvetto and Ouvrard. The findings of this conference were
submitted to the Allied ambassadors, accepted by them and
immediately forwarded to the various Courts.
   In the meantime Ouvrard managed to entice Baring and
Labouchere to Paris, although they had told him that they were
not prepared to become involved in further negotiations till the
Allies and the French had reached a definite decision; he per¬
suaded the somewhat credulous Ambassador, d’Osmond, to
invite them to visit Richelieu, who was taken by surprise when
they arrived on 14th January. But Ouvrard’s stratagem worked,
for it was now possible to complete the final details and save a
considerable amount of time. The Allies were relieved to find
that there was at last a real prospect of being paid in full and they
gave their unreserved approval.* On 10th February the first
tranche of the loan was issued, a round nine million rentes
at an interest rate of 5 per cent and repayable at 100 francs a
   * Although Wolff does not specifically say so, the reminiscences of the
 Comtesse de Boigne (daughter of the French Ambassador in London,
d’Osmond) make it clear that Ouvrard employed the same kind of bluff
that he had used in the peace negotiations of 1810. He now speciously repre¬
sented to the other parties that Baring and Labouchere were prepared to
undertake the loan issues on terms acceptable to everyone when in fact the
bankers had not even discussed any such terms. When his bluff was even¬
tually called, it was Wellington and the Russian Ambassador Pozzo di

 142                                 Ouvrard

piece. The Allies bought the whole issue at a take-over of 52^
francs.*
   The operation was staggeringly successful. Everything turned
out exactly as Ouvrard had predicted, and Alexander Baring’s
precautions proved for the most part to be unnecessary. For as
soon as it became known that the loan issue was being purchased
abroad, and indeed by the Allied governments themselves,
France’s credit was fully restored. No sooner did people realize
that, for the time being at least, the bonds were not for sale, than
they were anxious to buy them. As a result there was a slow but
steady improvement in the price. The clearest indication of
Ouvrard’s success was the fact that Barings and Hopes, who
until then had firmly declined to invest in the loan themselves,
suddenly expressed an interest in taking over the entire issue
instead of merely acting as brokers for the Allied governments.
The scene at the Bourse was reminiscent of the South Sea Bubble.
All the streets leading to it were blocked and people literally
came to blows in their eagerness to obtain some of the new

   It will be noted that as the French Government’s credit steadily improved,

government annuities. The army had to be called in to restore
order.
   As further instalments were issued what had seemed the most
grotesque of Ouvrard’s prophecies came true, namely that as
the number of bonds increased, their value would go up. A
further eight million were issued in April at 55$ instead of 52J.
The July issue (nine million) rose to 6i\. By March 1818 the
issue price for two million had gone up to 64J, and when Barings
and Hopes issued the last twelve million, they started at 67. By
December 1818 they stood at 75.
   A complete transformation had been wrought in France’s
financial position. Whereas in 1816 the Treasury had only been
able to raise money on the domestic market at 15 to 20 per cent
per annum, in 1817 large funds were being offered voluntarily
at 4 to 5 per cent. As for the occupation forces, they had been
reduced by a fifth, bringing the support costs down by 20 per
cent.* The most remarkable achievement, however, was that
within an astonishingly short time most of the war debts had
been paid off, and France’s internal stability gave her a con¬
tractual right to demand the early departure of the occupation
forces.
   In September 1818 the Allied monarchs met in Aix-la-Chapelle
(or Aachen) to consider whether the occupation should be ended
after three instead of five years. As the King of Prussia, the
Emperor of Austria, the Tsar of Russia and finally the Due de
Richelieu arrived with full military pomp, the old imperial city
relived something of its glorious past, its thirty-six coronations
and its twenty-seven imperial assemblies. Yet to a casual visitor
the streets, which had been completely transformed, must have
suggested a trade fair rather than a political conference; there was
an impressive display of trinkets, china, jewellery, paintings,
tapestries and all kinds of other goods, while the centre of the
town was chock-a-block with Highnesses and Excellencies, with

  * Although the nominal rate of interest offered was 5 per cent, investors

Royal Prussian chamberlains and Imperial Russian generals and
Ministers. There was also a large camp-following of artists,
singers and painters, of English boxers and balloonists, all of
whom hoped to do good business. There were plenty of elegant
ladies like the beautiful Madame de Recamier, who went out
walking with Prince August of Prussia, and, as a contemporary
chronicler observed, the citizens of Aix-la-Chapelle “were sure
to catch a glimpse from time to time of the most illustrious
sovereigns, radiating geniality and charm, and to have an oppor¬
tunity of paying their due respects with childlike confidence”.
   Meanwhile, the Allied leaders had set to work behind closed
doors to try to clear up the last remaining problems. On 9th
October the Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle was signed:* Allied troops
would leave French soil by the end of November; Allied claims
still outstanding were fixed at 265 million francs, 100 of which
were paid at once to the Allied governments, in French rentes,
the remainder in bills on Hopes and Barings.|
   Ouvrard’s great plan had thus been fully realized. He himself
had little more to do in Aix-la-Chapelle than take the air and
arrange dinner-parties. But the importance of his contribution to
these negotiations, which were so vital to France, was reflected
in the presence of his business friends, Barings and Hopes, at all
stages of the discussions. “Was not the settlement of France’s
financial problems,” he enquired of his friends in Aix-la-Chapelle,
“at a time when the country was in danger of collapsing under the
sheer weight of its obligations, one of the greatest miracles which
we have experienced in an age so rich in miraculous events?”
   Ouvrard, one would have thought, had earned the gratitude of
the French Government in particular. For months he had worked
with endless patience and skill, not, of course, merely out of
enthusiasm for his scheme or out of patriotism, but because, like
Hop es and Barings, as a financier he was interested in making
