government bonds should be sold to him—against cash—at the
Allies’ issue price, so that he would at least receive some benefit
 from the improvement in the rate, which he had predicted. The
 Finance Minister, Count Corvetto, who had played only a minor
 role in the whole operation, refused this concession on the
 grounds that he could no longer remember having given Ouvrard
any verbal assurance. So once again Ouvrard emerged empty-
handed, for he had been relying upon the good faith of the
 government and had not claimed any commission from Barings.
 On the other hand, Barings and Hopes, according to one of their
employees, made a profit of eight million francs. In the circum¬
stances it was understandable that Ouvrard should complain
bitterly that the Finance Ministry had given him the most solemn
assurances as long as it needed him but had dropped him as soon
as he had done what was required of him.
    Ouvrard had occasion before long to use the same words in
quite another context. Since the fall of Napoleon, he and Vanler-
berghe had been pressing an old claim for 13,200,000 francs,
which, in their view, was still outstanding from the sixty-seven
million which they had spent up to 1804 on supplies for the
planned invasion of England. When Napoleon arbitrarily liquid¬
ated the Mexican undertaking in 1806, he had declared the
remainder of the debt null and void. Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe,
who had never accepted Napoleon’s declaration, eventually
succeeded in having their claim included in the French budgets of
1814-19. But when in 1819, the year following the Congress of
Aix-la-Chapelle, payment was finally demanded, the Minister
of Marine vetoed it. The case was brought before an administra¬
tive tribunal, not a proper court of justice, and the Minister’s
veto was upheld. The Cabinet, which was no longer headed by
Richelieu, confirmed the verdict and threw out the merchants’
claim. Vanlerberghe was completely ruined. Two days later he
was dead.
  Ouvrard, however, was still very much alive. A year later, in
146                          Ouvrard

July 1820, he submitted to Richelieu, who had returned to
office as Prime Minister, a new plan which provided for a re¬
duction in the property tax by 100 million francs. Before another
year had passed, in June 1821, he came forward with a plan for
developing France’s network of waterways and so stimulating
agricultural production. But in both cases luck was still against
him, for work to implement these schemes had hardly been
started when for the second and last time Richelieu laid down
the burden of office which he had carried so reluctantly. He
resigned.
                                  Ten




                     Aristocratic Relations




D    ESPITE the fact that for twenty years, ever since 18th
Brumaire, Ouvrard’s grandiose schemes had ended unsatis¬
factorily, even sometimes disastrously, the tireless optimism
with which he always made a fresh start was deeply tinged with
realism. Such was his versatility and his innate ability that he
never failed to discover a new avenue, which, suddenly and
surprisingly, opened up yet another horizon full of promise. So,
although in 1818 and 1819 he was denied enormous profits to
which he believed he had a moral and material right, it did not
occur to him to accept defeat.
   He now embarked on the kind of venture which had not so
far figured in his very varied repertoire. At the outset of his career
he had confined himself to purely commercial transactions. Then
he took to politics in order to expand what seemed to him a too
narrow sphere of operations. Finally, in 1820-21, he decided that
the time had come to make an investment of his family. His
daughter Elizabeth was twenty-five and therefore ripe for mar¬
riage. Social convention assumed that she would marry not for
love but as a deliberate act of policy. So Ouvrard behaved quite
properly when he began looking round for a son-in-law who
would further his business interests. Naturally it must be some¬
one of noble birth. For all Ouvrard’s professional self-assurance
and individual way of life, he was not without the sense of in¬
feriority of a humble bourgeois towards the old aristocracy. It
had given him a unique feeling of pride to entertain such celeb¬
rities as Madame de Montesson, morganatic widow of the Due
                                 147
148                           Ouvrard

d’Orleans, the King of Etruria and the Duke of Wellington in
his house. Moreover, in seeking a distinguished match for his
daughter, Ouvrard was merely following a long-established
tradition, for it had become the accepted practice, even under
the old regime, for men of noble family to marry the daughters of
successful merchants. The Intendant General of Taxes, Crozat,
even succeeded in marrying his daughter to a Bourbon, the
Comte d’Evreux. The Intendant General of Taxes, de Pondre,
became the father-in-law of a La Rochefoucauld. The daughter
of the Court banker, de Laborde, became Comtesse Noailles. In
Ouvrard’s own lifetime such alliances between money and blood
were common occurrences; Napoleon had specialized in them.
When General Junot, later Due d’Abrantes, asked to be allowed
to marry a certain Mademoiselle Permon, who had not a penny
to her name, on the grounds that all the eligible heiresses were as
ugly as maggots, the Emperor, who had become deeply indebted
to the Permon family during his youth, advised Junot to think
again and marry a rich woman. In 1805 he himself suggested to
General Rapp that he should marry Mademoiselle Vanlerberghe,
daughter of Ouvrard’s business colleague. Marshal Marmont,
Due de Ragusa, married the daughter of the banker Perregaux.
Mademoiselle Laffitte became Princesse de la Moskowa. Not that
all these men found much happiness with their rich wives.
General Rapp observed with telling brevity in his memoirs:
“Unfortunately the marriage was not a happy one.” Marshal
Marmont’s wife soon grew tired of being left so much on her own,
and, when the Due de Ragusa returned from the wars for good, he
was virtually driven out of his own house by his wife’s friends.
  Little is known either of Elizabeth Ouvrard’s physical attri¬
butes or of her moral qualities. But in 1820 it was quite enough,
despite the setbacks her father had suffered, that he could offer a
prospective son-in-law a dowry of a million francs. There was
no lack of suitors. The Spanish General, Joseph Palafox de Melzi,
was one who tried and failed. A betrothal with Raoul de Mont¬
morency had to be broken off, when the bearer of this dis¬
tinguished name found that, with the death of an uncle, he could
marry his aunt. But Ouvrard soon had reason to be more than
                        Aristocratic Relations                    149

grateful for this apparent reverse, when the opportunity presented
itself of entering into a family relationship with the Prime
Minister himself, the Due de Richelieu. This opened up entirely
new prospects, for, if Richelieu had hitherto given the most sym¬
pathetic hearing to Ouvrard’s plans, family ties would doubtless
make him even better disposed.
  The son-in-law, whom Ouvrard welcomed so warmly, was
the Comte Louis Victor Leon de Rochechouart, a nephew of
Richelieu, who had been taken as an orphan into the childless
Richelieu house and had become like a son to the Duke. At
one time he had been Richelieu’s adjutant, then adjutant to the
Tsar Alexander of Russia; in 1821, at the age of thirty-three, he
was Chamberlain to Louis XVIII. Before long he was appointed
Commander of the Paris garrison and Richelieu himself presented
him with a not inconsiderable part of his private fortune as a
marriage-gift.
  Quite apart from any business plans or prospects, Ouvrard
had every reason to feel extremely gratified by the marriage, for
it linked his family with one of the most illustrious in France.
The Rochechouarts could trace their ancestors back to the year
965, among them Athenals de Rochechouart, Marquise de
Montespan, who had managed to hold the affections of Louis XIV
for twelve years and who had borne him seven children. The
motto of the Rochechouart family, which Ouvrard’s son-in-law
still carried on his coat-of-arms, was a typically French device:
                                  ,
“Avant qus la mer fut au monde Rochechouart portoit les ondes      ”
(“Before the sea was created, Rochechouart carried the waves”).
  On 5 th January 1822 in St. Roche de Paris, in the presence of
the Due de Richelieu, the Comte de Rochechouart married the
daughter of Ouvrard, the merchant. At the grand levee that
followed, the King himself signed the marriage contract. (Later,
in his   memoirs,   Rochechouart paid        the following   tribute:
“Mademoiselle Elizabeth Ouvrard was very agreeable, without
being particularly beautiful; she pleased me well.” It is hard to
reconcile this dispassionate remark with the statement that he
was the happiest of all men.) Then “two years later everything
collapsed”. That was in 1824, the year in which Ouvrard lost
150                           Ouvrard

both his fortune and his reputation in a gigantic public scandal.
But at least the son-in-law had one satisfaction: the dowry of a
million francs had been paid in full. The scoffers who maintained
that Rochechouart had received a wife but Ouvrard had kept
the dowry were mistaken. Nevertheless, it was disconcerting for
a Rochechouart to have a father-in-law who was a bankrupt and
in prison.
   Meanwhile Richelieu had been inconsiderate enough to retire
from political life in the period between the betrothal and the
marriage. His deep-seated aversion to politics had grown rather
than lessened since 1818. He was afraid to return to Paris from
Aix-la-Chapelle because, after such a resounding success, there
seemed no prospect that the King would release him. And if he
remained in office a little longer, it was not his appointment as
Chevalier du Saint Esprit which kept him, nor the handsome
gift of a portrait of Louis XVI, incorporating a strand of Hugh
Capet’s hair, but simply the King’s appeal to his sense of duty. In
February 1820, when the Due de Berry was assassinated, he was
reluctantly compelled once again to answer his sovereign’s call.
But at the end of December 1821 the situation in France was calm,
and to Ouvrard’s dismay, who had woven so many plans around
him, Richelieu finally retired into private life. In the following
year, 1822, he died.
   Count Rochechouart’s career also failed to come up to
Ouvrard’s expectations. Before the wedding year was up, he was
out of favour for no apparent reason, and was suddenly relieved
of his post in Paris; he was given twenty-four hours to move out
of his service quarters, although his wife had gone into confine¬
ment only four days before. Some time later, in 1830, he took part
in the Algerian expedition as commander of a reserve brigade, but
thereafter, from the age of forty-four onwards, he was obliged to
live as a private citizen. He spent most of his time at his chateau
at Jumilhac, which he had bought with his wife’s dowry.
  So, owing to events over which he had no control, another
of Ouvrard’s ventures had failed. Fortune, who had smiled on
his early rise to power, seemed to have abandoned him. He was
fifty-two years old.
                              Eleven




      Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain




With the resignation and subsequent death of his new
kinsman, the Due de Richelieu, in 1822, Ouvrard was forced
to abandon a number of ambitious plans, especially his hopes
of renewing the old ties between his business interests and
a settlement of the Franco-Spanish differences. At a time when
fresh political crises were looming up, Ouvrard regarded such
a settlement as supremely important. Although sixteen years had
passed since Napoleon made him responsible guarantor for the
Spanish Government’s debts and took the precaution of con¬
fiscating his millions, Ouvrard had never given up hope of
recovering the money which he had paid so reluctantly. On the
other hand, he had little if any legal claim. It seemed to him, as
a businessman, that the Spaniards would feel no inclination to
make such a belated payment, unless he managed to interest the
Spanish Government, which had since completely changed, in
his present affairs.
  The situation looked promising in the years 1820-22, when the
Spanish Crown issued more and more urgent appeals for foreign
support against its own subjects. Spain was in the throes of a
revolution. Ferdinand VII, son of Charles IV, who had been
restored to the throne in 1814, had returned no wiser than his
Bourbon cousins in France and continued to rule as an absolute
monarch. The King and his lackeys reimposed a tyranny as harsh
as any that had gone before, stirred up class-strife, pampered the
nobility and the clergy and allowed the common people to starve.
 152                           Ouvrard

But the army was also neglected, and the first to revolt were the
 officers of some regiments due to sail from Cadiz to Central
America. The cry “Long live the constitution” echoed throughout
the country. As had happened in France, this was hailed as “the
first year of the constitution for the well-being of the people”;
the upper classes were divided into factions called “servile”
and “liberale”. The members of the Spanish assembly, the Cortes,
were so carried away by the army’s success that they forgot how
in 1789 (that momentous year!) they had fallen on their knees
before the King, when he appeared in parliament in person to
send the members home. Ferdinand VII might well complain
that times had changed, for this same Cortes now proceeded to
clip his wings, despite his sudden decision to recognize the Con¬
stitution of Cadiz drawn up by the rebels. The Cortes, on the
other hand, had reason to mistrust him, for the King tried again
and again to arrange foreign armed intervention. The remarkably
long-suffering revolutionaries allowed this to go on until the
European Great Powers actually began to take the Spanish
problem seriously.
   A complex political situation of this kind had an obvious
appeal for a man like Ouvrard. As a connoisseur of Spanish affairs
and an addicted gambler, he immediately saw enormous possi¬
bilities. The French were bound to take some action. Since the
defeat of Napoleon, Europe had been dominated by men of
exceedingly reactionary temperament who saw the Spanish
Revolution as a serious threat to the peace of Europe and its
restored monarchies. They were now faced with the necessity
of leaving the rebels in power or undertaking armed intervention.
  The only man who had a different and better solution to offer
was Ouvrard. Thanks to his intimate knowledge of the country,
he was in a position to interpret the news from Spain with con¬
siderable accuracy. He realized that this revolution, which was
causing so much anxiety throughout Europe, was very super¬
ficial. It was confined to certain influential circles and to a small
section of the army. The common people had never taken much
interest in the constitutional issue. The clergy believed that their
destiny was inextricably bound up with that of the monarchy—
                  Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain           153

a belief that was fortified by the occasional plundering of monas¬
teries by the rebels—and had ample opportunity to voice the
battle-cry of the royalists: “Long live the King and the Holy
Inquisition”.
  It was on this internal split in Spain that Ouvrard based his
plan. A full two years before the European governments finally
reached a decision, Ouvrard had made up his mind. On 6th April
1821 he submitted a memorandum to the then Prime Minister,
Richelieu, in which he underlined the dangers inherent in military
intervention by the Allies. Either France would be entrusted with
the actual operation, in which case the military and the domestic
consequences were quite unpredictable, or a joint Austrian,
Prussian and Russian expedition would be organized, in which
case France, as military base, would once again be saddled with
foreign troops only a few years after she had rid herself of them.
All the risks and the expense involved could be avoided, Ouvrard
argued, because there was a much simpler way to restore Spain
to her monarchy, her religion and her ancient customs. The in¬
ternal split in the peninsula must be exploited to revitalize the
royalist party, which appeared to be the weaker, but, if one took
the attitude of the masses into account, was in fact the stronger.
Then the Bourbons would themselves be in a position to restore
order. The royalists lacked only one thing. They had political
leaders, officers, arms and ammunition; what they did not have
was the money to pay the army. If the royalists were given the
money, the clergy could be relied upon to muster an “army of
the   faithful”    which would       radically change     the   situation.
Seven or eight corps of loyalist Spaniards would march on
Madrid, force the Cortes to capitulate and free the King from its
control.
  Ouvrard’s proposal should, on the face of it, have had a dual
appeal for the Allied governments: it would reduce the risk of
war and it would save a great deal of money. For it was inherent
in Ouvrard’s plan that the millions supplied to the Spanish
royalists should be a loan against Spanish government bonds.
Unfortunately, Richelieu, who had shown himself receptive to
Ouvrard’s ideas, was replaced by Monsieur de Villele, a man by
154                               Ouvrard

nature averse to taking final decisions and basically opposed to
intervention. He had to contend, however, with a powerful
war faction headed by Chateaubriand.
   This was the position in October 1822, when Ouvrard hurried
off to the Congress of Verona where the Spanish question was to
be finally settled by the Great Powers. He arrived just in time,
for the Congress was on the verge of collapse. Ouvrard was
received by Metternich and, in the presence of Gentz, Metternich’s
chief political adviser, he again underlined the hazards of a
military intervention. It was agreed that his proposals should be
considered but no decision was reached.
   Ouvrard was not motivated by any passionate love of peace.
He had sound commercial reasons for advising against an armed
intervention. If his claims of 1806 were to be met, then he must
place the King of Spain under some obligation, and this could
only be achieved if the King owed his freedom not to the Euro¬
pean Powers but entirely to Ouvrard. This plan, grotesque as it
appeared to be, became a practical possibility provided aid to
Spain was made a purely French concern. From the beginning
it was Ouvrard’s intention to give the Spaniards their loan with¬
out any support from the French Government. All he asked of
Villele was that he should recognize another Spanish body as
legal representatives of the captive King, namely the three-man
“Regency” which had been set up in August 1822 in the moun¬
tain fortress of Urgel after its capture by the royalists. The
Regency consisted of a lawyer, a priest and a soldier. The Marquis
Mataflorida, who had worked his way up from a private law
practice to the Ministry of Justice, was a millionaire who had
committed   his    entire     fortune   to   the   counter-revolutionary
struggle; the Archbishop of Tarragona had the support of all
those who believed that the future of the Church was linked to
that of the monarchy; the Baron d’Eroles had displayed his
military talents   in   the    Spanish War of Fiberation against
Napoleon, which marked the beginning of the Emperor’s
downfall.
  When the Spanish Regency at Urgel sent a delegate to Paris
to obtain more money, French officials gave him plenty of
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain      155

comforting words, promises, good advice and reproaches, but
no money. “In an enlightened age,” remarked Ouvrard drily,
“the only ideas which arouse any enthusiasm are those which cost
nothing.” But in his heart he was naturally delighted that the
government had turned a deaf ear to this request, leaving the
Regency’s emissary with no alternative but to apply to him. The
request for two or three hundred thousand francs was turned
down flat. Ouvrard acted on the familiar principle that a small
credit was a waste of money, whereas an enormous loan was a
safe investment. So he told his astonished visitor that what the
Regency needed was not 300,000 francs but 400 million and that
he would obtain it. The draft contract which Ouvrard drew up
and duly handed over on 1st November 1822 provided for a loan
at 5 per cent interest, 2 per cent amortization, and—the sting in
the tail—the deduction from the loan of the money which, in
Ouvrard’s view, the Spanish Government had owed him for so
many years.
   It is more than doubtful if this method of collecting an old
debt, which Ouvrard had used with Napoleon several times with
success, would have worked in this case. The Regency of Urgel
would certainly have declared that any such commitment was
far beyond their powers. But neither party was called upon to
make a final decision, for Ouvrard had laid down one condition
which was not fulfilled, namely that the Regency should be
officially recognized as legal government of Spain either by the
Congress of Verona or at least by the French Government.
  But Ouvrard’s dreams were finally and irrevocably shattered
when the European Powers decided, largely under pressure from
Russia, to intervene in Spain. France, which only some thirty
years before had launched a world revolution, was now called
upon, “in the name of Europe”, to crush the Spanish Revolution.
The King’s speech from the throne on 20th January 1823 carried
a declaration of war on the Spanish Cortes.
  There was only one consolation for Ouvrard: if the war was
actually conducted by France, then this must provide an opening
for him. Admittedly, the head of the government, Monsieur de
Villele, had made no secret of his dislike for him. Villele was
156                          Ouvrard

simply opposed to anyone connected with his predecessor,
Richelieu. But Ouvrard had foisted his services on a bigger man
than he, Napoleon Bonaparte.
   In Paris, however, where the Minister of War, Monsieur de
Bellune, was in charge of all the preparations, Ouvrard did not
make much progress. Estimates had already been publicly invited
and supply-contracts given to a variety of firms. For the time
being at least, Ouvrard had to be content with the order placed
with the firm of Dubrac, in which he had a controlling interest.
But this was much too modest for Ouvrard’s liking. If he could
not get what he wanted by direct means, then he must try to
find some other way of upsetting the Minister’s arrangements.
So Ouvrard packed his bags and set out on a long coach
journey through southern France, arriving at Bayonne on 3rd
April.
  Bayonne was the focal point of the military preparations, the
Army Headquarters where the Intendant General, Sicard, was
stationed. In and around the town were massed all the troops,
equipment and supplies. The War Minister, the Due de Bellune,
had also gone down from Paris to inspect the army in person. Here
at Bayonne, where Napoleon had lured the Spanish Bourbons
into a trap in 1808 and forced them to abdicate in favour of
Joseph, Ouvrard played a similar trick on the horrified Minister.
While Bellune went round, beaming and blissfully confident that
he had done everything to ensure the success of the campaign,
the feeling grew amongst all those in responsible positions that
the supplies for the army and particularly the transport facilities
were completely inadequate. The Intendant General, Sicard,
was wringing his hands, because he could no longer feel sure
of guaranteeing the army’s food-rations in Spain. The Bourbon
Prince, the Due d’Angouleme, who was Commander-in-Chief,
had little or no idea what was going on and was merely waiting
with mounting impatience for the moment when the bugles
would sound and the war begin. Ouvrard, the expert, was given
a warm welcome and made a thorough investigation which con¬
firmed the Intendant’s worst fears. He reported that in Bayonne
there was just enough grain for ten days, enough biscuits for
               Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain      157

two to three days, and there were no mills; where there was flour,
there were no bolting-hutches;* where there were bolting-hutches,
there were no ovens or bakers; infantry posts had been supplied
with forage while cavalry posts had none; the number of baggage-
vehicles and horses was completely inadequate. This picture
was by no means borne out entirely by the facts, but even an
impartial observer like Marshal Marmont said of the Due de
 Bellune that his vague and often conflicting orders did not give
the impression that France had ever waged a war before. Ouvrard
doubtless did not fail to remind those in charge, the Chief of
 General Staff, Count Guilleminot, his assistant, General Borde-
soulle, and the Intendant, of the fate of Junot’s invading army in
 1807, when, because of lack of supplies, the Spanish roads were
soon crowded with stragglers and sick men.
   As soon as the leaders in Bayonne realized that the transport
available was quite inadequate to keep open the army’s supply
lines, Ouvrard’s offer to guarantee supplies in Spain for the
army seemed a godsend. And as, on the testimony of Marshal
Marmont, he was generally considered to be equal to any emer¬
gency, he was called into consultation on 4th and 5 th April in
Bayonne, with a view to changing all the arrangements and
placing them in private hands. The only one who knew nothing
of the new contracts which were being drawn up was the War
Minister, Bellune, who alone was, strictly speaking, authorized
to sign them. Instead, the sanction of the impatient Due d’Angou-
leme was obtained, so that there was little the Due de Bellune
could do. It was not until 8th April, when he was on the point
of returning to the King in Paris, that the War Minister learned
of the two contracts which “the distinguished son of France”
had signed, together with Ouvrard, on the 5 th. Completely taken
by surprise, he had no choice, for the time being at least, but to
give his approval. Complaining of his rheumatism and of the
miserable way his excellent administrative arrangements had
been carried out, he entered his carriage and set off for Paris.
And the man who had been Purveyor General to the Directory
and to Napoleon during his hundred days had now taken up the
  * Used for sifting the bran after milling.—T.A.B.C.
i58                             Ouvrard

same post with the Bourbon army, despite the fact that all
supplies had already been contracted out to other firms.
   The War Minister had ample time on his return journey to
consider what had really happened. He now had an opportunity
to study the thirty-seven clauses in the two contracts, which
had completely upset his own arrangements. A careful perusal
of the details was not designed to improve his temper. They were
substantially as follows:
  As long as French troops were on Spanish soil Ouvrard was
entirely responsible for their supplies.
  The prices were fixed: a 750 grammes ration of bread 30§ cen¬
times, 550 g. of biscuit 46 cts.,   \ litre wine 25 cts., fa litre cognac
      cts., etc. If food prices rose more than 10 per cent, Ouvrard
would be paid correspondingly more.
  All foodstuffs and army equipment already supplied or about
to be supplied would be placed at Ouvrard’s disposal at the
agreed prices, but he received a “handling fee”: 5 centimes per
ration of bread, 7! centimes per ration of forage.
  Any bread ordered by Ouvrard for troops on the march but
not consumed would be taken over by the army or sold in Spain
at the army’s risk.
  As prices were not available for forage, Ouvrard only agreed
to provide it against a commission of 2 per cent and a further
7I centimes per ration on distribution. Fuel supplies were also
to be provided at a commission of 2 per cent.
  Any losses suffered through act of God or by enemy action
would be borne by the State; full payment would be made for
any foodstuffs destroyed. The State would bear the cost of all
buildings for storage and distribution and of all depots.
  Purchasing and distribution would be carried out by Ouvrard’s
employees; in fact the administrative personnel of the army, who
would be paid by the State.
  Eleven-twelfths of all such payments would be made in
advance and within the first five days of each month. The State
was obliged to pay in cash. Any disputes between Ouvrard and
any government department arising from this contract would be
submitted to a court of arbitration.
             Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain       ljg

  “The registration fee for the foregoing contract is fixed at
one franc.”
   The War Minister must have winced as his eye fell on the
last signature: “Louis Antoine (de Bourbon, due d’Angouleme)”.
Then followed a second contract, in which there were twenty-
one clauses covering transport. Its main provisions were that:
   Ouvrard had to provide 600 rack-waggons and 6,000 mules,
saddled and harnessed. The State would hand over to him, against
payment, all the mules already available.
   The crews of the mule-brigades—one driver to every two
animals—would be paid, clothed, fed and quartered by the State,
which would also be responsible for feeding and stabling the
animals.
   Ouvrard would receive three francs a day for the hire of
each mule. For the hire of each waggon he would receive n\
francs per day per yoke; the normal waggon would have four
yokes.
   The State would be responsible for any losses incurred by
act of God, enemy action or plague and also for losses incurred
by a breakdown in food supplies, which Ouvrard had agreed
to provide on a commission basis. The indemnity rates were:
600 francs per waggon, 500-600 francs per draught animal.
   The State was obliged to buy from Ouvrard at these prices
any waggons and animals which had become superfluous by the
end of the first year. In the second year the State was required
to pay only a sixth of these prices.
   Any animals remaining in Ouvrard’s possession after hostilities
had ceased would be fed by the State for another three months
in order to guarantee the entrepreneur favourable selling con¬
ditions.
   An arbitration tribunal would also deal with any disputes
arising from this contract.

   By the time he had come to the end of these documents the
War Minister, Bellune, was also at the end of his tether. He had
spent two months making preparations, and now Ouvrard was
entitled by these contracts to make his first deliveries from the
i6o                           Ouvrard

stocks accumulated by Bellune himself along the Pyrenees. His
grain, his cognac and his draught-animals were simply to be
handed over to the Purveyor General; it was not Ouvrard who
was supplying the State but the State which was supplying
Ouvrard. And yet, as if to add insult to injury, it was he, Monsieur
de Bellune, who was being blamed in Paris for having made such
inefficient arrangements that this emergency contract had become
inevitable. And Ouvrard had not even money to offer! He was
not required, as was otherwise customary, to put down a sub¬
stantial deposit. On the contrary, he received almost the whole
of his money in advance at the beginning of the month. And
there was nothing to be done about it; the contract ran for the
duration of the war and Ouvrard had had the impertinence to
insert an arbitration clause, which invalidated the normal pro¬
cedure of revising contracts unilaterally by simple administrative
decree.
  While the Minister drove on towards Paris in a state of mount¬
ing rage and frustration, the army began to march, on 7th April
1823, from Bayonne. It was almost ten years since Napoleon’s
army, in June 1813, had been compelled to evacuate Spain so
ingloriously. Under the terms of the contract, the Purveyor
General was paid 1,000,000 francs in cash on 6th April, 2,000,000
francs on 7th April, 3,400,000 francs on nth April, a total of
6,400,000 for the month. He was able to draw on the War
Minister’s stocks for ten days and only made his first purchases,
1,500,000 rations, in the second half of the month.
  With his usual enthusiasm Ouvrard concentrated mainly on
the most difficult problem, transport. He sent out his agents
along the country roads to buy up every vehicle they could find
at any price. In spite of that, only a part of the ammunition and
half the artillery could be moved in the first month.
  It was very soon clear that Ouvrard had been right in his
assumption that supplies could not be sent forward from France
but must be obtained, but not under any circumstances requisi¬
tioned, in Spain itself. Requisitioning, which must have been a
part of the War Minister’s vague plans, could only serve to
drive the loyalists amongst the Spanish people into the arms of
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain        161

the constitutional party. In fact, Ouvrard gave a practical demon¬
stration of a principle which he had tried to impress upon Napo¬
leon, the principle of purchasing supplies inside enemy territory,
and in this case it saved the situation. After a few days’ march
the army found itself in the kind of mountainous, inaccessible
country where roads were few and far between. The first reaction
of the peasants, who still remembered the harrowing guerilla
war against Napoleon, was to keep out of the way and only sell
their produce—if at all—at very high prices. But Ouvrard’s
knowledge of human nature proved invaluable. His solution was
a very simple one: he looked for the chink in the peasants’
armour. He showed them plenty of hard cash, gold and silver,
let them feast their eyes on it, and said, in so many words: It is
all yours.
  At Tolosa, when the officers demanded to know where his
stores, his depots and his supplies were, Ouvrard gave orders for
tables to be set up in the market-place, spread out his money
on them, then summoned the local council, the clergy and the
merchants and asked them to get the peasants to deliver food and
supplies for cash: bread, vegetables, meat, oats, hay, horses and
waggons. Anything delivered before eight in the morning would
fetch ten times the normal price, before nine, nine times, before
ten, eight times, and so on. “Self-interest is a motive that never
fails” was one of Ouvrard’s mottoes. At Tolosa the peasants fell
over one another to prove how true it was. From the early hours
they could be seen hurrying across the hills in order to be amongst
the first. Very soon the law of supply and demand began to work
in Ouvrard’s favour, so that he had no further need to pay
abnormal prices. All the army’s needs were more than adequately
met; the Second Corps had enough supplies for ten days in
advance.
  To begin with, of course, Ouvrard had been obliged to pay
enormous prices, which no professional army officer would
have dared pay, but it was justified by events. From then on the
army enjoyed an excellent reputation, and Ouvrard managed to
feed the troops throughout the entire campaign without requisi¬
tioning and without building up any reserve stocks. Spanish
   M
162                           Ouvrard

traders became interested and soon Ouvrard was able to revert
to his old method of letting sub-contractors do the work.
  Everything would have turned out splendidly for Ouvrard,
had it not been for Monsieur de Bellune sitting in Paris and
wondering how he could get rid of a Purveyor General he had
never wanted. He tried in vain to vent his rage on the Intendant
General, Sicard, who had also put his signature to the contracts.
Sicard, it is true, had been relieved of his post, but the Military
Intendant of the Tenth Army, Bourdon, whom the War Minister
had appointed in his place, was not even allowed to enter Bayonne
and had to return immediately to Paris. In the meantime, at the
Prime Minister’s instigation, a Monsieur Regnault had been
despatched to the front with the rank of Royal Commissioner to
undertake an enquiry into the contracts. The War Minister gave
Regnault, who had a strong personal bias against Ouvrard,
strict orders to cancel the agreements and not to conclude any
further contract until the Minister had seen it. The final result of
this manoeuvre was that on 2nd May, a few days after Regnault
had seen Ouvrard, the Vittoria Agreement was drawn up—
without the Minister’s knowledge—which by and large confirmed
the agreements made at Bayonne. Certain changes were made in
the prices, but while some were reduced, others were even in¬
creased. The War Minister was beside himself when he heard
that, despite all his telegraphic instructions, his new Intendant
had not only renewed the Bayonne contracts but even extended
them. For at Vittoria Ouvrard was also given complete control
of the forage and fuel supplies, which he had hitherto provided
only on commission. And the 10th and nth Divisions, which
had been supplied by the government, were also included in
Ouvrard’s new contract.
  Naturally Monsieur de Bellune did not take this lying down;
the third Intendant General followed his predecessors into
obscurity, to be succeeded by the Baron de Joinville. By this time
it was the end of June and Ouvrard had reached Madrid with the
troops. Monsieur de Joinville immediately investigated prices
throughout the country, had discussions with the Prince, the
Chief of General Staff, the Assistant Intendant, and naturally
                  Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain      163

with Ouvrard, and came to the conclusion that, while the cam¬
paign was in progress, it was impossible to make any radical
change. On 26th July yet anodier contract was drawn up in
Madrid with Ouvrard, who again had to accept certain minor
modifications in his prices. The bread ration was now 29 centimes
instead of 30§, forage 2-67 instead of 3*15, cognac 8 instead of 8j.*
 But once more the Bayonne contracts were substantially con¬
firmed. A price-index clause was even added, which spoke only
of increases in the market prices and higher rates of reimburse¬
ment but not of any drop or further reduction. To start with,
the reimbursement for the meat ration was even increased by
8j centimes.
   This whole affair, which everyone took so seriously, suggests
 that neither the War Ministry in Paris nor the Army Headquarters
had anything more important to think about. And indeed this
was so, for though France was at war with Spain yet the troops
had no worries apart from sleeping, eating and drinking. They
encountered no resistance. The Due d’Angouleme rode at the
head of his army, as though he were leading a march past. The
rebel army of 130,000 men, which, early rumours had it, would
oppose his 100,000, did not materialize. The fearsome mountain
passes, scenes of countless ambushes in the Napoleonic war,
were now deserted. Only the occasional group of shepherds or
peasants gazed down curiously from the mountains—at the
strange host of armed men passing along the roads below. When
the French troops entered Madrid by the north gate, the troops
of the Spanish constitutionalists left it by the south gate.
  So the calculations Ouvrard had made at the time of the
Congress of Verona had been correct. The broad mass of the
people was loyal to the monarchy and the revolutionary move¬
ment’s fighting strength was negligible. But the wealthy intelli¬
gentzia was deeply committed to the cause of freedom and a
constitution and gave the French an unfriendly welcome. In one
house where officers were quartered the lady of the house
appeared at table with a napkin over her arm and said to one of
Ouvrard’s staff: “Now that the French are here, we must regard
  * See p. 158.
 164                           Ouvrard

 ourselves as slaves; I am starting my service.” In another house
 when Ouvrard paid compliments to the daughter and said she
 would be overwhelmed with suitors, she replied: “I would
 rather die a virgin than belong to a slave.” The different behaviour
 of the common people, though they had waged guerilla war
 against Napoleon, was explained by Ouvrard as follows: “In¬
 dependence is something people defend in blind fanaticism, out
 of national pride, whereas civil freedom can only be defended by
 those who understand it and appreciate its value. In the struggle
 for independence every man is a soldier and the whole people
 takes to arms; only fully fledged citizens will fight for their
 personal freedom, and they are in a minority in most countries,
 particularly in Spain.” Even in the capital the mass of the people
 were for the King, and the Due d’Angouleme had the greatest
 difficulty, after the Spanish troops had withdrawn, in protecting
 the remaining supporters of what was in fact a very moderate
 constitution from the fury of the population.
    The indignation of the people of Madrid was all the greater
as the rebel army had taken the King with it when it left Madrid
on 23rd May. Incredible as it may seem, the French Commander-
in-Chief made no attempt to rescue him, when he allowed the
Spanish troops to withdraw. The only one who could derive any
satisfaction from this was Ouvrard, for it meant that the war and
his contract were prolonged for another four months. The
French army was compelled to pursue the fleeing rebels through¬
out the length of Spain, first to Seville, then to Cadiz, where the
Trocadero, which was generally considered to be impregnable,
was taken by storm on 30th August. Four hundred French
soldiers were killed, the only casualties of the entire campaign.
But the siege of the town still continued, because the French
fleet which should have made a simultaneous attack on the town
had not arrived.
  As soon as the army was stationed for any length of time at
one spot, Ouvrard ran into difficulties. Before long, all food
supplies in the vicinity of Cadiz were exhausted, and Ouvrard
was compelled, not for the first time, to do something out of the
ordinary. Failing the treasure of Mexico, which was of no further
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain        ids

use to him, there was the Emperor of Morocco. Armed with
letters of recommendation from the Due d’Angouleme he set
off in the brig Zebra, but even on the short crossing to Tangiers
she was blown back time and again by contrary winds. But
his nephew, Victor Ouvrard, succeeded in reaching Gibraltar,
with the help of a robber chief, and crossed from there to the
African coast. Gifts to the Pasha of Tangiers and the Emperor
in Fez produced the desired effect: in November a trade agree¬
ment was signed, enabling Ouvrard to import 300,000 cwt. of
barley and 4,000 oxen.
   This agreement, however, was one of many somewhat un¬
orthodox transactions, the conclusion of which gave Ouvrard
so much satisfaction and aroused great expectations only to
lead to the most appalling complications and eventually end in
complete chaos. The conclusion of the agreement had, in the
nature of things, taken some time, and, before the negotiations
had been completed, Cadiz had fallen. The French fleet had
finally appeared and bombarded Cadiz at the end of September,
the army of the Cortes had capitulated, and by 1st October the
King was at liberty again and the French troops were marching
slowly northwards. The contract with the Emperor of Morocco
was transferred to an agent, Ducroc, who was to continue supply¬
ing Cadiz. Unfortunately, he went bankrupt, which led to bill
protests, the forfeiture of pledge guarantees and official re¬
criminations.
   The collapse of this venture was followed by premature ter¬
mination of his contract with the government. Ouvrard had
suffered so often from the bad faith of governments that he can
hardly have been surprised by this. His services were no longer
required—at least not so urgently. The Army Intendants had
got to know the country and had learned all they needed to know
of Ouvrard’s methods of obtaining supplies from the Spaniards,
while the Generals could now dismiss all possibility of enemy
action and the complications that might ensue. So the Due
d’Angouleme, nephew of the King of France, who had hitherto
been Ouvrard’s mainstay, at last gave way to the War Ministry in
Paris, where official feelings were still ruffled. He asked Ouvrard
i66                           Ouvrard

for premature cancellation of his contracts. Ouvrard made
certain counter-proposals and a fresh agreement was concluded.
“In order to give the Purveyor General proof of our satisfaction
and, at the same time, compensation for the premature termina¬
tion of his contract”, on 14th November the Due d’Angouleme
decreed that the price reductions introduced in the Madrid
contract as against the Bayonne contracts should be “declared
invalid once and for all”.
   So Ouvrard gradually dismantled his whole organization. He
now had time to study at close quarters the political consequences
of this strange crusade. One day, for example, he saw the Due
d’Angouleme leaving Ferdinand’s house, flushed with anger,
after the King had abruptly rejected the suggestion that he should
adopt a more liberal constitution and, above all, should show
leniency towards the rebels. In the words of the royal proclamation
of 1st October, the King had “been restored to the throne of the
saintly Ferdinand—by the wise and just hand of the Almighty”.
In Seville, where the Feast of the Holy Rosary was being cele¬
brated even more joyously than usual, the voice of the people
echoed the will of the ruler: “Long live the absolute King and
the Holy Inquisition.” And in Madrid the first grim example was
made when General Riego, who had sought the protection of the
French troops in vain, was held prisoner in a chapel for three
days and then, dressed in the white shirt of a condemned man,
was dragged behind a donkey through the streets of the city,
in a basket which was held just clear of the ground by monks;
then he was delivered to the gallows. A few days later the King
returned to his palace; the streets were crowded yet he received
a cold welcome; the open carriage moved at a swift trot
through the rain, while a lackey held an umbrella over the royal
pair.
   Having thus witnessed the end of the campaign, Ouvrard
lost no time in turning his wartime commitments into more
peaceful projects. He had not forgotten the plans which began
to take shape in his mind when he argued so passionately at
Verona for purely financial intervention in Spain. He was still
dreaming, in fact, of the treasures of Central America, and he
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain          i6j

could not believe that the good old times of 1804-5 were gone
for ever, when he “so to speak balanced the accounts of the
Spanish monarchy in both Continents and drew up a budget
for them”. Although the armed intervention had frustrated
Ouvrard’s plan to free the King for the Spanish legitimists by
means of a loan arranged entirely by himself, he had taken every
opportunity during the campaign to renew his acquaintance with
them. At Bayonne he had personally advanced money to several
penniless Spanish leaders, and he claimed to have made a loan of
two millions to the Regency temporarily set up by the Due
d’Angouleme at Vittoria in May. After the army entered Madrid,
he entered into negotiations for a very big loan, the interest and
eventually the capital to be paid out of the quicksilver mines, but
the government in Paris put a stop to this. Then in June 1823,
with the help of the bankers Wismann & Co. and an English
Consul, he managed to pass large sums of money to the King
in Seville to facilitate his release. The first coach and horses which
the King used in Cadiz after his escape belonged to Ouvrard,
who placed them at the ruler’s disposal, knowing that other
means of transport would be difficult to come by. In October
the King expressed his gratitude in an extremely friendly letter,
so Ouvrard had reason to hope that he would find an attentive
audience if he once more preached the gospel of the Promised
Land, America.
   But Ouvrard knew from his experience of 1804 how long it
took for decisions to be reached in Spain. He found himself a
suitable residence in Madrid and settled down to plan a mag¬
nificent social debut for himself. It was not difficult, for he had
only to copy the parties he had given at Rainey or the receptions
at the Palais Montesson. And it worked. As before, he chose a
house with historic associations, a palais in the most fashionable
part of Madrid, which the French Ambassador had decorated in
the best French style. The stables could accommodate 200 horses,
the coach-house sixty carriages. The great attraction was the
estate of 600 acres; formal gardens in the French style were divided
into great squares edged by box-hedges with flower-beds in the
middle; the park was full of splendid trees and the whole place
i68                              Ouvrard

 was watered by numerous small streams. A fine lawn, such as not
 even the British Embassy could boast, was laid with turf cut on
 the banks of the Manzanares.
    This was the sort of milieu in which Ouvrard could happily
renew his old contacts with the leading families of Spain. His
 son-in-law, the Count de Rochechouart, and his daughter did
the honours with much elegance and skill. At a ball held by
 Ouvrard, everyone who counted in society or in the army would
be among the five or six hundred guests. Those who remembered
the beautiful women at Ouvrard’s house in 1804 now met their
charming daughters. The entire diplomatic corps would be
there. One of the regular guests was Princess d’Ossuna, one of
the most distinguished ladies in Spain, whose life Ouvrard had
once saved at a reception for Barras in Paris, when her dress
caught fire and Ouvrard, with great presence of mind, wrapped
a carpet round her. Ouvrard gave a special dinner-party for
Pozzo di Borgo, the Russian Ambassador in Paris, who was sent
to Madrid to congratulate Ferdinand on his restoration.
    On one occasion, at a masked ball, the twenty-two-year-old
Abbe Meziere appeared disguised as an elegant Parisienne. The
following day he was sharply reprimanded by his superiors. A
more serious note was struck with a visit by Father Cirillo de la
Meda, the spiritual ruler of 15,000 Franciscans, whom Ouvrard had
known from the very beginning of the military campaign as one of
the most active supporters of the King. He was only thirty-six,
elegant and yet, unlike the heavily perfumed abbes of Louis XV,
possessing all the dignity of a Prince of the Church. This man was
of particular interest to Ouvrard, who could see how the enor¬
mous power he wielded was based on an excellent information
service supplied by all his monasteries throughout the peninsula.*
   Ouvrard now had ample time to enjoy life. He bought a pack
of hounds and went hunting. In the economic crisis following
the war, which compelled many people to sell their valuables,
he bought precious stones, pearls and old paintings. A court
    *When Ouvrard returned the visit, he found him with a large basket of
letters, from which he selected one from his monks at Bergara reporting the
capture of an officer of the constitutional party and asking for Christian
guidance on whether to let him escape or have him stoned.
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain        169

jeweller made two magnificent necklaces for his two daughters.
The younger of the two took painting lessons with Ribera.*
Ouvrard himself maintained that this splendid mode of living
was quite within his means. There was, indeed, only one dis¬
tinction which he coveted and could not himself acquire; his
great ambition was to be rewarded for his services to the war
effort with a hereditary title, “in order to perpetuate the memory
of his achievements in the family”. It is hard to believe, however,
that Ouvrard can have seriously expected such an ambition to
be fulfilled, for it was soon clear to everyone that the last thing
Paris had in mind was to do him honour.
  For all his splendid way of life, Ouvrard naturally did not
forget for one moment why he had remained in Madrid. When¬
ever an opportunity presented itself of engaging the appropriate
Spanish Minister in a serious conversation, he employed all his
considerable powers of persuasion on behalf of his new project,
his new American plans. Circumstances demanded—this was his
latest thesis—that America should be used to bolster up an
ageing Europe. One must, therefore, see to it that the Continent
did not continue to lose the vast sums which had been sadly
lacking since Joseph Bonaparte took Charles IV’s place in 1808
and trade with metropolitan Spain had almost entirely dis¬
appeared. Ouvrard believed that Europe could not afford to
lose Latin America. Indeed, he believed that the independent
South American States might develop into a direct threat to the
industry of Europe. The day must come when the new States of
America, once peace had been restored, would begin to copy
European methods of industrialization, to employ new inventions
and new machines, and to grow European agricultural products
themselves. Spain, and particularly France, would suffer the same
fate as England, whose production was, he believed, being almost
strangled. “Blinded by the economic boom, which brought with
it a rapid, day-to-day improvement in the means of production
but also put them out of date, so to speak, from one day to the
next, the English did not realize the problems they were creating

   * Ribera y Fernandez, Don Juan Antonio [1779-1860] became Court
Painter to Charles IV.
1 JO                         Ouvrard

for themselves; they lost sight of the fact that while capital and
running costs could only be covered by a steady increase in
production, there was no corresponding increase in consump¬
tion; in the end the manufacturers became so dependent on
their machines that they, not the manufacturers, became the
masters.” This, Ouvrard maintained in 1824, was the problem
with which France and Spain would also inevitably be confronted
but which would become even more acute if the prevailing
economic relations at least with Central America were not main¬
tained. “Unfortunately,” Ouvrard added pointedly, “no one
paid any attention to me when I suggested to the Prince of La
Paz in 1807 that the Spanish monarchy should transfer its official
residence to the new world of America”; there the monarchy
could so easily have found “support in the love and loyalty of
these primitive peoples, who look upon their kings as gods in
earthly form”.
   Ouvrard was naturally not prepared to revive this particular
plan. More than one and a half decades had passed, during which
time, he also felt, conditions beyond the seas had so completely
changed as to make the survival of the Spanish Crown much too
uncertain. In fact, all the South American colonies had broken
free from Europe; in the Argentine the independence movement
had begun as early as 1809, in Venezuela in 1811; since 1814
Paraguay had been ruled by a national dictator; Chile became
free in 1818; Bolivia and Peru only achieved full independence
in 1824. In Mexico itself the Creole immigrants had sided with
Spain against the native Indians and even defeated the 100,000-
strong army of independence, which the Spanish priest, Miguel
Hidalgo, had collected; but the Creoles suddenly changed sides
and, after several fruitless appeals to Spain between 1820 and
1822 to create an independent Mexican monarchy under a Spanish
prince (which would virtually have realized Ouvrard’s dream of
1810), the Creole officer Iturbide made himself Emperor of
Mexico. Within a year the Spaniards had to admit that even the
monarchical system could not survive, for by 1823, when Fer¬
dinand regained the Spanish throne, the independent States of
Central America had a federal republican constitution.
             Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain       171

  One might have assumed that in face of this ineluctable
process of history, Ouvrard would finally have abandoned his
dreams of the Mexican treasure. But he found it impossible to
give up the most cherished ambition of his life, which had been
the basis of his plans in 1804 and 1810 and which he now revived
for the third time. The apostle of credit was still so completely
under the spell of the mountain of gold which his brother had
seen in the Mexican State Treasury at the beginning of the cen¬
tury, that he was capable of tossing even the most sacred of his
principles overboard. He had suddenly forgotten the lesson he
had always tried to teach Napoleon, that there was more to be
achieved with one clever man than with 500,000 soldiers, and
persuaded himself that swords and firearms had their virtues.
The whole of his new American plan was based on armed inter¬
vention, on a military reconquest. What he had in mind was
something on the lines of the British East India Company with
its private army of 150,000 men and 20,000 sailors. In his many
conversations with Spanish Ministers Ouvrard pressed more
and more for the establishment of a “West Indies Company”,
which would first bring the overseas possessions of the King
“under the administrative control of the company, having sub¬
dued them by force”, and would then have an exclusive trading
monopoly with America.
  Ouvrard even drafted an agreement:


    1. A   “Compagnie   armee   espagnole au Nouveau Monde”
would be set up in the form of a joint-stock company.
   2. The total share capital would be 400 million reals or 100
million francs.
    3. The money accruing from the shares would be paid to the
National Exchequer.
   4. The company would be run by a Board headed by three
native Spaniards, each of whom must possess a nominal share-
capital of 100,000 francs.
    5. The Board would appoint all civil and military officials
and fix their emoluments.
   6. The initial military force would be 30,000 men strong. It
17 2                            Ouvrard

would consist of Spaniards and Swiss, and it would subsequently
be reinforced from the native population.
       7. The State would grant this company a monopoly of trade
with America for a period of thirty years.
       8. All State-owned installations in the Spanish colonies would
be ceded, together with all that they contain, to the company.
       9. The company will have the right not only to intervene
militarily but also to undertake political negotiations regarding
the status quo and the form of government in each individual
territory.
   10. Should the colonies at any time be granted political inde¬
pendence, they would have to pay an indemnity totalling 1,000
million francs or 4,000 million reals, for all the territories to¬
gether.
   11. This indemnity would be paid to the National Exchequer;
the company would receive 10 per cent as commission.
   12. The company would be obliged to issue a yearly balance
sheet and to publish it.


   One feature of this splendid plan was that Ouvrard had
apparently left himself out of account, for as a native Frenchman
he would be unable to play any leading part. By a remarkable
oversight, however, Ouvrard had managed to omit the stipula¬
tion that all the shares in the company should be registered shares
and that they should only be held by Spaniards.
  In view of the developments in the New World, Ouvrard’s
scheme must have seemed, at first glance, highly Utopian to his
contemporaries in 1824, particularly as not only the Central
American States with Mexico at their head but also the former
Spanish colonies in South America—Colombia, Peru and Chile
—were to be handed over to the New West Indies Company.
On the other hand, there is little doubt that Ouvrard’s plans
were politically practicable, given speedy and determined execu¬
tion. Independence had not put down such deep roots either
amongst the natives or amongst the Creoles that they would
not have bartered it against the sort of economic advantages
which the thirty-year trade agreement offered them. The next
                Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain               175

few decades were to show that most of these countries, including
Mexico, were only republics in name and were, in fact, ruled by
dictators. Moreover, the whole independence movement would
not have materialized, had it not been for the severance of all
links during the Spanish wars. Not one of these colonial territories
could muster a fighting force capable of standing up to a well-
trained European army of 30,000 men.
   If Madrid kept putting off a decision on Ouvrard’s plan and
finally let it die through sheer neglect, it was not because Ouvrard
had found it impossible to convince the Spanish statesmen that
his plan had at least economic advantages. While the discussions
were going on, there was a most unwelcome interruption. Great
Britain, which had been forced to swallow the independence of
her North American colonies, saw no reason why Spain should
fare any better in South or Central America. Most important of
all, Britain’s trade with the new autonomous States in the south,
which had developed so nicely, would be seriously affected and
possibly even killed altogether by a West Indies Company with
a trade monopoly. So the British Ambassador at the Spanish
Court, Sir William A’Court, employed every weapon of secret
diplomacy he could lay his hands on, in order to wreck Ouvrard’s
scheme.* He did not have much difficulty, for Spain was a highly
vulnerable country, which had just managed to find its feet again
with foreign help. So Ouvrard’s third American project suffered
the same sad fate as its predecessors.
   Yet Ouvrard’s spirit remained unbroken. His mind was brimful
of ideas. But, before he could produce a new one, events took a
turn which dashed once and for all the hopes which had brought
him to Spain. For steps were taken in Paris early in 1824 to wind
up the military expedition to Spain both politically and finan¬
cially. The Due d’Angouleme received a delirious welcome

   * The weapons of secret diplomacy whereby British representatives
abroad—particularly nineteenth-century ones such as Stratford de Redcliffe

that he did not take Ouvrard’s schemes very seriously; in any case, as Wolff
admits, Spain’s precarious internal condition gave little hope that such a
plan could ever become a reality.—T.A.B.C.
174                              Ouvrard

home. For four weeks he was hailed as the hero of “this glorious
and memorable war”, as a “noble scion of Saint Louis and
Henry IV”, as “a worthy descendant of the virtuous Louis XVI”,
as “the illustrious warrior” who, to quote the speech from the
throne, had led his troops “with courage as well as wisdom”.
To his dinner guests Louis XVIII compared his nephew with the
homeric Achilles, the thunder of whose voice put even the brave
Greeks to flight.*
  The wave of enthusiasm died away, however, a few months
later, on 5 th April 1824, when the French Prime Minister mounted
the rostrum in the Chamber to explain just how much this
glorious page in France’s history had cost. He was reluctantly
compelled to ask for a supplementary credit of no million
francs, as the war credits so far approved, exactly 100 million,
did not cover even half the cost. Immediately there was a gigantic
scandal. The deputies thought that this was going much too far
and that the victory, which had “restored the foundations of the
social system”, had nevertheless been won at too high a price.
Had the army left 10,000 dead, instead of a mere 400, in Spain,
the elected representatives of the people would have found no
difficulty in voting a few more war memorials, but 100 million
francs was not so easy to swallow. A scapegoat must be found.
  None of the politicians was, of course, prepared to take the
blame; neither the Prime Minister nor the Finance Minister, and
still less the War Minister, who had always said that the contracts
signed with Ouvrard were scandalous. Once the magic name of
Ouvrard had been uttered, it dominated the whole acrimonious
discussion for the next two years. It gave the French public a
focal point for all its grievances. Only fifty to sixty of the total
208 million francs had been spent on supplies, despite the fact that
the armed promenade had been conducted without any real
military expenditure. But this did not make any difference. There
was at least one man who could be thrown to the wolves; the
whole of Paris was in a turmoil. Ouvrard was accused of having
lined his own pockets with thirty-three million out of the fifty
or sixty million supply budget. Particularly well-informed people
  * In fact, Achilles’ voice threw the Trojans into chaos!—T.A.B.C.
             Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain        175

also claimed to know that, as soon as Ouvrard received his
monthly advance, he put three-quarters by and spent only a
quarter on supplies. The Prime Minister, Monsieur de Villele,
realized with dismay that his Cabinet was in danger. Then he
devised an apparently excellent scheme for bringing the whole
business to an end. He mounted the rostrum one day and declared
before God and the world that Ouvrard, and Ouvrard alone,
had been responsible for the smooth conduct of the campaign and
that, thanks to his experience and skill, the food supplies of the
army had been guaranteed throughout the campaign. Without
Ouvrard’s co-operation the war could not have been concluded in
six months. Ouvrard had saved the country money, because in¬
experienced administrators would have been much more costly.
  But even diese strong words produced no effect. The texts
of the contracts signed at Bayonne, which had made such dis¬
tressing reading for the War Minister on his return journey,
had now been made public. Such agreements, it was said, could
only have been concluded in one way: by bribing highly placed
officials. In the Chamber Ouvrard was openly accused of this
crime by Count Labourdonnaye, and the following day it was
printed in the Moniteur. The Chief of General Staff, Count
Guilleminot, and his deputy, General Bordesoulle, together with
the Intendant General, Sicard, were more or less openly accused
of having suffered from itching palms. Unfortunately, the Due
d’Angouleme had also signed the contracts, had indeed warmly
welcomed them and defended them throughout the campaign
against all the War Minister’s onslaughts. But the poor Prince,
the illustrious warrior, of whom it would have been almost
blasphemy to expect that sort of expertise, had simply been an
innocent victim of his unscrupulous advisers. The whole affair
was said to be “an attack on the loyalty and the good faith of a
generous prince”.
  Much to the regret of the Prime Minister a Committee of the
Chamber was set up to investigate. Their findings two and a half
months later completely vindicated Ouvrard: “The food rations
of the army on the eve of its departure for Spain were inadequate;
there was no forage. Worst of all, however, was the shortage of
176                            Ouvrard

transport. The Commission recognized the need for new measures
—the measures finally adopted were completely justified.” The
Chamber had no alternative but to approve the supplementary
credit of no million francs on 29th June 1824. The general
public, however, were still not satisfied. There were further
references in the Press to the “thieving hand of shameless and
unscrupulous swindlers”; one pamphlet even recalled the case
of M. de Marillac, Marshal of France in the reign of Louis XIII,
who was condemned to death for having received bribes and,
to the delight of the populace, was executed in the Place de
Greve. So Monsieur de Villele felt obliged to reverse his previous
judgement on Ouvrard’s contracts and to set up a second Com¬
mission of Enquiry. On 30th June 1824 a royal decree announced
the names of its members: a retired Field Marshal, an Admiral,
a former Imperial Intendant, two serving officials and Count
Daru, who, as Napoleon’s Military Intendant, was accused by
the Emperor in July 1809 of having allowed 18,000 prisoners on
the island of Lobau to die of starvation. These, then, were the
gentlemen who had to decide whether to vindicate the organiz¬
ing ability of their colleague, the Minister for War, Due de
Bellune, or the work done by the merchant Ouvrard. The choice
cannot have been difficult.
   Ouvrard himself was no longer in Madrid, witnessing these
developments in the Chamber and the Cabinet from a respectable
distance. Monsieur de Bellune and the government were not
content merely to bring the Spanish contract to a premature end,
they were also determined to put a stop to his quasi-political
activities at Ferdinand’s Court. Monsieur de Villele was not pre¬
pared to let a private individual, and still less a supporter of the
liberal   Richelieu,   represent France’s   interests   in   Spain.   So
Ouvrard was more or less smoked out of Madrid. On 17th May
an Order of the Day was issued which was clearly directed at
Ouvrard; it ordered all persons previously employed by the
army to leave Spain by        1st June. The quartermasters were
instructed to send any outstanding accounts not to Ouvrard but
to the Quartermaster General. The papers in Ouvrard’s office,
for which he had rented an entire house, were bundled together
