              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain        177

in two days, packed in old boxes and despatched in open hay-
waggons to Toulouse, where Monsieur de Bellune had decided
to make the final settlement. Ouvrard’s departure on 14th June,
when a French military escort was provided, was not without
incident. Ouvrard had never shown a passionate interest in paying
his debts at the best of times, but when the Army Paymaster,
obeying orders from Paris, had stopped any further payments to
Ouvrard on 2nd January, Ouvrard adopted the same attitude
towards his Spanish creditors, clearly hoping either to force the
French Government’s hand or place it in an embarrassing position.
But he also refused to accept accounts, even when they were per¬
fectly correct and countersigned by his employees, if the prices
were higher than those he himself received. So when he finally de¬
parted it was amidst a chorus of cat-calls from an organized street
mob. It was a very different scene from the one in spring 1805
when he left Madrid in triumph. Ouvrard ironically suggested that
it would have been more appropriate to be “taken in handcuffs to
Toulouse by the very people for whom I paved the way to Spain”.
  When he reached Paris the situation seemed more favourable
than he had dared hope. The Committee of the Chamber had
just announced, on 21st June, that it regarded the Bayonne
contract as inviolable. But then came a setback with the appoint¬
ment of an official Commission of Enquiry. Five long months
passed before this bunch of aristocrats had completed its inter¬
rogation, compared all the lists, collected all the documents and
seen all the reports. Only one man was not summoned to appear
before the Commission, the man whose whole livelihood and
reputation was at stake: Ouvrard. All the army quartermasters,
all the assistant quartermasters, all military officials and store
superintendents were interrogated about the supply position in
Spain; only the head of the Commisariat, Ouvrard, was not
questioned. So Ouvrard decided to give his evidence in the form
of a printed “Note du munitionnaire-general de VArmee d’Espagne”.
This was his contribution to the flood of pamphlets, which were
issued by all concerned in an attempt to sway the Commission.
  All of them were playing the popular game of pinning the
blame on someone else. The Chief of General Staff, Count
   N
178                          Ouvrard

Guilleminot, blamed the War Minister for a deficit of 3,500,000
rations at the start of the campaign; the War Minister was furious
with Count Andreossy, the officer in charge of the Commissariat,
for not carrying out his instructions; Andreossy held Andre
Marchand, the former Inspector General of the Commissariat,
responsible; Marchand complained that the time had been too
short and the circumstances unfavourable; as far as the Intendant
General, Sicard, was concerned, the sole culprit was the War
Minister, and the War Minister accused the Quartermaster
General, whom he himself had appointed, of abysmal ignorance.
So it went on, till every divisional quartermaster and store
superintendent had said his piece.
  Finally, on 30th November 1824, the Commission of Enquiry
produced a Rapport au Roi. ... Its length was phenomenal: it
ran to five volumes in large quarto size with more than 2,000
pages. It was touching to see how the resume endeavoured to
cast a mantle of Christian brotherly love over the shortcomings
of the civil servants and to conceal the fact that the report was a
shattering indictment of the War Minister and the whole military
bureaucracy under him. It was, indeed, possible, by juggling
with figures, by adding or subtracting millions of rations, to
show that the War Minister had really accumulated adequate
supplies of provisions at the Pyrenees frontier or arranged for
their delivery.   Given this kind of sweeping generalization,
Ouvrard’s assertion that no preparations had been made at
Bayonne was described as incorrect. There were, in fact, only
1,500,000 rations of forage for the 25,000 horses of the 100,000-
strong army, and even the Commission considered this too little.
There was virtually no dry biscuit, although it was important
as the only substitute the soldiers had for bread. This too the
Commission found inadequate. But a cautious reckoning showed
that at the beginning of April diere were sufficient stocks of
flour, rice, vegetables and cognac at the frontier to keep the army
well supplied for the first few weeks of the campaign. Unfortun¬
ately no one knew of their existence, neither the Due d’Angou-
leme nor the Chief of General Staff, nor, for that matter, the
Intendant General, Sicard, himself. Before       Ouvrard arrived
             Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain         179

at Bayonne it was generally assumed that the supplies were
completely inadequate.
  All this the report could not deny, so it moved on hastily
to deal with the vast reserve supplies which the far-seeing War
Minister had built up for the months after the start of the
campaign. For a period of six months, the Commission stated,
the army could have drawn on these reserve supplies, but it was
compelled to add, regretfully, that there were no means of trans¬
porting diem to the army. For the War Minister had omitted to
provide transport. It would have required 7,200 provision or
munition waggons, 34,900 horses with crews. The War Minister
had ordered only about 500 waggons and not all of these had
actually arrived. On the other hand, no plan had been drawn up
to tap the only possible alternative source of supply, Spain; the
money was there but no single individual who could have made
the necessary decision on the spot. So at the Bayonne headquarters,
the report continued, feeling was running high, when Ouvrard
suddenly appeared on the scene. The Commission could not
very well deny that everyone, and especially the Prince, gave a
sigh of relief when someone arrived who was known to be a
man of action and of experience.
  All the more reason for the members of the Commission to
move on rapidly to the price Ouvrard demanded to save the
campaign. It was shameful of him to ask for advance payments,
to require that his staff be paid by the government, to stipulate a
completely   unbureaucratic    system   of accounting,    and—the
height of impertinence—to demand that any disputes be sub¬
mitted to an arbitration tribunal. Naturally great prominence
was given to the unusual contract concerning vehicles and the
hire of horses, but the report actually admitted at a later stage
that Ouvrard, by applying his principle of living on the country,
was still using only 300 waggons two months after the campaign
had begun. The Commission expressed consternation at the
War Minister’s comparative figures, the source of which was
never divulged, showing the prices then current in Spain and
the prices fixed in Ouvrard’s contract. The War Minister was
confident that he had discovered a discrepancy of thirty million
180                           Ouvrard

francs on food and forage supplies alone. This did not emerge
very convincingly in the report, for it showed that, of the sixty-
five million claimed by Ouvrard, a total of fifty million was
approved. Actually the Commission’s report itself ruled out any
possibility of Ouvrard having made such net profits by pointing
out that Ouvrard had handed over the actual business transactions
to Spanish sub-contractors. In other words, as he had done in
1789 with the paper deal and later with the Mexican project, he
resorted to the same method of leaving the subsequent running
profits to others.
  Army quartermasters, who to their chagrin had found them¬
selves learning the tricks of their own trade from the civilian
Ouvrard and even taking orders from him, testified before the
Commission that the transfer of business to Spanish contractors
had not benefited the army. If the detailed evidence that filled
the entire fourth volume of the report was to be believed, then a
remarkable picture emerged. At the head of the marching columns
the military officials were busily collecting foodstuffs from the
local inhabitants against promises of money, while at the tail-end
the population were pestering the officers or Ouvrard’s staff for
money which was no longer there, because it had been paid to
Ouvrard in advance. Although, only a few sentences before,
Ouvrard had handed over all deliveries to sub-contractors, his
hirelings were bringing up the rear of each column, collecting
receipts in the villages and only paying cash at a substantial dis¬
count. There can be no doubt that Ouvrard stood to gain quite
considerably by the Bayonne contracts—he did, after all, per¬
form a major rescue operation—but to represent him as a failure
was to ignore the fact that, right up to the end of the campaign,
the Due d’Angouleme refused time and again to have him re¬
placed by die military bureaucrats. So it is only one of many
contradictions in the mass of documents published in 1824 when
the report concludes, with an obsequious bow to the Due
d’Angouleme: “If, even so, it was found possible to effect the
deliveries, this was due entirely to the general confidence in the
sterling qualities of the Prince, in his speedy decisions, in the
skill of his military operations, and—it must be said openly—
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain         181

in the honour which everyone derived from being privileged to
contribute to his success.”
  The tribute to the Prince was followed by a slap in the face for
Ouvrard: “The business transactions initiated by Ouvrard were
ruinous to the public interest.” The Commission did not think
it necessary to investigate other items of expenditure not con¬
cerned with supplies.
  While the government were still debating what action should
be taken following this adverse report on Ouvrard, he was
suddenly confronted by a new and, for the moment, much more
dangerous enemy: Armand Seguin. Seguin was well known in
Paris as a double-dyed scoundrel. By profession a chemist, he
had laid the foundations of his great fortune about the same time
as Ouvrard, in 1795. He had managed to convince the Conven¬
tion that he could reduce the normal period required for tanning
leather, from one to two years, to seven to eight days, and in the
case of calfskin even to twenty-four hours. The Convention gave
him two properties, a great deal of money and a contract for
the entire supply of army boots. Seguin supplied the leather and
the boots, but the soles were like cardboard. Yet together with this
commercial instinct he possessed exquisite taste. His palais in the
Rue d’Anjou contained a famous picture gallery and a delightful
music-room. (He had one peculiar mania: he took to pieces old
Italian violins from the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries in
an attempt to discover the secret of their beautiful tone.) Next to
his bedroom was a chemical laboratory, in which he also pro¬
duced an incredible number of pamphlets. So that no one could
see into his park, he surrounded it with a twenty-foot-high
embankment covered with shrubs. In his riding-stable he had
forty magnificent horses, which he seldom rode himself but
constantly admired. Although he also kept eight coach-horses,
when his wife went out she was given thirty sous to take a cab.
  This strange character had a lively sense of humour in every¬
thing except money. Ouvrard was made painfully aware of this
in December 1824. In February 1823, two months before Ouvrard
left for Bayonne, Seguin had fought a successful legal action
against him over the unhappy liquidation of the Mexican affair,
182                               Ouvrard

which Napoleon had so abruptly terminated. When Ouvrard went
to Spain in 1805, Seguin had taken over part of the payments on
the large loan which Ouvrard had made to the government *
Seguin earned a fixed commission, all the proceeds going to
Ouvrard and Vanlerberghe, who, however, guaranteed Seguin
against any losses he might suffer. When the scandal broke and
Napoleon held all those involved responsible, he demanded
1,600,000 francs, from Seguin. Ouvrard paid it to the State
Treasury. But it was immediately set off by the authorities
against Ouvrard’s debts and Seguin had to pay the same amount
himself. He quoted the indemnity clause. Ouvrard, incensed at the
idea of having to pay twice, refused. Seguin had to wait twenty
years before he saw an opportunity of getting his money back.
On 27th February 1823 the Court condemned Ouvrard to repay
Seguin’s debt with compound interest—a total of three million
francs. Ouvrard refused before the war and he continued to re¬
fuse after the war. He had spent three years in prison rather than
pay Napoleon and he proposed to treat Seguin in the same way.
The challenge was accepted. On Christmas Eve 1824, just as he
was leaving his office in the Rue du Brae for home, a heavy hand
fell on his shoulder. Ouvrard was escorted—for the third time—
to Sainte-Pelagie. It was the fifth time he had been arrested.
  This time Ouvrard apparently had reasons of his own for not
making his imprisonment quite so comfortable as before. He
acquired two rooms from other inmates, one for himself, the
other as an office, but he furnished them very modestly. The floor
was covered with a plain baize cloth; there was a plain stove, a
few chairs, a mirror costing forty francs, an alabaster clock and an
old iron camp-bed. For Ouvrard life in prison could not have been
simpler. But his hopes of being left in peace to enjoy these simple
surroundings were soon dashed, for within three weeks he had
started on a round tour of the prisons of Paris. On 12th February
1825 he was transferred to the criminal prison. Having sampled
Sainte-Pelagie, the Abbaye and Vincennes, he finally saw the
inside of the Conciergerie, in which so many State prisoners,
  * The rate of commission on Seguin’s loans was the excessively high one
of 18 per cent, for Ouvrard, liable as he was at any time to the depredations
of Napoleon, was a very bad risk.—T.A.B.C.
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain        lSj

amongst them Marie-Antoinette, had spent their last hours.
Following the Rapport au Roi, the Minister of Justice was in¬
structed “to proceed according to the laws of the Realm”, and
Ouvrard was accordingly accused of having bribed senior State
officials and generals ot the French army. It was rumoured that
Guilleminot had engineered a lucrative post for his brother-in-
law, and that General Bordesoulle had been in a position, on his
return home, to buy an estate worth 600,000 francs. The Baron
de Joinville was said to have received 400,000 francs for giving
his approval in Madrid to the Bayonne contracts.
  With Ouvrard’s incarceration in the Conciergerie the wheel
had come full circle. For in 1824 he entered the same prison from
which, thirty years before, he had rescued his fellow citizens
from Nantes, when they fell foul of the Revolutionary Tribunal.
Strangely enough, even in the Conciergerie he managed to
enjoy considerable freedom of movement. He was given per¬
mission to transfer his belongings from Sainte-Pelagie, two
clerks were allowed to work in the vestibule, and, when this
became too uncomfortable for Ouvrard, he was allowed to
break down the wall between the two rooms and turn them into
one. On Sundays he entertained his family, and a frequent visitor
was the famous actor, Talma, with his small son. Ouvrard was
even at liberty to attack the government, both verbally and in
print, a freedom unusual for the age.
  Ouvrard began his attacks on a purely commercial basis.
Monsieur de Villele, the latest of his great adversaries, tried to
reduce the State’s heavy interest payments by converting the 5
per cent loan to 3 per cent. There were indignant protests from
all those who held 5 per cent rentes. Ouvrard tried to whip up
this indignation by publishing letters in the Paris newspapers in
September 1825, in which he warned the public against the Prime
Minister’s intentions and offered to give details of another form
of conversion which would be quite safe, quite simple, less ruth¬
less and just as productive. Naturally this offer was not taken up.
  Ouvrard was soon to have an opportunity of giving an
account of himself. But for the time being he lapsed into silence
in order to obtain trial by the Court of Peers. In December 1825,
184                          Ouvrard

when he was brought before the Cour Royale, the Public Prosecu¬
tor, Bellart, complained of the obstinacy of the accused: “What¬
ever questions one puts to him—he never replies. If one asks him
to explain a very significant fact—he does not know what one is
talking about. If one shows him a letter—he does not know who
it came from. If one asks him to clarify a certain passage—he does
not know what the correspondent had in mind. If one enquires
how he came by such and such a paper—he does not remember.
If he is called upon to say whether a certain matter turned out
thus—he thinks: yes or he thinks: no. But he is unable either to
confirm or to deny it.”
  But Ouvrard’s tactics proved effective, for the Supreme Court
found the whole affair so embarrassing that it declared itself in¬
competent to deal with it: only the Chamber of Peers could
decide whether its    two   members,   Count    Guilleminot and
General Bordesoulle, had accepted bribes. Until this decision was
reached, judgement could not be passed on Ouvrard.
  The Court of Peers decided to take the matter up. In
February 1826 Ouvrard had to return to the Conciergerie. Two
months later the hour of freedom seemed to be at hand. On 22nd
May a Commission declared that it was convinced of the inno¬
cence of the accused generals. But on 10th June the Court of
Peers decided to make a fresh investigation of the Bayonne
contracts, regardless of the bribery charges. A second edition of
the five-volume report of the previous Commission was issued.
A fresh report by the Chairman was a shattering indictment not
merely of the War Minister but also of the first report. Such food
supplies as had been accumulated around Bayonne were not even
available for the troops when the advance began. All the War
Minister’s orders came too late. Forage: “There was neidier hay,
nor straw, nor oats.” Transportation: not even the 495 waggons
were there as stated. Administrative staff: hardly trustworthy and
undermanned; of 157 doctors only forty were present when the
troops marched off. The final conclusion: “The contract writh
Ouvrard may have been carelessly drawn up, but it was justified
by events.” So on 3rd August the Court of Peers acquitted all
the accused, including Ouvrard, of the charge of corruption.
              Comedy and Scandal: with the Army to Spain          185

None the less, the question of attempted corruption was referred
back to the ordinary courts. It was decided that Ouvrard should
remain in custody till this question was settled.
  Another three months elapsed before judgement was passed.
Then at last, on 9th November 1826, the final stage was reached.
“All lawsuits,” remarked Ouvrard, “have a natural tendency to
expand indefinitely; mine was unique in that it contracted more
and more.” And, in fact, this mammoth trial petered out miserably
in a common police court.
  Ouvrard was acquitted and released. Even the Public Prosecu¬
tor had withdrawn his charges. The general public had almost
entirely lost interest. “It is our greatest weakness in France,” said
one contemporary observer, “that we are immediately excited by
a major event. In a burst of enthusiasm we put pen to paper but
almost always we catch no more than a glimpse of the truth. We
do not give it time to make a proper impact on our minds. When
it eventually does, the matter has long since been settled and has
lost all interest.” Ouvrard would certainly have found little to
add to this summing-up. An innocent man, he had wasted two
years of his life in custody. He had been severely punished for
saving the army and the country from disgrace and perhaps from
disaster, against a War Minister’s will.
   Even now, however, Ouvrard was still not a free man. As the
government was not prepared to fulfil the Bayonne contracts or
to accept arbitration, but was prepared to go back on the solemn
word of the Due d’Angouleme as commander-in-chief, Ouvrard’s
disputed millions were simply not paid. In the circumstances, he
could not see his way to paying Seguin the three million francs, a
debt imposed on him by another quirk of fate. So he returned to
Sainte-Pelagie, where he remained a further three years. Not until
the end of 1829 was he finally set free. The moment of his release
was fortunate. For once again France was about to be faced with
crucial political decisions and, as before, there was everything to
be said for making certain long-term provisions. And—some¬
thing that meant a great deal to Ouvrard—he was soon to have
the opportunity for a final showdown with competitors who were
steadily growing in power, the Rothschilds of Paris and London.
                              Twelve




      The Slump of 1830j Battle with Rothschild




D   URING his five years of imprisonment Ouvrard had been
far from idle. Whereas in Vincennes in 1810 he found it necessary
to scatter and pick up pins in order to preserve his sanity, he
spent the period from 1824 to 1829, while he was waiting to be
released, in planning, discussing, corresponding and dictating. He
did not employ two clerks in prison merely to create an impres¬
sion. Apart from the documents he printed in his defence, he also
produced several pamphlets attacking his        former colleague
Tourton, who was suing him for part of the profits from the
Spanish campaign. MacGregor, one of the generals in the
Spanish War of Independence, enlisted his help to get property
acquired in Colombia legally recognized in Madrid. A large
number of people visited Ouvrard in the debtors’ prison in the
hope—it was a vain hope!—of interesting him in the sort of
wild speculation that was rife at the time. Ouvrard was primarily
interested in a plan, in which Lord Cochrane in London was
also involved, to restore the Order of the Knights of St. John of
the Cross in Malta and the independence of Greece by setting up
a Levant Company, which was simply the old idea of an armed
West Indies Company in a new form.
  In 1828 he even tried to revive his Mexican scheme in another
part of America, when he offered to help Colombia pay off debts,
amounting to 150 million francs, on condition that he was given
an “eternal” concession to exploit public lands, forests, lakes and
salt-mines. The offer was not accepted.
  Of vastly greater importance, however, was the writing of
                               186
               The Slump of 1830: Battle with Rothschild           187

his memoirs, in which he set out not merely to answer the
accusations and suspicions directed against him but also to depict
his own career in the wider economic, historical and political
context of his time. For all their bias and the frequent signs of
revision, these memoirs make such impressive reading that one
might almost suspect they had been written not by a merchant
but by a professional writer of very high standing. Ouvrard’s
efforts did not go unrewarded: the three volumes comprising a
thousand pages had to be reprinted three times in one year.
  There was no lack of topicality. The then Prime Minister,
Monsieur de Villele, came in for frequent and bitter attacks. He
was described as a man “who in times of emergency displays so
little courage and, when the danger has passed, plays the despot”;
a man “of petty and unscrupulous vanity, of vulgar ambitions
and quite detestable envy”. Ouvrard referred to the government
“lies”. He said that the charge of corruption must be thrown back
at it, that the refusal to accept arbitration resulted in nothing more
nor less than “common robbery” and was “the hall-mark of a
predatory system”; that the Finance Minister was “dishonest
and malicious” and that the War Minister was guilty of “an
incredible dereliction of duty, for he endangered the army’s
supplies in the face of the enemy”.
  So Ouvrard did not mince matters in defending his contracts,
a defence which he was allowed to print with impunity. He lost
all sense of proportion, however, when he came to discuss the
government’s dealings with the Rothschild brothers. The expan¬
sion of this firm touched him on the raw. In France he alone had
been in a position, which no one had seriously challenged, to
devise the really big and complex schemes. “Now the houses of
Rothschild think they are destined to be the financiers of the
world’s States.” The time was long since past, however, when the
Rothschilds could be dismissed contemptuously as mere traders,
who dealt in tobacco and liquorice, walking-sticks and lanterns,
in short, anything and everything. Ouvrard had seen how, year
after year, slowly but surely, they had expanded.
  In 1800 Nathan went to England, in 1811 James appeared in
Paris, in 1820 Solomon settled in Vienna. In 1815, when Ouvrard
l88                          Ouvrard

was going round with his plan to finance France’s war-indemnity
through Baring and Hope, the Rothschild brothers tried to
squeeze in, but at that stage all they managed to achieve was the
transfer of a few millions, which France was still able to pay in
cash to the Allies. Ouvrard had also seen them prowling around
at the Congress of Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818, by which time they
were being invited to lunch with Metternich but still had to leave
the lion’s share to Baring and Hope. He met them again in the
autumn of 1822 at the Congress of Verona, where he tried to
bring about a peaceful settlement of the Spanish problem, and at
that time he even watched them conducting margin deals on the
Paris Bourse by spreading rumours that the intervention which
Ouvrard was urging would not take place. It may be that Ouvrard
also learned to his chagrin that after the Congress of Aix-la-
Chapelle the influential Austrian Councillor of State, Gentz,
wrote to his friend Adam Muller (the great conservative econo¬
mist) that the most profound reasoning of Alexander Baring in¬
spired less confidence in him than a penetrating (off the cuff)
judgement by one of the more intelligent Rothschilds. At all
events, it must have upset Ouvrard to see heaped upon the three
brothers so many of the honours which, for all his efforts, had
completely passed him by. Solomon had been made a Prussian
Commercial Counsellor in i8i5,Nathan Austrian Consul General
in 1822, and eventually all three were raised to the nobility,
something that Ouvrard had always coveted. Solomon even
received the Order of Saint Vladimir in return for a loan of ten
million pounds to Russia.
  Ouvrard might perhaps have resigned himself to all this, had
the Rothschild brothers not become involved in the financial
affairs of the French Government, and this at a time when
Ouvrard was in no position to defend himself, except with his
pen. So he suddenly emerged as a custodian of the public con¬
science, an aggrieved patriot, an honest taxpayer. On its monetary
transactions for the government in the Spanish War of 1823, the
house of Rothschild, it seemed, had earned 3,300,000 francs;
this was completely unwarranted. In the same year Monsieur
de Villele had “wangled twenty-three million rentes” for this
               The Slump of 1830: Battle with Rothschild         189

firm; before they were put on the market, which had to be
stimulated with secret offers, he had told James de Rothschild
privately what was the highest offer so far made, so that the latter
was able without risk to tender only slightly more. Most scandalous
of all, however, was the fact that the brothers did not even have to
pay for the rentes they had bought—they paid the Treasury
4 per cent for them and earned 6 per cent from them. Prior to the
Treasury audit, Rothschild paid in an uncovered cheque, and
after the audit it was returned to him. Finally, the conversion of
the rentes in 1824-5, that unjust and cruel exchange of a 5 per
cent for a 3 per cent loan which was to save the State thirty-four
million a year in interest, was concocted by the Rothschilds. For
Ouvrard, who was allowed to print all these accusations with
impunity, this was the last straw.
  He and he alone in France should be permitted to conduct this
sort of business transaction, which, incidentally, he claimed to
have suggested in his newspaper appeal, though, of course, with
much greater regard for public welfare. He now let himself go.
This whole conversion was “nothing but a clever manoeuvre”,
which would guarantee the Rothschild brothers a net annual
profit of 1,400,000 francs. He pulled out the patriotic stop: what
induced the Minister to be “so generous to the German banker”?
Ouvrard hinted at some kind of involuntary treason by Monsieur
de Villele: the whole affair was, after all, “suggested by bankers,
who were in very close contact with foreign Cabinets and acted
to some extent as their tools”. Then Ouvrard, sitting in the
debtors’ prison, appealed to the anti-Semitic instincts of the
populace: “these bonds, issued by royal decree and bearing the
Arms of France, do not gain in value by first passing through
Israelite hands”. There was an appeal to the man-in-the-street:
the Minister was “filling Rothschild’s coffers with the French
taxpayers’ money”. In this way Ouvrard “exposed the crooked
methods by which the Finance Minister extracted money from
the Treasury and transferred it to Rothschild’s pockets. Any
other Minister would have been found guilty of misappropriating
public funds. By rights Monsieur de Villele should be sitting in
my place in the Conciergerie.”
igo                           Ouvrard

  Neither Rothschild nor Villele answered this attack. These
diatribes appeared, unchallenged, in four successive editions.
Presumably neither party was anxious to have his affairs discussed
in public before the courts. The result could have been, to say the
least, embarrassing. So Ouvrard had to wait for another oppor¬
tunity to bring his enemy into the open. The opportunity even¬
tually came, as indeed it was bound to come, for Rothschild
speculated “a la hausse”, Ouvrard “a la baisse”, and both in the
same stocks, namely, French rentes. In the long run only one of
the two could be right; sooner or later one of them was bound to
take grist from the other’s mill.
  It is an interesting fact that Ouvrard, the incurable optimist,
felt very pessimistic about the future in the years 1825-6. The
wave of wild speculation, which broke over France after the
Spanish war, filled him with misgiving. Whereas at the time of
the 1817-18 war loan the issuing of rentes was still an event, the
markets were now flooded with new loans, especially for American
countries. The most insecure scrip was immediately bought up.
Everyone was dealing in export goods; factories were working
day and night. Mining shares soared, Paris was in a fever of
excitement, new consortiums and companies shot up overnight
like mushrooms. “People looked for profits,” Ouvrard reported,
“in the mists of Nicaragua and the savannahs of Texas, just as
they had looked for it in Law’s time in the mists of the Mississippi
and the salt trade of Honduras.”
   Ouvrard did not allow this to unsettle him, although the trans¬
atlantic commerce in which he was so deeply interested was very
much involved, and the building fever in Paris left him completely
unmoved. All available building-sites were being bought and
broken up into fresh lots. “Paris is like a town which has been
stormed by architects and bricklayers.” A man could go to bed
poor and wake up rich. He tells the story with obvious amuse¬
ment of a laundry in Neuilly covering six arpents (about six
acres); one day the owner was asked how much he wanted for it
and named what seemed to him the very high figure of 30,000
francs; to his astonishment the potential buyer said: “Thirty
thousand francs per arpent seems a bit much,” and then paid him
               The Slump of 1830: Battle with Rothschild       igi

six times 30,000 francs. To Ouvrard this boom seemed utter
lunacy, for he could see no possibility of property rents rising
correspondingly.
   Ouvrard was proved right. In fact, his forecast of the conver¬
sion came true remarkably quickly. Instead of 150 million francs
only 30 million could be converted. Ouvrard had always main¬
tained that the 5 per cent rente was not high enough. Now the
new 3 per cent issue slowly collapsed: from 75 to 70, from 70 to
68, from 68 to 63, to 62. The Rothschilds came hurrying to
Paris from all points of the compass, till four brothers stood
together beside the grave of a beautiful dream.
   The year was 1825. There were no major crashes, the political
situation remained stable, Europe stood firm, and yet Ouvrard
was still filled with misgiving. The remarkably inept behaviour
of the Bourbons justified his pessimism. In 1814, when Louis
entered Paris, there were tremendous scenes of rejoicing. The
streets were so festooned that whole rows of houses were no
longer visible; more than 20,000 members of the National Guard
carried flowers on their muskets; the “Te Deum” in Notre-Dame
was immensely impressive and moving. “Only those who were
actually present,” said Rochechouart, “could have any idea of
the jubilation, the acclamation and the many signs of quite
insensate joy, which Paris displayed.” Napoleon’s dictatorship
was over, and in its place was a king who, since 4th June 1814,
had promised a constitution which would guarantee the rights of
the people. But Louis died ten years later and Charles X, Comte
d’Artois, was out of sympathy with the whole trend of events.
Although parliament had become completely reactionary under
de Villele’s leadership, its mere existence disturbed the King, who
felt that the aristocracy, which had long since resumed the habits
and privileges of the ancien regime, must nevertheless be given
an even stronger position; the position of the clergy was also
strengthened as never before, so that, for example, army chap¬
lains were promoted to higher rank than divisional commanders
and made regular reports on the conduct of individual officers,
which the War Minister frequently consulted before recom¬
mending promotion.
192                          Ouvrard

   The power of the Jesuits grew by leaps and bounds; a law was
passed which imposed the same penalty for sacrilege as for parri¬
cide. The freedom of the Press and scientific development were
increasingly restricted. The feeling of resentment in Paris grew
from year to year. In 1827, at the parade on the Champ de Mars,
the obsequious servants of the King were greeted with cries of
“Down with the Ministers”, “Down with Villele”, “Down with
the Jesuits” from the ranks of the National Guard. The response
was not long in coming: the National Guard, the citizens’
army of Paris, was disbanded. Before another two years had
passed, even Villele’s Cabinet of diehard royalists was no longer
good enough for the King. Ouvrard, who was still probably the
only bear speculator in Paris, was not at all displeased by the
growing tension. He certainly expressed his satisfaction when his
arch-enemy had to make way for Prince de Polignac, an Ultra of
the deepest dye, even although the change came six years too
late for Ouvrard.
   All Ouvrard’s misgivings throughout these years, vague as
they were, were now substantiated. In August 1829 Polignac
and his friends seized power; in May 1830 the unco-operative
Chamber was dissolved; in mid-July the election results were
declared, showing that at most 125 out of 428 deputies supported
the government. What followed could not have been foreseen
by even the shrewdest of bear speculators: the King and his
Ministers agreed on a coup d’etat. Behind locked doors they
decided to dissolve the Chamber before it reassembled, to change
the electoral law by royal decree, to fill the parliament with
King’s men, and to ban all newspapers and periodicals which did
not have a special licence.
   The King and his Ministers were agreed that, if this calculated
coup was to succeed, then the element of surprise was essential.
So all rumours of a coup, which no one seriously believed, were
ironically shrugged off by the government. Mid-July passed in
an atmosphere of general unrest, no one knowing what would
happen with the resumption of parliament. But few doubted
that it would resume.
   The only one who had doubts was Ouvrard. He had been at

liberty for six months and was once more extremely well in¬
formed, better informed than the Ministers’ closest friends. The
Public Prosecutor, Bellart, once said that Ouvrard knew more
about the Ministries than the Ministers themselves. This was no
exaggeration, although for the moment he knew no more than
the Ministers, but that was quite enough.
  It is said that the London Rothschild was present at the
battlefield of Waterloo. As soon as he saw that the tide had
turned against the French, he set out hotfoot for the Channel,
and, after a stormy crossing, arrived at the Stock Exchange in
London in time to make a handsome profit. (Ouvrard was also
present at Waterloo, as Purveyor General. “I was there,” he
related subsequently with obvious gusto, “and witnessed the
finest stage drama I have ever seen.”) Fifteen years later, in 1830,
Ouvrard made hotfoot for the Channel and arrived at the
London Stock Exchange in time to set the ball rolling.
  His moment of revenge had come at last, the moment when
either bull or bear, Rothschild or Ouvrard, must come out on top.
One fine day in July—it must have been about the 20th—when
Nathan Rothschild entered the London Stock Exchange at his
usual time and at his usual unhurried pace, he attracted even more
attention than normal. What would he do? There was Ouvrard, the
Paris financier, selling French rentes at steadily falling prices, a
transaction in those same State bonds in which the Rothschilds
held such a high stake. It was, after all, the Rothschild brothers
who, only a few months before, had underwritten the last French
Government loan, which was to pay for the Algerian campaign.
There   were   78,300,000    at   102   per   cent   and   Rothschild
had met with strong competition from other firms. Nathan
Rothschild was completely at a loss to understand what Ouvrard
was up to. His firm had an exclusive news service and, if there
were any grounds for alarm in Paris, his brother James would
certainly have informed him with all possible speed. It was only
a few days later, when Ouvrard still continued to sell million
after million on surrender, without himself possessing the scrip,
that Nathan became worried. Not that there was much he could
do now. If he was to prevent the exchange-rate of the rentes from
194                           Ouvrard

collapsing completely, then he had to buy what Ouvrard was
offering. Ever since 1824 Ouvrard had been waiting for just
such a “rare opportunity to find a solvent opponent on a bear
market”.
  Which of the two was correctly informed? Nathan or Ouvrard?
As James had still not sent any message, Nathan despatched an
express courier to Paris. James was no less bewildered than
Nathan. If Ouvrard was acting so confidently, then he must have
his reasons, but he himself knew of none. Although the 25 th
July was a Sunday, James leapt into his carriage and drove to the
Ministry of the Interior. He implored the Minister to tell him the
truth about the official decrees, of which there was so much talk,
and about the resumption of parliament, on which there was some
doubt. James reminded the Minister that as State banker he had
certain responsibilities and that he must be informed of any
political plans before anyone else. The Minister said that he could
not understand how such a shrewd man as Rothschild could
become so excited over baseless rumours.        The government
knew of nothing, was planning nothing and feared nothing.
Moreover, the Prince de Polignac himself had given the Russian
Ambassador, Pozzo di Borgo, on Saturday night his most solemn
assurance that no major development was contemplated.
  The following morning, Monday, 26th July 1830, the official
decrees were printed in the Moniteur. Only a few hours before
Rothschild saw the Minister of the Interior, Charles X, having
attended High Mass, had put his name to the suspension of the
constitution. The King and his Court took the precaution of
retiring to St. Cloud, outside the gates of Paris. “The citizens
of Paris,” Marshal Marmont once remarked, “always see them¬
selves as heroes in time of peace.” Marmont, commander of the
Paris garrison, learned to his cost in July 1830 how misplaced
his bon mot had been. On Tuesday the 27th Paris realized
that it either had to fight or lose its freedom and its civic
rights.
   The struggle began with clashes in the Rue St. Honore, the
street in which Robespierre had once lived. Gendarmes were
pelted with stones and opened fire. A state of siege was declared.
               The Slump of 1830: Battle with Rothschild       195

Troops were brought in to occupy key-points in the capital: the
Boulevard des Capucines, the Place du Carrousel, the Pont Neuf,
the Place Vendome and the Place de la Bastille. The mob felled
trees in the boulevards and set up barricades. The soldiers were
bombarded with slates torn off the roofs. The white flags of the
Bourbons were trampled in the gutter. “Down with the Bour¬
bons.” A murderous hail of bullets poured down on the troops
from the windows. Whole columns were cut off and massacred.
The mob took a special delight in storming and plundering the
Archbishop’s Palace; a Jesuit monastery was the next target.
Such was the fury of the mob that 12,000 regular soldiers were
thrown back. Many isolated groups and three complete regiments
joined forces with the civilians. Marmont concentrated the
remainder around the Louvre and Tuileries, but they too had to
be abandoned. The Marshal withdrew his troops, leaving over
1,000 dead behind, more than twice as many in three days as
had been killed during the whole Spanish War of 1823.
  The Court, which remained as optimistic as ever, issued only
one order; namely, that the royal treasure should be taken to St.
Cloud. But on 30th July even Charles X saw the red light: he
rescinded the official decrees, but it was three days too late. On
2nd August he was forced to abdicate and flee to England. Prince
de Polignac was caught disguised as a servant and was condemned
to lifelong imprisonment. Louis Philippe, Due d’Orleans, a
liberal, ascended the throne. Soon all that the people of France
remembered of Charles X’s reign and of his Ministers was a
series of amusing and not very flattering caricatures.
  Marshal Marmont was not the only one who was impressed by
the energy and singlemindedness of the citizens of Paris in their
fight for freedom. Ouvrard, still in London, must also have been
surprised and delighted. The effect on the price of French State
bonds exceeded even his wildest hopes. On the first day the rente
dropped only 4 points in Paris; the Dauphin remarked confidently
at St. Cloud: “It will go up again.” But it dropped another 20,
then 30. The 3 per cent loan, which had stood at 75-80 before the
coup, now' stood at 50. By December it had pulled up to 58^,
but by February 1831 it was down to 48. In March it was still
196                          Ouvrard

only worth 53*20, in June it had risen to 60, but only ten years
after the Revolution did it return to normal at 73.
  The Rothschild brothers were beside themselves. Solomon
and James had remained in Paris throughout the troubles, not
merely their money but also their lives in jeopardy. As financial
advisers to the old regime, they were afraid of being lynched. But
no one molested them. Their financial losses had, on the other
hand, been very heavy; when the house of Rothschild in London,
Paris and Frankfurt worked out the extent of the damage, it
came to thirty-four million francs.
  A substantial part of this money went into Ouvrard’s pocket.
His great ambition, as he explained in 1824 to the London banker
Sir Isaac Goldsmid, had been “to divert Rothschild’s money
legally into our safe”. The bonds, which he had sold at short rates
before the Paris coup to Nathan Rothschild—who was compelled,
however reluctantly, to back them—he was now able to buy
cheaply in order to hand them over to their “rightful owner”.
  The duel was over and Ouvrard returned to Paris. It is im¬
possible to say how much he had gained. One of his firm’s bill-
brokers estimated his net profit at two million francs, but it
must have been considerably more. In any case, Ouvrard was
once more the man of the hour, the lion of the Bourse. The
Conciergerie was forgotten; his memories of Monsieur Seguin
and the debtors’ prison had faded. He rented a suite of rooms
behind the Bourse and furnished them, according to contemporary
reports, “with indescribable lavishness”. It became a sort of ante¬
chamber for agents de change and a host of acquaintances, who
came from near and far to see the great man in his sanctum in the
hope of picking up a useful tip.
   But Ouvrard, never the sedentary type, was soon back in
London. The Rothschilds followed his every movement with a
suspicion born of bitter experience. “The famous Ouvrard”,
Nathan wrote to Solomon on 5 th March 1831, “today bought a
great many stocks, so tomorrow will probably bring better
reports from Paris.” He clearly found it difficult to make this man
out, for he was now engaged in bull-buying at a time when
Austria’s armed intervention in Italy had led everyone else to sell.
                 The Slump of 1830: Battle with Rothschild             ip/



Yet nine days later, on 14th March, he was back in Paris, indulging
once more in bear operations. “Casarus,* Ouvrard’s illegitimate
son [by Madame Tallien], is selling rentes”, James wrote to
Solomon in Vienna. “This is frightening people from buying,
and any slight drop immediately has a damping effect on their
spirits.”
   Many other letters in the same vein passed from London to Paris,
from Paris to Vienna or Frankfurt, and reflected the anxiety which
the Rothschild brothers felt about Ouvrard’s activities. They were
afraid of him, because he had beaten them. They still bore the
marks of the lion’s claws. And yet there could be no doubt that
the centre of financial power in France was no longer Ouvrard
but Rothschild. The past ten years of Ouvrard’s life had taken too
heavy a toll; his five years in prison had drained his energy. He
would never again completely recover from the innumerable set¬
backs he had suffered. He would still try his hand at various
schemes; finding a husband for the Duchesse de Berry in 1833,!
raising a loan for Don Carlos in Spain in 1836—but these gestures
lacked the breathtaking grandeur which had characterized so
many of his transactions up to the July revolution. From now on
Ouvrard was more or less confined, and with varying degrees of
success, to gambling on the Stock Exchange.
   When Ouvrard reached this turning-point in his life he was
sixty years old.
