                 The Last Quarter-Million




FrOM the time Ouvrard abandoned politics, his tracks become
hard to follow. He was playing the market now here, now there;
at one moment in London, at another in Paris, depending upon
which of the two places offered the best prospects in any par¬
ticular deal. But finally Ouvrard appears to have overcome his
restlessness to the point of taking up permanent residence in
London.
  It was in London that a tragi-comedy of speculation began,
which showed that not only the Rothschilds had reason to curse
the French rente. In 1840 it was the turn of Ouvrard after his
resounding, much admired victory in 1830. The story, which is
of slight importance in itself, must be told because it revealed him
in an entirely different light from the generous and intelligent
man one has met so far. Ouvrard had worked out a new form of
margin-trading in rentes. It could only succeed, however, if he
himself remained in the background. Every move he made was
still being watched for market trends.
   It was by no means a simple operation. More than one middle¬
man was necessary to guarantee secrecy and all his reliable
nominees were well known. Finally Ouvrard thought he had hit
upon a solution. Looking back afterwards, he must often have
bitterly regretted his carelessness, for soon he was to meet in the
court room the very men whom he chose as his agents. Legal
records, still extant, enable us to reconstruct these events quite
accurately.
  Ouvrard began by transferring a considerable sum of money
                                198
                       The Last Quarter-Million                    jpp
from London to one of the lesser Paris banks. But not in his own
name. He used the name of an Englishman, Francis de Berkem,
who was a bill-broker on the London Exchange, and who, as a
safety measure, was to open the deal himself in Paris and there
to introduce the bankers, Louis d’Eichtal et Fils, to their new
customer. So Mr. de Berkem arrived in Paris in July 1840 and
started with a minor rente operation. He made use of a local
broker, a certain Textoris, whose security was the credit balance
with Eichtal et Fils. Berkem then departed and Ouvrard himself
set off from London via Brussels, carrying a letter from de Berkem
to Eichtal et Fils, authorizing the bearer to carry on where he had
left off. The current credit account was, of course, still in Berkem’s
name.
  The first thing Ouvrard did on reaching Paris on 18th August
was to fill in the blank space in the letter with the name Paul de la
Gironniere, a gentleman of private means. In what dark corner
he found this second man of straw with the beautiful name is not
known. He installed him in his own hotel in Paris where he
would be readily available and could not make any stupid mistakes,
for Ouvrard was clearly more concerned about his money than
about the personal comfort of his hireling. Monsieur de la
Gironniere did not, in fact, make any stupid mistakes but he got
up to some tricks which were not at all to Ouvrard’s liking.
  To begin with, however, it was the broker Textoris who gave
Ouvrard trouble. The returns for the month of August were
satisfactory to all concerned, but a new deal which started in the
beginning of September came to an abrupt and rather disreputable
end. Textoris was summoned to the Hotel de Paris, where de la
Gironniere gave him written instructions, signed by himself but
dictated by Ouvrard, to buy 120,000 francs’ worth of French
3 per cent rentes, nominal value four million francs, as soon as
the premium rate stood at fifty centimes. It was such a simple
operation that Ouvrard, who in any case did not want to be seen
at the Bourse, took a few days off and spent them with his son
Julien at his Chateau Gilly and with his daughter, the Comtesse
de Rochechouart, at Chatillon-sur-Seine.
  He thought it safer to take de la Gironniere with him. But he
200                          Ouvrard

was sadly mistaken in thinking that all was well, for at Chatillon
de la Gironniere received a letter from Textoris on ioth Sep¬
tember, in which he explained, with every appearance of inno¬
cence, the extraordinary way in which he had carried out his
instructions. He had bought 120,000 francs’ worth of rentes, at
rates of 77-50-78-25, although the closing price, “due to a panic,
the reason for which I could not ascertain”, was 73-50. The best
part of it was that Textoris ignored the strict instructions given
him to buy only at a premium of fifty centimes and paid a one-
franc premium.
   Ouvrard was beside himself. Not only was there no hope of
making a profit but there was a real danger of losing money.
He immediately returned to Paris and wrote to London to de
Berkem, through whose account the whole business was passing,
that he should refuse to recognize the deal as it was contrary to
instructions. Strangely enough, however, he apparently did not
tell de la Gironniere to lodge a protest with Textoris. He may
have thought that, by some fluke, he would still break even on
settlement of the September accounts and that, in any case, he
had Textoris well under control. Ouvrard certainly assumed that
he was covered. Textoris had exceeded his instructions and had
therefore acted without authority. Moreover, the whole opera¬
tion, in Ouvrard’s view, was a swindle, as Textoris had not, in
actual fact, bought at 77-50-78-25 but at the closing price of
73-50. He was hoping to use the difference to make good, at
Ouvrard’s expense, losses he had previously incurred. The truth
would come out, as soon as Textoris was made to present his
books and the proofs of purchase!
  Legally there is no doubt that Textoris had exceeded his in¬
structions and that the rente purchase was not binding on the
person who had issued the instructions. Unfortunately Ouvrard
had overlooked one small point; namely, that it was not he or
Francis de Berkem in London who had formally issued the in¬
structions but Paul de la Gironniere. And de la Gironniere now
started to be awkward. He suddenly decided to act on his own.
He could afford to do so. Textoris, it is true, had paid Ouvrard
100,000 francs, but that was before the dispute began, and the
                      The Last Quarter-Million                 201

money had been used for further speculation, again in Gironniere’s
name though on Ouvrard’s instructions and this time without
the help of Textoris.
  For a swindler it was a very happy situation and, when the
time came to settle the September accounts, Textoris had no
difficulty in persuading Gironniere to wind up the whole affair,
including the disastrous September speculation, in defiance of
Ouvrard, which meant recognizing the rente purchase at high
rates and with one-franc premiums, which had been directly
contrary to Ouvrard’s instructions. The sum involved was
227,220 francs. Ouvrard tried to get some kind of temporary
injunction but he was too late. An hour before he entered Tex-
toris’s office with the legal document in his hand, Gironniere
had received the balance of 22,324-75 francs from Textoris and
moved hurriedly out of the Hotel de Paris.
   Ouvrard was, to all intents and purposes, out on the street. He
had no control over de Berkem’s account with Eichtal et Fils,
in other words he could not draw on his own account. Nor did
he have any evidence of the 227,220 francs owed him by Textoris
and Gironniere, for the latter had absconded with it. Gironniere
had not even paid Ouvrard the first 100,000 francs, which Textoris
had refunded on 20th September in Ouvrard’s presence and
which Ouvrard had re-invested in Gironniere’s name.
   Ouvrard was compelled to start on a long and wearisome
journey through the courts. He brought a simultaneous action
against Textoris and Gironniere. As far as Textoris was con¬
cerned, he had known by the end of September at the latest that
de la Gironniere was only acting as Ouvrard’s man of straw and
consequently had no authority to settle with him. As for his
original purchase, that was to be declared null and void, because
Textoris had contravened instructions and had fraudulently
misrepresented the transaction, which was completely fictitious.
De la Gironniere should be condemned to make good all losses
incurred and to give a full account of the whole business, in
particular to refund the first 100,000 francs. He should be made
to repay immediately the balance of 22,324 francs which he had
misappropriated.
202                          Ouvrard

  To Ouvrard’s dismay, the court decided on 23rd July 1841
that the Gironniere-Textoris settlement was in order. Textoris
could not be expected to know that he was dealing with Ouvrard
and not with de la Gironniere, as the latter put his signature to
hundreds of thousands of francs and issued all instructions.
The rente purchase of 9th September, however, was held to be
genuine, as the books presented by Textoris provided document¬
ary evidence that the purchase had been made at the high rates
stated. There was no reason to suspect fraud. As far as exceeding
the instructions was concerned—buying at a premium of one
franc instead of fifty centimes—this could only be the subject
of legal dispute between the real contracting parties, Textoris
and Gironniere. Monsieur de la Gironniere had lodged no com¬
plaint. The judges were unable to form a sufficiently clear
picture of Gironniere’s obligations towards Ouvrard, and both
parties were advised to try to reach a settlement out of court with
the help of a third party nominated by the court. The judges
found that Ouvrard should pay all costs.
   Although he was now over seventy, Ouvrard was still as
tough as ever and refused to take the very explicit judgement of
the court lying down. In the appeal submitted by his lawyers
in April 1842, he once more went into the attack, all guns firing
and trained upon the whole of Paris: “Justice cannot be fobbed off
with words and pretexts. She must have facts, and she knows how
to assess them. She must have the truth. She seeks it with end¬
less care and persistence, and it is hard to evade her searching
enquiries.”
   This was the last court-case in which Ouvrard fought for
high stakes, with the help of French advocates, with all the cut
and thrust of legal dialectic and with prodigious pathos. Unfor¬
tunately it is not known whether Dame Truth displayed in the
court of appeal those features which Ouvrard would have liked
her to display. But, to whichever side the scales were finally
tipped, Ouvrard, having been forced to admit the validity of that
foolhardy purchase of rentes, doubtless considered himself
fortunate when he recovered the amount Paul de la Gironniere
had received from Textoris.
                       The Last Quarter-Million                  203

  Such, then, was the outcome of the ambitious, carefully planned
gamble, which       had brought Ouvrard hurrying across from
London to Paris and for which he had enlisted the services of
two “dummies” and a broker. His enemies, of whom he had
many right up to his death, must have rubbed their hands at what
they regarded as an act of poetic justice; that the old fox, who had
stolen so many grapes in his lifetime, should at last have fallen
into a trap. But, had Ouvrard written his memoirs in 1842 instead
of 1826, he would surely have maintained, with a profound and
genuine sense of grievance, that this trap had also shown how
almost all his carefully laid and honestly devised schemes had
come to grief on the baseness of his agents. The impartial observer,
on the other hand, can only express his astonishment that a specu¬
lator of such genius should have made his last bow in such a
pitiable fashion.
                            Fourteen




                    The Final Reckoning




SlX years after the abortive deal in rentes, Ouvrard settled his
final account. He died in London in October 1846. There is
no record of those last hours when he had to leave the life to
which he had been so attached.
  Ouvrard’s death passed unnoticed. His contemporaries were
unmoved and, in fact, uninterested. Sixteen years had passed
since he had fought his last major battle in July 1830, and during
those years he had grown old. He had outlived his time. Friends
and enemies had died before him. Madame de Montesson, the
charming old lady whose circle of acquaintances had proved
invaluable to him, died in 1806; Josephine, the second of the
women who had helped him, in 1814. Berthier, Napoleon’s
chief of staff, through whose friendship he had come by untold
wealth, threw himself out of a window in Bamberg in 1815. His
wife died in 1818. Vanlerberghe, who had been both friend and
ally, succumbed, a broken man, in 1819. Fouche followed him
in 1820. Napoleon, who was only a year older than Ouvrard,
died in 1821, and Richelieu in 1822. In 1824 it was the turn of
Cambaceres; in 1826 Ouvrard lost his youngest daughter. Barras
survived at the Chateau of Chaillot near Paris in bourgeois
splendour until 1829. Madame Tallien, Comtesse de Caraman,
Princesse de Chimay, passed away in Brussels in 1836 at the
age of sixty, followed the same year by Seguin. The same year
the news of Nathan Rothschild’s death sent a shock of disbelief
through the London Exchange. Barbe-Marbois hung on, as old
                                 204
                        The Final Reckoning                    205

as Methuselah, until 1837. Talleyrand, who had been French
Ambassador in London since 1830 and had led such a full life,
died, at the ripe age of eighty-four, in 1838. The Bourbon chief
of staff, Comte Guilleminot, was cultivating his garden until
1840, and in 1841 Ouvrard had the satisfaction of seeing the
War Minister, Bellune, carried to his last resting-place. Only
Alexander Baring survived Ouvrard by two years.
  So   Ouvrard outlived not only four different regimes—
Republic, Consulate, Empire and Bourbon Monarchy—but also
all the leading men of his day with whom he had had dealings.
It was little wonder that he soon became a spent force, that he
looked round in vain for familiar landmarks. When he died in
1846 Ouvrard had seventy-six phenomenally eventful years
behind him. What did he get out of them? His financial position
at the end remains something of a mystery. Three diarists, who
knew him personally, have spoken of the hardships of his closing
years. When Nolte met Ouvrard in 1835 in Rome, he described
him as neatly but shabbily dressed. Captain Gronow even claims
that the rente speculation of 1830, which began so well, finally
ruined Ouvrard, because he was reckoning that Louis Philippe
would soon go and that another slump would follow. The
Comtesse de Boigne states quite categorically that Ouvrard died
a poor man. Yet he can hardly have died a pauper in London. A
few years before he had visited his son at his chateau at Gilly,
which was almost certainly a present from his father. And if
his brother was able to live in another chateau at Douy, then it
was probably as Ouvrard’s nominee. His daughter, the Comtesse
de Rochechouart, also had her chateau at Chatillon-sur-Seine. In
1840 he could still gamble with a quarter of a million francs, so
that his private fortune must have been considerably larger.
Ouvrard does not appear to have fallen into the same desperate
straits as Law, who in his declining years had to pawn his diamond
ring and, when he died, left only a few pictures.
  One thing is certain: Ouvrard lost a great deal of money. Yet
the countless millions he lost always seemed to him merely a
step towards further gains. With his imperialist vision, he aimed
at heights of financial power, which he only achieved for brief
206                            Ouvrard

periods. Nothing came of the financial dynasty, which he dreamt
of establishing on the Baring model. His son, Dr. Cabarrus, who
had a great talent for commerce, did not bear his name but the
maiden name of his mother, Madame Tallien; as for his only
legitimate son, Julien Ouvrard, all that he could achieve, with
the help of his father’s money, was a seat in the chamber, which
led to some kind of administrative post on its Finance Committee.
Fate also denied Ouvrard many of the public honours which
were heaped upon other successful financiers such as the Roths¬
childs. And yet few of these men, who were given such pref¬
erential treatment, ennobled and covered with honours, could
stand serious comparison with him. They lacked his tremendous
vitality. They lacked his vision. Few of them possessed his mental
agility. He surpassed them all in charm and courtesy. Some made
even higher profits, but no one could compare with him for sheer
brilliance.
  At the same time, Ouvrard was sadly mistaken when he
ascribed the failure of his schemes to external circumstances alone,
to lack of ability in his partners or malice in his adversaries. He
himself was the final arbiter of success or failure. He trusted all
too much in his star, which had led him to such dizzy heights in
the ten short years from the beginning of the Revolution to the
end of the Directory. Like his great adversary, Napoleon, he
finally lost all sense of reality, of what was really possible. Specu¬
lation on a big scale gave him so much personal enjoyment that
the success he looked for was out of all proportion, whereas he
greatly underrated the difficulties. He became obsessed with
more and more astronomical figures and chose to ignore the
relatively confined space in which business had to be conducted.
And when he could no longer turn a blind eye to confines of
reality, he tried to employ political weapons, of which he had
no experience, in order to widen the field of commerce. His main
weakness, however, was that he lacked an interest in detail which
would have curbed his passion for expansion; that his tremendous
capacity for imaginative planning was not matched by an equal
ability to execute the practical details of his plans. So inevitably
the scales were weighted against Ouvrard as they had been
                        The Final Reckoning                     207

twenty-five years before against Napoleon Bonaparte. Both men
failed magnificently, but the result—from a commercial and a
social viewpoint—was a fiasco.
  Yet in 1846 Ouvrard could look back on a life that had been
remarkably full and successful, a life of frequent, outstanding
triumphs. He was also sufficient of a philosopher not to allow his
setbacks to blind him to the richness and variety of his life. Sit¬
ting at his English fireside in those closing months, he could
review many decades in which he had not only witnessed but
also helped to shape a momentous period of world history. And
the parting was not made any easier by the knowledge that one
final, splendid throw could still be made. For the time bad
almost come to make good any losses he may have suffered in
1830, when he is said to have miscalculated the length of Louis
Philippe’s reign. The year 1848, in which Louis Philippe actually
lost his throne, was already throwing out faint shadows, and
Ouvrard was certainly contemplating yet another major flutter
when death decided otherwise. But even if he had scored one
further resounding success, it would not have changed the overall
picture of his life. When death closed the account in October 1846
the final statement had already been drawn up.
                            Epilogue

     In the original {German) edition of this book., Wolff’s
     collaborator, Dr. Alfred L. Schmit{, of Berlin, outlined
     some of the sources for Ouvrard’s life. In order to make
     room for the additional material that is now available to
     historians, the original Epilogue has been abbreviated.
                                              —T.A.B.C.



It has been possible to tell the story of Ouvrard’s life as it is
told here, with its many dramatic episodes and frequent flashes
of comedy, largely because of the memoirs which Ouvrard wrote
in prison and published in 1826-7. This three-volume work,
running to close on a thousand pages, is often breathtaking and
at times almost incredible. It astonished contemporaries just as
it astonishes us; many of them, indeed, were highly embarrassed
at the candid revelations contained therein. However, the fact
that it ran to three editions in two years without evoking any
serious refutation from those involved in the events described
or mentioned by name who were still alive, suggests that the
memoirs are basically reliable.
  This hypothesis has been confirmed as a result of checking
Ouvrard’s account against the most trustworthy supporting
evidence, which falls into two categories: on the one hand, the
official archives and, on the other, the correspondence of eminent
men of repute.
  The archives show, for instance, that during the Terror he
did undoubtedly destroy the list of condemned men at Nantes;
an anonymous note to this effect has survived. They also amply
confirm the prominent role he played as supply agent to the
Directory’s armies and the vast sums that passed through his
hands as naval victualler-in-chief. Napoleon’s correspondence
                                  208
                              Epilogue                           209

similarly helps to chart the vicissitudes of the Emperor’s relation¬
ship with the financier whom he could never entirely do without.
Among the documents included are the demand for repayment
of fourteen million francs in 1800 when the supply contracts with
Ouvrard were renewed, a further claim by the Emperor for com¬
mission on the export of grain to relieve the Spanish famine of
1804, and a reference to Ouvrard’s memorandum of 1810 out¬
lining his peace proposals vis-a-vis Britain.
   Ouvrard’s account of the Mexican silver affair is supplemented
by the long reports from Vincent Nolte, Hope’s agent in New
Orleans, the accuracy of which Dr. Schmitz was able to verify
in 1931 from the Hope Archives in Amsterdam. The despatches
and letters of the agent in Philadelphia, David Parish, are also
of value; see P. G. and R. Walters: “The American Career of
David Parish”, Journal of Economic History (1943-4). The
various phases of the transaction are chronicled in R. W. Hidy:
The House of Baring in American Trade and Finance (1949).
The British Government’s attitude to the affair is still “little
known and obscure”, according to F. Crouzet in Heconomie
britannique et le blocus continental 1806—1813 (1958). He found
next to nothing of relevance in the British official archives after
an exhaustive search, apart from an interesting memorandum
from Baring to Pitt and Huskisson. M. Crouzet plans to devote
an article to the whole subject, but this does not seem to have
been published as yet.
   Another Frenchman who worked in both the British and
French government archives was P. Coquelle, the translation
of whose important work appeared in 1904 as Napoleon and
England 1803-1813, and who investigated fully the peace negotia¬
tions of 1810 with Britain. Talleyrand’s complicity in these
negotiations was accepted by G. Lacour-Gayet in volume II
of Talleyrand (1930). The memoirs of Pasquier and Savary, Due
de Rovigo, also provide supplementary evidence.
   Ouvrard’s services in bringing about the early evacuation
of France by Allied occupation forces, through arranging the
series of government loans, have been confirmed by letters
from Richelieu, the Prime Minister, and the memoirs of the
210                           Epilogue

Comte de Rochechouart and of the Comtesse de Boigne. The
latter was the daughter of the French Ambassador in London,
d’Osmond, and described Ouvrard as “the most capable if not
the most honest of financiers”. Hidy: House of Baring, gives
useful details of the various loans.
   There is a vast fund of contemporary material on the Bayonne
scandal of 1823 to set against Ouvrard’s account. Relevant
memoirs include those of Castellane, Pasquier and Villele. Some
interesting British diplomatic reports on Ouvrard’s proposals
that year for a “West Indies Company” are contained in C. K.
Webster’s Britain and the Independence of Latin America (1938).
The protracted legal wrangles between Ouvrard, Seguin and the
State have left in their trail many documents, particularly the
reports in the Gazette des tribunaux.
  Ouvrard died too obscurely for his death to be noted in The
Times, the Annual Register or the Gentlemans Magafme for
1846, nor was a will proved for him at Somerset House. An entry
for his son Dr. Cabarrus is included in the Dicdonnaire de bio-
graphie fran$aise (1956); this work has not yet progressed beyond
the letter C.
  An important annotated article, overlooked by Wolff, is
G. Weill’s “Le financier Ouvrard”, in the Revue Historique for
1918. J. Savant’s Tel fut Ouvrard (1954), although written in a
popular style, is on the whole reliable and is amply provided with
reference notes. He attributes Ouvrard’s failure, in spite of ex¬
ceptional gifts, to reach the summit of success—which is generally
put down to his unmethodical cast of mind—to a lack of vanity;
he also emphasizes the way in which Ouvrard shunned publicity
and the limelight in order to give the best possible chance to his
audacious schemes.
  A. Arthur-Levy’s Un grandprofiteur de guerre (1929—extracts
were published in the Revue de Paris, August and September
1929) was described by D. S. Landes in Bankers and Pashas (1958),
p. 44m, as “simply a frothy popularization”; it misleadingly depicts
Ouvrard as a greedy war profiteer whose memoirs are a tissue of
untruths and historically worthless. In contrast to that of Wolff,
neither   Savant’s   nor   Arthur-Levy’s   biography   makes    any
                              Epilogue                           211

attempt to assess Ouvrard’s role in relation to the domestic and
international events of his time. Landes’s own brief studies, in
Chapter 2 ol Bankers and Pashas, of the psychology of the
“prudent, thrifty, discreet” nineteenth-century merchant bankers
who firmly closed their ranks against a “maverick” such as
Ouvrard, and of Ouvrard himself doggedly “opposing their
wealth with his imagination, their influence with his energy and
persuasiveness, their self-righteous complacency with his mer¬
curial ambition” are well worth reading.
  Of general histories that mention Ouvrard, G. Lefebvre:
Napoleon (3rd edn. 1947), volume XIV in the Peuples et Civilisa¬
tions series, skilfully and concisely explores all aspects—including
the economic one—of Napoleon’s reign; there is no substitute
for this superb and lapidary work. L. Madelin’s Histoire du
Consulat et de VEmpire (1937—54), on the other hand, runs to
sixteen volumes and is seriously defective on the economic and
social sides. Volume IX, for instance, entitled La crise de VEmpire
1810-11, completely ignores the vitally important         industrial
crisis of those years; for other criticisms of the whole work see
the notes to Savant’s book.
  Volume IV of M. Marion: Histoire financiere de la France
depuis 1715 (1925), covers the years 1797-1818; his account of the
financial panic of 1805 is especially valuable. He quotes extracts
from Barbe-Marbois’s letters to Napoleon, including the one of
21st August 1805 which may have caused Napoleon to abandon
his attempt to invade England and attack Austria instead. The
industrial crisis of 1810-11 in the context of the Emperor’s
practical efforts to foster French industry is described by O.
Viennet in Napoleon et VIndustrie firanfaise (1947).
  Not surprisingly, Ouvrard’s colourful career has inspired two
novels: directly, E. J. Tobi’s Speculant onder Napoleon (Amster¬
dam 1950, apparently never translated into English), and in¬
directly Hervey Allen’s Anthony Adverse (New York, 1933;
London, 1956). The hero of the latter novel was portrayed as
Hope’s agent in New Orleans during the Mexican silver affair.
                              Appendixes



     OUVRARD’s VERSION OF THE TRADING CONTRACT WITH CHARLES IV
     OF 26TH NOVEMBER 1804 COVERING TRADE WITH THE SPANISH
            COLONIES. (OUVRARD’s MEMOIRS, VOL. I, PP. 95-6)



   1. Deed of partnership between H.M. Charles IV and the firm of Ouvrard
& Co. for the duration of the Anglo-Spanish war.
  2. The firm receives permission for the duration of the war to import all
goods and foodstuffs into all ports in the New World and, for the same
period, to export from these colonies all indigenous products and the total
output of gold and silver.
   3. H.M. C(harles IV) promises to grant the firm all the necessary con¬
cessions for the despatch of ships from Europe to America, on the basis of
demands made by myself as sole responsible director of the firm.
   4. The profits accruing from all the firm’s transactions will be divided
equally between H.M. C(harles IV) and M. Ouvrard.


                                   II

     TEXT   OF   SUPPLEMENTARY AGREEMENT TO        CONTRACT    OF   26TH
     NOVEMBER 1804 COVERING TRADE WITH THE SPANISH COLONIES.
                 (OUVRARD’S MEMOIRS, VOL. II, PP. 338-42)



Article I. The firm Frangois Ouvrard is obliged to convey to Europe all
  goods and colonial products, which H.M. Charles desires to have exported
  from America. The agents of H.M. C(harles) will hold the goods ready for
  the firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co. in the ports of Santa-Cruz, Havana,
  Caracas and Montevideo and will deliver them to the representatives of
  the firm Frangois Ouvrard. The firm may claim at least a third of the
  cargoes it ships to Europe. The agents of H.M. are obliged to deliver the
  goods punctually on the dates fixed for sailing; should they not have
  sufficient goods ready by the date of sailing for a complete cargo, then the
  firm Frangois Ouvrard may complete the cargo on its own account.
Article II. The firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co. is further obliged to ship all
  foodstuffs and goods, which H.M.C. should wish to export from Europe
  to the ports of Vera-Cruz, Havana, Caracas and Montevideo. The goods
  in question will be conveyed by H.M.’s agents to the various European
  ports and delivered to the firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co. in good time, so
   that the loading and departure of the ships can be effected without delay.
                                     212
                                 Appendixes                               213

Article III. All goods and foodstuffs, which, in accordance with the two
  previous articles, are shipped from Europe to America and from America
  to Europe for H.M.’s account, must be insured against all risks. The firm
   Frangois Ouvrard & Co. will be entitled to recover the insurance costs.
Article IV. Likewise the firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co. will be reimbursed
   for the transport costs and local incidental expenses, which arise from the
   shipment of goods from Europe to America and vice-versa, in so far as
   they fall upon H.M.’s portion.
Article V. The firm Francois Ouvrard & Co. will sell for H.M.’s account,
   against a commission given below, all goods and foodstuffs which are
   transported from America to the various European ports, with the ex¬
   ception of direct shipments to Spanish ports. The sale must be effected
   immediately after the arrival of the ships at their port of destination,
   and the firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co. must render an account to H.M.
   within one month.
Article VI. In return for these services the firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co.
   receives a commission from H.M.C. which is made up as follows:
    1. One per cent of the total value of all goods shipped from Europe to
       America, which fall to H.M.’s portion. The value will be based on the
       price of the goods in Europe.
   2. One per cent of the total value of all goods and foodstuffs, which
        are shipped on H.M.C.’s account from America to Spain.
    3. Two per cent of the proceeds from the sale of goods or foodstuffs,
        which are transported from America to European ports, with the
        exception of Spanish ports.
Article VII. In Article 14 of the contract of 26th November 1804 the
   commission on shipments of gold and silver from America to Europe on
   H.M.C.’s account is fixed at one per cent of their value. This commission
    is herewith reduced to one half per cent.
Article VIII. Further to Article 18 of the contract of 26th November 1804
   concerning the import and export duties to be paid to H.M.C. by the
   firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co., it is reaffirmed that Ouvrard & Co. must
   pay these duties in full at the standard rates, in accordance with the law
   and with royal decrees. Only those exceptional conditions of payment
    quoted in the said article also remain in force.
Article IX. To facilitate a speedy and smooth fulfilment of the contract, the
   firm Frangois Ouvrard & Co. will take all the necessary steps and bear
   all the costs, to obtain letters of safe-conduct from England.
Article X. The costs incurred in obtaining these letters of safe-conduct,
   in so far as they concern H.M.C.’s share of the shipments, will be carried
   in full by him, the remainder to be met by the firm Frangois Ouvrard
    & Co.
                           Given at Madrid in duplicate
                              on 4th December 1804
                             Frangois Ouvrard & Co.
                              Manuel Sixto Espinosa.
Approved
I, the King.
                                     Ill

     ROYAL DECREE OF CHARLES IV IN 1804 ON THE EXPROPRIATION OF
     RELIGIOUS FOUNDATIONS. (ISSUED AS PART OF OUVRARD’s PLAN
     to clean up Spain’s national finances: ouvrard’s memoirs,
                     VOL. II, pp. 102-5 (unconfirmed))


    “We, by the Grace of God, King etc. On my royal command of 1st
December last year, my Council in the West Indies was sent a copy of the
ordinance of 28th November together with the relevant instructions for the
sale of the real estate owned by all religious foundations in my West Indian
Kingdom and the Philippines. The instructions are as follows:
    “In my royal decree of 19th September 1798 I commanded, for reasons
stated therein, that the real estate owned by all religious foundations should
be sold and the proceeds of their sale paid into the royal amortisation fund.
Interest of three per cent per annum will be charged on their sale value and
this interest will be covered by the revenue from certain customs duties. The
money which is paid into the royal treasury will be employed to settle the
national debts, in particular to redeem the rentes. The rights of the property
owners will be safeguarded both as regards their outstanding liabilities and
their other sources of revenue, on which interest of three per cent will also
be payable. It was not my original intention to make these regulations
applicable to my oversea possessions, but I decided to do so after they had
proved effective and beneficial in Spain both to the religious foundations
themselves, which, freed from all risk, can better fulfil the aims of their
founders, and to the monarchy and my subjects. For these reasons and out of
love for my American subjects, I wish to grant them the same benefits. I
command, therefore, that throughout all my realms overseas the real estate
property of any kind belonging to religious foundations should be sold
and that the proceeds from their sale be paid into my royal amortisation
fund. An appropriate amount of interest will be paid at local rates on the
funds of these foundations. The revenue from certain customs duties, which
were given in detail in my ordinance of 30th August, will serve as security
for these interest payments. This pledge and the solicitude of my Cabinet
are a guarantee that the obligations entered into will be scrupulously ful¬
filled. But in order to provide additional security, I have also pledged the
revenue from the tobacco monopoly and the turnover tax, as well as the
royal funds which are paid into the local treasuries, to indemnify dispossessed
landowners. I leave it to the owners to decide in what form they prefer the
indemnity to be paid. I hereby declare all sales and contracts arising from
the aforesaid ordinance to be exempt from turnover tax and all other taxes
and I call upon the reverend Archbishops, Bishops and Prelates to support
me in the execution of this decree in a manner which I expect of their devotion
and loyalty. This is my royal will, which is to be made known to all con-
                                       214
                                Appendixes                              215

cemed, principally to my West Indian Council, so that it can take all steps
necessary for its fulfilment.

                  Signed by H.M. the King’s own hand
                       on 28th November 1804. a.d.
                            Miguel Cayetano Soler.
Certified copy of the original signed by His Majesty, which is in my posses¬
sion.
                                                   Miguel Cayetano Soler.”


                                    IV

                THE TREATY OF AIX-LA-CHAPELLE OF 1818


Article I. The Troops composing the Army of Occupation shall be with¬
  drawn from the Territory of France by the 30th of November next, or
  sooner, if possible.
Article II. The Strongholds and Fortresses which the said Troops occupy
  shall be given up to Commissioners named for that purpose by His Most
  Christian Majesty, in the state in which they were at the time of their
  occupation, conformably to the IXth Article of the Convention concluded
  in execution of the Vth Article of the Treaty of 20th November, 1815.
Article III. The sum destined to provide for the pay, the equipment, and
  the clothing of the Troops of the Army of Occupation, shall be paid, in
  all cases, up to the 30th of November next, on the same footing on which
  it has existed since the 1st of December, 1817.
Article IV. All the accounts between France and the Allied Powers having
  been regulated and settled, the sum to be paid by France, to complete the
  execution of the IVth Article of the Treaty of 20th November, 1815, is
  definitively fixed at 265,000,000 of francs.
Article V. Of this sum the amount of 100,000,000 effective value, shall be
  paid by Inscriptions of Rentes on the great book of the Public Debt
  of France, bearing interest from the 22nd September, 1818. The said
  Inscriptions shall be received at the rate of the Funds of Monday the 5 th
  of October, 1818.
Article VI. The remaining 165,000,000 shall be paid by nine monthly instal¬
  ments, commencing on the 6th of January next, by Bills on the Houses
  of Hope and Co. and Baring, Brothers and Co., which, as well as the
  Inscriptions of Rentes, mentioned in the above Article, shall be delivered
  to Commissioners of the Courts of Austria, Great Britain, Prussia, and
  Russia, by the Royal Treasury of France, at the time of the complete and
  definitive evacuation of the French Territory.
Article VII. At the same period, the Commissioners of the said Courts shall
  deliver to the Royal Treasury of France, the six Bonds not yet discharged,
  which shall remain in their hands, of the fifteen Bonds delivered con¬
  formably to the Ilnd Article of the Convention concluded for the execu¬
  tion of the IVth Article of the Treaty of 20th November, 1815. The said
216                              Appendixes

  Commissioners shall, at the same time, deliver the Inscription of 7,000,000
  of Rentes, created in virtue of the Vlllth Article of the Said Convention.
Article VIII. The present Convention shall be ratified, and the Ratification
  thereof exchanged at Aix-la-Chapelle, the 9th Day of October, in the
  Year of our Lord 1818.

(L.S.) CASTLEREAGH
(L.S.) WELLINGTON
(L.S.) RICHELIEU

                                      V

      ouvrard’s contract to supply transport vehicles for the
      FRENCH ARMY IN SPAIN    (1823). (THE TRANSPORT CONTRACT WAS
      COMPLEMENTARY TO THE MAIN CONTRACT WHICH COVERED FOOD
           SUPPLIES.) OUVRARD’S MEMOIRS, VOL. II, PP.       354-63.


   Between the undersigned: M. Charles Sicard, Intendant General of the
French army in Spain, with the consent of H.R.H. the Due d’Angouleme,
Commander-in-Chief of the Army
and
M. Victor Ouvrard, landowner, resident in Paris, 27 Rue de Richelieu, at
present in Bayonne, his guarantor being M. Gabriel-Julien Ouvrard, resident
in Paris, 24 Place Vendome, at present in Bayonne, 34 Rue Bourg Neuf, the
following contract has been concluded:

Article I. Victor Ouvrard commits himself to hold at the permanent disposal
  of the army six hundred four-span rack-wagons and six thousand mules
  complete with saddles and harness.
Article II. Three quarters of the said number of wagons and mules will be
  held at the disposal of the Intendant General at Bayonne and one quarter
  at Perpignan.
     The draught animals must be five to six years old, must stand at least
  four feet six inches chest-high, and must be sound and strong; as it will be
  difficult, however, to muster so many mules of the same type, the con¬
  tractor may, in exceptional cases, take bigger animals.
Article III. The mules purchased in the provinces of Poitou and Limousin
  will be transported in troops of a hundred or more. They will receive the
  prescribed ration of forage and will remain until further notice with the
  tenth and eleventh divisions. The mules purchased in Velay will also
  proceed by easy stages to the tenth and eleventh divisions, after they have
  been handed over to an assistant-quartermaster and the usual experts.
  They will receive the same ration of forage.
Article IV. The harness of a pack animal consists of a pack-saddle, a trough,
  a snaffle, a halter, two straps, two saddle-bags and a blanket, or, failing a
  blanket, a curry-comb, a brush and a wire-brush. The horses and mules
  yoked in the wagons will have the same harness as horses in French or
  Spanish wagons.
                                  Appendixes                               217

Article V. Under no circumstances should a wagon be loaded with more
  than five hundred kilos gross weight per yoke and a mule with more than
  a hundred and twenty-five kilos.
      In view of the mountainous terrain and the bad roads in Spain this
  restriction is essential; in France it need not be so rigorously applied.
      The laden wagons and mules may not travel more than six miles per
   day.
Article VI. The pack- and draught-animals will be divided into formations.
   Each mule brigade consists of a hundred and twenty-five mules with one
   mule-driver for every two animals, as well as eight reserve drivers in¬
   cluding the trumpeter and the blacksmith. It also contains:
                              1 Brigade Commander
                              1 Adjutant
                             2 Quartermasters
                             4 Cavalry N.C.O.s.
Article VII. The brigades of wagons will be composed according to the
  instructions of the Intendant.
      The brigades will be at the disposal of the Intendants and their deputies
  but may not under any circumstances be employed for other than purely
  military purposes; the rates of pay of the crews will be determined by the
  Intendant General.
Article VIII. As employees must provide their own clothes and mounts,
   they will receive one extra month’s pay as service bonus.
Article IX. Clothing and arming wagon- and mule-drivers will be the
  responsibility of the State; if the contractor is made responsible, he will
  receive compensation in accordance with the clothing regulations. Should
  the war last so long that the point is reached, as foreseen in the clothing
  regulations, where a fresh issue of clothing is necessary, then the cost will
  be borne by the State.
Article X. Employees, drivers and mule-drivers will receive food, forage
  and quarters in accordance with their service-grades. Wagon-horses and
  mules will receive the same forage rations as artillery horses, pack-
  animals as light artillery, in accordance with the regulations. These
  rations will be issued both in camp and on the march.
Article XI. The contractor will receive eleven francs fifty centimes per day
  and per yoke for the running costs of the six hundred wagons, which
  must be kept in a constant state of readiness. From this he must maintain
  the wagons, harness, horseshoes etc., but the cost of forage and stabling
  will be covered by the State.
Article XII. The hire of each properly harnessed and equipped mule will
  amount to three francs per day.
Article XIII. The money to be spent on wagon-drivers, mule-drivers,
  horses and mules will be paid to the contractor on the first of each month
  by the Army Paymaster on presentation of a monthly account. The final
  settlement will be made at the end of each month. Payment will be in
  cash, either in French or Spanish currency at the daily rate or in Treasury
  bills at the daily rate.
     If no settlement has been made within thirty days of the date fixed and
218                              Appendixes

  the Commissary General has not been paid in full, the sum still due to
  him will carry interest at five per cent per annum, till it is paid in full.
Article XIV. Losses incurred through unforeseeable circumstances will be
  borne by the State. Amongst these are:
      1. Capture by the enemy.
     2. Abandonment of equipment to the enemy.
     3. Losses through pestilence.
     4. Losses caused by shortage or inferior quality of foodstuffs or by
         forced marches.
  Losses must be recorded by the assistant-quartermasters. As compensation
  for these losses the contractor will receive six hundred francs for each
  wagon, five hundred francs for each horse or draught animal, six hundred
  francs for each pack animal with harness. It is expressly agreed that the
  contractor will be indemnified monthly for these losses on presentation of
  a list, which is compiled from the assistant-quartermasters’ records, in
  accordance with the regulations in Article XIII.
Article XV. The contract is valid for the whole duration of the campaign
  or as long as French troops are stationed in Spain. Should the mule and
  wagon brigades be partially or entirely disbanded during this period, the
  contractor will receive compensation on the following terms: If the mule
  and wagon service is entirely or partially disbanded within a year after
  the present contract comes into force, then the contractor will be paid the
  full value of each wagon and mule at the rates determined in the previous
  article.
     If disbanding only takes place in the second year, then compensation
  will be reduced to a sixth of the value. In both cases the animals and the
  wagons become the property of the State.
Article XVI. On expiry of the present contract, mules and wagons remain
  the property of the contractor.
Article XVlI. In order to give the contractor a reasonable time to sell his
  wagons and mules, the State will feed the horses and mules for three
  months after expiry of the contract or pay the contractor a corresponding
  sum in compensation. The latter will be at the rate of two francs daily
  per horse and mule.
Article XVIII. Should the means of transport specified in the contract prove
  inadequate, so compelling the contractor to requisition further horse- or
  ox-wagons, he will receive for these vehicles the price laid down by the
  appropriate local authority. From this money he must pay compensation
  to the original owners. For the trouble he has taken he will also receive
  ten per cent of the total amount.
Article XIX. The contractor will take over all mules which the State has
  already acquired and will be held responsible for them. The value of
  these mules and their harness will be determined by experts.
Article XX. The present contract is binding upon both contracting parties
  until the end of the campaign.
Article XXI. Should divergences of opinion arise over the interpretation or
  execution of the present contract, either with the Intendant General of
  the Army or between members of the Intendant’s staff and the Com-
                                   Appendixes                                 219

  missary General’s employees, they are to be settled by an arbitration
  tribunal. Each party will nominate an arbitrator and, if they cannot agree,
  they will choose a third who will have a casting vote.
     If the two arbitrators cannot agree on the third, each of them will
  write down two names: lots will then be drawn to decide which of the
  four persons suggested is to give the casting vote.
Article XXII. The registration fee for the present contract will be one franc.
  In duplicate at Bayonne on 5 th April, 1823.
                                                      Signed V. Ouvrard.
As guarantor
                                                          Signed J. Ouvrard.

   Seen and approved by the Chief Intendant of the Army, subject to the
   agreement of the Generalissimo of the Army, H.R.H. the Due Louis
   Antoine d’Angouleme.

                                       VI

      OUVRARD ON IMPRISONMENT FOR DEBT OR PENDING INVESTIGATION



                                                    Written in prison, 1826
                                                    Memoirs vol III pp. 103-21

                           1. Imprisonment for debt

   (The Paris prison) Sainte-Pelagie stands at the entrance to the Faubourg
Saint-Marceau next to the Jardin du Roi and is extremely uncomfortable
for inmates and visitors alike. Small rooms without fireplaces and long,
narrow, badly ventilated corridors, in which collect all the fumes from the
small cooking-stoves on which the prisoners must prepare their own meals,
make living-conditions almost unbearable. The courtyard is surrounded by
high walls and is so small that there is neither fresh air to breathe nor space to
move about.



. . . Sainte-Pelagie is also very uncomfortable for the prison staff, because its
structural shortcomings make it impossible to carry out the prison regula¬
tions. There can hardly be another prison in which such chaotic conditions
prevail. It was in vain that Baron Pasquier drew up reasonable prison regula¬
tions in the year 1811 and had them displayed on all the walls; even he could
not bring about any improvement in the situation, because it was essentially
a problem of accommodation. Baron Pasquier was more successful with the
second wing of the building, the department for convicts, which was organ-
220                              Appendixes

ized like a large factory, in which all prisoners, including children, were
employed on the manufacture of uniforms and other items of military
equipment, for which there was then a great demand. The atmosphere of
industry and comparative well-being in this department was in sad contrast
to the enforced idleness and the misery in the debtors’ prison. . . .
. . . The legislators of Germinal in the year VI were vastly mistaken, when
they assumed that imprisonment for debt was essential for trade! In my
view it harms trade just as much as the lotteries and gambling-halls, which
were reintroduced in the same period. By this I mean that moneylenders,
borrowers and gamblers were all equally ruined by these laws. The sole
beneficiary is the national exchequer.




   My personal experiences in Sainte-Pelagie will give a clearer picture of
conditions than any theoretical arguments.
   The number of people who are imprisoned for debt is about three hundred
a year, and this number rises—contrary to the general opinion—with an
increase in prosperity and in the circulation of money, and falls in times of
depression and shortage of money. In the last three years, which have wit¬
nessed an unprecedented commercial boom, the number of persons im¬
prisoned for debt has more than doubled.
   To give the reader a fair idea of the advantages which trade derives from
imprisonment for debt, I propose to consider the social status of the three
hundred prisoners as well as the nature and cause of their debts. Then I shall
deal with their creditors.
   Of the three hundred prisoners twenty or thirty are usually small traders,
who have borrowed from moneylenders. Ten to fifteen are bankrupt mer¬
chants who are held in custody until their affairs have been wound up. Not
surprisingly, they are always very small in number, because the appropriate
authorities have no interest in allowing matters to drag on too long, as there
is usually nothing to be gained by it and as trade in general is opposed to
imprisonment for debt. Its speculative transactions are based, after all, on
credit and not on the partners’ freedom. All the other prisoners, nearly three
hundred of them, are either young men of good family from all over France,
who have lost money, health, morals, inheritance and finally liberty in Paris,
or students, soldiers of all ranks, minor civil servants and pensioners,
foreigners and a few unfortunate workers, who had put their names to bills
in a moment of weakness. Almost all are victims of usury or revenge.
   A few examples may serve to prove this.
   If a prisoner is held for several months in Sainte-Pelagie, then it is no
longer because his creditors wish to force him to pay, for they already know
that he cannot pay, but out of revenge because the unfortunate culprits,
whose pleas to be set free pass unanswered, have been stung into using
insulting language.
   The French love freedom so much that no one remains five years in
Sainte-Pelagie because he will not pay but only because he cannot pay.
When he is set free at the end of five years, he is completely destitute.
                                    Appendixes                                  221

   The records of Sainte-Pelagie also have some very instructive things to
say about creditors, about the nature of their credit and the reasons behind
these cruel penalties. There are creditors or people who buy up outstanding
claims merely in order to land a hated opponent in prison.
   Women employ a third party to have their husbands imprisoned, formerly
to obtain a divorce, today with a view to obtaining a judicial separation or
to enjoying their extra-marital pleasures in greater freedom. Partners in a
business have a troublesome colleague locked up, thereby depriving him of
his rights in the business and of his share of the profits.
   Trustees or solicitors, who were described at one time as “cut-throats”,
employ stooges to have their former clients imprisoned for debt.
   The prison records of Sainte-Pelagie contain the names of countless
officers of the old and the new army, from Second-Lieutenants to Lieutenant-
Generals, who are on the retired list or on half-pay; of small rentiers and
officials on pension, all of them victims of moneylenders, whom they went
to for help and who now shamelessly exploit their desperate plight.
   Suppose, for example, that a man with a rente of a thousand francs wants
to borrow three thousand francs from a moneylender. He gets the money, if
he sui renders his rente or pension to the moneylender for five years and also
pays him a certain commission. As double security the moneylender demands
further that the borrower should sign ten promissory notes for five hundred
francs each, payable a few days before the end of the first half-year, but he
gives him a verbal assurance that he will make no use of these IOU’s. As the
borrower is seldom in a position to meet any of these, even when the first
falls due, he has to throw in his life insurance; if he refuses, then he is arrested,
 condemned and imprisoned.




   Charming ladies employ this method to get rid of lovers, whom they have
already ruined. Monsieur de Jouy, in his book Ermites en prison, quotes a
pretty poem to a woman, the creditor and mistress of a young man, for
whom, with the help of her dressmaker, she engineered a prison sentence.
   There is also an outstanding scholar in Sainte-Pelagie, who holds an
important post in the civil service and has never signed an IOU in his life. In
a moment of weakness he gave his wife full power of attorney, and, when she
lost heavily at gambling, she turned to a moneylender. She told her husband
nothing of this, and it was only when the bailiffs arrested him in the street
that he learned that he was heavily in debt.




   Imprisonment for debt, therefore, only benefits the moneylender, brings
ruin to a great many young people and misfortune to workers, small rentiers,
officials on pension, officers etc., etc., undermines public morals and en¬
courages crime.
222                               Appendixes

   When a prisoner, who has no family behind him, is released from Sainte-
Pelagie, he is liable to become all too often a burden on public or private
charity. Many find their way back to Sainte-Pelagie, after spending some
time in La Force and the Conciergerie! Those poor wretches, who are
released completely destitute from prison, where they have acquired more
vices than virtues, will probably be less scrupulous than before in earning a
livelihood. Must we not conclude from this that imprisonment for debt is
the basic cause of their misery, of their lack of scruples and of the consequent
lapses, which brought them into conflict with the law?




   The position of those in debtors’ prisons is even more desperate than is
generally supposed. Only someone who has himself witnessed it day after
day can form a true picture. If their creditors visit them at any time in prison,
they must be not only entirely devoid of humanity but also so obsessed by
hate as to be blind to their own interests, if they do not immediately arrange
for their debtors to be released. Each debtor is entitled to claim twenty
francs a month from his creditor, of which at least eight francs go towards
the compulsory charge for accommodation, leaving twelve francs, or forty
sous a day, for subsistence. In winter there is even less remaining for sub¬
sistence, for a stove must be rented and the heating paid for.
   I have suggested that imprisonment for debt should be confined to sums
over three thousand francs, for the simple reason that all debtors are made to
pay the same living-costs, which, therefore, fall particularly heavily on the
minor offenders.


                2. Imprisonment pending investigation

   I have given much thought to our penal system and would like to make a
few comments on it at this point.
   Before the introduction of the laws governing judicial inquiry, which we
owe to State Councillor Treilhard, there were three kinds of court order: an
order to appear before the police, an order to appear before the Court and an
order for arrest. The last of these could only be issued following an inquiry,
in order to safeguard the ordinary citizen against imprisonment on unjust
charges; furthermore, the arrest warrant must give the name, profession and
address of the arrested man, the reason for his arrest and the law under
which the arrest was being made; otherwise the prison authorities could not
take delivery of the accused without incurring a charge of false imprison¬
ment. The Court of Prosecution, a useful institution which was unfortun¬
ately abolished under the tyranny of the Empire, then pronounced judgment.
    The man who was intent upon gathering all power into his own hands
(Napoleon) was not content to abolish this time-honoured institution but
also introduced imprisonment pending investigation as a fresh penalty.
The warrant for arrest pending investigation need only give the name of the
accused, not die reason for his arrest. Whether the arrest is just or unjust,
                                  Appendixes                                223

 the prisoner can only be released after at least fifty to sixty days, for an
 investigation of even the simplest charge takes that amount of time.
    The new process of arrest pending investigation means, therefore, that a
 man can be put in prison in order to make it easier to try him, whereas
 formerly he could only be put in prison after he had been tried.
    By abolishing the preliminary investigation and the usual formalities,
 without which a warrant for arrest was once invalid, and by relieving the
 prison authorities of any responsibility, a three-fold assault was made, in my
 view, on the freedom of the individual. The facts prove better than all
 theoretical observations how much damage is done to society by these
 measures. In Paris 2,300 people a year are thrown into prison by this con¬
 venient and rapid expedient and only afterwards does a more or less pro¬
 tracted judicial inquiry begin. Only two thirds of the accused are handed
 over to the criminal police court or the assizes; a third, that is at least 800
 people, must be released, because, in spite of the most intensive inquiry,
 nothing incriminating can be laid against them. So, but for the introduction of
 the system of imprisonment pending investigation, eight hundred people
would not have been deprived of their freedom. Perhaps the unhappy
 consequences of this measure would be less severe if the judges entrusted
with the investigation were not, for the most part, young people or men
engaged on a temporary basis.
    At best I would grant the validity of such a summary process where
vagabonds are concerned, but, as the majority of the people arrested are
from the working-class and are permanently resident in Paris, I do not
understand why a judge should be given the dangerous and unpleasant
power to have these people imprisoned merely on suspicion, instead of
allowing them to remain with their families and at their work and simply
summoning them to appear before the Court.
    As a result of this misuse of prisons, the prison-accommodation, which is
in any case inadequate, is grossly overcrowded, a further burden for the
cost of forty to fifty thousand prison-days is placed upon the Exchequer,
the general public are demoralised and an irremediable injustice is done to
the victims as well as to their wives and children.
    Every patriotic and humane citizen must condemn the excessive severity
of our penal system, yet he must at the same time admit in all fairness that,
due to the deep sense of justice and humanity of our judges, their worst
effects have been considerably reduced. How often it happens that the judges
make a special effort to cut down the period of arrest and even pay com¬
pensation out of their own pockets to someone who has been wrongfully
arrested! The prison authorities, on the other hand, levy the same charge for
their “separate rooms” on the acquitted as on the condemned.
    How much injustice would be averted, how much misfortune remedied,
if the generous idea of compensation for wrongful arrest were made law!
I quote as an example the high-minded Minister of the unhappy Louis XVI,
whose humane efforts are in particularly blatant contrast to the tyranny
and severity of our present Ministers.
